# The Baby Bear Book

# Clinical Manifestations in the Neonatal and Early Infancy Period

- Acute metabolic encephalopathy
Seizures or neurological deficit
Hypotonia
Jaundice and liver dysfunction
Cardiac disease (e.g. cardiomyopathy)

# Approach

## History

Typical clinical presentation
Usually non-specific and subtle
» Lethargy or irritability
» Poor feeding
Vomiting
Floppy, seizures
» Failure to regain birth weight during neonatal period
- Pattern of presentation — well initially with development of symptoms (e.g. poor feeding, lethargy) later on or overwhelming neurological symptoms (e.g. seizures) during early neonatal period
- Birth history is usually unremarkable
- Consanguinity
Family history of IEM, early neonatal death

## Clinical Examination

- Few with dysmorphic features
Hypotonia/hypertonia
Jaundice, hepatomegaly
Respiratory distress
Cardiomegaly, heart failure

## First-Line Metabolic Investigations

- Blood gas pH and bicarbonate
Blood glucose
- Plasma lactate
- Plasma ammonia
- Plasma amino acid profile
- Plasma acylcarnitine
- Urine organic acid
- Urine (or blood) ketones

## Biochemical Abnormalities that may Suggest IEMs

- Severe persistent high anion gap metabolic acidosis

# Inborn Errors of Metabolism

- Severe lactic acidosis
- Respiratory alkalosis (may suggest urea cycle defect [UCD])
- Persistent hypoglycaemia, ketotic or non-ketotic
- Severe ketonuria (abnormal during neonatal period)
Hyperammonaemia

## High Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis

- Anion gap is defined as the difference between the serum \(\mathrm{Na^{+}}\) and the sum of serum \(\mathrm{Cl^-}\) plus \(\mathrm{HCO_3^-}\) (Normal anion gap \(= 7 - 16\mathrm{mmol / L}\))
- Metabolic acidosis with raised anion gap reflects an increase in an unmeasured anion that may be suggestive of an IEM causing acidosis

Organic acids — organic acidaemias (methylmalonic acidaemia (MMA), propionic acidaemia (PA), isovaleric acidaemia (IVA), glutaric aciduria [GA] type I)
Lactic acidosis — primary lactic acidosis, mitochondrial disorders, organic acidaemias, FAODs, gluconeogenesis defects
Ketosis (especially in neonates) — ketolytic defects (marked unanticipated ketosis without fasting), most IEMs except FAODs

- Hypoglycaemia, and/or hyperammonaemia in the presence of significant acidosis are highly suggestive of IEM

## Lactic Acidosis

- Lactic acidosis \(>2\mathrm{mmol / L}\)
- Hyperlactataemia is due to the elevation of pyruvate, the NADH/NAD ratio, acidosis or both
- Based on Cohen-Woods classification, lactic acidosis can be due to:

Tissue hypoxia or hypoperfusion (Type A) secondary to asphyxia/hypoxia, decreased cardiac output/left ventricular failure, severe anaemia, carbon monoxide poisoning
Underlying disease process (Type B1) — sepsis, renal failure, hepatic failure, seizures
Toxin or drug mediated (Type B2) — ethanol, methanol, ethylene glycol, salicylates
Inborn or acquired error of metabolism (Type B3) — primary lactic acidosis, mitochondrial disorders, organic acidaemias, aminoacidopathies, glycogen storage disorders (GSDs)

- Majority of the cases are caused by hypoxia, hypoperfusion due to sepsis, myocardial dysfunction.
- Lactic acidosis with ketosis is a nearly constant finding in most IEMs (except in pyruvate dehydrogenase, [PDH], deficiency and FAOD), whereas ketosis is absent in most hyperlactataemias secondary to tissue hypoxia.
- Sampling and handling errors can give spurious results. The blood sample should be a free-flowing venous or arterial sample to avoid spurious elevations. The blood sample tube should be placed in a biohazard specimen bag and then immersed into another bag with crushed ice and sent down to the lab immediately.

# The Baby Bear Book

## Hyperammonaemia

Hyperammonaemia

>100 μmol/L in neonates
>50 μmol/L in children
>35 μmol/L in adults

- Acute hyperammonaemia is a medical emergency for which immediate action must be taken to minimise permanent brain damage. If in doubt, a metabolic physician should be consulted.
- The work up of an encephalopathic child of unknown cause should always include the measurement of ammonia. Persistent hyperammonaemia will lead to cerebral oedema and brain herniation if left untreated.
- Severe hyperammonaemia \((>500\mu \mathrm{mol} / \mathrm{L})\) is either caused by a UCD (especially with respiratory alkalosis, no ketosis) or by an organic acidaemia (PA, MMA, IVA with metabolic acidosis, ketosis).
- Sampling and handling errors can give spurious results. The blood sample should be a free-flowing venous or arterial sample to avoid spurious elevations. The blood sample tube should be placed in a biohazard specimen bag and immersed into another bag with crushed ice and sent down to the lab immediately.
- To consider high caloric nutrition with no protein and sodium benzoate in severe hyperammonaemia in acute decompensation of UCD.

## Basic Principles in Acute Management

- The main aim in the acute management of IEMs is to stop further build-up of potential toxic metabolites by promoting anabolism and preventing catabolism.
- Stop the offending substance during acute decompensation.
Supportive care

Cardiorespiratory support
Neurological

Neuroprotection in acute encephalopathy
Seizure management

- Fluid and electrolyte/acid-base correction and maintenance

To consider sodium bicarbonate/acetate correction in persistent severe metabolic acidosis due to IEMs
Correction of hypoglycaemia

- Treat underlying infection

- High calorie hydration/nutrition

Dextrose

1g dextrose provides 3.4 kcal
To provide intravenous (IV) dextrose \(10\%\) /sodium chloride \(0.9\%\) with electrolyte supplementation as first line of hydration/nutrition during acute decompensation

# CHAPTER 60

# Anaemia

Lee Ming Wei; Rajat Bhattacharyya; Lam Ching Mei, Joyce

## General Approach to Anaemia Definition

True anaemia occurs when there is a decrease in the circulating red cell mass, leading to impaired ability to meet the body's demand for oxygen. Spurious anaemia occurs when there is a dilutional effect of an increase in plasma volume, e.g. in fluid overload and cardiac failure.

## Clinical Symptoms

Symptoms include fatigue, breathlessness, dizziness, headaches and blackouts.

Severity of clinical symptoms depends on:

- Severity of anaemia
- Speed of onset — Gradual onset better tolerated
- Age and cardiovascular status of patient — Better tolerated in young

## History

- Previous blood tests and transfusion history — For comparison
Duration of symptoms — Recent or longstanding
Family history — Consanguinity, congenital anaemias, gallstones
- Dietary history — Goat's milk ingestion, meat intake, vegans
- Drugs and exposure to toxic chemicals
Blood loss — Including menstrual history
Abnormal bruising
History of other illnesses — Diarrhoea, signs of hypothyroidism, autoimmune disorders
Medical history — Neonatal hyperbilirubinaemia in G6PD deficiency

## Clinical Examination

Stature - Short in Fanconi anaemia
- Skin and sclera — Pallor, jaundice, purpura, bruises, petechiae
Signs of nutritional deficiencies — Iron, ascorbic acid
Signs of chronic illness — Thyroid, renal
Cardiovascular - Heart failure

- Organomegaly — Lymph nodes, liver, spleen
- Rectal examination (if necessary) — Melaena

## Investigations

Full blood count (FBC), peripheral blood film (PBF) and reticulocyte count can give a lot of information and help in deciding what further investigations need to be done.

Blood investigations like liver function test (LFT), renal panel, thyroid function test (TFT), iron studies, haemoglobin (Hb) electrophoresis, alpha thalassaemia mutation, direct Coombs test (DCT), autoimmune markers, Vitamin B12 and folate are ordered on the basis of the suspected diagnosis.

Table 15.1 Possible Common Causes of Anaemia with Other Cell Lines Abnormalities

|  Associated Cell Line Abnormalities | Causes  |
| --- | --- |
|  Pancytopaenia | Leukaemia Drugs Aplastic anaemia Hypersplenism Vitamin B12/folate deficiency Infection Autoimmune diseases  |
|  Anaemia and low platelets | Haemolytic uraemic syndrome Thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura Disseminated intravascular coagulation Evans syndrome  |
|  Anaemia and high platelets | Iron deficiency Infection Post-splenectomy anaemia  |

## When to Do a Bone Marrow Aspirate

- Malignancy suspected — Blasts, primitive white cells in the PBF, tumour elsewhere
- Myelodysplastic change seen — Hypogranular, hypolobulated neutrophils
- Aplasia suspected — Reticulocytopaenia, pancytopaenia
Anaemia of uncertain origin

Send bone marrow aspirate (BMA) for morphology, immunophenotype and cytogenetics. If lymphoma or a metastatic solid tumour is suspected, bilateral BMA and trephine biopsies should be performed.

## Treatment

- Treat underlying condition. In iron deficiency, remember to continue treating for at least 3 months after Hb normalises and a minimum of 6 months to replenish body iron stores.
- Treat symptoms of anaemia, e.g. heart failure.
- Transfuse when there is ongoing blood loss/haemolysis or if there is symptomatic anaemia. In longstanding anaemia, there is no need to transfuse if Hb is stable and patient is not symptomatic.
- If Hb is very low, correct anaemia slowly in stages, e.g. if Hb is \(3\mathrm{g / dL}\), correct to Hb of \(5\mathrm{g / dL}\) on first day, then to Hb of \(7\mathrm{g / dL}\) the next day.

## Useful Formulae in Anaemia

Volume of packed cell transfusion (ml) = (Desired Hb minus Current Hb in g/dL) × (Body weight in kg) × 3.5

Mentzer Index = Mean corpuscular volume (MCV)/Red blood cell (RBC) (<13 more likely thalassaemia trait, >13 more likely iron deficiency)

# Anaemia

![img-2.jpeg](img-2.jpeg)

## Iron Deficiency Anemia

Iron deficiency is an important cause of anaemia in children, and often presents as a mild to moderate microcytic, hypochromic anaemia. Chronic iron deficiency can result in other non-haematological consequences, including effects on neurocognition and immunity. Causes of iron deficiency anaemia (IDA) include nutritional insufficiency, gastrointestinal blood loss, malabsorption syndromes, menstrual losses, infants on an exclusive fresh cow's milk diet.

## Diagnostic Workup

Conditions that can produce a hypochromic, microcytic anaemia and be confused with IDA include hereditary anaemias (commonly thalassaemia in Singapore) and anaemia

of chronic disease. The initial workup should always include a thalassaemia screen to exclude the concomitant thalassaemia trait. However, it should be noted that co-existing iron deficiency can artifactually result in a low $\mathrm{HbA}_2$ level, masking the diagnosis of the beta-thalassaemia trait. A repeat thalassaemia screen should be performed if microcytosis persists despite correction of iron deficiency.

Common laboratory tests used to diagnose iron deficiency include:

- Serum iron — There is diurnal variation with higher serum values late in the day. Levels also fluctuate with dietary iron intake. Serum iron cannot be used on its own to diagnose iron deficiency.
- Serum transferrin/total iron binding capacity (TIBC) — Transferrin is the carrier protein of iron in the blood and is assayed directly as serum transferrin or indirectly as the TIBC of the patient. This has limited utility on its own, but has some value when used to derive the transferrin saturation.

Transferrin saturation = Serum iron/TIBC

# Anaemia

433

- Iron deficiency is more likely to be present when the transferrin saturation is $<15\%$

- Serum ferritin is a sensitive parameter for the assessment of iron stores in healthy subjects. However, ferritin is an acute phase reactant and may be elevated in the presence of chronic inflammation, infection, malignancy or liver disease. Values of \(< 30\mathrm{mcg/L}\) are suggestive of iron deficiency in children. A serum ferritin level of \(>100\mathrm{mcg/L}\) suggests that iron deficiency is unlikely to be present.
- Soluble transferrin receptor is upregulated when the iron supply is inadequate and is a useful measure of iron status as it is not affected by inflammation or infection. Serum levels are directly proportional to the degree of iron deficiency. This test is available only in some specialised laboratories in Singapore.
- Bone marrow iron staining is considered the gold standard for the diagnosis of iron deficiency, but is not routinely performed due to the invasive nature of the test.

## Management

Successful treatment of IDA in infants and children lies in appropriate dosing and scheduling of oral iron therapy, dietary modifications and follow-up assessment for response.

- Oral iron therapy with \(3 - 6\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) of elemental iron. The supplement should be given between meals and with juice (not milk, as iron is chelated by calcium in milk). See Table 15.3 for a summary of the elemental iron content of common iron supplements.
- Dietary modifications — Iron-containing complementary foods should be introduced at 4–6 months of age. Exclusive feeding with fresh cow's milk for infants younger than 12 months is not advised as it is a poor source of iron. High iron-content food products include red meats, cereals fortified with iron and dark green leafy vegetables.
- Parenteral iron therapy should be reserved for patients with severe, persistent anaemia who have proven intolerance to oral supplements, malabsorption or poor compliance to oral therapy. Parenteral iron should be used with caution because of the risk of anaphylaxis. Oral iron preparation should not be administered at the same time as intravenous (IV) iron infusion as absorption of iron is reduced. Oral iron therapy should only be commenced at least 5–7 days after iron infusion. See Table 15.3 for a summary of the elemental iron content of common iron therapy preparation.

A patient's total iron deficit can be calculated using the Ganzoni formula. However, it is not commonly used in clinical practice as parenteral iron preparations usually state specific dosing requirements. See Table 15.4 for a summary of the dosing recommended for parenteral iron preparations.
Ganzoni formula:
Iron deficit (mg) = Body weight (kg) × (Target Hb [g/dL] - Actual Hb [g/dL]) × 2.4 + iron stores (mg)*

*Iron stores: Can use 500 mg for ≥35 kg patient or 15 mg/kg for <35 kg patients

- **Red cell transfusions** should only be reserved for patients with severe, symptomatic anaemia compromising end-organ function. Administer with caution to avoid precipitating cardiac failure.

## Assessment of Response to Therapy

Response to iron replacement therapy can be monitored with serial reticulocyte counts and Hb assessments. After therapeutic doses of oral iron, a reticulocytosis should be seen within 72 hours. Hb should rise by at least 1 g/dL within 4 weeks and should be monitored subsequently every 2–3 months until it reaches the age-adjusted normal range. Iron supplements should then be continued for a minimum of 3 more months to replace storage iron pools. Early discontinuation of iron therapy will frequently lead to a recurrence of iron deficiency.

The most common causes for non-responders to iron supplements are compliance failures and intolerance to medication as iron formulations do result in gastric irritation. Ongoing gastrointestinal blood loss or other causes of anaemia also have to be evaluated in non-responders.

# Anaemia

![img-3.jpeg](img-3.jpeg)

Figure 15.3. Approach to diagnosis, management and assessment of treatment response in iron deficiency anaemia.

ACD — anaemia of chronic disease; Hb — haemoglobin; IDA — iron deficiency anaemia; Retic — reticulocytes; Transferrin saturation — serum iron/total iron binding capacity

## Thalassaemia

Thalassaemia is the most common genetic disease in the world. About 4% of the population of Singapore carries the alpha or beta thalassaemia gene. Adult Hb comprises 2 alpha (α) and 2 beta (β) globin chains in a tetramer (α2β2). Defect in any of the globin chains will cause thalassaemia — α globin chain defect causing α thalassaemia and β globin chain defect causing β thalassaemia.

## Beta (β) Thalassaemia

Two β-globin genes are present — one on each chromosome 11. More than 300 mutations have been described in this gene with a resultant reduction in the production of normal β-globin chains.

## β Thalassaemia Minor or Trait

Clinically asymptomatic. Presents with microcytic anaemia (Hb 9–11 g/dL). PBF shows microcytosis, hypochromia, target cells. Hb electrophoresis shows raised HbA₂, which in conjunction with microcytic anaemia is diagnostic of β thalassaemia trait. No treatment or routine follow-up is necessary. However, family screening, genetic counselling and referral to the National Thalassaemia Registry (NTR) are important.

## β Thalassaemia Major

Clinical Presentation

- Presents at <1 year of age, usually at 3–6 months of age.

b4854_Sec-15.indd 435

12-Dec-22 11:08:09 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

» Anaemia and mild jaundice. Hepatosplenomegaly is present.

Diagnosis

» Severe microcytic anaemia.
» PBF: Microcytic hypochromic RBCS, target cells, polychromasia, nucleated RBCs.
» Hb electrophoresis — Absent or severely reduced HbA, raised HbF and HbA₂. HbH stain negative unless concomitant α thalassaemia is present.

Treatment

» Blood transfusion

15-20 ml/kg every 4-6 weeks. Keep baseline Hb 9-10 g/dL.
Blood is ABO and Rh matched. Preferably extended red cell phenotype (C, c, D, E, e) matched
Leucodepletion - At donation or by bedside filter

- Monitor for long-term complications of transfusion

Iron overload (each bag of blood contains about \(200\mathrm{mg}\) of iron). Effects of iron overload include: Cardiomyopathy, liver cirrhosis, endocrinopathy (short stature, delayed puberty, hypothyroidism, hypoparathyroidism, diabetes mellitus), skin pigmentation
» Risk of blood-borne infections — Hepatitis B, Hepatitis C, HIV, Syphilis, Malaria, Cytomegalovirus.

- Iron Chelation

» Desferrioxamine (DFO)

Start when serum ferritin \(>1000\mathrm{mcg / L}\). Usually after 10-20 transfusions and 18-24 months of age.
Dose \(20 - 50\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) 5-7 days/week. Start at a lower dose and increase slowly.
Infuse subcutaneously over 8-12 hours each night.
Side effects: Painful swelling at injection sites, visual and auditory neurotoxicity, osteoporosis.
Increases risk of Yersinia enterocolitica infection; Stop during diarrhoea and abdominal pain.
Concomitant Vitamin C 50-100 mg 1 hour after start of injection daily helps iron excretion.

» Deferiprone (Ferriprox/L1)

First oral iron chelator licensed for use
Particularly effective for myocardial iron removal
Dose \(75 - 100\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) TDS given with meals
Risks: Agranulocytosis \((< 1\%)\) regular FBC monitoring is recommended, arthralgia of large joints, transaminitis.

» Deferasirox (ExJade)

Oral iron chelator
Dose \(20 - 30\mathrm{mg / kg / dose}\) once daily before meals. To be dissolved in water or apple juice.

# Anaemia

437

Side effects — Skin rash, gastrointestinal disturbances, renal toxicity (glomerulopathy/tubulopathy)
Licensed for use in children older than 2 years

- Oral chelators can be combined with DFO in severe iron overload conditions.

- Splenectomy for Hypersplenism

Can be considered when older than 5 years. Reduces requirement for transfusion.
Mostly by laparoscopic approach with minimal morbidity.
Indications: Transfusion requirement \(>220\mathrm{ml / kg}\) year, hypersplenism, mechanical discomfort.
Pneumococcal, meningococcal and Haemophilus Influenzae B vaccinations before splenectomy.
Penicillin prophylaxis after splenectomy: \(250\mathrm{mg}\) BD long term.
Risks of thrombosis, pulmonary hypertension, infections by encapsulated organisms to be considered before performing splenectomy.

- Haematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation (HSCT)

Only curative option in thalassaemia. However, treatment-related morbidity and mortality is around \(10\%\).
HLA-matched sibling HSCT is the best option with \(>80\%\) thalassaemia-free survival rate.
Alternate donor transplant, including matched unrelated donor, cord, haploidentical transplants are also possible options.

Supplements

Folic acid 1-5 mg once a day
Vitamin C 50-100 mg once a day if on DFO therapy
Calcium supplements

## β Thalassaemia Intermedia

Severity of the disease is in between thalassaemia major and thalassaemia minor. The disease spectrum ranges from intermittent transfusion requirements (transfusion intervals more than every 3 months) to no transfusion at all. Presents usually after 1 year of age with variable pallor, jaundice and hepatosplenomegaly. However, extramedullary haematopoiesis, skeletal changes and iron overload can be problematic, even in those with minimal transfusion requirements, and hence need to be monitored closely.

Causes
HbE/β thalassaemia
Homozygous \(\beta +\) thalassaemia genes
» Concomitant α thalassaemia
Diagnosis
» Moderate microcytic hypochromic anaemia (Hb between 6 and \(9\mathrm{g / dL}\))

# Anaemia

439

PBF shows hypochromic microcytic RBCs, target cells, polychromasia, nucleated RBCs
Hb electrophoresis: No or severely reduced HbA, raised HbF and presence of HbE (in HbE/β thalassaemia)

Treatment

Folic acid - 1-5 mg once a day
Transfusion as necessary, during intercurrent infections, growth failure, skeletal changes
Splenectomy may be considered in case of significant enlargement of spleen
Hydroxyurea (hydroxycarbamide) may reduce transfusion requirement and extramedullary haematopoiesis

## Alpha (α) Thalassaemia

### α Thalassaemia Minor

(1 or 2 gene deletion or non-deletional mutation)

Clinically asymptomatic
Diagnosis

FBC parameters may show microcytosis and hypochromia in 2-gene deletion.
However, single gene deletion can present with a completely normal FBC and are called silent carriers.
Diagnosis can be done only by genetic testing as Hb electrophoresis and HbH stains are normal.
HbH stain may be positive for HbH inclusion bodies in two-gene deletion carriers.
DNA analysis shows either one or two alpha gene deletions or rarely nondeletional changes like Hb Constant Spring \((-a / aa, -a / -a, -- / aa, -a / acs)\).

Treatment

- No treatment or follow-up is necessary. Lifespan is normal. However, family screening, genetic counselling and referral to the NTR is important.

## Haemoglobin H (HBH) Disease

Clinical Presentation

Clinical presentation is that of thalassaemia intermedia, unless concomitant non-deletional \(\alpha\) thalassaemia is present (e.g. Hb Constant Spring), resulting in a more severe phenotype
Usually presents with pallor, mild jaundice and hepatosplenomegaly
However, early presentation in infants is also possible

Diagnosis

PBF shows microcytosis, hypochromia and striking poikilocytosis
HbH stain shows typical golf ball appearance (HbH Inclusion bodies)
DNA analysis commonly shows --/a or --/acs

Treatment

Folic acid supplementation \(1 - 5\mathrm{mg / d}\)
Blood transfusion as necessary (infections, growth failure, skeletal changes, etc.)
Splenectomy if significant splenomegaly causing mechanical discomfort and hypersplenism

# The Baby Bear Book

## Fetal Hydrops Syndrome/Bart's Hydrops Fetalis

Clinical Presentation

Maternal toxaemia during pregnancy
Hydrops fetalis
Stillbirth or early postnatal death

Diagnosis

Should be suspected antenatally in case of fetal hydrops and investigated appropriately
FBC shows very severe anaemia. HbH band in Hb electrophoresis may be seen
DNA analysis shows \(- / -\) (no \(\alpha\) chains)

Treatment

Prevention is the best therapeutic strategy because of the severity of the disease.
It may be possible to do fetal transfusions at regular intervals and early delivery followed by transfusions after birth and early transplant. There are some reported survivors in literature.

## Co-Inheritance of $\alpha$ & $\beta$ Thalassaemia

Since the prevalence of both $\alpha$ and $\beta$ gene defects are common in Southeast Asia, co-inheritance of $\alpha$ and $\beta$ thalassaemia in various combinations are not uncommon. This actually reduces the severity of the clinical presentation as the globin chain imbalance is less, causing less haemolysis. DNA analysis for both $\alpha$ and $\beta$ thalassaemia genes is important to make the exact diagnosis. Family screening, appropriate genetic counselling are important, along with referral to the NTR.

## Genetic Counselling and Prenatal Diagnosis

In Singapore, all citizens and permanent residents with confirmed thalassaemia should be referred to the NTR. The NTR collates national data on thalassaemia, provides free counselling on thalassaemia and offers thalassaemia screening to family members at subsidised rates.

Couples who are both heterozygous for the same type of thalassaemia carry a 25% risk of having a child with thalassaemia major phenotype. Through counselling and screening, these couples can be prospectively identified and offered the option of prenatal diagnosis. The preferred method for prenatal diagnosis is by chorionic villus sampling, and this is done at around 12 weeks of gestation and the sample is then subjected to DNA analysis. Providing a confirmed genetic diagnosis will enable the couple to make an informed decision about the pregnancy, especially in cases of homozygous $\beta$ thalassaemia and 4-gene deletion $\alpha$ thalassaemia, where the clinical phenotype is predicted to be thalassaemia major and fetal hydrops, respectively.

Pre-implantation genetic diagnosis (PGD) is now possible by selecting embryos that are not affected by thalassaemia. Theoretically, if there is an older sibling with thalassaemia major, PGD for thalassaemia can be combined with tissue matching with the affected sibling, with a view to providing tissue-matched stem cells for transplantation

# CHAPTER 61

# Bleeding Disorders

Lee Ming Wei; Rajat Bhattacharyya; Lam Ching Mei, Joyce

## Physiology of Haemostasis

Haemostasis refers to the maintenance of vascular integrity when there is injury, at the same time limiting the clot to the site of injury without systemic propagation. It involves an interplay between platelets, the vascular endothelium and the coagulation factors. It consists of a primary phase, which results in the production of a platelet plug and a secondary phase with activation of procoagulant proteins resulting in the formation of a cross-linked fibrin clot.

When uncontrolled, the haemostatic response can lead to thrombosis and tissue damage. The patency of vessels in normal, uninjured tissues is maintained by natural inhibitors of coagulation, consisting of:

- Tissue factor pathway inhibitor
Antithrombins
Protein C/Protein S pathway
- The fibrinolytic system, which degrades fibrin and is necessary to allow for complete wound healing

## Workup of a Child with Suspected Bleeding Disorder

### History

Nature of the bleed:

» Location and type — Bleeding from skin, mucosal surfaces, petechiae and purpura are typically seen in thrombocytopaenia, whereas joint, muscle, soft tissue bleeds and palpable ecchymoses are more likely to be seen in a coagulation defect.
» Duration — Bleeding that stops and then recurs quickly is suggestive of a true underlying bleeding disorder.
» Time of onset — Acute bleeds that have occurred recently over a period of days to weeks without prior history is suggestive of an acquired disorder; bleeding shortly after birth (e.g. oozing when cord separates, post-circumcision) suggests a congenital condition.
» Menorrhagia in pubertal females — Up to \(20\%\) girls with menorrhagia at menarche have now been recognised to have a bleeding disorder.

442

b4854_Sec-15.indd 442

12-Dec-22 11:08:10 AM

# Bleeding Disorders

» Any precipitating factors, e.g. trauma, dental extractions and surgery — Bleeds precipitated by relatively minor injuries or surgery are more likely to be due to a significant underlying bleeding disorder.

Family history and pedigree:

» Ask about bleeding history from all members of the family, especially in male relatives to exclude X-linked disorders.
» Haemophilia can result in abnormal bleeding in a female carrier.
» Menstrual history and obstetric history of female relatives.

- Drug history:

» Aspirin, non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs)
» Anticonvulsants and antibiotics (e.g. penicillins) causing drug-induced thrombocytopenia
» Warfarin, low molecular weight heparins

## Clinical Examination

- Skin for petechiae/purpura/telangiectasia
- Bleeding from nasal and oral mucosae
- Pallor and other signs of anaemia
- Joints — Swelling or chronic changes (e.g. contractures) secondary to repeated bleeding episodes
- Deep tissue bleeds and intramuscular (IM) swelling
Systemic illness, e.g. liver or collagen vascular disease
Evidence of non-accidental injuries

## Investigations

- Full blood count (FBC) with a peripheral blood smear — look at all three cell lines:

» Haemoglobin (Hb) may be decreased if there is significant blood loss.
» Bicytopaenia or pancytopaenia may indicate bone marrow failure syndromes or infiltrating marrow lesions like leukaemia/lymphoma.
Platelet counts to determine severity of thrombocytopenia. Platelet morphology to look at the size of the platelets. A decreased count may be the result of platelet clumping from EDTA-dependent antibodies (spurious thrombocytopenia). Large platelets are seen in Bernard-Soulier Syndrome and May-Hegglin anomaly. Decreased platelet size may indicate Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome. Platelet morphology is important, as a mean platelet volume from an automated count may not accurately reflect the platelet size.

- Prothrombin time (PT) and activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT):

» Surveys the secondary phase of haemostasis.
» Normal ranges are age-dependent. In newborns, PT and aPTT may be mildly raised due to immaturity of the coagulation system. Most revert to adult levels by 6 months of age.
The PT evaluates the extrinsic and common pathway.

b4854_Sec-15.indd 443

12-Dec-22 11:08:10 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

The INR is commonly reported with the PT. However, this should be used only to guide warfarin therapy and not as a laboratory test of coagulation.
The aPTT evaluates the intrinsic system and common pathway.
See Table 15.6 for interpretation of an abnormal platelet, PT and/or aPTT results.

- Mixing studies of PT and aPTT:

Abnormal PT and aPTT results without a history of bleeding should be followed by a mixing study unless obvious clinical signs of bleeding are present, indicating a likely bleeding disorder.
This is performed by mixing equal volumes of a patient's plasma with pooled normal plasma containing normal levels of clotting factors.
If the PT or aPTT normalises, a factor deficiency may be present.
If the PT or aPTT is persistently prolonged beyond the upper limit of the normal range, an inhibitor may present (e.g. specific factor inhibitor, lupus anticoagulant)
Note that the presence of a lupus anticoagulant only causes an in-vitro prolongation of the PT or aPTT and does not result in any clinical bleeding symptoms.

Factor assays:

When the aPTT mixing study indicates a likely factor deficiency, quantitate factors VIII, IX, XI and the von Willebrand factor, as these deficiencies are associated with clinical bleeding.
Deficiencies in factor XII, prekallikrein and high molecular weight kininogen can cause an increase in the aPTT but are not associated with clinical bleeding.
When the PT mixing study indicates a factor deficiency, factor VII should be quantitated.

Thrombin time (TT):

- Laboratory test looking at the time required for a clot to form when thrombin is added to plasma. Increased TT can be due to:

Low fibrinogen activity

Dysfunctional fibrinogen
Presence of fibrin split products

Heparin contamination

- The reptilase clotting time can be used to exclude heparin contamination as the action of reptilase is not affected by heparin. It will be normal if the TT is prolonged due to heparin contamination.

- Fibrinogen measurement:

- Fibrinogen can be measured quantitatively and functionally to detect hypofibrinogenaemia and dysfibrinogenaemia

- Platelet aggregation studies:

These measure the degree and pattern of platelet aggregation after the addition of platelet agonists (adenosine diphosphate [ADP], adrenaline, collagen, thrombin, arachidonic acid, and ristocetin)
» Allows for the diagnosis of storage pool defects, Bernard-Soulier Syndrome and Glanzmann thrombasthaenia

## Thrombocytopaenia

This is defined as a platelet count of $<100 \times 10^9/\text{L}$. A low platelet count needs to be confirmed by a review of the peripheral smear, especially in an asymptomatic child. False values (pseudothrombocytopaenia) can arise from aggregation of platelets in the syringe caused by agglutination by EDTA antibodies or from a clotted sample.

## Causes of Thrombocytopaenia

- Platelet destruction/consumption
» Immune-mediated

## Immune Thrombocytopaenia

ITP is an acute self-limiting condition with a peak incidence between 1 and 6 years of age and usually lasting <12 months in duration. Children younger than 1 year or older than 10 years tend to have a more chronic course. ITP is a diagnosis of exclusion based on a suggestive history and absence of findings indicating an alternative cause of thrombocytopaenia.

The onset is acute, usually after an antecedent viral illness 2–3 weeks earlier. Symptoms include the sudden onset of petechiae, purpura, epistaxis, oral mucosal bleeding and, less commonly, haematuria and gastrointestinal tract bleeding.

Physical examination usually reveals a healthy child with no evidence of pallor, weight loss or chronic illness. There should be no hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy or skeletal defects.

## Investigations

- FBC and peripheral blood film (PBF)

- Thrombocytopaenia should be the only finding, without evidence of involvement of other cell lines. The PBF should be examined to exclude spurious thrombocytopaenia or abnormal platelet morphology.

b4854_Sec-15.indd 447

# The Baby Bear Book

- Autoimmune markers (anti-nuclear antibody, double-stranded DNA antibodies, complement)

- This is not mandatory but can be screened if there is any suggestive history or signs of an underlying autoimmune disorder, especially in older children.

- Viral serologies/polymerase chain reaction

- This is not mandatory, but can be useful to find out if an underlying viral aetiology can be identified, especially in very young infants.

- Bone marrow studies

- This is only indicated if there is any evidence of bone marrow pathology, e.g. involvement of other cell lines, abnormal blasts, organomegaly. A bone marrow aspirate (BMA) is **not routinely required** for the diagnosis of ITP. It may be performed prior to the commencement of corticosteroid therapy in some institutions to exclude an underlying leukaemia, as steroids can mask an early presentation of haematological malignancies, but this is also not mandatory.

## Management

The decision to offer conservative or medical treatment remains controversial as there is no universally accepted 'safe' platelet level. However, observational studies have shown a lower rate of major spontaneous haemorrhages if the platelet count is $\geq 20 \times 10^9/\text{L}$. A careful discussion should be held with the family regarding risks and benefits of treatment options.

- Conservative

Most children will recover spontaneously without any medical treatment within 12 months
However, care must be taken to avoid contact sports, rough play and IM injections
» Drugs that interfere with platelet function, e.g. aspirin, NSAIDS, should be avoided

- Intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG)

» Blocks Fc receptors on reticuloendothelial organs, e.g. spleen, responsible for the destruction of antibody-coated platelets
» Dose is between 1 and \(2\mathrm{g / kg}\)
Response in platelet count is expected within 72 hours
Adverse effects include fever, nausea, headache, anaphylaxis and serum sickness
» Slowing the rate of IVIG infusion may alleviate side effects

Corticosteroids

Interferes with the production of anti-platelet antibodies by B-lymphocytes
A BMA is not mandatory, but may be performed according to institutional practice before commencing steroid therapy as it can mask an early presentation of leukaemia
» Choice of corticosteroids includes prednisolone 2–4 mg/kg/day up to 120 mg daily for 5–7 days or dexamethasone 0.6 mg/kg/day up to 40 mg per day for 4 days
» Repeated courses may be necessary for long-term remission

# Bleeding Disorders

## Chronic Immune Thrombocytopaenia

About 10–30% of children with acute ITP develop chronic persistent thrombocytopaenia beyond 12 months of duration. An underlying autoimmune disorder/collagen vascular disease has to be excluded, especially in older children. Patients with chronic ITP may not always need treatment, especially if the platelet counts remain ≥20 × 10⁹/L as the platelet count alone does not predict the risk of haemorrhage.

Spontaneous recovery can still occur a few years after the original diagnosis. In refractory, symptomatic patients, treatment options include thrombopoietin receptor agonists, rituximab, azathioprine, danazol, vinca alkaloids and splenectomy.

## Neonatal Thrombocytopaenia

The platelet count in both pre-term and term infants at birth is the same as adult values (150–400 × 10⁹/L) as platelet production is established towards the end of the first trimester of pregnancy. The incidence of thrombocytopaenia in the neonatal intensive care setting is ~25%.

## Causes

In a ‘sick’ infant

Placental insufficiency
Perinatal asphyxia
Congenital viral infection (TORCHES infection)
Necrotising enterocolitis
Neonatal sepsis
Congenital heart disease

In a ‘well-looking’ infant

Neonatal alloimmune
Thrombocytopaenia
Maternal autoimmune thrombocytopaenia
Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome
Amegakaryocytic thrombocytopaenia
Trisomy 13, 18 (there will be characteristic syndromic features)

## Management

Management of neonatal thrombocytopaenia is dependent on the clinical condition of the child. There are no widely accepted guidelines for platelet transfusions in neonates. Factors to consider include the gestational age of the infant, the underlying condition of the child, whether any active bleeding is present and if any invasive procedures are being performed. As a general rule, platelets should be given to thrombocytopaenic neonates when the degree of thrombocytopaenia alone or in combination with other complications results in an unacceptable risk of haemorrhage, and they should be given in tandem with aggressive therapy for the underlying conditions precipitating the thrombocytopaenia. Most centres would aim to keep the platelet count above 20–50 × 10⁹/L.

# The Baby Bear Book

## Neonatal Alloimmune Thrombocytopaenia (NAIT)

This is a condition similar to the rhesus haemolytic disease of the newborn where the mother produces anti-platelet antibodies directed against paternal platelet antigens present in fetal platelets. The incidence has been quoted as 1 in 1000 pregnancies although it is believed to be rarer in Asian populations. Human platelet antigen 1a (HPA-1a) is the most common antigen implicated.

## Presentation

- \(40\%\) can occur in the first pregnancy with no prior sensitisation.
- The typical presentation is a well-looking newborn with petechiae and purpura.
However, about \(20\%\) of neonates can present with an intracranial bleed.
- The degree of thrombocytopenia can be very severe with platelet counts frequently \(< 10 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\).
- Resolution of the thrombocytopaenia occurs in 3-6 weeks after delivery.

## Investigation

- There is no confirmatory test for the diagnosis of NAIT.
- However, the diagnosis is highly suggestive if an anti-platelet antibody can be demonstrated in the mother with the corresponding platelet antigen shown to be present on the father and infant.

## Management

- For the affected neonate, cranial imaging should be performed to exclude intracranial haemorrhage.
- If clinically indicated, transfusion with antigen-negative platelets is preferable. However, antigen typing of platelet units is not routinely done in Singapore. Transfusion with maternal or random platelets is the next best option.
- IVIG at a dose of \(1 - 2\mathrm{g / kg}\) can also be used to increase the platelet count.
- The risk of NAIT and its consequences increases with each subsequent pregnancy.
- Management of future pregnancies needs to be coordinated with a specialist obstetrician, as monitoring of fetal platelet counts, maternal administration of corticosteroids/IVIG or intrauterine platelet transfusions may be necessary.

## Congenital Bleeding Disorders

### Haemophilia A and B

Most patients with congenital bleeding disorders will be issued a local *Medik Awas* (Medic Alert) card, as well as the World Federation of Haemophilia (WFH) card, stating their diagnosis and treatment plans.

|  Severity | Clotting Factor Level % Activity | Bleeding Episodes | Usual Age of Diagnosis  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Mild | >5–40% | Severe bleeding with major trauma or surgery | Often later in life  |

|  Severe | <1% | Spontaneous bleeding, predominantly in joints and muscles | 1st year of life  |

# Background

Haemophilia is an X-linked bleeding disorder affecting males. The prevalence of Haemophilia A and Haemophilia B in Singapore is 1 in 10000 male births and 1 in 50000 male births, respectively. Haemophilia A is Factor VIII deficiency.

Haemophilia B is Factor IX deficiency.

The bleeding phenotype of patients with haemophilia is dependent on the baseline Factor VIII or Factor IX activity (Table 15.7).

The standard treatment for severe haemophilia includes regular replacement therapy with clotting factor concentrates (prophylaxis) to prevent bleeds.

A group of children is being treated with non-factor replacement therapy, such as emicizumab, which is a bispecific antibody mimicking the action of Factor VIII. This is given as a subcutaneous injection weekly to once a month. Children **without inhibitors** who are treated with emicizumab should be treated with the usual recommended doses of Factor VIII according to body weight and bleed severity.

# Management of Acute Bleeds in Haemophilia

# Assessment

- Prompt bleeding or suspected bleeding in the head, neck, chest, gastrointestinal tract and abdominal regions should be treated with clotting factor immediately, even before assessment is complete.
- Clotting factor replacement should not be delayed by diagnostic imaging.
- Assess site and extent of bleeding and the impact on function.

# Management

- Most bleeds will require factor replacement except for bruises and minor soft tissue injuries that do not impact function and mobility.
- Invasive procedures such as arterial puncture, lumbar puncture must only be performed after clotting factor replacement.
- Prompt clotting factor replacement reduces pain and long-term consequences of bleeding.

# The Baby Bear Book

- Avoid IM injections, aspirin or NSAIDs.

## General Measures for Bleeds

### R.I.C.E.S. for Joint and Muscle Bleeds

For muscle and joint bleeds RICES will limit bleeding and reduce pain.

### Initiate on Arrival

- \( \mathrm{R} = \) Rest (in position)
- I = Ice (cold pack to reduce bleeding and pain)
- \( C = \) gentle Compression bandage
E = Elevation
- S = Splint

### Venous Access

- Venous access is often the major fear and stressor for children with haemophilia and their parents. Use distraction and relaxation techniques. Ask parents about their preferred method for distracting/comforting their children.
- Treat veins with care; they are the lifeline for patients with haemophilia. Apply pressure for 3 minutes post-venepuncture.

### Analgesia

- Paracetamol/codeine is sufficient in most cases; morphine or tramadol can be used for severe pain.
- Splinting/immobilisation is an effective adjunct for reducing severe pain.
- Avoid using products containing aspirin or NSAIDS (e.g. ibuprofen, diclofenac) as these potentially interfere with platelet function.

### Antifibrinolytic therapy (Tranexamic acid)

- Often helpful for mucosal bleeding (most common form of bleeding), e.g. mouth bleeds, epistaxis, menorrhagia.
- Reduces breakdown of blood clots and is effective for treating and preventing recurrence of mouth bleeds and epistaxis.
- May be given alone or as adjunct to desmopressin (DDAVP)/factor concentrate.
- Dose of tranexamic acid (500 mg tablets) is 15–25 mg/kg/dose (max.: 1.5 g/dose) TDS orally for 5–7 days.
- Contraindicated in haematuria

| --- | --- |
|  <20 | 250 mg tds  |
|  20–30 | 500 mg tds  |
|  30–40 | 750 mg tds  |
|  >40 | 1000 mg tds  |

# Physiotherapy

- Referral to physiotherapy for joint and muscle bleeds is essential.
- Physiotherapy should commence as soon as pain has subsided and bleeding has been controlled.

# Specific Clotting Factor Replacement in Haemophilia A Factor VIII

Dose of Factor VIII (IU) = Body weight (kg) × 0.5 IU/kg × desired increase in plasma Factor VIII (%). Doses should be rounded to the nearest **whole vial size** to avoid wastage.

# The Baby Bear Book

# Dose of Factor VIII for surgical procedures:

Major surgical procedures require Factor VIII levels to be raised to 100% (i.e. 50 IU/kg). Maintain circulating Factor VIII activity of 80–100% for the first 7 post-operative days and 30–50% for up to the 14th post-operative day. The duration of bleeding risk should be discussed with the surgeon and used to guide factor replacement therapy.

# Dose of Factor VIII for dental extractions:

- Minor dental extractions
  » Factor VIII activity of 50% before extraction.
  » To maintain levels of at least 50% for treatment of persistent oozing.
- Major dental procedures
  » Loading dose = 50 IU/kg followed by
  » Maintenance dose = 15 IU/kg every 8–12 hours for the first 1–2 days, then same dose 1–2 times a day for 5 days or until wound is adequately healed.

Tranexamic acid should be given up to the 7th post-operative day for all dental procedures.

# Desmopressin (DDAVP)

- Releases stored Factor VIII into the circulation.
- To be used only in patients with mild Haemophilia A where there is documented evidence in the medical record of safe and satisfactory response to a DDAVP challenge
- Not adequate for haemostasis in major bleeding
- Generally not recommended in young children (<3 years) due to documented reports of hyponatraemia and seizures. Relatively contraindicated in children with previous seizure disorders.
- IV Desmopressin: 0.3 mcg/kg infusion (to monitor BP and pulse during infusion)
- Intranasal Desmopressin: 150 mcg (for patients <50 kg); 300 mcg (for patients >50 kg)

# Management Options for patients with Factor VIII Inhibitors

- Some patients may develop inhibitors (antibodies that bind to Factor VIII or IX) in the course of haemophilia treatment, which renders treatment with normal factor replacement therapy ineffective.
- This occurs in approximately 20% to 30% of people with Haemophilia A and 3% of people with Haemophilia B on factor replacement therapy.
- The severity of the inhibitor is determined using the Bethesda assay (BU). A high titre inhibitor is any inhibitor that is >5 BU.
- Treatment options for acute bleeds include:
  » Swarming with Factor VIII:

☐ Only effective if inhibitor level <5 BU

☐ Initial Dose: 70–140 IU/kg

bolus

» Activated prothrombin complex concentrate (APCC/Factor eight inhibitor bypassing activity [FEIBA])

Initial Dose: 50-100 IU/kg/dose, depending on severity of haemorrhage. Dosing Interval: Up to 4 times a day
* Avoid concomitant use of tranexamic acid with APCC due to increased risk of thrombosis. Avoid the use of APCC for patients on emicizumab (Hemlibra).

» Activated Factor VIIa (Novoseven)

Initial Dose: \(90\mathrm{mcg / kg}\) dose to be repeated 2 hourly until bleeding stops.

# Specific Clotting Factor Replacement in Haemophilia B

# Factor IX

Dose of Factor IX (IU) = Body weight (kg) × 1.0 IU/kg × Desired increase in plasma Factor IX (%). Doses should be rounded to the nearest whole vial size to avoid wastage.

Dose of Factor IX for surgical procedures:

Major surgical procedures require Factor IX levels to be raised to 100% (i.e. 100 IU/kg). Maintain circulating Factor IX activity of 80–100% for the first 7 post-operative days and 30–50% for up to 14 post-operative days. The duration of bleeding risk should be discussed with the surgeon and used to guide factor replacement therapy.

Dose of Factor IX for dental extractions:

- Minor dental extractions

Factor IX activity of \(50\%\) before extraction.
To maintain levels of at least \(25\%\) for treatment of persistent oozing.

Major dental procedures

» Loading dose = 100 IU/kg followed by
» Maintenance dose = 30 IU/kg every 8–12 hours for the first 1–2 days, then same dose 1–2 times a day for 5 days or until wound is adequately healed.

## Von Willebrand Disease

### Background

Von Willebrand disease (vWD) is the most common inherited bleeding disorder affecting up to 1% of the population, with most cases caused by a mild quantitative deficiency of the von Willebrand factor (vWF). Quantitative or qualitative deficiencies of vWF cause inadequate platelet adhesion and secondary deficiency of Factor VIII. It affects males and females equally. It is characterised by easy bruising, bleeding from mucosal membranes (particularly epistaxis, oral mucosa and menorrhagia) and post-operative bleeding (see Table 15.11) in more severe cases.

Table 15.11 Phenotypes of von Willebrand Disease

|   | vWF Defect | Typical Bleeding Pattern  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Type 1 (common) | Mild to moderately reduced levels of vWF | Typically associated with mild bleeding  |
|  Type 2 (uncommon) | Abnormal structure and function of vWF, several variants including: 2A — Decreased formation of high molecular weight vWF multimers, interfering with collagen binding 2B — Abnormal affinity of platelets, leading to significant thrombocytopaenia 2M — Decreased affinity of the vWF molecule for platelet glycoproteins 2N — Decreased affinity of the vWF molecule for Factor VIII can present with symptoms similar to haemophilia | Variable bleeding pattern  |
|  Type 3 (rare) | Severe quantitative defect of vWF | Patients may behave like those with moderate to severe haemophilia  |

# Management of Acute Bleeds in vWD Patients

# Antifibrinolytic Therapy (Tranexamic Acid)

- Bleeds in patients with mild vWD tend to be minor and occur from mucosal surfaces. Treatment with tranexamic acid is usually sufficient to control bleeding.
- May be given alone or as an adjunct therapy to DDAVP/ factor concentrate.
- Dose of tranexamic acid is \(25\mathrm{mg / kg}\) /dose TDS orally for 5-7 days.
- Referral to the ENT surgeon may be required for local control of bleeds from epistaxis.

# Desmopressin (DDAVP)

- Patients with mild to moderate Type 1 vWD can be treated with DDAVP when there is documented evidence in the medical record of safe and satisfactory response to a DDAVP challenge.
- Occasionally effective in Type 2 vWD, never effective in Type 3 vWD
- Contraindicated in Type 2B vWD as it worsens thrombocytopaenia
- Expect two- to three-fold increase in Factor VIII/vWF level.
- Generally not recommended in young children (younger than 3 years) due to documented reports of hyponatraemia and seizures. Relatively contraindicated in children with previous seizure disorders.
- IV Desmopressin: \(0.3\mathrm{mcg / kg}\) infusion (to monitor BP & pulse during infusion)
- Intranasal Desmopressin: \(150\mathrm{mcg}\) (for patients \(< 50\mathrm{kg}\)), \(300\mathrm{mcg}\) (for patients \(>50\mathrm{kg}\))

# Factor VIII/vWF Plasma Concentrate for vWD

- An intermediate purity plasma-derived product containing both Factor VIII and vWF concentrate can be used to treat bleeding episodes in vWD.
- Dose of factor concentrate to be used will depend on clinical severity of disease and bleed and is dosed according to the target Factor VIII level.
- *Please round up the dose to the nearest whole vial size. Do not waste any factor.
- The dose of Factor VIII/vWF concentrate to be given should be calculated based on the formula:

- Dose of factor = Body weight (kg) x (Desired Factor VIII level [%] – Baseline Factor VIII level [%]) / 2

Minor dental extractions
Target Factor VIII activity of \(30 - 50\%\) before extraction
Major dental extractions
Target Factor VIII activity of \(50 - 100\%\) before major extraction or other invasive procedures

Tranexamic acid should be given up to the 7th post-operative day for all dental procedures.

# Other Bleeding Disorders

# Haemorrhagic Disease of Newborn

Newborn infants have lower levels of Vitamin-K-dependent clotting factors (Factors II, VII, IX, X) due to poor transplacental passage of Vitamin K and low levels stored in the neonatal liver. Endogenous production of Vitamin K in the infant gut occurs only after a few weeks when the intestinal microflora is established. Infants who have not been given exogenous IM or oral Vitamin K after birth are at risk of developing haemorrhagic disease of newborn (HDN).

# Presentation

Most common sites of bleeding are from the umbilicus, mucous membranes, gastrointestinal tract, circumcision and venepuncture sites. Haematomas can occur at

b4854_Sec-15.indd 458

12-Dec-22 11:08:12 AM

# Bleeding Disorders

sites of trauma, including sites related to instrumental delivery. Intracranial bleeding is a risk.

- Early HDN

Presentation is within first 24 hours.
Associated with maternal medications, e.g. anticonvulsants affecting Vitamin K metabolism.

Classic HDN

Presents 2-7 days after birth.
» Seen in infants who did not receive Vitamin K prophylaxis at birth.

- Late HDN

Presents 2-12 weeks after birth.
Most commonly seen in breastfed infants who did not receive Vitamin K prophylaxis at birth as human milk is low in Vitamin K content.
» Can also be seen in infants with chronic diarrhoea, malabsorption syndromes and chronic antibiotic therapy.

## Management

- Severe episodes of bleeding can be treated with fresh frozen plasma-prothrombin complex concentrate.
- Vitamin K can be given subcutaneously with local pressure if bleeding is not severe. Avoid IM injections because of the risk of haematoma.
- IV Vitamin K can be given; however, it must be administered as a slow infusion due to the risk of anaphylactic reactions.

## Disseminated Intravascular Coagulopathy

DIC is an acquired clinicopathological syndrome characterised by systemic activation of coagulation leading to generation of fibrin clots that cause microvascular thrombosis and organ failure, as well as concomitant consumption of platelets and coagulation factors that can result in bleeding. Causes include sepsis, malignancies (commonly seen in acute promyelocytic leukaemia), haemolytic transfusion reactions, toxins and trauma.

Clinical features include signs of bleeding into skin and mucosa at multiple sites. There is oozing from venepuncture sites, petechiae and purpura. There can be fulminant bleeding from internal organs such as the gastrointestinal tract, brain and lungs, leading to shock and end-organ failure. At the same time, microvascular thrombi in arterial and venous sites also contribute to end-organ failure, especially in the kidneys and brain.

## Diagnosis

- The diagnosis of DIC is based on a combination of clinical features supported by relevant laboratory tests.
- Suggestive laboratory findings include:

Thrombocytopaenia
Red cell fragmentation seen on the PBF
Prolonged PT, aPTT

12-Dec-22 11:08:12 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

» Low fibrinogen
» Raised markers of fibrinolysis — Raised D-dimer, fibrin degradation products

# Management

- The underlying cause of DIC must be treated.
- Management is mainly supportive, with correction of any factors that can further precipitate or worsen DIC, including hypotension, hypoxia and hypothermia.
- Maintain platelet counts \(>50 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\).
- Fresh frozen plasma/cryoprecipitate to correct coagulopathy if there is active bleeding.
- Maintain fibrinogen \(>1\mathrm{g / L}\)

# CHAPTER 62

# Thrombosis and Thrombophilia

Rajat Bhattacharyya; Lam Ching Mei, Joyce

## Introduction

Venous and arterial thrombosis are rare events in children. The incidence of venous thromboembolism (VTE) in children at a general paediatric population level is extremely low, reported as 0.07–0.14 per 10000 children. In hospitalised children, this rate is increased 100–1000 times to 58 per 10000 admissions; hence, neonatal and paediatric thrombosis is mostly seen in tertiary care hospitals. Risk factors for thrombosis in children differ from adults and nearly 95% of VTE in children are provoked events. The most common precipitating factor is the presence of a central venous access device (CVAD), which is responsible for almost 90% of VTE in neonates and 60% in older children. The highest rate of thrombosis in children occurs in two age groups — neonatal period and adolescents older than 13 years. Rudolph Virchow in the 19th century had described reasons for development thrombosis in a very simple way, which is known as Virchow’s triad and still holds true. These include blood vessel injury (endothelial damage), stasis (slowing of blood flow) and changes in constituents of blood (hypercoagulability), and these can explain all thrombotic events.

## Predisposing Conditions

- Indwelling catheters (most important risk factor)
- Infection/trauma/surgery/vascular malformation/cardiac disease/prosthetic valves
- Inflammatory diseases — Systemic lupus erythematosus, rheumatoid arthritis, inflammatory bowel disease
- Renal diseases — Chronic renal failure, nephrotic syndrome
- Drugs — High-dose steroids, asparaginase
- Inherited thrombophilias

## Symptoms and Signs

VTE

Warmth, tenderness and swelling of a limb
» Inability to flush or draw blood from an indwelling catheter

461

b4854_Sec-15.indd 461

12-Dec-22 11:08:12 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

Chest pain, haemoptysis, palpitations (pulmonary embolism [PE])
Headache, neurological impairment (cerebral venous sinus thrombosis)

Arterial thrombosis

Neurological symptoms, e.g. hemiplegia (paediatric stroke), convulsion
Impaired limb perfusion

## Radiological Investigations

- Doppler ultrasound to diagnose upper or lower limb deep venous thrombosis/ catheter-related thrombosis.
- Upper limb thrombosis may require computed tomography (CT) venography or magnetic resonance (MR) venography as Doppler ultrasound may be relatively insensitive for the detection of intra-thoracic thrombosis.
- CT pulmonary angiography (CTPA) is recommended as the imaging modality for suspected PE.
- CT or MR imaging of the brain with MR angiography and MR venography for diagnosis of paediatric stroke.

## Thrombophilia Testing

Thrombophilia refers to a genetic predisposition to the development of venous and arterial thromboembolic disease. The contribution of inherited thrombophilia in paediatric thrombosis is unknown, as the majority of young children who develop thrombosis have multiple risk factors. Thrombophilia was detected in one case series in 60% of adolescents with unprovoked thrombosis. The utility of thrombophilia testing during an acute event is controversial, as thrombosis itself will result in transient changes in serum levels of antithrombotic proteins. Thrombophilia testing is advisable in adolescents with unprovoked VTE and in children with a positive family history for VTE but has little value in neonates and children with a first episode of provoked thrombosis, e.g. catheter-related VTE. The identification of an inherited thrombophilia does not influence the acute management of thrombosis in a child, but may influence the need for long-term anticoagulation, depending on the clinical situation.

## Whom to Test

- Routine testing in unselected children with a first episode of VTE, especially if catheter-related, is not recommended.
- Consider testing if there are recurrent episodes of unprovoked VTE.
- Adolescents with spontaneous, unprovoked VTE.
- Testing of asymptomatic children with a positive family history is controversial and should only be made after careful discussion with the family.

## What to Test

Protein C, Protein S, Antithrombin
Factor V Leiden*, Prothrombin G20210A* gene mutation

b4854_Sec-15.indd 462

# Thrombosis and Thrombophilia

463

- Lupus anticoagulant, anticardiolipin antibody of immunoglobulin M (IgM) and IgG, anti-beta 2 glycoprotein 1 antibody of IgM and IgG
- Fasting homocysteine

(* Prevalence in Asians is <1%)

## When to Test

- Testing for thrombophilia in the acute setting may result in an incorrect diagnosis of inherited thrombophilia as levels of Protein C, Protein S and antithrombin may show a transient decrease during acute thrombosis and during therapy with heparin and warfarin.
- Thrombophilia testing should ideally be carried out when the acute event has resolved and anticoagulants have been taken off for at least 6 weeks.
- Genetic tests are not affected during an acute episode and can be performed any time.

## Management of Venous Thromboembolism

The aims of anticoagulant therapy (ACT) in children with VTE include:

- Reducing the risk of thrombus extension and embolisation
- Reducing the risk of recurrent thrombosis
- Reducing the incidence of post-thrombotic syndrome

## Anticoagulant Therapy

- Low molecular weight heparin (LMWH) — subcutaneous

Usually used as first-line therapy for management of acute VTE.
Monitored using serum Anti-Xa levels, targeting a therapeutic level of 0.5-1 U/ml.
Caution should be exercised in children with renal impairment as LMWHs are renally excreted.
Anticoagulant effect is only partially reversed by protamine and withholding therapy for 24 hours for therapeutic dosing before any surgical intervention is necessary.

- Unfractionated heparin (UFH) — intravenous

Indicated in situations where quick reversal is necessary for bleeding risk as the half-life is shorter (60 minutes) compared to LMWH (4 hours).
Anticoagulant effect can be \(100\%\) neutralised by protamine.
Disadvantages include the intravenous route of administration and need for frequent monitoring of activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) every 4-6 hours to achieve the therapeutic target of between 60 and 80 seconds. Ideally heparin should be monitored by Anti-Xa levels with a desired level of \(0.35 - 0.7\mathrm{U / ml}\), but this is time consuming, and round the clock availability of a testing facility is a challenge.

- Warfarin

Vitamin K antagonist.

b4854_5ec-15.indd 463

12-Dec-22 11:08:12 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

» Indicated where long-term anticoagulation therapy (ACT) is required beyond 6 to 12 months.
» Multiple interactions with other medications and food.
» Monitored using international normalised ratio (INR), usually targeted between 2 and 3 for management of VTE.

Duration

» Duration of ACT is guided by reason (provoked or spontaneous), presence of ongoing risk factors and also vascular recanalisation.
» Shorter duration is recommended (6 weeks to 3 months) for provoked thrombosis with resolution of risk factors and establishment of venous flow in the area of thrombosis.
For unprovoked thrombosis, longer duration of up to 6 to 12 months is recommended. However, the risk benefit ratio of recurrence of thrombosis and bleeding has to be assessed individually and the duration decided based on these factors.
General principles include:

Catheter-related VTEs — ACT for 6 weeks to 3 months
Secondary VTE with risk factors — ACT for no more than 3 months, or until resolution of the precipitating factor
First unprovoked VTE—ACT for 6 to 12 months
Recurrent unprovoked VTEs — Consider long-term ACT

## Thrombolytic Therapy

- The use of thrombolytic therapy is usually not indicated in the majority of children with VTE, but may be considered in the presence of extensive life-threatening venous thrombosis or in critical organ ischaemia such as intra-cardiac/PE with haemodynamic instability or impaired limb perfusion.
- Tissue plasminogen activator is the preferred agent for thrombolysis.

## Recommendations in Specific Areas of Thrombosis

PE

- Children with symptomatic PE should be anticoagulated. Thrombolysis followed by ACT is indicated only if there is life-threatening haemodynamic compromise.

CVAD-related thrombosis

» ACT for 6 weeks to 3 months is recommended for symptomatic thrombosis. If the line is functioning and required for treatment, then line removal is not necessary.
» A non-functioning line should be removed after initiation of ACT for 3-5 days.
Asymptomatic thrombosis can be monitored radiologically and treated if there is progression. ACT for 6 weeks to 3 months is also reasonable.

- Cerebral sinus venous thrombosis (CSVT)

b4854_Sec-15.indd 464

12-Dec-22 11:08:12 AM

# Thrombosis and Thrombophilia

» ACT is recommended even in the presence of haemorrhage because the bleeding is usually secondary to venous hypertension from the thrombosis and obstruction.
In the presence of significant haemorrhage, close radiological monitoring and starting ACT if there is progression of thrombus is also reasonable.

- Renal vein thrombosis (RVT)

» Unilateral RVT — ACT should be considered, especially if there is progression into the inferior vena cava to reduce long-term renal complications.
» Bilateral RVT — ACT is necessary. Thrombolysis followed by ACT is recommended for life-threatening bilateral RVT.

- Portal vein thrombosis (PVT)

- ACT is recommended in PVT if there is occlusive thrombus, post-liver transplant or idiopathic PVT.

- Neonatal thrombosis

» CSVT in neonates — Evidence for benefit of ACT is less established but generally recommended as there is evidence that the risk of progression is reduced without an increased risk of mortality or morbidity with anticoagulation.
» Catheter-induced arterial thrombosis, e.g. peripheral or umbilical artery thrombosis — Treat with a short course of anticoagulation for 5–7 days with UFH or LMWH and catheter removal.
» Neonatal arterial ischaemic stroke (AIS) — If there is no cardioembolic source, then ACT is not recommended as the recurrence risk is extremely low.

- Arterial thrombosis

» AIS in children should be treated with UFH or LMWH until a cardioembolic cause, including dissection, has been excluded and then continued with aspirin (3–5 mg/kg/day) for at least 2 years.
» AIS associated with vasculopathy should be treated with heparin for at least 3 months and then further therapy should be guided by repeat vascular imaging.
» Recurrent AIS should be treated with ACT continued long term.

## Direct Oral Anticoagulants

- Oral administration, short onset of action, predictable pharmacological profile with low risk of food and drug interactions, and no need for routine laboratory assessment of anticoagulant activity are major advantages of direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs), especially in paediatric population.
- The direct thrombin inhibitors, e.g. dabigatran and direct oral factor Xa inhibitors (e.g. rivaroxaban, apixaban and edoxaban) have been approved or are currently under clinical trials in children.
- Dabigatran and rivaroxaban, when compared to standard anticoagulation, showed similarly low recurrence rate of thrombosis and bleeding in paediatric VTE (DIVERSITY and EINSTEIN Jr trials). Similar results were found in its use

b4854_Sec-15.indd 465

12-Dec-22 11:08:12 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

in CVAD-associated thrombosis (EINSTEIN-Jr CVC-VTE trial), establishing its safety and efficacy.

- As more safety and efficacy data are available from ongoing trials of DOACs in the paediatric population, they are likely to replace long-term use of warfarin and heparin in the future.

# CHAPTER 63

# Transfusion Therapy

Lam Ching Mei, Joyce

Transfusion of red cells and other blood components in paediatrics may be required in clinical situations such as acute blood loss, bone marrow failure and in preparation for major surgical procedures. However, transfusion of blood products is associated with a definite risk of adverse reactions and should only be carried out when the expected benefits are likely to outweigh the potential hazards.

## Safety Issues Related to Transfusion of Blood Products

There must be a clear, documented clinical indication for transfusion. Informed consent should be obtained from parents or guardians prior to transfusion of blood components. The person giving consent should be informed of the benefits, risks and potential complications associated with transfusion of blood components.

Correct patient identification is critical when taking blood samples for group and cross matching and when transfusing blood components to patients. **Errors in mislabelling and patient identification can result in an incompatible blood transfusion, which has potentially fatal consequences for the patient.**

## Determining the Choice of ABO Groups for Blood Components

- Persons who are ABO Group A have red cells expressing antigens and have circulating Anti-B antibodies in plasma.
- Persons who are ABO Group B have red cells expressing antigens and have circulating Anti-A antibodies in plasma.
- Persons who are ABO Group AB have red cells expressing both A-antigens and B-antigens and do not have any circulating Anti-A or Anti-B antibodies in plasma.
- Persons who are ABO Group O have red cells that do not express either the A-antigen or B-antigen and have circulating Anti-A and Anti-B antibodies in plasma.
- A pack of red cells of a particular ABO blood group is expected to contain the corresponding red cell antigens.

467

# The Baby Bear Book

- Platelets, fresh frozen plasma (FFP) and cryoprecipitate contain large amounts of plasma and are expected to carry antibodies associated with the corresponding ABO blood group.
- The choice of ABO groups for transfusion of different blood components can thus be summarised below:

Table 15.13 Choice of ABO Groups for Blood Components

|  Patient's ABO Group | ABO Group of Blood Component to be Transfused  |   |   |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|   |  Red Cells | Platelets | FFP  |
|  A First Choice Second Choice | A O | A AB | A AB  |
|  B First Choice Second Choice | B O | B AB | B AB  |
|  AB First Choice Second Choice | AB A/B | AB A/B | AB —  |
|  O First Choice Second Choice | O — | O A/B | O A/B  |

## Red Cell Transfusions

- The aim of red cell transfusions is to increase the oxygen-carrying capacity of the blood by increasing haemoglobin (Hb) concentration in patients with anaemia.
- The decision to transfuse should not be merely based on a 'number' but on a clinical indication.
- There are few large studies looking at the effectiveness of Hb transfusion thresholds in children.
- In general, red cell transfusion may be beneficial, especially in symptomatic patients or in patients with ongoing blood loss when \(\mathrm{Hb} < 7\mathrm{g / dL}\).
- There is usually very little indication for red cell transfusion when the Hb is \(>10\) g/dL.
- By consensus, see Table 15.14 for the transfusion triggers based on haemoglobin levels used in paediatrics.
- The volume of red cells to be transfused can be calculated using the formula \( \text{Volume to be transfused (ml)} = (\text{Desired Hb minus Current Hb in g/dL}) \times (\text{Body weight in kg}) \times 3.5 \)
- The volume of red cells to be transfused is usually between 10 and \(15\mathrm{ml / kg}\) and should not exceed \(20\mathrm{ml / kg}\).
- A transfusion of \(10\mathrm{ml / kg}\) of red cells is expected to raise the Hb by \(2 - 2.5\mathrm{g / dL}\). However, this is variable and dependent on the underlying clinical condition of the child.
