## Platelet Transfusions

- The degree of thrombocytopenia does not always correlate with bleeding severity, especially in conditions such as immune thrombocytopenia.
- The decision for platelet transfusion should not be based on platelet counts alone but on the clinical situation and other risk factors for bleeding.
- There are also few large studies looking at the effectiveness of platelet transfusion thresholds in children.
- By consensus, see Table 15.15 for the common transfusion triggers based on platelet counts used in paediatrics.
- The volume of platelets to be transfused is \(10\mathrm{ml/kg}\) or 4 units/\(\mathrm{m}^2\) — this is expected to raise the platelet count by \(30 - 50 \times 10^{9}/\mathrm{L}\).
- In Singapore, platelets for paediatric patients can be ordered either as single units of 'apheresed platelets paediatric' (APP) or 'cell-separated platelets' (CSP).
1 unit of CSP is equivalent to 4-6 units of APP.
- In general, children weighing \(>40\mathrm{kg}\) should be transfused with a unit of CSP.

Table 15.15 Common Platelet Transfusion Triggers

|  Clinical Situation | Platelet Transfusion Trigger (x10⁹/L)  |
| --- | --- |
|  Neonate | 25–50  |
|  Bone marrow failure (e.g. aplastic anaemia) | 10–20  |
|  Patients undergoing chemotherapy |   |
|  Stable | 10  |
|  Unstable/febrile | 20  |
|  Prior to lumbar puncture | 50  |
|  Prior to surgical procedures | 50–80  |
|  Prior to neurosurgical procedures | 100  |

## Fresh Frozen Plasma Transfusions

- FFP contains coagulation factors, fibrinogen, albumin and immunoglobulins.
- FFP should not be transfused merely to correct an 'abnormal' PT or APTT without a prior assessment of the underlying cause of coagulopathy.

b4854_Sec-15.indd 469

12-Dec-22 11:08:12 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

- The indications for FFP transfusion include:

» Reversal of warfarin overdose, in combination with prothrombin complex concentrate (PCC) and Vitamin K
» Acute disseminated coagulopathy
» Coagulopathy associated with liver disease
» Clotting factor deficiencies for which no alternative clotting factor concentrates are available, e.g. Factor XI deficiency
Massive transfusion

- The recommended dose of FFP is 10–15 ml/kg.

## Cryoprecipitate

- Cryoprecipitate contains high amounts of Factor VIII, von Willebrand factor, fibrinogen and Factor XIII.
- It is not recommended for clotting factor deficiencies where specific clotting factor concentrates are available (e.g. Haemophilia A and B).
- Because of the higher concentration of fibrinogen in cryoprecipitate compared to FFP, it is useful in clinical situations where potential volume overload is an issue.
- Indications for use of cryoprecipitate include:

Hypofibrinogenaemia
Disseminated intravascular coagulopathy
Advanced liver disease
Massive transfusion

- The recommended dose of cryoprecipitate is 5–10 ml/kg.

## Modified Blood Components

### Leucodepleted/‘CMV-Negative’ Blood Products

- White blood cells present in red cells and platelets are associated with various adverse effects such as non-haemolytic febrile transfusion reactions, HLA alloimmunisation and transmission of intracellular organisms, such as cytomegalovirus (CMV).
- In Singapore, due to the high CMV seropositive rate, 'CMV-negative' blood products are not obtained from CMV-seronegative individuals, but are produced from leucoreduction of donated blood.
- From 2012, a change in the production method of platelets resulted in all platelet concentrates in Singapore being leucodepleted. There is thus no need to specially request for 'CMV-negative'/leucodepleted platelets.
- However, universal leucodepletion of red cells is still not practised in Singapore. Hence, special requests for 'CMV-negative'/leucodepleted red cells must be made when indicated.

b4854_Sec-15.indd 470

12-Dec-22 11:08:13 AM

# Transfusion Therapy

471

- Indications for use of leucodepleted blood components include but are not limited to:

Haematopoietic stem-cell transplant recipients
Solid organ transplant recipients
» Immunocompromised patients at risk of infection with intracellular organisms
Congenital immunodeficiencies
» Premature neonates
Intrauterine transfusions
Patients experiencing frequent non-haemolytic febrile transfusion reactions

## Irradiated Blood Products

- Transfusion-associated graft-versus-host disease (TA-GvHD) is a potential complication of transfusion of any blood component containing viable T lymphocytes when there is a disparity in the histocompatibility antigens between the donor and recipient.
- Gamma irradiation of red cells and platelets renders T-lymphocytes non-viable.
- Irradiation of blood products hence prevents TaGVHD mediated by donor T-lymphocytes.
- FFP is an acellular blood product and does not require irradiation
- Indications for use of irradiated blood components include but are not limited to:

Intrauterine transfusions
Congenital immunodeficiency, especially in T-cell immunodeficiency syndromes, e.g. DiGeorge syndrome, severe combined immunodeficiency
Haematopoietic stem cell transplant patients
Patients treated with purine analogues, e.g. fludarabine
Transfusion from first- and second-degree relatives
All granulocyte products

## Washed Red Cells

- Washing of red cells removes the majority of plasma and plasma proteins from the unit.
- There is a red cell loss of \(10 - 20\%\) associated with the procedure.
- Units of washed red cells must be used within 24 hours as the procedure is carried out in an open system.
- Indications for use of washed cells include:

Patients with IgA deficiency
Patients with a history of severe anaphylactic transfusion reactions or recurrent allergic transfusion reactions
Patients with haemolytic uraemic syndrome, although this is not necessary if the patient is already planned to receive products containing plasma, e.g. platelets, FFP

b4854_5ec-15.indd 471

12-Dec-22 11:08:13 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

## Massive Blood Transfusion

- Massive transfusion in children is defined as a transfusion of more than \(40\mathrm{ml/kg}\).
- In cases of massive bleeding, e.g. in polytrauma, the lethal triad includes coagulopathy, acidosis and hypothermia.
- Massive blood loss may result in coagulopathy due to consumption of coagulation factors, as well as reduced activity of coagulation factors from prolonged shock, hypoxia, hypothermia.
- In addition, there are dilutional effects of blood replacement on coagulation proteins and platelets. This occurs because packed red cell transfusions are essentially devoid of plasma and platelets.
- Hence, with a massive transfusion of blood for haemodynamic resuscitation, there is a concurrent need to correct the coagulopathy with early use of FFP, platelets and cryoprecipitate.
- A ratio of at least 1 FFP:2RBC is recommended in early resuscitation of a major bleed.
- Platelets in \(15 - 20\mathrm{ml / kg}\) aliquots are to be considered after every \(40~\mathrm{ml / kg}\).
Ratios should be modified accordingly once laboratory parameters are available.
- A Massive Transfusion Protocol should be in place to ensure rapid provision of blood components for children with massive bleeds (See Figure 15.6).
- Both acidosis and hypothermia interfere with the normal functioning of the coagulation system and should be avoided in massive blood loss situations.
- Other complications of massive blood transfusion include:

Hypocalcaemia due to large amounts of citrate given with blood transfusion, since blood is anticoagulated with citrate.
Hyperkalaemia due to potassium leakage from RBCs after prolonged blood storage or irradiation

## Adverse Transfusion Reactions

- An adverse transfusion reaction is defined as an undesirable response in a patient temporally associated with the administration of a blood component.
- Transfusion reactions can be acute (presenting within 24 hours) or delayed.
- It is important to recognise a transfusion reaction as many of the early signs and symptoms can be non-specific, including fever, chills, rigors and urticarial rash.
- Allergic reactions and febrile non-haemolytic transfusion reactions are common but less serious, whereas acute haemolytic transfusion reactions, bacterial contamination of blood products and transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI) are less common but life-threatening.

## What to Do when an Adverse Transfusion Reaction is Suspected

- All suspected transfusion reactions must be reported to the issuing blood bank.
- Any ongoing transfusion of the suspected unit must be stopped immediately.
Monitor the patient's vital signs.
- Do an immediate clerical check at the bedside to detect any misidentification and major ABO mismatch. This is the most common cause of an acute haemolytic transfusion reaction.

# CHAPTER 64

# The Febrile Child

Karen Donceras Nadua

## Definition and General Principles

Fever is defined as a regulated elevation of the body's core temperature in response to an insult that triggers the body's inflammatory defences. The most commonly cited definition of fever is a body temperature measurement of $\geq 38^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$.

Rectal temperature is the closest approximate of core temperature; however, due to comfort, oral, axillary and tympanic measurement methods are generally preferred. Axillary measurement can be used at any age. Tympanic thermometers are not reliable for infants less than 6 months old. Regardless of the method of measurement used, it is important to be consistent.

While infection is an important and common cause of fever in a child, other causes that trigger the body's inflammatory response should also be considered, such as immune-mediated and autoimmune diseases, neoplastic diseases and drugs.

## Fever without Source

- Fever without source (FWS) is defined as fever with no apparent cause.

- The extent and tempo of investigations for FWS depend on the child's age, immune status, height and duration of fever, and clinical status.

- For example, neonates have underdeveloped immune systems and are at higher risk for serious bacterial infections (SBIs, such as urinary tract infection [UTI], bacteraemia, meningitis, lobar pneumonia, osteomyelitis). A normal physical examination is insufficient to rule out SBI for this age group; therefore, a thorough workup for SBIs and empiric antibiotics are warranted, regardless of clinical condition.

- In older children, viral infections are more common as causes of fever, and antibiotics are often not necessary, especially if the child is clinically well.

- FWS can be a diagnostic dilemma in children aged $\geq 1$ month to 36 months, as occult bacteraemia can occur in young well-appearing infants. This incidence of occult bacteraemia due to *Streptococcus pneumoniae* has greatly declined with increasing vaccine coverage.

- Management of this age group is dependent on clinical assessment, sometimes in conjunction with laboratory tests.

## Approach

History

478

b4854_Sec-16.indd 478

12-Dec-22 11:08:57 AM

# The Febrile Child

» To include: Exposure history such as sick contact and travel history, history of immunodeficiency or immunosuppression, history of chronic illness, vaccination history, drug history (including pre-treatment with antibiotics).

Clinical examination

Assess vital signs, perfusion and consciousness, and stabilise as necessary.
Thorough physical examination to look for occult infection, such as otitis media, sinusitis, osteomyelitis and septic arthritis, and meningitis and central nervous system infection.

- Red flags that warrant immediate attention and evaluation of a child with FWS

Toxic or ill-appearing child or unstable vital signs
Age \(\leq 28\) days
» Immunocompromised
» Underlying chronic illness resulting in reduced physiologic reserves, e.g. congenital cyanotic heart disease

Initial investigations

The laboratory tests (see Table 16.1) below are often used in conjunction with clinical assessment of the child with FWS. Be aware of the limitations of these tests and that they have limited sensitivity in detecting SBIs in the current setting of lower incidence of S. pneumoniae occult bacteraemia.
UTI is the most common SBI in young children. For febrile children age \(\leq 24\) months, urinalysis and urine culture should be done if he/she is ill enough to warrant immediate antimicrobial therapy or if risk factors for UTI are present. These risk factors are: temperature \(\geq 39^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\), boys with fever \(\geq 24\) hours, girls less than 12 months of age, girls with fever \(>2\) days, and absence of another source of infection.

Table 16.1 Laboratory Tests

|   | Cut-off | Notes  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Full blood count |  |   |
|  Total white blood cells count | ≥15 x 10⁹ cells/L |   |
|  Absolute neutrophil count | ≥10 x 10⁹ cells/L  |   |
|  Immature band count | ≥1.5 x 10⁹ cells/L  |   |
|  C-reactive protein (CRP) | ≥20 mg/L | Rises 6–8 hr after onset of infection; can also rise in other conditions, such as other inflammatory diseases, trauma and burns.  |
|  Procalcitonin | ≥0.5 ng/ml | Rises earlier than CRP at 3 to 4 hr after onset of infection and is more specific for invasive bacterial infection.  |

# The Baby Bear Book

» Chest X-ray (CXR) should only be performed if there is clinical indication as pneumonia in the absence of symptoms and lower respiratory tract signs is highly uncommon.

- Initial management (see Table 16.2)

Table 16.2 Initial Management

|   | Relevant History Needed | Management  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Age ≤ 28 d | Thorough antenatal and birth history, to include: - maternal setup for sepsis: prolonged rupture of membranes (≥18 hr), peripartum fever, per-vaginal discharge - Maternal Group B streptococcus (GBS) colonisation - If colonised, whether she received adequate intrapartum prophylaxis (1 dose of intravenous [IV] Penicillin given at least 4 hr before birth) - Presence of maternal genital ulcers, history of herpes simplex infection in pregnancy | All neonates with fever must be hospitalised for investigation and treatment. Full septic workup: Full blood count C-reactive protein (CRP) or Procalcitonin Blood culture Urinalysis Urine culture Perform lumbar puncture for cerebrospinal fluid studies: microscopy, biochemistry, bacterial culture, PCR for enterovirus, par-echovirus, herpes simplex virus After obtaining microbiological studies, start empiric antibiotics**: - IV Ampicillin meningitic dose - IV Gentamicin If herpes infection is suspected or confirmed, add IV Acyclovir.  |
|  Age >1 mth to 3 mth* | As per clinical approach and to include additional history above. | Admit the child for investigation and observation. Assess clinical status. For unwell infants, full septic workup and empiric antibiotics** are recommended as above. For well-appearing young infants, the following can be considered: - Do full blood count, CRP or procalcitonin, blood culture, urinalysis, and urine cultures and closely observe the child. - If urinalysis is suggestive of UTI, then do urine cultures, blood cultures and start empiric antibiotics for UTI. - If urinalysis is normal, but total white blood cells, CRP or procalcitonin are elevated, then consider doing a partial OR full septic workup with blood, urine cultures +/- lumbar puncture and start empiric antibiotics** (IV Ampicillin + IV Gentamicin or IV Ceftriaxone). - If fever is persistent and the urine culture is negative, reassess the child for other sources of infection such as meningitis and consider performing a lumbar puncture.  |

## Fever of Unknown Origin in Children

- FWS can evolve into Fever of Unknown Origin (FUO), which is defined as temperature \(\geq 38^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\) for at least one week with no source identified.
- In FUOs where the cause is eventually identified, the most common conditions are infectious (50%), followed by collagen-vascular (10–20%) and oncologic (10%).

FUO is often an uncommon presentation of a common disease.
Presence of constitutional symptoms may be due to oncologic or autoimmune diseases. In endemic countries including Singapore, tuberculosis (TB) should also be considered.
Parents should be counselled that a child with FUO may have a prolonged stay and may require multiple investigations.

## Approach

- A comprehensive history and a detailed physical examination will help direct the evaluation of an FUO with targeted investigations.
- It is useful to identify the clinical syndrome of the FUO (such as fever with rash, fever with cervical lymphadenopathy, pneumonia, meningitis).
History

Demographics age, sex, ethnicity
Objective documentation of fever, fever pattern
Onset of symptoms — whether community acquired or nosocomial

# The Baby Bear Book

» Presence of constitutional symptoms
» Sick contacts, including exposure to TB
» Zoonotic exposure

» Host immune status — Is the patient neutropenic or immunodeficient?
» Vaccination history

• Clinical Examination

» Examination is guided by history. If there is no localising source, a thorough physical examination is needed to look for occult infection, such as otitis media, sinusitis, osteomyelitis and septic arthritis, and meningitis and other central nervous system infection.

• Investigations

» Investigations will be directed by findings from the history and physical examination.
» Initial investigations should include full blood count, renal panel, liver function test, urinalysis, and urine and blood cultures.
» Other investigations to consider:

☐ Inflammatory markers such as C-reactive protein, procalcitonin, erythrocyte sedimentation rate.

☐ Radiology tests if there is clinical indication.

• Management

» Empiric antibiotics can be started after relevant cultures are obtained. The choice of antibiotics depends on the clinical syndrome identified.
☐ If a child is well-appearing, antibiotics may be held off. Instead, the child will require serial reviews to look for change in clinical status or appearance of new localising signs and symptoms.
» Non-essential medications should be stopped as drugs can also cause FUO.
» If the initial investigations' yield is negative and FUO persists, then the child will need to be referred to the paediatric infectious disease specialist and more invasive investigations or detailed imaging may be needed.

# CHAPTER 65

# Common Viral Infections

Tan Woon Hui, Natalie; Chong Chia Yin; Thoon Koh Cheng

## Chickenpox (Varicella)

### Introduction

- **Incubation period**

Eight to 21 days, generally 14 to 16 days.
Can be as long as 28 days in patients given passive immunisation.

- **Infectious period**

Two days before onset of rash until all scabs have dried up (about 1 week, longer in immunocompromised patients).

- **Transmission**

Person-to-person direct contact
Chickenpox and disseminated herpes zoster: Airborne and droplet spread of respiratory secretions and vesicle fluid
For herpes zoster: Direct contact with or droplet spread of vesicle fluid

### Clinical Features

- Prodrome of fever, cough, malaise and pruritus
- Generalised maculopapular rash that progresses to clear vesicles, then cloudy vesicles and finally scabs
- Skin lesions start over the face or trunk, appear in crops and spread outward to the limbs
- Oral ulcers can occur
- Fever generally lasts 3 to 5 days
- Suspect secondary bacterial infections if fever lasts \(>5\) days
- Mild, atypical and inapparent infections can occur

### Complications

Secondary bacterial infections: cellulitis, necrotising fasciitis
Varicella pneumonitis
- Encephalitis, cerebellar ataxia, meningitis, transverse myelitis
Reyes syndrome
Thrombocytopenia
- Hepatitis
Arthritis

# Common Viral Infections

- Glomerulonephritis
- Disseminated varicella
- Long term: Herpes zoster

# Treatment

- Immunocompetent patients

  » Oral acyclovir only if:

    ☐ Secondary contact case in a family

    ☐ Older than 12 years

    ☐ Chronic skin or pulmonary conditions

    ☐ On long-term salicylate therapy

    ☐ On short- or long-term, intermittent or aerosolised steroids

  » Start oral acyclovir within 72 hours (preferably within 24 hours) of rash onset
  » Intravenous (IV) acyclovir for complicated varicella, e.g. encephalitis, disseminated varicella

- Immunocompromised patients

  » IV acyclovir until all lesions have crusted, then oral acyclovir for another 3 days

- Acyclovir Dose

  » Oral 20 mg/kg/dose Q6H

  » IV younger than 1 month: 10 mg/kg/dose Q8H

  » IV older than 1 month: 500 mg/m²/dose Q8H

- Isolation

  » From 8 days after exposure until all lesions have scabbed

  » Patient is contagious from about 2 days before onset of illness

  » If given varicella immunoglobulin (VZIG), isolate until 28 days after exposure

- Immunocompetent patients exposed to chickenpox

  » If no history of previous chickenpox, assume patient is susceptible to varicella. Varicella vaccine can be offered. It may prevent or modify the illness provided it is given within 72 hours of exposure, i.e. index case is at Day 1 to 2 of illness. May not protect against disease if the patient was exposed at the same time as the index case.

  » The vaccine is given as two doses subcutaneously 3 months apart in children 12 years or younger, or 6 to 10 weeks apart if older than 12 years.

- Immunocompromised patients exposed to chickenpox

  » Ask patient for previous history of chickenpox

  » Check patient's record for varicella antibody result (if previously done)

  » If varicella immunity is unknown, screen patient's blood for varicella-zoster virus (VZV) IgG antibody (call Virology Lab for urgent testing)

  » If result shows antibody positive, no action is required

  » If result shows antibody negative, give VZIG within 10 days of exposure. Please refer to your institution's Varicella Post-Exposure Prophylaxis Protocol.

  » Dose: 25 IU/kg, or 1 ml/kg (vials of 125 IU/5 ml)

  » Do NOT overdose, as this may lead to fluid overload and hyperviscosity

# The Baby Bear Book

Refer to ID/Infection Control team to implement post-exposure prophylaxis protocol
- Discharge if fit, otherwise isolate from other patients for 28 days (from date of exposure).
- If patient is susceptible to chickenpox but has received IV immunoglobulin (IVIG at \(400\mathrm{mg / kg}\)) in the past 3 weeks, VZIG is not required.

# Bone Marrow Transplant Patients

- VZIG should be given within 10 days of exposure if significant exposure has occurred to the following group of Bone Marrow Transplant (BMT) patients, regardless of previous varicella antibody result:

Patients who are undergoing conditioning regimen
Allogeneic patients \(< 24\) months after BMT
<24 months after BMT and on immunosuppressive therapy
Acute or chronic Graft-versus-host Disease
» Varicella antibody negative BMT patient undergoing conditioning treatment, who is exposed to VZV vaccine and having a varicella-like rash

# Neonates

- Give VZIG within 96 hours of exposure if:

Mother develops chickenpox within 5 days before delivery till 2 days after delivery
Hospitalised, is premature \(>28\) weeks of gestation and mother has no history of chickenpox and is varicella antibody negative
Hospitalised, is premature \(< 28\) weeks of gestation, or \(< 1\mathrm{kg}\) regardless of maternal history or varicella antibody status

# Types of Significant Exposure for Susceptible Persons

Household: Residing in the same household
- Playmate: Face-to-face indoor play
Hospital:

» Varicella patient in the same 2 to 4 bedroom or adjacent beds in a large ward
» Face-to-face contact with an infectious patient or staff member, or a visit by a person deemed contagious
Herpes zoster: Intimate contact (e.g. touching or hugging) with a person deemed contagious

» 3 to 14 days, usually 5 to 7 days

# - Infectious period

» 1 day before until 5 days after the onset of illness, during the period of viraemia

» *Aedes aegypti* and *Aedes albopictus* mosquitoes, which bite during the day

# Clinical Features

- An acute febrile illness lasting 2 to 7 days, associated with two or more of the following:

» Maculopapular rash, flushing or petechiae, with islands of sparing
» Headache
» Retro-orbital pain
» Myalgia
» Arthralgia

# Dengue Haemorrhagic Fever (DHF)

- All the following four criteria must be fulfilled:

» Fever, or recent history of acute fever
» Haemorrhagic manifestations: Bruising and bleeding at venepuncture sites, gums or the gastrointestinal tract. Other examples are epistaxis, a positive tourniquet test or petechiae
» Low platelet count (<100 × 10⁹/L)
Objective evidence of plasma leakage:

Elevated haematocrit
Low serum protein

□

Ascites

☐ Pleural or pericardial effusions

- The following suggest impending decompensation:

» Severe abdominal pain
» Prolonged vomiting
» Abrupt change from fever to hypothermia
» Change in level of consciousness (irritability or somnolence)

# Dengue Shock Syndrome (DSS)

The four criteria for DHF, and
Evidence of circulatory failure:

» Rapid and weak pulse
» Narrow pulse pressure of <20 mmHg
» Hypotension
» Cold, clammy skin
» Altered mental status

- Usually develops 3 to 6 days after onset of symptoms.

Table 16.3 2009 WHO Revised Dengue Case Classification

|  Dengue without Warning Signs Fever and two of the following: • Nausea, vomiting • Rash • Aches and pains • Leukopenia • Positive tourniquet test  |
| --- |
|  Dengue with Warning Signs** Dengue as defined above with any of the following: • Abdominal pain or tenderness • Persistent vomiting • Clinical fluid accumulation (ascites, pleural effusion) • Mucosal bleeding • Lethargy, restlessness • Liver enlargement >2 cm • Laboratory: increase in haematocrit concurrent with rapid decrease in platelet count ** Requires strict observation and medical intervention  |
|  Severe Dengue Dengue with at least one of the following criteria: • Severe Plasma Leakage leading to: » Dengue shock syndrome » Fluid accumulation with respiratory distress • Severe bleeding as evaluated by clinician • Severe organ involvement » Liver: AST or ALT ≥1000 » CNS: impaired consciousness » Failure of heart and other organs  |

Signs of possible decompensation:

Disappearance of fever
Drop in platelets
Increase in haematocrit

## Other Complications

- Central nervous system: Encephalopathy, encephalitis, aseptic meningitis, transverse myelitis, Guillain-Barre Syndrome
- Myocarditis
- Transaminitis
Haemoglobinuria — Intravascular haemolysis
Vertical transmission to newborn babies

## Investigations

Full blood count: Thrombocytopenia, leukopenia or lymphocytosis with atypical lymphocytes, neutropenia, raised haematocrit
- Dengue serology and non-structural protein 1 (NS1) antigen assay

b4854_Sec-16.indd 488

# Common Viral Infections

Not routine, only if child is unwell/has possible features of pre-shock/shock:

- Renal panel: Hyponatraemia
- Liver function test: Elevated transaminases (AST > ALT)
- Coagulation studies: Prolonged prothrombin time and activated partial thromboplastin time
- Group and crossmatch: May need transfusion
- Chest X-ray if chest signs present, or work of breathing increased (pleural effusion)

## Management

- Routine

Usually only supportive care is required
» Paracetamol for fever (avoid non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs)
Complete rest in bed if platelet count \(< 50 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\)
No intramuscular injections
Intravenous access
Monitor vital signs including blood pressure least every 4 hours
» Strict intake/output (I/O) chart. Maintain hydration
Daily platelet count and haematocrit from Day 3 of illness
» Dengue is a notifiable disease — Notify Ministry of Health (MOH) via Communicable Diseases Live and Enhanced Surveillance (CDLENS) within 24 hours

- Hypovolaemia

In patients with a rising haematocrit or falling platelet count, administer an IV bolus of normal saline (10 to \(20\mathrm{ml / kg}\)), followed by a maintenance dextrose/saline infusion
» Stop IV fluids when the haematocrit \(< 40\%\) and adequate intravascular volume is present; avoid volume overload

- Bleeding

Platelet transfusion only for active bleeding
» Fresh frozen plasma is indicated if there is a consumption coagulopathy
» Use whole blood or packed cells to replace blood loss
» Oxygen can be given for patients with respiratory symptoms

Further Management

- Some indications for admission to Children's Intensive Care Unit are:

☐ Requirement for continuous monitoring

□ Requirement for inotropic support
Significant bleeding, especially in the setting of severe thrombocytopenia or coagulopathy
Evidence of end-organ hypoperfusion, e.g. altered mental status, oliguria despite fluids
Respiratory compromise, e.g. secondary to pleural effusions

# The Baby Bear Book

# Hand, Foot and Mouth Disease and Herpangina

## Introduction

- Caused by Enteroviruses (EVs)
- About 25 EVs have been shown to cause Hand, Foot and Mouth Disease (HFMD), especially EV71, Coxsackie A16, A6 and echoviruses
- A variant of HFMD is herpangina, which presents with mouth ulcers but no rash, and is due to the same group of EVs
- Incubation period

- Three to five days (2 days to 2 weeks)

**Contagious period**

- Virus excretion occurs from a few days before, during the acute stage of illness and continues for 3 to 4 weeks from the saliva and 6 to 12 weeks from the faeces

- **Transmission**

Direct contact with saliva, nasal secretions and fluid from vesicles
Oral-faecal transmission via contaminated food, drink and fomites

## Clinical Features

- Generally a mild disease affecting young children
- Recovery in about 1 week
Typically presents with fever for up to 5 days:

Mouth ulcers
Vesiculo-papular rash lasting 7 to 10 days over the palms, soles and buttocks
Sometimes there are papules over the shins

- Herpangina patients have multiple mouth ulcers over the posterior pharynx, buccal mucosa and tongue, but no rash
- Complications are rare: Myocarditis, pulmonary oedema, interstitial pneumonitis, brainstem encephalitis, aseptic meningitis, acute flaccid paralysis and even death

## Management

- Isolate the patient in a single room or cohort patients with the same disease condition.
- Symptomatic treatment with close attention to hydration.
- Close monitoring for complications:

Vomiting, change in sensorium, seizures, myoclonic jerks
Hypertension/hypotension, tachycardia out of proportion to fever (normally pulse increases by about 10 bpm above baseline for every \(1^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\) rise in temperature)
Raised total white cell count

IVIG is a possible therapy for patients with complicated disease.
Medical certificate for 10 days after onset of illness.
- No swimming for next 3 months in order to decrease transmission to susceptible hosts.
- For complicated patients, in addition to throat swab, send stools and vesicular fluid for EV PCR +/- EV culture.

# CHAPTER 66

# Common Bacterial Infections

Tan Woon Hui, Natalie; Chong Chia Yin; Thoon Koh Cheng

## Bacterial Meningitis

### Introduction

The three most common organisms causing haematogenously acquired bacterial meningitis in otherwise healthy children (beyond the neonatal period) are *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Neisseria meningitidis* and *Haemophilus influenzae* Type b (Hib).

### Symptoms and Signs

Fever
- Headache
Photophobia
Vomiting
- Neck stiffness
Back pain
Myalgia
Rash
Confusion and disorientation
- Lethargy
Seizures
- Kernig's and Brudzinski's signs
- Sixth nerve palsy (suggesting raised intracranial pressure [ICP])
- Papilloedema (uncommon)
- Coma

Symptoms and signs are dependent on the patient's age and the duration of illness. Infants may not have neck stiffness but may be irritable and have inconsolable crying. They may also feed poorly and have vomiting and diarrhoea. A bulging fontanelle may indicate raised ICP but is not a highly sensitive or specific sign for meningitis. Grunting respirations indicate a critically ill infant.

### Investigations

- Lumbar puncture (LP) — Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) for cell count, gram stain and culture:

- Cells — Predominantly polymorphs (normal CSF WBC: younger than 1 month <20/uL, 1–2 months old <10/uL, older than 2 months <5/uL; and no polymorphs)

492

b4854_Sec-16.indd 492

12-Dec-22 11:08:57 AM

# Common Bacterial Infections

» Protein — Elevated (normal CSF protein in children: younger than 1 month \(0.3 - 1.2\mathrm{g / L}\), 1 to 3 months old \(0.2 - 0.6\mathrm{g / L}\), older than 3 months \(0.1 - 0.4\mathrm{g / L}\))
Glucose — Reduced (normal CSF glucose concentration should be approximately two-third of serum levels; younger than 6 months 2.0–5.6 mmol/L, older than 6 months 2.4–4.3 mmol/L)
Pressure — Accepted upper limit during LP with patient in the left lateral decubitus position is \(150\mathrm{mm}\) of water in older children and \(85 - 110\mathrm{mm}\) of water in younger children. A normal range between \(0 - 60\mathrm{mm}\) of water has been reported in neonates.
Latex agglutination tests for detection of polysaccharide antigen is most reliable for Hib (85–95%), followed by S. pneumoniae (50–75%) and N. meningitidis (33–50%)
Polymerase chain reaction for detection of \(N\). meningitidis
> Contraindications to LP:

Signs of raised ICP
Cardiorespiratory instability
□ Infection in the area through which the LP needle will pass
Evidence of coagulopathy

» Interpretation of blood-contaminated CSF (‘Traumatic Taps’):

Traumatic LPs occur in up to \(20\%\) of cases. Simple calculations to correct for blood contamination are based on the assumption that the ratio of white to red cells in the CSF attributable to blood contamination is approximately 1:500
Another approach would be to determine the Observed: Predicted WBC (O:P) ratio: CSF WBC (Predicted) = CSF RBC x Blood WBC/ Blood RBC.
The predicted value is subtracted from the actual or observed CSF WBC.
True CSF leucocytosis exists when the observed CSF WBC count is greater than the predicted WBC count (although this method appears to offer better sensitivity and specificity than using the CSF white to red cell ratio alone, it may overcorrect the WBC count)
It has been suggested by some authors that contamination of the CSF by less than 10000 red cells \(\times 10^{6}\) does not influence the CSF white cell count and many authorities still believe that it is safer to interpret blood contaminated CSF using the same criteria as a non-contaminated CSF.

Full blood count
- C-reactive protein (CRP) — Useful in distinguishing bacterial from viral meningitis but has the limitation of low specificity.
- Procalcitonin — Rises earlier than CRP at 3–4 hours after onset of infection and is more specific for invasive bacterial infection.
- Blood culture — Positive in most children with bacterial meningitis, especially that caused by Hib or S. pneumoniae

12-Dec-22 11:08:57 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

- Serum or blood glucose at the time of LP

## Management

- Please refer to your institution's antimicrobial guidelines for meningitis therapy.
- Dexamethasone administered just before or concurrently with the first dose of intravenous (IV) antibiotics significantly diminishes the incidence of neurologic and audiologic deficits due to Hib meningitis. Early administration also improves outcome in pneumococcal meningitis. The recommended dose of IV dexamethasone is \(0.2\mathrm{mg / kg}\) /dose Q8H for 2-4 days.
- Supportive care — Unless the patient is mildly affected, the initial care should be in the children's Intensive Care Unit, as most life-threatening complications occur early and require urgent intervention.

## Prognosis and Sequelae

- The mortality rate is \(< 5 - 10\%\) for the three most common pathogens. Case fatality rate and neurological sequelae are greatest with pneumococcal meningitis.
- Sensorineural hearing loss is the most common sequelae. It occurs in \(20 - 30\%\) of patients after S. pneumoniae meningitis and in \(5 - 10\%\) of cases after meningitis due to Hib or N. meningitidis. Hearing should be tested within 1 month of discharge to detect hearing loss as early as possible.

## Prevention

- Hib conjugate vaccine has had a dramatic impact on reducing the incidence of invasive Hib disease.
- Pneumococcal conjugate vaccine (Prevenar 13) is part of the National Childhood Immunisation Programme (NCIP) in Singapore. Children older than 2 years who are at risk of developing invasive pneumococcal disease should also receive the 23-valent polysaccharide vaccine in addition to the conjugate vaccine.
- Quadrivalent meningococcal conjugate vaccine against A, C, Y and W-135 strains is recommended for high-risk children (e.g. asplenia). Meningococcal serogroup C conjugate vaccine is routinely administered in some countries.
Chemoprophylaxis:

» Meningococcal disease (see 'Meningococcal Infections')
Hib - Rifampicin prophylaxis is recommended for all household contacts

0-3 months: \(10\mathrm{mg / kg}\) once daily for 4 days
3 months to 12 years: \(20\mathrm{mg / kg}\) once daily for 4 days
>12 years and adults: \(600\mathrm{mg}\) once daily for 4 days

## Meningococcal Infections

- Caused by Neisseria meningitidis
- Can result in bacteraemia, meningitis, septic shock, disseminated intravascular coagulation and focal infections
Usually serogroups A, B, C, W-135 and Y

b4854_Sec-16.indd 494

12-Dec-22 11:08:57 AM

# Common Bacterial Infections

495

Vaccination available against A, C, W-135 and Y
Children with terminal complement defects, properdin deficiencies, functional or anatomic asplenia are at high risk for meningococcal infections
- Incubation period 1–10 days, usually \(< 4\) days

## Treatment

IV Penicillin G (300000 units/kg/day), or
IV Ampicillin (200-400 mg/kg/day), or
IV Ceftriaxone (100 mg/kg/day) or Cefotaxime 300 mg/kg/day (if IV drip contains calcium)
- Treat for 5 to 7 days if meningitis, 10 to 14 days if septic shock
- Give Rifampicin to eradicate nasopharyngeal carriage, unless the primary treatment was with Ceftriaxone

## Transmission and Precautions

- Droplet transmission of respiratory tract secretions
- Respiratory isolation for 24 hours after initiation of effective therapy
- Place the patient in a single room
- When a single room is not available and cohorting is not achievable, maintain spatial separation of at least 3 ft between the infected patient and other patients and visitors
- Wear a mask during examination or when suctioning of patients with suspected meningitis/meningococcal infection within 24 hours of initiation of therapy

## High-risk Contacts for which Chemoprophylaxis is Recommended

Household contact, especially children
- Childcare or nursery school contact during the previous 7 days
- Direct exposure to patient's secretions during the 7 days before the onset of disease, e.g. kissing, sharing toothbrushes or eating utensils
- Frequently sleeps or eats in same dwelling as index patient
- Mouth-to-mouth resuscitation
- Unprotected contact during endotracheal intubation

## Prophylaxis Regime for Meningococcal Exposure

Healthcare workers and adult household contacts
» PO Rifampicin \(10\mathrm{mg / kg / dose}\) (max. \(600\mathrm{mg})\) Q12H \(\times 2\) days, or
» IM Ceftriaxone \(250\mathrm{mg}\times 1\) dose (pregnancy), or
» PO Ciprofloxacin \(500\mathrm{mg}\times 1\) dose (liver dysfunction)
Children
» PO Rifampicin:
- Older than 1 month: \(10\mathrm{mg / kg / dose}\) (max. \(600\mathrm{mg})\) Q12H \(\times 2\) days
- Younger than 1 month: \(5\mathrm{mg / kg / dose}\) (max. \(600\mathrm{mg})\) Q12H \(\times 2\) days, or
» IM Ceftriaxone \(125\mathrm{mg}\times 1\) dose (younger than 15 years)
Note: 1 capsule Rifampicin = 150 mg

# CHAPTER 67

# Tuberculosis

Tan Woon Hui, Natalie; Karen Donceras Nadua; Chong Chia Yin; Thoon Koh Cheng

## Introduction

- Caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis
- Acid-fast bacillus (AFB)
Transmission usually air-borne

## Presentation

- History of contact with an adult with active tuberculosis (TB)
- Risk of disease in exposed children highest in infancy (43% vs. 5–10% in adults)
Higher risk of extrapulmonary disease in children
Higher mortality rates if \(< 4\) years

## Latent TB Infection (LTBI)

- Asymptomatic infection with no abnormal physical findings
Positive or reactive mantoux test (MTT) or T spot TB
- Chest X-ray (CXR) may be normal or show only granulomas or calcification in the lung, regional lymph nodes or both
LTBI may evolve into active TB disease

## TB Disease

Pulmonary tuberculosis (PTB):

Most asymptomatic
Early manifestations include fever, weight loss, cough, night sweats and chills

TB Lymphadenitis:

Most common form of extrapulmonary TB
Usually cervical, supraclavicular and submandibular nodes
Can be unilateral or bilateral
Lymph nodes generally firm and rubbery, non-tender and fixed to underlying tissues
Can progress to affect several nodes
May be associated with active PTB

- Meningitis/miliary TB (rare):

497

b4854_Sec-16.indd 497

12-Dec-22 11:08:57 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

» Miliary disease is a disseminated form of TB which occurs when massive bacteraemia causes disease in two or more organs
» Clinical manifestations are protean depending on the site of involvement

- Other extrapulmonary involvement includes pericardium, pleura, abdominal viscera, bone/joint, kidney.

## Diagnosis

• CXR findings:

» Hilar adenitis
» Segmental lesions
» Collapse or consolidation
» Calcification occurs >6 months after infection
» Multiple lung foci or cavitation
» Pleural effusions (rare)
» Miliary disease

- Positive MTT and/or Interferon Gamma Release Assay (IGRA)

» MTT

☐ Site: Clean volar surface of left forearm
☐ 0.1 ml of tuberculin injected intradermally
☐ 26 G tuberculin needle angled upwards
☐ Tense intradermal wheal 6–10 mm should be obtained immediately during injection
☐ Do not cover or dress the injection site
☐ Read mm of induration 48–72 hours later (transverse diameter only)
☐ Incubation period from infection to a positive MTT is 2–12 weeks (median 3–4 weeks)
☐ 10% of immunocompetent children with culture-documented disease do not have an initial reactive MTT
☐ Children who are immunosuppressed, or who have viral infections (especially measles, varicella and influenza), and miliary TB are often anergic and do not demonstrate a reactive MTT
☐ Positive mantoux reaction:

• Induration >10 mm (younger than 12 years)

>15 mm (older than 12 years)

>5 mm (any age) if no previous BCG and exposure to contagious index patient or immunosuppressed patient

• Vesiculation or pustular reaction of any size

» Interferon Gamma Release Assay (IGRA)

☐ Two main tests
• T-spot TB
• Quantiferon Gold

# Tuberculosis

499

• Positive microbiology Investigations

» Specimens collected according to site of suspected infection should be sent for AFB smear, AFB culture, TB nucleic acid amplification tests (PCR)

Site Specimen

|  PTB | 3 specimens from gastric lavage (early morning) or sputum or induced sputum ± bronchoalveolar lavage ± pleural fluid  |
| --- | --- |

Lymphadenitis Biopsy and histology of the affected lymph node(s)

Meningitis Cerebrospin al fluid

» Isolation of M. tuberculosis by culture from the above specimens establishes the diagnosis of TB
» Organisms are isolated from fewer than 50% of children and 75% of infants with PTB
» Culture material should be obtained from children with evidence of TB disease, especially when:

□ An isolate from the source case is not available
□ Immunocompromised, e.g. Human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) infection
□ Extrapulmonary disease

Treatment

Site of TB Drugs and Duration of Treatment

|  PTB, Lymphadenitis | 6 months: 2 months HRZ, followed by 4 months of HR; or9 months of HR; or2 months HRE followed by 7 months of HR  |
| --- | --- |
|  Extrapulmonary | 9–12 months: 2 months HRZE, followed by 7–10 months of HR  |

(H: Isoniazid; R: Rifampicin; Z: Pyrazinamide; E: Ethambutol)

- If drug resistance is suspected, initial therapy should include a fourth drug, either ethambutol or streptomycin, until drug susceptibility results are available
- Pyridoxine is recommended if:

» Diet deficient in milk and meat/nutritional deficiencies
» Symptomatic HIV-infected children
» Breastfed infants and their mothers
» Pregnant adolescents and women

• Complete TB notification form on diagnosis

- Refer to TB Control Unit for Daily Observed Therapy (DOT) if compliance is a problem or multi-drug-resistant (MDR) TB

# Persons at Increased Risk for Drug-Resistant TB Infection or Disease

• History of treatment for active TB
• Source case for the contact received treatment

b4854_Sec-16.indd 499

12-Dec-22 11:08:58 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

- Contacts of a patient with a drug-resistant contagious TB disease
- Foreign-born persons
- Residents of areas where the prevalence of drug-resistant TB is documented to be high
- Persons whose source case has positive smears for AFB or cultures after 2 months of appropriate anti-TB therapy

## Chemoprophylaxis

- Indicated for contacts with:
  » Reactive MTT or T-spot TB but clear CXR, or
  » MTT/T-spot TB conversion when retested after 8–12 weeks.
- Rifampicin 4 months daily (if Rifampicin-susceptible)

## Newborn Infant Whose Mother (or Other Household Contact) has LTBI or TB Disease

Management of the newborn infant whose mother (or other household contact) has LTBI or TB disease (see Table 16.4):

- Based on categorisation of the maternal or household contact infection
- Protection of the infant from infection is of paramount importance
- Separation of the infant from the mother or household contact should be avoided when possible

## Infection Control and Prevention

- The following children are considered contagious, and standard and airborne precautions must be observed:

  » Cavitating or extensive PTB
  » Positive sputum AFB smears
  » Laryngeal involvement
  » Open abscess

- Nurse in a negative-pressure isolation room
- Doors to the room should be kept closed at all times
- All procedures should be carried out within the room
- No cough-inducing procedures, e.g. chest physiotherapy
- High-filtration TB (N95) masks on entry into room. Use a surgical mask on the patient, if movement out of the room is necessary
- Limit visitors
- Do not allow children or immunosuppressed visitors
- All family members who are visitors may require CXR to rule out active PTB as well (consult infectious disease specialist)
- De-isolate if sputum or gastric lavage for AFB smear negative × 3, and no cough

The infant and mother or contact should be on DOT therapy | TB in the mother or contact should be reported immediately to the Department of Clinical Epidemiology so that investigation of all household members can be performed promptly All contacts should have a MTT, chest X-ray and physical examination The infant should be evaluated at monthly intervals during treatment Note: Patients with multiple drug-resistant TB should be on DOT therapy  |

# CHAPTER 69

# Immunisations

Thoon Koh Cheng; Tan Woon Hui, Natalie; Karen Donceras Nadua

# Golden Rules in Vaccination

- All vaccines can be administered at the same visit concurrently with other vaccines.
  » No intervals are required between two or more inactivated vaccines.
  » No intervals are required between live and inactivated vaccines. However, for a live vaccine, do consider the expected side effects of the live vaccine (e.g. for measles, mumps and rubella [MMR] or varicella vaccine, fever or rash can occur 5–14 days after vaccination) when administering an inactivated vaccine at a later date.
  » Two or more different live or intranasal vaccines should be given on the same day, or at an interval of at least 28 days apart (except yellow fever after measles/MMR vaccine, and Bacillus Calmette-Guerin [BCG] before or after oral poliovirus vaccine [OPV]).
- Inactivated vaccines are generally not affected by circulating antibody to the antigen.
  » No intervals are generally required between blood products and inactivated vaccines.
- Live vaccines may be affected by circulating antibody to the antigen.
  » An interval is required between blood products and live vaccines (e.g. 11 months after intravenous immunoglobulins for Kawasaki Disease).
- Vaccine doses should not be given at less than minimum intervals or minimum ages recommended for a particular dose of a specific vaccine.
  » Vaccine doses given up to 4 days before the minimum interval or age can be counted as valid, but should not be abused for scheduling purposes.
- For combination vaccines, the minimum intervals for the individual components must still be met unless otherwise specified by the manufacturer.

# The Baby Bear Book

- It is not necessary to restart the series or add doses when trying to catch-up for any delayed vaccination series.
- A contraindication is a condition in a recipient that greatly increases the chance of a serious adverse reaction. A precaution is either a condition in a recipient that might increase the chance or severity of an adverse reaction or which might compromise the vaccine's ability to produce immunity.

- True contraindications are rare (e.g. true anaphylaxis or allergies to vaccine components, encephalopathy not due to another identifiable cause occurring within 7 days of pertussis vaccination, use of live vaccines in pregnant or immunocompromised persons).

- In persons with bleeding disorders (e.g. haemophilia), where the disease severity is mild to moderate, vaccines should be given in the route recommended by the manufacturer due to safety and efficacy concerns (e.g. intramuscular [IM], use a 23-gauge needle and apply direct pressure for $\geq 2$ minutes). Where the disease is severe, then IM vaccination may cause harm, and the route of vaccination must be decided by the primary physician and benefits and risks clearly explained to parents (especially regarding potential loss of efficacy and more severe local reactions to IM vaccines given via subcutaneous/intradermal route); alternatively, when anti-haemophilia or other similar therapy are given, this should be followed by vaccination in the recommended route.

- Healthy household contacts of immunocompromised persons should receive MMR, varicella and influenza vaccines when indicated (risk of wild-type infections outweigh risk of live vaccine-related infections, which are mostly mild). Rotavirus vaccine should also be offered to these healthy household contacts though available data is much less compared with the above.
- Unscientific allegations of connections between vaccines or vaccine components (e.g. MMR or thimerosal with autism) should be vigorously defended.

- However, we should still work with patients and parents who refuse the vaccines on these grounds, in the hope that they might change their minds.

- For travel-related vaccines, remember there is no vaccine for lack of common sense, e.g. petting wild monkeys/dogs, having unprotected sex, eating uncooked/unwashed food.

# CHAPTER 70

# Classification of Newborn Babies

Tan Pih Lin; Khoo Poh Choo

Newborn babies can be classified according to their gestational age, birth weight and other physical attributes.

## Gestational Age

### Obstetric Methods

- Last menstrual period (LMP): Gestation age (GA) can be calculated from the first day of LMP. The estimated date of delivery (40 weeks' gestation) is calculated by adding 1 year and 7 days to the first day of LMP, then subtracting 3 months from that.
- Ultrasound: Most reliable method, especially if performed within the first trimester. Various measurements may be used such as crown-rump length, biparietal diameter, femur length and abdominal circumference.

### Neonatal Methods

- Dubowitz Score (see Figure 17.1): Performed 12-24 hours after birth.
- Ballard Score (see Figure 17.2): A simplified version of the Dubowitz Score. Infant does not need to be alert or vigorous. Accuracy is \(\pm 2\) weeks.

The best estimate of neonatal GA is determined from both obstetric and neonatal methods. If the two methods differ from each other by >2 weeks, it is customary to take the neonatal estimate.

Extreme preterm: \(< 28\) weeks
- Very preterm: 28 weeks to \(< 32\) weeks
Moderate preterm: 32 weeks to \(< 34\) weeks
- Late preterm: 34 weeks to \(< 37\) weeks
Term: 37 weeks to \(< 42\) weeks
Post-term: GA at birth is \(>42\) weeks

## Weight

- Extremely Low Birth Weight (ELBW): \(< 1000\mathrm{g}\)
- Very Low Birth Weight (VLBW): \(< 1500\mathrm{g}\)
- Low Birth Weight (LBW): \(< 2500\mathrm{g}\)

# Classification of Newborn Babies

## Relative Size

Plot parameters into growth chart (see Figures 17.3 and 17.4).

- Small for gestational age (SGA): Birth weight \(< 10\)th percentile for estimated GA
- Appropriate for gestational age (AGA): Birth weight between 10th and 90th percentile for estimated GA
Large for gestational age (LGA): Birth weight \(>90\)th percentile for GA

## Symmetrical and Asymmetrical Growth Retardation

In symmetrical growth retardation, the baby is small but the growth parameters (head circumference, length and weight) are proportionate to each other. This pattern commonly occurs in early pregnancy and may be related to a genetic syndrome, intra-uterine infection or constitutional.

In asymmetrical growth retardation, head growth is relatively spared as compared to length and weight. Babies are born appearing wasted and malnourished. Typically, this happens in late pregnancy. Common causes include placental insufficiency, pregnancy induced hypertension and multiple pregnancies.

## Bibliography

1. Ballard JL, Novak KK, Driver M. (1979) A simplified score for assessment of fetal maturation of newly born infants. J Pediatr 95(5 Pt 1):769-774.

2. Dubowitz LM, Dubowitz V, Goldberg C. (1970) Clinical assessment of gestational age in the newborn infant. J Pediatr 77(1):1-10.

3. Fenton, Tanis R, Kim, Jae H. (2013) A systemic review and meta-analysis to revise the Fenton growth chart for preterm infants. BMC Pediatr 13:59.

# CHAPTER 71

# Newborn Resuscitation

Quek Bin Huey; Abdul Haium Abdul Alim

## Introduction

Approximately 1–10% of newborns delivered in hospital require active resuscitation. Majority only requires simple stabilisation. Goal of newborn resuscitation is to deliver healthy, breathing, pink babies OR when not possible, to transfer seriously asphyxiated babies to the Neonatal Intensive Care Unit (NICU) in as good condition as possible!

Changes from in-utero to ex-utero environment:

- Lungs expand with the first cry or few breaths
- Lungs are filled with air whilst fluid is absorbed
- Increase in blood flow to the lungs
Gas exchange takes place
- Closure of intra and extra cardiac shunts
- Oxygen transported to tissues of other parts of the body

Failure or disruption of these changes results in failure of gas exchange, persistence of foetal circulation, increasing acidosis and hypoxia. If this asphyxia is prolonged, it leads to cell death, and eventually permanent neurological damage and even death.

The phases of asphyxia consist of:

- Primary apnoea — the breathing becomes deeper and more rapid, then regular breathing ceases and consciousness is lost. Heart rate (HR) is still maintained, and at this phase, the baby will respond to stimulation.
- Secondary apnoea — if the insult continues, the baby will start to gasp and go into terminal or secondary apnoea. The HR and blood pressure fall, and there is increasing acidosis. The baby will require active resuscitation with positive pressure ventilation and even chest compression.

The baby in secondary apnoea will die without intervention and may die despite intervention. Primary and secondary apnoea may occur in utero. It is not possible to distinguish at birth. Thus, when faced with an apnoeic baby at birth that does not respond to simple interventions, active resuscitation must be started immediately.

Health professionals who attend to delivery of newborns must thus be proficient in at least the initial steps of stabilisation and resuscitation and be able to work as a team for effective resuscitation. They should be accredited in neonatal resuscitation (such as the Singapore Neonatal Resuscitation Course [SNRC]). The recommendations for newborn stabilisation are constantly being updated based on best available evidence. The most recent guidelines come from the International Liaison Committee on Resuscitation (ILCOR) 2020 Consensus on Science and Treatment Recommendations (CoSTR), updated

516

b4854_Sec-17.indd 516

# Newborn Resuscitation

Paediatric & Neonatal resuscitation guidelines from the American Heart Association and European Resuscitation Council in 2021, and the Singapore Resuscitation and First Aid Council (SRFAC) Neonatal & Paediatric Resuscitation Guidelines Workgroup 2021.

The guidelines are organised in alignment with the newborn resuscitation algorithm processes:

- Anticipate need and preparation
- Umbilical cord management — Delayed cord clamping
- Initial steps — Airway and temperature maintenance
- Breathing (Positive pressure ventilation [PPV])
- Chest compression
- Drugs — Medications
- Post-resuscitation care

## Preparation

Preparation involves anticipation of high risk pregnancies, preparation of appropriate equipment, as well as adequate personnel at delivery.

## Anticipation

- Quick history:

What is the expected gestational age? This determines the equipment needed.
How many babies are expected?
Is the amniotic fluid clear? If meconium or heavily blood stained — be prepared for full resuscitation.
Are there any additional risk factors?

Antepartum, e.g. maternal diabetes, maternal hypertension, maternal haemorrhage, maternal infection, prolonged rupture of membranes.
Intrapartum, e.g. prolapsed cord, maternal sedation, abnormal foetal heart tracing, abruptio placenta.

What is the plan for cord management?

- Pre-brief: Identify roles (e.g. team leader, assign some in charge of airway, circulation, documentation, etc.), anticipate problems → Call for help early!

## Equipment

- Use a check-list:

Thermoregulation

Overhead radiant warmer, warm towels, polyethylene bag for newborns <33 weeks' gestation, woollen cap.

Airway

Bulb suction or appropriate size suction catheter, set suction pressure at \(80 - 100\mathrm{mmHg}\), meconium aspirator if liquor thickly stained with meconium.
Equipment for intubation: Laryngoscope and appropriate size endotracheal tube (ETT) for gestation.

b4854_Sec-17.indd 517

12-Dec-22 3:21:25 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

» Breathing

Oxygen source and blender, pulse oximeter
Ambu-bag or T-piece (preferred for preterm deliveries)
Appropriate size facemasks

» Circulation

ECG leads - if available
Drugs: Adrenaline, normal saline
Umbilical venous catheter (UVC)

# Personnel

- At every birth, there should be one dedicated person who is capable of initial steps & PPV.
- If additional risk factors are present, then a team with advanced skills, including a team leader should be present. The team should do a pre-briefing to go through role assignments during resuscitation and identify the risk factors.

# Umbilical Cord Management

Cord clamping may be delayed up to 30–60 seconds for vigorous term or preterm newborns (American Academy of Paediatrics/ILCOR recommendation 2020). However, do follow your hospital guidelines with regard to delayed cord clamping. The vigorous infant can be placed at the level of the introitus or on the mother's abdomen before the cord is clamped.

# Airway: Initial Assessment & Stabilisation

Assessment begins immediately after birth and continues throughout the resuscitation process until vital signs have normalised. The neonatal resuscitation algorithm is shown in Figure 17.5.

Assess the newborn immediately after delivery with these four questions:

Is the infant term?
- Does the baby have a good cry and good breathing effort?
Is the baby vigorous with good tone?
Is the liquor clear?

If the answers to the above questions are **Yes**, then the infant may have **routine care** and can stay with the mother. Dry the baby and continue temperature control by putting baby skin to skin with mother. Ensure the baby's airway is open and continue ongoing evaluation of breathing and activity.

# Initial Stabilisation

If the answers to the above questions are **No**, then the baby needs to be stabilised, with emphasis on temperature and airway.

- **Dry** → Place baby under the radiant warmer and dry the infant using pre-warmed towels. If it is a premature baby under 33 weeks' gestation, place immediately

b4854_Sec-17.indd 518

12-Dec-22 3:21:25 PM

# Newborn Resuscitation

519

in a polyethylene bag without drying. Put on a woollen cap as heat loss is maximal from the head. Temperature of baby should be monitored throughout resuscitation.

- Position \(\rightarrow\) Open the airway by placing head in a slightly extended position or use a shoulder roll.
- Stimulate \(\rightarrow\) If the newborn is not vigorous or does not cry after the above manoeuvres, stimulate by gently flicking the soles of the feet or rubbing the back.
- Suction \(\rightarrow\) Only if secretions are present. Suction the mouth followed by the nose if there are secretions. Suction pressure should not exceed \(100\mathrm{mmHg}\), and depth should not go beyond \(5\mathrm{cm}\) from the level of the lips. Not all babies need suction, and keep in mind that it can cause bradycardia.

If the newborn does not breathe well or cry after initial stabilisation, initiate **bag and mask ventilation** (i.e. breathe for the baby!). PPV **should not be delayed for more than 1 minute** after birth. You should also call for help for more assistance and a pulse oximeter should be placed over the baby's right hand or wrist. An extra person should also be in charge of the documentation of the resuscitation process. Check HR by auscultating the chest. Count number of heart beats in 6 seconds, and times 10 for HR in 1 minute.

## Management of Meconium Stained Amniotic Fluid

When meconium is observed in the amniotic fluid, and the newly born infant is depressed, the initial steps should be performed, including suctioning of the oropharynx for meconium. If infant continues to be depressed, start PPV as per guidelines. Intra-tracheal suction should be performed if there is suspected obstruction of airway from meconium, and if personnel capable of intubation are present.

## Breathing: Positive Pressure Ventilation

Indications:

- Apnoea or gasping
HR less than \(100 / \mathrm{min}\)
- Oxygen saturation below the target range despite free-flow oxygen or continuous positive airway pressure

Rate: Rate of bagging is 40 to 60 breaths per minute.

Oxygen: Start ventilation with room air (21% oxygen) for term and premature babies 33 weeks' gestation and above. For premature babies less than 33 weeks' gestation, start at 21 to 30% oxygen. Refer to the targeted pre-ductal saturation according to age in minutes in Figure 17.5.

**Ventilation is the most important and effective step in newborn resuscitation.** If done correctly, establishment of adequate ventilation will restore circulation. If you are performing effective ventilation, you should see adequate chest excursion with each inflation and equal air entry. The HR will start to improve (above 100/min), the baby should start to breathe spontaneously, the saturation will improve (look more pink) and tone should also improve.

# Newborn Resuscitation

Assessment of response after PPV:

- Check HR after 15 seconds of commencing PPV and assess chest movement (chest rise).
- If chest movement observed, continue PPV for another 15 seconds \(\rightarrow\) total 30 seconds.
- If no chest movement observed \(\rightarrow\) Perform ventilation corrective steps (MR. SOPA - see Table 17.1), then administer PPV for 30 seconds.

Table 17.1 Ventilation Corrective Steps (MR. SOPA)

|   | Corrective Steps | Actions  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  M | Mask adjustment | Reapply the mask, consider 2 hand technique  |
|  R | Reposition airway | Place head neutral or slightly extended  |
|  Try PPV and reassess chest movements  |   |   |
|  S | Suction mouth and nose | Use bulb syringe or suction catheter  |
|  O | Open mouth | Open the mouth and lift the jaw forward  |
|  Try PPV and reassess chest movements  |   |   |
|  P | Pressure increase | Increase the pressure in 5–10 cmH_{2}O increments, maximum 40 cmH_{2}O  |
|  Try PPV and reassess chest movements  |   |   |
|  A | Alternative airway | Place an endotracheal tube or laryngeal mask airway  |
|  Try PPV and assess chest movement and breath sounds  |   |   |

Re-assess HR after **30 seconds of PPV with good chest movement**. If HR improves to more than 100/min, check breathing. Stop PPV if continued good HR and sustained good respiratory effort and tone. If still apnoeic, continue PPV until respiratory effort improves. If HR drops to less than 60/min, start chest compression with coordinated PPV.

## Chest Compression

Indication: HR less than 60/min after 30 seconds of effective PPV.

The baby should ideally have an advanced airway placed by this time, i.e. ETT or laryngeal mask. If ECG leads are available, it should be placed to allow continuous heart rate monitoring as cardiopulmonary resuscitation is taking place. Always ventilate baby at the same time with oxygen increased to 100%. The frequency should be 3 compressions to 1 ventilation with an aim to deliver 90 to 120 compressions in 1 minute. The 'two thumb' technique (Figure 17.6) is preferred. Both thumbs should meet over the sternum with fingers around the chest wall with the sternum compressed to one-third of the anteroposterior chest dimension.

Assessment of response after chest compression:

- Check HR after 60 seconds of coordinated chest compression with ventilation.
- If HR remains less than \(60 / \mathrm{min}\), consider drugs.

# The Baby Bear Book

![img-15.jpeg](img-15.jpeg)

Figure 17.6 'Two-thumb' technique.

## Drugs — Medications

- Adrenaline

- Indication: HR less than 60/min despite at least 30 seconds of effective PPV followed by 60 seconds of coordination PPV and chest compression

Dose:

Intravenous (preferred route) — 0.1 to \(0.3\mathrm{ml / kg}\) of 1:10000 as rapid bolus. Can be repeated in 3 to 5 minutes.
ETT (less effective) — 0.5 to \(1\mathrm{ml / kg}\) of 1:10000

Normal saline

Indication: Evidence of volume or blood loss, hypovolaemic shock
Dose: \(10\mathrm{ml / kg}\) over 10 to 20 minutes

Blood can also be considered if evidence of significant blood loss such as maternal abruptio placentae.

UVC should be inserted for intravenous access.

Chest compression can be moved to head of the bed to free up space for UVC insertion

## Post Resuscitation Care

- Check temperature
- Secure all tubes — ETT, UVC
- Inform NICU of admission
- Post resuscitation debrief should be done after each resuscitation. It should be a team-centred approach where the team members talk to each other and identify the areas of resuscitation that went well, and what can be improved upon. The team leader's role is to facilitate the discussion, focusing on teamwork and communication.

## Conclusion

Effective resuscitation is also dependent on these key behavioural skills:

- Know your environment
- Use available information
- Anticipate and plan
- Clearly identify a team leader
- Communicate effectively
- Delegate workload optimally
- Allocate attention wisely
- Use available resources
- Call for help when needed
- Maintain professional behaviour

Remember:

- Time is life
- Speed is essential
- Be prepared
- Know what you're doing!
- Be cool (Do not panic!!!!!)
- Do not be over-optimistic (Expect the worst and be prepared for it)

# CHAPTER 72

# Breastfeeding

Chua Mei Chien; Pooja Agarwal Jayagobi; Tong Wing Yee

## Benefits

Breast milk is the preferred food for babies. A rich body of scientific evidence supports the advantages of breastfeeding for both mother and baby. Beyond the nutritional benefits and psychological bonding, breastfeeding protects the baby from infections, allergic disorders and reduces the risk of metabolic diseases, such as obesity, type 2 diabetes mellitus and cardiovascular disease. Therefore, it is the professional and moral obligation of healthcare providers to advocate breastfeeding and support the mother and baby in every possible way. Any period of breastfeeding is better than no breastfeeding at all.

The current recommendation is **exclusive breastfeeding** with no supplementary fluids until 6 months of age. Thereafter, appropriate complementary foods are gradually introduced. Baby can be breastfed up to 2 years of age or longer. The following are some of the benefits of breastfeeding:

- Protection against infection

- Breast milk contains many anti-infective agents, including secretory IgA, lactoferrin, macrophages and leukocytes, and reduces:

Respiratory tract infections
Otitis media
Viral diseases
Diarrhoeal diseases by promoting the growth of healthy and non-pathogenic bacteria in the intestine, preventing Escherichia coli from binding with the intestinal mucosa
The risk of necrotising enterocolitis (NEC) in premature infants

- Protection against atopy and allergy

Breastfed babies are less likely to develop eczema, asthma and other atopic diseases. Breastfeeding is especially beneficial for babies who are at high risk of developing atopic diseases due to a strong family history of allergy.
Breast milk contains species-specific protein. Complete breastfeeding delays introduction of foreign proteins such as cow's milk protein.

Nutritional benefits

Breast milk provides adequate nutrition for term babies up to 6 months of age.
The composition of breast milk evolves to reflect the changing nutritional needs as the baby gets older.

# *Breastfeeding*

» Breast milk contains many compounds (e.g. omega-3 fatty acids) that are not present in infant formulae.
» The iron in breast milk is much more easily available to the baby although the quantitative amount is lower compared to the level in cow's milk.
• Other benefits
» Breastfeeding does not require special preparation or a clean water supply; it is relatively pathogen-free, economical and comes at a neutral temperature.
» Promotes mother — infant bonding.

## Characteristics of Human Milk

• Colostrum
» Produced in the first few postpartum days.
» Yellowish in appearance.
» Rich in immunoglobulins, especially secretory IgA and other anti-infective components to boost passive immunity in the baby.
• Transitional milk
» Gradually appears several days postpartum.
» Increases in volume, caloric content and water-soluble vitamins.
» Immunoglobulin components are less than colostrum.
• Mature milk
» There is a great biological variation in the characteristics of milk between individuals and even in the same individual at different phases of lactation.
» As the baby matures, human milk undergoes considerable variation to keep up with the baby's nutritional needs.
» Large part is water, and fat content is most variable.
» Caloric density is about 67 calories/100 ml.
» Fore milk
☐ Early part of the feeding.
☐ More dilute and contains less fat.
» Hind milk
☐ Later part of the feeding.
☐ More concentrated and contains more fat.
☐ As the energy content is higher in the hind milk, it is important to empty the breast completely during feeding.

## Stool and Urine Pattern in Breastfed Babies

Breastfed babies have characteristic stool and urine patterns. This helps in the assessment of the adequacy of feeding.

• First 24 hours
» Passes meconium 1–2 times
» Passes urine once or twice

b4854_Sec-17.indd 525

12-Dec-22 3:21:27 PM

# *The Baby Bear Book*

- 24–48 hours
  - More meconium, appearance of transitional stool
  - Passes urine 2–4 times over 24 hours
- 48–72 hours
  - Transitional stool changing to yellowish breastmilk stool
  - Urination frequency increases to 3–4 times over 24 hours
- Beyond 72 hours
  - Frequent loose yellowish stool
  - Passes urine 6–8 times over 24 hours
  - Failure to pass adequate amounts of urine and delayed appearance of yellowish mature stools are objective indicators of inadequate feeding

### **The Baby-Friendly Hospital Initiative (WHO/UNICEF)**

The Baby Friendly Hospital Initiative (BFHI) accreditation ensures that exclusive breastfeeding is promoted and protected. An accredited baby-friendly hospital is fully compliant to the 10 steps to successful breastfeeding as well as the international code of marketing of breastmilk substitutes.

#### **Ten Steps to Successful Breastfeeding**

- A written breastfeeding policy that is routinely communicated to all healthcare staff.
- Training all healthcare staff in skills necessary to implement this policy.
- Antenatal education to inform all pregnant women about the benefits and management of breastfeeding.
- Achieving skin-to-skin contact between mother and baby within 5 minutes of birth for at least 1 hour for both vaginal births and caesarean births without general anaesthesia (GA). Skin to skin can be performed for births after GA once the mother is alert.
- Showing mothers how to breastfeed with proper latching and positioning and how to maintain lactation, even if they should be separated from their infants.
- Giving newborn infants no food or drink other than breast milk unless medically indicated. Supplemental feeding if indicated for medical reasons should be given by cup or spoon feeding to avoid nipple confusion.
- Practising rooming-in to allow mothers and infants to remain together 24 hours a day. Medical examination and procedures should be done by the bedside whenever possible.
- Encouraging breastfeeding on demand with no restriction on the frequency and duration of feeding. Early and frequent feeding of at least 8 to 12 times a day helps establish mother's milk supply, promotes clearance of meconium, reduces incidence of jaundice and minimises breast engorgement.
- Giving no artificial teats or pacifiers to breastfeeding infants.
- Ensuring mothers have access to community breastfeeding support groups and other breastfeeding resources on discharge from the hospital or clinic.

b4854_Sec-17.indd 526

# Breastfeeding

# International Code of Marketing of Breastmilk Substitutes

Full compliance with the International Code of Marketing of Breastmilk Substitutes is also vital in the protection of exclusive breastfeeding. The hospital and healthcare providers are not to solicit or accept sponsorships or gifts from companies producing or marketing breastmilk substitutes.

# Monitoring the Adequacy of Feeding

The mother-and-baby dyad should be monitored closely and frequently in the first week to assess adequacy of feeding and to allow the identification and correction of breastfeeding difficulties. An adequately breastfed baby has the following characteristics:

- Good suck and swallow pattern
- Frequent feeding at least 8–12 times a day
- Normal frequency of urine and stools — See 'Stool and Urine Pattern in Breastfed Babies'
- Acceptable weight loss (<7% by Day 3 of life)
- Full-term breastfed babies most often regain birth weight by 10–14 days; a weight loss of >7% of the birth weight during the first week calls for a comprehensive review of the mother-and-baby dyad.

# Contraindications to Breastfeeding

A positive hepatitis B serology and Venereal Disease Research Laboratory (VDRL) test are NOT contraindications to breastfeeding.

There are very few contraindications to breastfeeding:

- Galactosaemia in the baby
- Maternal psychosis
- Maternal active tuberculosis
- Maternal human immunodeficiency virus (HIV)
- Maternal herpes with active lesion on the breast
- Maternal chemotherapy or radiotherapy for cancer treatment
- Selected medication: Radioactive iodine, anti-malaria drugs

Some drugs have no data on breastfeeding safety and alternatives should be offered whenever available.

# Pasteurised Donor Human Milk

When the mother's own milk is inadequate, contraindicated or not available, pasteurised donor human milk (PDHM) is the recommended alternative. PDHM can be provided to eligible infants who are hospitalised or at home.

Current eligibility criteria for referral to our centre's Human Milk Bank are as below:

- Infants with significant illness:

  - Preterm infants <35 weeks
  - Infants <1.8 kg

» Ex-prems with co-morbidities (e.g. chronic lung disease, necrotising enterocolitis, retinopathy of prematurity)
» Congenital heart disease
» Gastrointestinal disease
» Other medical conditions (e.g. encephalopathy)

• Infants in whom their own mother's milk is contraindicated due to:

» Medical conditions (e.g. HIV)
» Medical treatment (e.g. chemotherapy)
» Significant mental illness
» Drug misuse

• Infants in whom their own mother's milk is unavailable:

» Adopted infants
» Infants whose mothers are deceased

• Other compassionate reasons

## Indications for Referral to Lactation Consultants

Lactation consultants are professionally qualified nurses or healthcare professionals who are invaluable in supporting breastfeeding in both hospital and community settings. In Singapore, most hospitals provide lactation support services. These are some general indications for referral to lactation consultants:

• Decreased or insufficient milk supply
• Difficulty in latching-on
• Difficulty with milk expression
• Breast problems: Engorgement, sore nipple, nipple confusion, short or inverted nipples
• High-risk infants: Premature, very-low-birth-weight babies
• Returning to work
• Maternal anxiety
