# CHAPTER 73

# Neonatal Dermatoses

Odattil Geetha; Koh Jean Aan, Mark

## Erythema Toxicum

- Very common
- Appears within the first 48–72 hours of life
- Presents as scattered blotchy erythematous macules and papules, occasionally with small pustules
- Mostly occurs on face, trunk and upper extremities
- Baby is generally well
- No specific treatment is necessary, self resolves in a few days or a week

## Milia

- Presents as single or multiple small, white papules
- Most commonly appears on the forehead and face, especially the nose bridge
- Ruptures and disappears after few weeks but may take a few months
- No specific treatment is necessary apart from routine cleanliness

## Mottling (Cutis Marmorata)

- Scattered areas of erythema with pale areas in-between
Common following exposure to cold; becoming less obvious with warming
- If baby is unwell, need to exclude pathological associations, e.g. hypothermia, poor perfusion, shock, sepsis, central nervous system disturbances and hypothyroidism

## Transient Neonatal Pustular Melanosis

- More common in dark-skinned babies
- Maybe present at birth and last for 4-5 days
- Heterogeneous with pustules and vesicles and areas of dark macules
- Baby is clinically well
- No specific treatment is necessary
- May be confused with neonatal herpes and bacterial infection

530

b4854_Sec-17.indd 530

12-Dec-22 3:21:28 PM

# Neonatal Dermatoses

## Sucking Blister

- Believed to be due to vigorous sucking of the baby either in-utero or immediately after birth
- Presents in the accessible areas such as hand, forearm and lips but may also occur on foot
- Baby is well
- Should not be confused with neonatal herpes, bullous impetigo and epidermolysis bullosa

## Acne Neonatorum

- Appears in the first 2-4 weeks of life
- Appears as skin-coloured to pinkish, monomorphic papules, mostly on forehead and face
- Often appears together with features of seborrheic dermatitis
- Most undergo resolution without treatment
- Advise against heavy application of emollients
- Anti-fungal shampoos (e.g. ketoconazole), mild topical corticosteroids with or without topical anti-fungal (e.g. desonide lotion or miconazole/hydrocortisone combination), and a light moisturiser may be used for treatment

## Infantile Haemangioma

- Most common vascular tumour in infants and children
- Most common over the head and neck region, they can also affect other sites
- Not present at birth, they appear in the first few weeks of life and undergo a proliferative growth phase in the first 5-6 months of life, followed by a slow growth phase until about 1 year of age
- Will regress or involute thereafter but the rate will depend on the size of the haemangioma
- Regression may result in telangiectasias, skin atrophy or fibrofatty change
- Some large, segmental haemangiomas may be associated with syndromes and abnormalities in other organs:

- Facial segmental haemangiomas (PHACE syndrome — Posterior fossa abnormalities, Haemangiomas, Arterial abnormalities, Cardiac abnormalities and Eye abnormalities)

- Perineum segmental haemangiomas (PELVIS syndrome — Perineal haemangioma, External genital malformations, Lipomyelomeningocele, Vesicorenal abnormalities, Imperforate anus and Skin tag)

- If there are five or more discreet haemangiomas, there is a risk for visceral haemangioma, especially hepatic haemangioma

- Referral to paediatric dermatologists for treatment must be considered in the following circumstances:

- » 'Beard area' and neck haemangiomas — possible airway involvement
- » Periorbital haemangiomas — possible ocular axis occlusion, amblyopia, astigmatism
- » Lip, peri-oral, ear, anogenital and intertriginous region — risk of ulceration

  - » Small, clinically non-significant haemangiomas may be observed for self-involution
  - » Topical timolol may be used for small, superficial haemangiomas
  - » Oral propranolol may be considered for larger, complicated haemangiomas
  - » Other forms of treatment include pulsed dye laser and systemic corticosteroids

## Capillary Malformations

- • Malformed dilated capillaries in the skin
- • Appear as blotches of red or purple skin discolouration on any part of the body
- • Always present at birth, although they may become more obvious with time
- • Two common capillary vascular malformations:
  - » Naevus flammeus (salmon patch, stork bites)
    - ☐ Patchy light-red area
    - ☐ Common on forehead, eyebrow and nape of neck
    - ☐ Blanches on pressure
    - ☐ More intense in colour and noticeable when baby is crying
    - ☐ Most lesions on the forehead will spontaneously disappear within the first years of life
    - ☐ Nape lesions may be permanent
    - ☐ Most do not require treatment
  - » Port-wine stain
    - ☐ Intensely red to reddish-purplish macule
    - ☐ Commonly unilateral in distribution
    - ☐ If present on forehead or upper eyelids, it can be associated with the Sturge–Weber syndrome.

## Mongolian Blue Spot

- • Extremely common (up to 75% in Asian babies)
- • Large irregular areas of bluish or purplish-blue discolouration
- • Mostly on buttock or on the back
- • Many disappear in toddler years; some may persist
- • No treatment is necessary

# Neonatal Dermatoses

# Diaper Dermatitis

- Very common, especially after a few months of life
- May be caused by several factors, including irritation from urine and faeces, tight diaper bands, yeast/candida infection and bacterial infection
- Presents with erythema, scaling, fissuring and erosions if severe
- If satellite papules are present, consider secondary candida infection
- If lesions are oozy with pus discharge, consider secondary bacterial infection
- Treatment is with frequent diaper changes, topical moisturisers (e.g. white soft paraffin) and mild topical corticosteroids (e.g. desonide cream) or a combination of corticosteroid and anti-fungal agent (e.g. hydrocortisone/miconazole cream). A course of oral antibiotics may be necessary if evidence of secondary bacterial infection.

# CHAPTER 74

# Birth Trauma

Tan Pih Lin

# Risk Factors for Birth Trauma

- Maternal factors
  » Primiparous
  » Older multipara
  » Small malformed pelvis
- Obstetric factors
  » Breech position
  » Precipitous delivery
  » Abnormal presentation (transverse, face, brow)
  » Instrumental delivery (forceps or vacuum extraction)
  » Shoulder dystocia
- Foetal factors
  » Macrosomia
  » Prematurity

# Caput Succedaneum

This is a diffuse swelling of the scalp, resulting from an impairment of venous return due to prolonged delivery. Underlying collection is tissue fluid. It crosses the suture lines and is more pronounced at the time of delivery; the swelling can be indented. This undergoes natural resolution within 1–2 days and no active management is necessary.

# Cephalohaematoma

This results from the rupture of blood vessels from the skull to the periosteum. The swelling does not cross the suture line as the blood collects in the sub-periosteum. It may be less noticeable at birth and progressively becomes enlarged as the blood continues to collect. A linear skull fracture may be present. With time, it becomes calcified and forms a distinct bony swelling.

# Sub-Galeal Bleed

This results from the rupture of veins between the scalp and dural sinuses. It occurs more commonly with vacuum-assisted deliveries and is a rare but potentially severe form of bleeding in a newborn. Early recognition and management is crucial as it can lead

534

b4854_Sec-17.indd 534

12-Dec-22 3:21:28 PM

# Birth Trauma

535

to massive blood loss. The bleeding is diffuse over the scalp and fluctuant in nature. It tends to move towards the dependent side. Observe and monitor in special care nursery/ neonatal intensive care unit.

Both cephalohaematoma and sub-galeal bleed can aggravate neonatal jaundice.

## Clavicular Fracture

This is more common in a macrosomic baby with shoulder dystocia. Occasionally a 'snap' is heard during delivery. The features include asymmetric Moro reflex and restricted upper extremity movement on the affected side. The fracture may be detected on palpation.

## Erb's Palsy

This results from excessive traction of the neck during delivery. The severity varies from mild praxia with spontaneous recovery to more severe permanent damage. This is due to injury to the brachial plexus involving the cervical fifth and sixth nerves. This results in an inability to abduct the extremity at the shoulder, externally rotate the arm and supination. The infant holds the arm in adduction and internal rotation.

## Klumpke's Paralysis

This is a more severe form of brachial plexus injury. The nerves involved are the cervical seventh and eighth and first thoracic nerves. It commonly causes paralysis of the hand and may be associated with an ipsilateral Horner's syndrome.

The treatment of Erb's and Klumpke's paralyses is neuro-rehabilitation with an active and passive range-of-motion exercises.

## Facial Nerve Palsy

This is usually a peripheral nerve injury due to the compression of the facial nerve between the facial bone and mother's pelvis or obstetric forceps at the time of delivery. The face is asymmetrical with a deviation of the unaffected side during crying, absence of naso-labial fold on the affected side. The infant may not be able to close the eyelid of the affected side and proper eye care is necessary to prevent drying of conjunctiva.

## Bibliography

1. Rosenberg A. (2003) Traumatic birth injury. *Neoreviews* 4(10):e270-e276.
2. Akangire G, Carter B. (2016) Birth injuries in neonates. Pediatr Rev 37(11):451-462.

# CHAPTER 75

# Neonatal Jaundice

Abdul Haium Abdul Alim

## Introduction

Jaundice is the yellowish discolouration of the skin, sclera, and mucous membranes. It occurs due to elevated levels of bilirubin in the blood (hyperbilirubinaemia). It is one of the most common medical conditions affecting newborn infants.

Approximately 60% of neonates born at term and 85% of preterm neonates will develop neonatal jaundice (NNJ). Most of these neonates will develop 'physiological jaundice', which is usually benign. However, when unconjugated serum bilirubin (SB) level is too high, it could cause irreversible brain injury or hearing impairment. It is important, therefore, to identify those neonates at risk of severe neonatal hyperbilirubinaemia and treat them early to prevent the occurrence of acute bilirubin encephalopathy and kernicterus.

The following guidelines are applicable to inpatient management of NNJ in our institution.

## Assessment

- All newborn infants should be examined for jaundice at every opportunity in the first 72 hours of life.
- Visual inspection of baby for jaundice should be done preferably in natural light.
- Visual inspection alone should not be relied upon to estimate the severity of jaundice, and all babies with clinically suspected jaundice in the first two weeks of life should have a transcutaneous or SB measurement to estimate the severity of jaundice.
- Neonates with any of the high-risk factors, who are likely to develop severe hyperbilirubinaemia, should be identified early and monitored more closely for NNJ.

## Risk Stratification

All newborn infants in the first two weeks of life, who are at risk of NNJ, should be risk stratified for the purpose of management. The high-risk group is more prone to develop severe hyperbilirubinaemia and is at a higher risk for bilirubin-induced neurologic dysfunction.

536

b4854_Sec-17.indd 536

# Neonatal Jaundice

Table 17.2 Risk Stratification for Neonatal Jaundice

|  Normal Risk | High Risk for Severe Hyperbilirubinaemia  |
| --- | --- |
|  Any baby who is not in the 'high-risk' category^ | 1. Visible jaundice within 24 hr of age 2. G6PD deficiency and other haemolytic conditions 3. ABO incompatibility* defined as Mother Blood Group O, Baby Blood Group A/B, and DCT positive OR Maternal 'Anti-A' or 'Anti-B' IgG antibody titres ≥128 4. Rhesus incompatibility 5. Rapidly rising serum bilirubin (>103 μmol/L per day) 6. Late preterm (35–36 wk gestation) 7. Asphyxia (Apgar ≤5 at 1 and 5 min) 8. Family history of severe NNJ in siblings needing exchange transfusion 9. Inadequate breast feeding plus weight loss ≥10% 10. Infants with birth weight of 2000–2500 g * NB: If the mother's group and antibody titres unknown, manage the baby as 'high-risk' until the results are available  |
|  ^The following group of infants in the second week of life (8–14 days of age) could be considered as normal risk. 1. Mother's group and antibody titres still unknown 2. Exclusive breast feeding who had an initial weight loss of ≥10%, which improved and regained birth weight  |   |

## Estimation of Bilirubin Levels

- SB measurement is the gold standard for the diagnosis and monitoring of NNJ.
- Transcutaneous bilirubin (TcB) measurements can be used as a screening tool to measure jaundice levels in babies with gestational age of \(\geq 35\) weeks and birth weight \(\geq 2\mathrm{kg}\).
- Any TcB measurement that are close to phototherapy thresholds needs to be confirmed by SB measurement.

## Transcutaneous Bilirubin Action Levels

TcB action levels refer to the TcB level above which babies with NNJ should be subjected to SB confirmation or the TcB level below, which babies can be safely discharged without immediate risk.

The following table could be used as a guide for action levels above which the SB test should be performed for 'normal risk' and 'high risk' babies, respectively.

## Serum Bilirubin Measurements

- SB measurement remains the 'gold standard' for jaundice treatment decisions.
- SB should be tested if:

## Investigation

In a neonate with significant hyperbilirubinaemia requiring phototherapy or exchange transfusion, the following investigations may be considered to exclude haemolytic disease of the newborn. Additional investigations such as sepsis work-up, renal function or liver function tests could be carried out depending on the clinical presentation.

Full blood count, including blood film report and reticulocyte count
- Blood group, rhesus type and direct Coombs test of the baby ('NNJ profile' for baby)
- Blood group, rhesus type and abnormal antibodies of the mother ('NNJ profile' for mother)
- SB, if \(>4\) hours have elapsed since the last bilirubin determination

# Neonatal Jaundice

539

## Treatment

Phototherapy is a safe, effective, non-invasive and inexpensive method to remove bilirubin from body. Phototherapy involves the exposure of as much of the baby's skin as possible to blue fluorescent lights, which emit wavelengths in the 430–490 nm range.

- Phototherapy decreases the bilirubin level by enhancing the conversion of bilirubin in the exposed skin to a more easily excreted form.
- Increasing the amount of skin exposure to blue lights can enhance bilirubin excretion.
- Use eye covers to prevent damage to the baby's eyes.

## Single Blue Phototherapy

- The exposure of one plane of body surface (e.g. either the baby's front or back) to the phototherapy light. Regularly turning the baby helps to maximise the exposure of all surfaces.
- Once started on single blue phototherapy, it is advisable to continue phototherapy at least for 24 hours to maximise efficacy and prevent rebound jaundice.
- Recheck SB level 12-24 hours after starting phototherapy depending on clinical status.

## Double Blue Phototherapy

- The simultaneous exposure of two body surface planes to two separate sets of blue lights, i.e. both front and back.
- Recommended if the SB level is above the double blue phototherapy criteria or if the rate of rise of SB is \(>5\mu \mathrm{mol} / \mathrm{L} / \mathrm{hr}\) despite being on single phototherapy.
- Recheck SB level 6-12 hours after starting double phototherapy depending on clinical status.

## Intense Phototherapy

- Involves the use of four lights to achieve higher irradiance/light intensity. This maximises exposure of all body surfaces in four planes (front, back, right and left sides).
- Start intense blue phototherapy when SB levels are close to (e.g. within \(25\mu \mathrm{mol} / \mathrm{L}\)) exchange levels or above the exchange level and while exchange transfusion is being arranged.
- Recheck SB level 4-6 hours after starting intense phototherapy depending on clinical status of neonates.

## Criteria for Phototherapy

- There are two sets of phototherapy criteria, each tailored to a specific group of babies:

Babies born at \(< 35\) weeks' gestation and/or with birth weight \(< 2\mathrm{kg}\)
Babies born at \(\geq 35\) weeks' gestation and with birth weight \(\geq 2\mathrm{kg}\) (fall under the National NNJ guidelines)

# The Baby Bear Book

# Infants Born <35 Weeks' Gestation and/or with Birth Weight <2 kg

For babies born at less than 35 weeks' gestation or with birth weight less than 2 kg (who do not fall under the standard criteria), the phototherapy guidelines in the Table 17.4 are used.

For this group, abnormal criteria are applied if:

- Jaundice is observed within 24 hours of age
- Blood group incompatibility with positive direct Coomb's test, or antibody titre \(\geq 128\)
G6PD deficiency
Perinatal asphyxia
Sepsis
Significant cephalohaematoma or bruising

Double blue lights are used when the SB is <35 µmol/L below the exchange level or the rate of rise of SB is >5 µmol/L/h.

Phototherapy could be stopped if the SB level is 35 µmol/L below the phototherapy start level.

Table 17.4 Phototherapy Guidelines for Infants Born <35 Weeks' Gestation and/ or with Birth Weight <2 kg

|  Birth Weight (g) | Photo Level (µmol/L) |   | Exchange Level (µmol/L)  |   |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|   |  Normal | Abnormal | Normal | Abnormal  |
|  <1250 | 150 | 120 | 220 | 190  |
|  1250–1499 | 170 | 140 | 250 | 220  |
|  1500–1999 | 200 | 170 | 310 | 270  |
|  2000–2499 | 220 | 190 | 340 | 300  |
|  ≥2500 | 260 | 230 | 400 | 340  |

# Infants Born ≥35 Weeks' Gestation and with Birth Weight ≥2 kg

For babies born at ≥35 weeks' gestation or with birth weight ≥2 kg, the phototherapy guidelines in the Tables 17.5 and 17.6 are used.

# Monitoring While on Phototherapy

- Ensure adequate feeding and hydration. Add \(10 - 20\mathrm{ml / kg}\) /day extra feed volume, if tolerated. Monitor vital signs closely.
- Change position of baby frequently to expose different skin area for phototherapy.
- Ensure eyes are covered to prevent glaring and discomfort.

# Discontinuation of Phototherapy

- Once the 'off phototherapy' level has been reached.
- There is no need to routinely test for rebound jaundice, unless there are clinical indications.
- The baby can be discharged immediately after discontinuation of phototherapy if it remains clinically well.

## Use of Intravenous Immunoglobulin

- Systematic review of the use of high dose intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) has shown to significantly reduce the need for exchange transfusions.
- It is recommended to consider IVIG infusion (at a dose of \(500\mathrm{mg / kg}\), slow infusion) as an adjunct to intense blue phototherapy in babies with isoimmunisation from Rhesus or ABO haemolytic disease, where the SB level continues to rise by more than \(8.5\mu \mathrm{mol} / \mathrm{L} / \mathrm{h}\) despite the child on phototherapy and adequate hydration.
The suggested rate of IVIG infusion is:

0.5 ml/kg/h × 15 min followed by
1 ml/kg/h × 15 min followed by
2 ml/kg/h × 15 min followed by
3 ml/kg/h × 15 min followed by
4 ml/kg/h until end of infusion

# The Baby Bear Book

## Exchange Transfusion

- Exchange transfusion should be considered if:

SB level reaches the threshold for an exchange transfusion and is not decreasing after hydration and intensive phototherapy for 4-6 hours.
Associated significant neonatal anaemia from haemolysis.
SB levels above the exchange transfusion threshold and clinical signs of encephalopathy. In such infants, intense phototherapy should be commenced urgently, and exchange transfusion should be initiated immediately.
SB level reaches the threshold for an exchange transfusion and there are factors that disrupt the blood brain barrier, e.g. acidosis, asphyxia, sepsis, hypo-albuminemia, hypothermia or prematurity.

- Exchange transfusion needs to be considered as an urgent treatment:

In babies with high levels of bilirubin and who demonstrate any of the clinical features of acute bilirubin encephalopathy.
If SB level is \(>85\mu \mathrm{mol} / \mathrm{L}\) above the exchange transfusion threshold. Meanwhile hydrate and start the baby on intensive phototherapy.

## Other Considerations

- Feeding

» Feeding can be continued; nil by mouth if the baby needs exchange transfusion.
Increase feeds by at least \(10\%\) over the usual expected intake.
» Continue breastfeeding.
If not near or at the exchange transfusion level, the baby can be taken off the lights for up to 30 minutes to breastfed.

- Parental and caretaker education

- Prior to discharging the baby, emphasise the importance of early follow-up:

- Especially important for babies discharged before age 48 hours

- Emphasise the risks and manifestations of acute bilirubin encephalopathy and its consequences

» Breastfed babies:

Give thorough breastfeeding advice
Teach mothers and caregivers the signs of dehydration

- Borderline premature babies (35–36 weeks’ gestation):

A vulnerable group
Tend to be discharged from hospital as early as full-term well babies because of their larger physical size and apparent maturity

- At higher risk of developing jaundice, requiring phototherapy

- Advice to parents of this particular group must be particularly instructive

# CHAPTER 76

# Respiratory Distress

Kong Juin Yee

Respiratory distress in babies can manifest as many signs/symptoms, including nasal flaring, grunting, tachypnoea, chest wall retractions, tachycardia, cyanosis/desaturations, stridor, carbon dioxide ($\mathrm{CO}_{2}$) retention, irritability and apnoea (usually the last sign).

## General Management Principles

- Overall care: Keep baby in a thermoneutral environment (nurse in an incubator or under a radiant warmer set to maintain skin temperature at $36.5^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ by using servo control). Minimise disturbances and handling by grouping together blood sampling and procedures. Any disturbance of a sick baby may cause apnoea, bradycardia and desaturations. Babies who are intubated or on continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) might sometimes need sedation (morphine drip or chloral hydrate), though this should be used judiciously in the preterm population due to side effects like hypotension and respiratory depression.

- Ongoing monitoring: Continuous monitoring of heart rate, respiratory rate, oxygen saturation ($\mathrm{SpO}_{2}$) and temperature (servo probe on baby's skin) would be needed. Blood pressure (BP) can be measured non-invasively with an oscillometric BP cuff (may over-read) or with an intra-arterial line (more reliable and continuous). Blood gas (arterial preferred, via peripheral arterial line or umbilical arterial catheter) should be monitored every 4–6 hours, or even more frequently during the acute stage of respiratory illness. When ventilator parameters are changed, follow-up blood gas should be done 30–60 minutes after change. Calculation of the oxygenation index and alveolar-arterial oxygen gradient ($\mathrm{AaDO}_{2}$) should be done with every blood gas when the infant's respiratory illness is severe.

- Investigations: Investigate for cause of respiratory distress (Chest X-ray [CXR]), infection markers, e.g. full blood count, C-reactive protein, blood cultures, blood glucose, nasopharyngeal aspirate for respiratory viruses and cranial ultrasound). Repeat CXR should be considered if any acute or gradual worsening of respiratory status or escalation of ventilator support. If there is a change in nature of secretions, consider sending endotracheal aspirates for gram stain and culture though this should be interpreted with caution as positive results may indicate colonisation as opposed to true infection.

# Respiratory Distress

- Treatment: Different modes of ventilatory support can be used depending on the underlying cause for respiratory distress as well as the degree of support needed. Oxygen should be humidified and blended to keep target saturation ranges (term versus preterm) in order to prevent side effects from hyperoxia or hypoxaemia. Treat the underlying causes, for example: surfactant (respiratory distress syndrome [RDS]), antibiotics (infection), caffeine (apnoea of prematurity) while supporting the baby's respiratory status.

## Oxygen Delivery

Oxygen is a drug. It should be given judiciously just enough to maintain targeted SpO₂ (88–95% for preterm, and 93–98% for term) with partial pressure of oxygen (PaO₂) of 40–60 mmHg generally. Targeted SpO₂ and PaO₂ might differ depending on clinical conditions, e.g. babies with persistent pulmonary hypertension of newborn (PPHN) might need higher targets, as opposed to babies with cyanotic heart disease, whom lower targets are acceptable. Hence, oxygen blending should be used at all times if available to avoid hyperoxia-related complications.

Oxygen can be delivered to infants without positive pressure via:

- Hood box: Infant's head is placed in a hood infused with humidified oxygen (titrated to desired concentrations with an oxygen concentration analyser placed near the infant to accurately measure the fraction of inspired oxygen \((\mathrm{FiO}_2)\) received by the baby)
- Nasal cannula (NC): Usually used in infants with chronic lung disease when they fail to maintain target \(\mathrm{SpO}_2\) after depronging from CPAP. High flow NC (with flow 2-6 L per min [LPM]) and low flow NC (with flow 0.5-2 LPM) can be used depending on the need for support. \(\mathrm{FiO}_2\) 0.21-1.00 should be titrated to aim for \(\mathrm{SpO}_2\) within the target range. Some babies with significant chronic lung disease can be discharged home on NC oxygen under home care arrangement using a home oxygen concentrator and \(\mathrm{SpO}_2\) monitoring.

## Continuous Positive Airway Pressure

In CPAP, a continuous flow (usually 6 LPM) of heated and humidified air (with or without oxygen) is circulated past the infant's airway at a set pressure (usually 5–6 cmH₂O) while the infant breathes spontaneously. FiO₂ and positive end expiratory pressure (PEEP) can be adjusted to keep target SpO₂ range and to provide support for infant's respiratory distress, respectively. It is usually delivered by nasal prongs either via the ventilator circuit or bubble (under water) CPAP system. An orogastric tube should be placed, as CPAP can cause gastric distension interference with lung inflation by pushing up the diaphragm.

CPAP might be contraindicated in babies with gastrointestinal anomalies (mostly obstruction, congenital diaphragmatic hernia), necrotising enterocolitis or immediate post gastrointestinal-related surgical repair. It sometimes causes agitation/excessive crying, especially near term or term babies, hence mild sedation (e.g. chloral hydrate) can be considered to prevent complications like pneumothorax.

b4854_Sec-17.indd 545

12-Dec-22 3:21:28 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

## Indications

- Disease with low functional residual capacity or poor compliance: RDS, transient tachypnoea of a newborn, pulmonary oedema due to patent ductus arteriosus (PDA)
- Recurrent apnoea: CPAP may reduce or terminate attacks in preterm babies with apnoea of prematurity
- Upper airway or airway closure disease: Obstructive airway (e.g. Pierre Robin syndrome), obstructive sleep apnoea, bronchopulmonary dysplasia, bronchiolitis, tracheomalacia
- As a weaning mode from the mechanical ventilator

## Failure of CPAP and Indications for Mechanical Ventilation

- \(\mathrm{FiO}_2 > 0.3 - 0.4\) (depending on gestational age and underlying lung disease) when on PEEP \(5 - 6\mathrm{cmH}_2\mathrm{O}\)
- Partial pressure of \(\mathrm{CO}_{2}\) \((\mathrm{PaCO}_{2}) > 65\mathrm{mmHg}\) on arterial blood gas
Marked retractions
- Frequent apnoea and bradycardia
- Intractable metabolic acidosis
- Cardiovascular instability or collapse
- Decreased respiratory effort (neuromuscular disease, deep sedation and paralysis)

Trouble shooting before intubation: Exclude errors in blood gas sampling, machine malfunction, improper CPAP application or pressures not achieved (may try chin strap or proper fit of prongs), and nasal obstruction by secretions or water in the tubing. Nasal intermittent mandatory ventilation (NIMV) mode can be considered prior to intubation; however, this might not be appropriate depending on the baby's status and underlying condition.

## Mechanical Ventilation

When non-invasive respiratory support, e.g. CPAP/NIMV modes are not sufficient, babies need to be intubated and supported via invasive mechanical ventilation.

## Conventional Mechanical Ventilation Mode

The most commonly used mode of conventional mechanical ventilation (CMV) in the neonatal intensive care unit is pressure limited ($\pm$ volume targeted), time cycled modes and synchronisation with the baby's inspiratory breaths, if any. In addition, volume targeted or volume guarantee (VG) is the preferred mode as it is associated with less ventilator-induced injury, e.g. pneumothorax, hypocarbia, or even serious complications like intraventricular haemorrhage (IVH) in preterm infants.

Following are some general principles regarding CMV in babies:

- Peak inspiratory pressure (PIP): PIP is adjusted to ensure adequate, but not excessive chest movement. It is generally set between 15 and 20 cmH$_2$O pressure (higher PIP may be needed if the compliance is low). In VG mode, the set PIP serves as the upper limit of pressure allowed for generating the volume prescribed

b4854_Sec-17.indd 546

12-Dec-22 3:21:28 PM

# Respiratory Distress

for the baby. If the baby’s lungs compliance improves, the actual measured PIP needed to generate prescribed volume might very well be lower than the set PIP (max. PIP); on the other hand, the set PIP might need to be increased in order to achieve the set tidal volume (Tv) if its lungs are stiff.

- PEEP: Usually set at 5–6 cmH₂O. PEEP, when is set too low, can lead to atelectasis of lungs and impairment of oxygenation and gas exchange. PEEP that is inappropriately high over-distends alveoli, causes pneumothoraces, decreases compliance, impedes venous return and diminishes cardiac output (hypotension).

- Tv: This is set as the desired Tv (whatever PIP needed to achieve volume is used, with the safety margin of the set max. PIP). Usually it is set between 4 and 6 ml/kg in newborn babies. This can be difficult when there is a large leak around the ETT, as the machine sensor would have difficulty in accurately measuring the Tv that the baby was actually getting.

- FiO₂: This is adjusted to maintain PaO₂ between 40 and 60 mmHg (target SpO₂ 90–95%) in preterm infants and PaO₂ between 50 and 85 mmHg (target SpO₂ 92–98%) in term infants. Avoid hyperoxia, as it is associated with increased risk of retinopathy of prematurity in preterm babies.

- Rate: The ventilator rate greatly depends on the clinical status of the baby. Initial setting is usually 30–40 breaths per minute and is adjusted to maintain PaCO₂ of 40–50 mmHg or 50–60 mmHg (permissive hypercarbia) in chronic babies if pH is maintained between 7.25 and 7.35. Hypocarbia (PaCO₂ <35 mmHg) should be avoided as it will cause reduced cerebral blood flow and is especially harmful in a premature brain as well as babies suffering from hypoxic ischaemic brain injury. Severe hypercarbia >60 mmHg has also been associated with higher risk of IVH, especially in the very preterm infants during the first days of life.

- Inspiratory Time (iT) and Expiratory Time (eT): These two parameters affect the ventilator rate. iT is generally kept between 0.35 and 0.4 s, the longer the better for oxygenation. eT should be longer than iT in order to effectively clear CO₂ from the lungs. Although strategies for ventilation are recommended according to the working diagnosis of the newborn, the response of the individual infant is variable and changes should be made quickly if there is no clinical improvement or deterioration while on mechanical ventilation.

- Ventilator flow rate: This should be adequate to generate required PIP, iT, and allow the wash-out of exhaled CO₂ and compensate for leaks around the endotracheal tube (ETT) and tubing. Generally, it is set at 6–8 LPM.

## High Frequency Oscillatory Ventilation Mode

In specific lung pathologies (e.g. PPHN, meconium aspiration syndrome, pulmonary hypoplasia and pulmonary interstitial emphysema) or failure of CMV mode, a different strategy of ventilation like the high frequency oscillatory ventilation (HFOV) mode can be used. HFOV uses a constant distending pressure (mean airway pressure [MAP]) with pressure variations oscillating around the MAP at very high rates. It has been proposed to reduce ventilator-associated lung injury by creating very small tidal volumes for gas exchange.

b4854_Sec-17.indd 547

12-Dec-22 3:21:28 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

Recommended settings when transitioning from CMV mode to HFOV mode:

- MAP: \(2 - 3\mathrm{cmH}_2\mathrm{O}\) above the MAP measured on the previous conventional mode. The MAP should be adjusted (titrated up or down by \(1 - 2\mathrm{cmH}_2\mathrm{O}\)) based on the lung expansion seen on CXR performed within 1 hour after being on HFOV mode. Optimal inflation is when the top margin of the right doom of diaphragm is at the \(8^{\text{th}}\) to \(9^{\text{th}}\) rib with good lung aeration bilaterally. Hypotension is one of the common complications of overdistension of lungs from high MAP, as this impairs venous return (hence filling of the heart) and reduces cardiac output.
- Amplitude \((\Delta P)\): The normal starting range for amplitude is generally between 20 and 30. The optimal level is attained when chest wiggle is seen over the chest and transmitted to the umbilical/lower abdominal region.
- Frequency (Hertz): For term infants, 10–12 Hertz (Hz); preterm infants, 12–14 Hz. Frequency should be adjusted only when \(\mathrm{PaCO}_2\) levels remain out of range despite optimisation of amplitude.
- Inspiratory: Expiratory (I:E) ratio: Commonly set at a 1:2 ratio and not to be adjusted usually.

Clinical assessment of chest expansion/wiggle, air entry, colour and peripheral perfusion is important. Blood gas should preferably be checked 30 minutes after any change in ventilator setting, and also to be repeated after a severely out of range $\mathrm{PaCO}_2$ level is detected (both hypocarbia and hypercarbia).

$\mathrm{PaO}_2$ and $\mathrm{PaCO}_2$ can be altered independently of one another:

|  Issues | Conventional Mode | High Frequency Oscillatory Mode  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  PaO_{2} low | Increase FiO_{2}, increase PIP (in steps of 1–2 cmH_{2}O), increase I:E ratio, and increase PEEP (in steps of 1 cmH_{2}O) | Increase MAP (not to overdistend as could impair venous return and cause hypotension)  |
|  PaCO_{2} high | Increase rate (in steps of 5–10 breaths per minute), increase PIP (in steps of 1–2 cmH_{2}O), increase or decrease PEEP (in order to increase ΔP, defined by PIP-PEEP) | Increase amplitude (by 2 at a time) Decrease frequency (by 1 Hz at a time)  |

Senior doctors should be consulted to explore possible use of different modes of ventilation (e.g. high frequencies), administration of surfactant, inotropes (dopamine, dobutamine), sedatives and analgesics (choral hydrate, phenobarbitone, morphine sulfate), volume expanders (normal saline, plasma), pulmonary vasodilators (e.g. inhaled nitric oxide [iNO], sildenafil or magnesium sulphate) as well as dexamethasone. In the setting of severe respiratory failure and not responding to maximum ventilatory support and adjuncts, extracorporeal membrane oxygenation can be considered as part of a collaborative decision between paediatric cardiothoracic surgeon, cardiologist, paediatric and neonatal intensivists, with input and consent from parents.

# Respiratory Distress

# Deterioration During Ventilation

## Sudden Clinical Deterioration

Usually manifests in the form of hypoxia (a fall in \(\mathrm{SpO}_2\) or clinically cyanosed), bradycardia or sudden hypercapnia in blood gas.
- Observe for air entry and chest expansion; consider ETT suction and direct laryngoscopy to ensure the tube is in the right position. A disposable endotracheal tube \(\mathrm{CO}_{2}\) (ETCO\(_2\)) detector can be a great tool to reconfirm that ETT is still in place, as a colour change will indicate correct placement (expired \(\mathrm{CO}_{2}\) changes colour from purple to yellow). An obstructed tube by secretions or pulmonary haemorrhage (especially considered when a baby has a large PDA) can be the reason for this. Consider re-intubation in cases of suspected blocked or dislodged tube, which resolve with good suctioning. T-piece resuscitator (Neopuff™) should be readily available to provide manual positive pressure ventilation or CPAP in between extubation and reintubation episodes.
- Exclude pneumothorax by auscultation or by transillumination. In an emergency situation, a tension pneumothorax can be drained by needle aspiration of the air in pleural space. Otherwise, confirmation with CXR is preferred, especially in extremely low birth weight infants, as a false positive transillumination test can be seen, given their thin chest walls.

## Gradual Deterioration

- This is usually accompanied by increased desaturation episodes or lability and/or an increase in \(\mathrm{PaCO}_2\). As this may be due to a displaced or blocked ETT or air leak as well, these should be excluded first.
- Other possible causes include:

Inappropriate and inadequate ventilator settings: Due to progressive deterioration of underlying disease state (pneumonia, pulmonary oedema from PDA, lobar collapse) that leads to poor compliance and concurrent atelectasis. Consider an increase in PIP, PEEP, iT, rate and/or \(\mathrm{FiO}_2\)
Baby fighting against ventilator: Blocked or mal-positioned ETT and inadequate ventilation should be excluded first. Sedation should be given after exclusion of other causes. Synchronisation of ventilation usually avoids this problem.
Worsening RDS: Babies with severe RDS may need a repeated dose of surfactant (can be done 6-12 hours after the last dose, depending on the type of surfactant used) if their \(\mathrm{FiO}_2\) requirement gradually increases beyond 0.30 to 0.40, with findings confirmed on CXR.
Infection: Nosocomial infections are common. Maintain vigilance and test appropriately. Consider changing the antibiotic therapy if the baby is already covered with empiric therapy and not improving

b4854_Sec-17.indd 549

12-Dec-22 3:21:28 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

» IVH: Pallor, bulging fontanelle, seizures, fall in haemoglobin, sudden desaturation, hypotension and acidosis may indicate IVH. This can be confirmed by bedside cranial ultrasound.
Hypotension: Check BP. Review fluid volume given to the patient. Consider administration of normal saline/plasma/vasopressors such as dopamine.
Anaemia: Frequently due to iatrogenic causes such as blood sampling (in the tiny preterm baby) and other causes of blood loss (IVH, haemolytic anaemia).
Metabolic imbalance: Check blood gases for acidosis, consider laboratory tests for electrolytes, urea, creatinine.
Poor environmental support: Avoid excessive handling, group and plan procedures, and ensure a thermo-neutral environment.

## Refractory Hypoxia

- If the SpO₂ and PaO₂ remains below target despite apparent adequate ventilation and FiO₂ ≥50%, consider the following:

The baby is fighting or breathing out of synchrony with the ventilator
» Increased intra-pulmonary shunting caused by progressive atelectasis or lobar lung collapse
Pulmonary hypertension with shunting through the foramen ovale and/or PDA
Fixed cyanotic heart disease

## Weaning Off the Ventilator

When the baby's condition has improved, ventilator settings should be weaned, whenever possible to prevent lung injury. If the baby is on HFOV mode, amplitude can be weaned by 2 each time to the lower range of 20, while MAP can be reduced in 1–2 cmH₂O steps to 8–10 cmH₂O as long as oxygenation is maintained, before switching to conventional ventilation or extubation. When on CMV mode, PIP can be reduced by 1–2 cmH₂O to 16–18 cmH₂O; set Tv (if on VG mode) to 4–4.5 ml/kg — monitoring inspiratory pressure needed to achieve volume set, the rate can be reduced (to be lower than baby's spontaneous breath but not lower than 25). When weaning is achieved to minimal settings as above, extubation should be considered. To maximise the success of weaning and extubation, sedation and muscle relaxant, if used, should be discontinued or at least reduced to the minimal dose. Administration of a loading dose of caffeine citrate should be considered in preterm infants of ≤34 weeks' gestation to prevent failure of extubation due to apnoea of prematurity. Adjunctive therapy like iNO as well as cardiac inotropes is generally weaned off before planning for extubation. Consider NIMV (especially for the very premature infants with expected low respiratory effort), CPAP or high/normal flow NC after extubation if anticipating baby to not tolerate room air (based on the severity of lung disease and pre-intubation need of support). After extubation, monitor the baby closely for worsening respiratory distress. Blood gas should be followed (30–60 minutes after extubation) if infants is extubated to non-invasive respiratory support and consider performing CXR to evaluate for any atelectasis if clinical worsening post extubation is observed (optional).

# Respiratory Distress

## Failure to Wean

- Immaturity: Maturation of control of breathing may be delayed in premature baby
- Laryngeal oedema, subglottic stenosis, lower tract obstruction. Nebulised adrenaline can be used, or pre-extubation dexamethasone can be considered.
- Chronic lung disease
Pulmonary oedema: PDA, fluid overload
- Central nervous system: Severe brain injury from severe IVH and hypoxic ischaemic encephalopathy, or congenital malformations, heavy sedation from medications
- Infection, aspiration of milk, atelectasis
- Metabolic and electrolyte imbalance
- Inadequate nutritional status, anaemia

# CHAPTER 77

# Neonatal Sepsis

Yeo Kee Thai

## Definitions

Neonatal sepsis describes a clinical syndrome related to the systemic infection, typically characterised by the isolation of microbial pathogens from the blood, with or without clinical signs of infection within the first 28 days of life. Early onset sepsis (EOS) is typically defined as infections that occur within the first 72 hours of life and late onset sepsis (LOS) occurs >72 hours of life.

## Risk Factors

Early onset infections are typically acquired through *vertical* transmission — ascending infection or acquisition of microorganisms during delivery. The associated risk factor for EOS originates in the antepartum/intrapartum period and includes factors such as maternal intrapartum fever and chorioamnionitis, prolonged rupture of membranes and perinatal microbial colonisation.

Late onset infections occur through *horizontal* transmission of microorganisms, which are acquired from the environment or carers. Risk factors for LOS includes the presence of intravascular devices, exposure to medications such as H₂ blockers and inappropriate antibiotic usage.

## Maternal Risk Factors

- Preterm delivery
- Group B Streptococcus (GBS) colonisation
- Prolonged rupture of membranes \(>18\) hours
- Clinical chorioamnionitis (maternal fever, uterine tenderness, purulent or foul smelling liquor, maternal tachycardia, foetal tachycardia)
- Maternal urinary tract infection

## Infant Risk Factors

Infant risk factors for sepsis are variable but would typically include:

Prematurity
- Low birth weight
Male gender

552

b4854_Sec-17.indd 552

12-Dec-22 3:21:28 PM

# Neonatal Sepsis

553

Presence of intravascular devices
- Skin or mucosal breakdown
- Exposure to \(\mathrm{H}_{2}\) blockers
- Prolonged & inappropriate usage of antibiotics

## Causative Organisms

Common organisms responsible for EOS include GBS and gram-negative organisms such as *Escherichia coli*. Other bacterial causes include *Listeria monocytogenes*, *Streptococcus viridans*, *Enterococcus* and other gram-negative bacteria.

The epidemiology and distribution of causative organisms for LOS differ across different settings. The commonly reported organisms include gram-negative bacteria (e.g. *E. coli*, *Klebsiella*, *Pseudomonas*), *Coagulase negative staphylococcus*, *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Enterococcus*, GBS and *Candida*.

Other non-bacterial causes of neonatal sepsis include viral pathogens such as respiratory viral infections (e.g. respiratory syncytial virus, parainfluenza virus, rhinovirus), herpes simplex virus and enteroviruses.

## Clinical Presentation

Initial clinical presentation of sepsis can be subtle and non-specific.

Signs include:

- Temperature instability: Hypothermia or hyperthermia
- Cardiovascular: Tachycardia, hypotension
- Respiratory: Apnoea, tachypnoea, nasal flaring, retractions, grunting
Gastrointestinal: Poor feeding, vomiting, feeding intolerance, jaundice
Neurological: Lethargy, irritability, seizures

## Investigations

- Full blood count: Specifically high or low white cell count, increased immature-to-total neutrophil ratio \((>0.20)\)
C-reactive protein, procalcitonin
Blood culture
Cerebrospinal fluid culture
- Urine microscopy and culture
- Chest X-ray

## Prevention

Intrapartum antibiotics prophylaxis is a strategy for the prevention of early onset GBS infection, and studies have documented decreases in the incidence of GBS sepsis.

Indications for intrapartum antibiotics include:

Positive antenatal cultures for GBS

b4854_Sec-17.indd 553

12-Dec-22 3:21:28 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

- Unknown maternal colonisation status with gestation \(< 37\) weeks
- GBS bacteriuria during the current pregnancy
- Previous newborn with invasive GBS disease

Appropriate prophylaxis for GBS is penicillin or cefazolin given 4 hours or more before delivery. Antibiotic susceptibility information may be required for women who have an anaphylactic reaction to penicillin. Clindamycin and vancomycin may be considered in these situations.

## Antibiotic Regimen

Selection of antibiotic regimen for the treatment of neonatal sepsis is based on local epidemiology and susceptibilities of infecting bacteria. Typical antibiotic regiment in early onset infection include penicillin G/ampicillin and gentamicin. The selection of ampicillin could be considered for the empiric coverage of *L. monocytogenes*. In late onset infection, the choice of antibiotics typically include cloxacillin and an aminoglycoside (gentamicin/amikacin).

In all situations, escalation of antibiotic coverage to agents such as third-generation cephalosporin and carbapenems would depend on the clinical condition and response to initial empiric antibiotics. Considerations should also be made to ensure coverage and treatment for the possibility of meningitis. Antibiotic coverage should be adjusted once the results of the culture and susceptibilities are made available.

# CHAPTER 78

# Necrotising Enterocolitis

Amudha Jayanthi Anand

Necrotising enterocolitis (NEC) is a condition characterised by varying degrees of mucosal necrosis and is predominantly seen in premature infants. Age of onset depends on gestation:

- Preterm infants: 2 weeks to 3 months of life
Term infants: First week of life

Incidence of NEC in the very low-birth-weight infants is about 5–7%.

## Risk Factors

- Prematurity
- Feeding — formula feeding and rapid advancement of feeds
- Hypotension requiring inotropes
Perinatal asphyxia
Polycythaemia
- Severe chorioamnionitis
- Use of \(\mathrm{H}_{2}\) blockers
Prolonged antibiotic use
- Umbilical catheters
- Maternal cocaine abuse
- Infections: Both bacterial and viral
Congenital gastrointestinal abnormalities
- Patent Ductus Arteriosus
Congenital heart disease

## Measures to Reduce Risk

- Oral care with colostrum.
- Start trophic feeds early within the first 24 hours if possible.

- Avoid nil by mouth and keep on minimal enteral nutrition or non-nutritive feeds of 20 ml/kg/day whenever possible.

- Encourage an exclusive breast milk diet (expressed breast milk or pasteurised donor human milk).
- Strictly adhering to the department/institution's standardised feeding protocol.
High index of suspicion in at-risk neonates.

556

# The Baby Bear Book

- Use probiotics.
- To continue with feeds during blood transfusion and to suspend additives for 48 hours.
- Limit the use of broad spectrum antibiotics. In all cases of presumed sepsis, empirical antibiotics must be stopped if final blood culture is negative.
- Use of high calorie formulas is to be considered after maximising feeding volume to \(180\mathrm{ml / kg / day}\) with human milk and should be prescribed in conjunction with dietician consultation.
- Avoid the use of additives with high osmolality.

» All oral medications must be diluted with an appropriate dilution guide.
It is recommended that only one oral medication should be administered per milk feed.
The osmolality of oral feeds should be kept below \(450\mathrm{mOsm / L}\)

## Clinical Features

### Non-Specific Signs and Symptoms

Lethargy
Tachycardia (Heart rate \(>160 / \mathrm{min}\)
- Temperature instability
- Apnoea and bradycardia with desaturations
Systemic hypotension

### Abdominal Signs and Symptoms

- Feeding intolerance
Vomiting
- Increased pre-feed residuals \(>30\%\)
- Abdominal distension with tenderness and discoloration
- Bloody or bilious gastric aspirates
Ileus
- Bloody stool

## Investigations

- Full blood count (8–24 hourly until stabilisation of neutrophils and platelets), C-reactive protein, blood culture, renal panel, arterial blood gas, blood sugar monitoring, coagulation profile (12–24 hourly until normal), group and crossmatch

- Non-specific laboratory findings: Neutropaenia, leucocytosis, hyponatraemia, metabolic acidosis, hyperglycaemia, disseminated intravascular coagulopathy (in severe NEC).

# Necrotising Enterocolitis

- Serial abdominal X-ray (anteroposterior and decubitus view) 6–8 hourly

- Radiological findings: Fixed dilated bowel loops or ‘sentinel loop sign’, pneumoperitoneum, portal venous gas, pneumatosis intestinalis (latter two pathognomonic for NEC).

- Stool for rotavirus, enterovirus and bacterial culture (if no stool, send rectal swab)

Table 17.7 Modified Bell Staging Criteria for Necrotising Enterocolitis

|  Stage | Systemic Signs | Abdominal Signs | Radiographic Signs | Treatment  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  IA: Suspected NEC | Temperature instability, apnoea, bradycardia, lethargy | Gastric retention, abdominal distension, emesis, occult blood in stools | Normal or mild ileus | Nil orally, antibiotics for 3 d  |
|  IB: Suspected NEC | Same as IA | Same as IA and grossly bloody stool | Same as IA | Same as IA  |
|  IIA: Confirmed NEC (mild disease) | Same as IA | Same as IA plus absent bowel sounds with or without abdominal tenderness | Intestinal dilatation, ileus, pneumatosis intestinalis | Nil orally, antibiotics for 7–10 d  |
|  IIB: Confirmed NEC (moderate disease) | Same as IA, plus mild metabolic acidosis, mild thrombocytopenia | Same IA plus abdominal tenderness, abdominal wall cellulitis and palpable abdominal mass | Same as IIA with portal vein gas | Nil orally, antibiotics for 14 d  |
|  IIIA: Advanced NEC (severe disease, bowel intact) | Same as IIB, plus hypotension, bradycardia, respiratory acidosis, metabolic acidosis, disseminated intravascular coagulation, neutropaenia | Same as I and II, plus signs of generalised peritonitis, marked tenderness and distension of abdomen | Same as IIB, plus definite ascites | Nil orally, antibiotics for 14 d, fluid resuscitation, inotropic support, ventilator therapy, paracentesis  |
|  IIIB: Advanced NEC (severe disease, bowel perforated) | Same as IIIA | Same as IIIA | Same as IIB, plus pneumoperitoneum | Same as IIIA plus surgery  |

# The Baby Bear Book

## Management

- Manage Airway, Breathing and Circulation (consider ventilator support in Stage 2 NEC and above)
- Continuous blood pressure monitoring (via intra-arterial insertion)
- Nil by mouth and initiate total parenteral nutrition
- Nasogastric decompression
- Fluid resuscitation: Normal saline bolus 10–20 ml/kg (as required)
  » Consider fresh frozen plasma for volume expansion if there is associated coagulopathy
- Lumbar puncture (only if stable and clinically indicated)
- Intravenous antibiotics for 7–14 days for definite NEC
  » Cloxacillin, gentamicin/amikacin and metronidazole
  » Escalate to cefotaxime/meropenem if there is worsening clinical status
- Fluconazole prophylaxis in Stage 2 NEC and above
- Early referral to paediatric surgeon
  » Absolute indication for urgent laparotomy is presence of pneumoperitoneum

## Prognosis

- Mortality rate between 20 and 40%
- Late complications include cholestasis, stenosis, strictures, short bowel syndrome and malabsorption
- Affected infants have about 25% risk of developing neurodevelopmental delay

# CHAPTER 79

# Polycythaemia

Shrenik Vora

## Definition

A haematocrit value >65% when obtained from a large, freely flowing peripheral vein.

## Pathophysiology

Incidence: 1.5–4% of all live births.

Haematocrit value peaks at 2–4 hours of life due to transudation of fluid out of the intravascular space.

The relationship between haematocrit and viscosity is almost linear till 65% and exponential after that. As the viscosity increases, there is an impairment of tissue oxygenation and perfusion and a tendency to form microthrombi, which may cause severe damage to gut, cerebral cortex, kidneys and adrenal glands.

## Aetiology

Causes of polycythaemia in neonates include:

- Intrauterine hypoxia (Due to increased red blood cells [RBC] production as compensation; as suggested by the increased number of nucleated RBCs)

Intra uterine growth retardation
Maternal pregnancy-induced hypertension
Maternal gestational diabetes mellitus
Maternal smoking
Maternal cyanotic heart disease
Post term

Transfusions:

Delayed cord clamping \((>3\) minutes)
Holding baby below the level of introitus
Twin-to-twin transfusion
Maternal-foetal transfusion
Perinatal asphyxia
Iatrogenic-blood transfusion

- Other causes:

Dehydration
Trisomy 13, 18, 21
Hypothyroidism, thyrotoxicosis

# Polycythaemia

- Respiratory: Respiratory distress
Gastrointestinal: Poor suck, vomiting, feeding difficulties
- Metabolic: Hypoglycaemia, pathological jaundice
Haematological: Thrombocytopenia

## Management

- Ensure adequate hydration.
- Correct underlying cause and treat complications.
- Definite treatment is partial exchange transfusion (PET) where blood is taken out slowly and replaced with normal saline (preferred) or plasma.
PET

Indications:

Symptomatic polycythaemia

Haematocrit >70% in asymptomatic infant

Target: To attain haematocrit of \(55\%\)
Route of PET: Intra-arterial line and intravenous line or umbilical venous cannulation
Volume of blood exchanged:

*Volume to be*

*exchanged* = $\frac{\text{Blood volume} \times (\text{Observed haematocrit} - \text{desired haematocrit})}{\text{Observed haematocrit}}$

*Observed haematocrit*

[Blood volume = 80 ml/kg in term babies and 90 ml/kg in preterm babies]

## Complications

- Neurological: Seizures, strokes, motor deficits, intellectual impairment
- Cardiovascular: Cardiomegaly, increased pulmonary resistance, decreased cardiac output, congestive cardiac failure
Gastrointestinal: Necrotising enterocolitis, ileus
- Renal: Renal vein thrombosis, renal failure, oliguria
- Others: Priapism, testicular infarct, peripheral gangrene

# CHAPTER 80

# Hyperinsulinaemic Hypoglycaemia

Suresh Chandran; Yap Kok Peng, Fabian; Victor Samuel Rajadurai

Hypoglycaemia in newborn infants is defined as plasma glucose levels $<3.0$ mmol/L in the first 48 hours of life and $<3.5$ mmol/L beyond that. Hyperinsulinism is suspected when an infant needs $>10$ mg/kg/min to maintain normoglycaemia (3.5–7 mmol/L) beyond 48 hours of life. While increasing the glucose infusion rate (GIR) in increments of 2 mg/kg/min to maintain glucose levels, critical blood samples, including plasma glucose, insulin, C peptide, ketone bodies, free fatty acids, cortisol and growth hormone, are collected during an episode of hypoglycaemia. Detectable insulin level when hypoglycaemic, along with hypoketonaemia, hypofattyacidaemia and a positive response to glucagon intravenous (IV)/intramuscular (rise in glucose concentration of $>1.5$ mmol/L within 30 minutes) with normal cortisol and growth hormone levels, is consistent with a diagnosis of hyperinsulinaemic hypoglycaemia (HH). Other causes for hypoglycaemia, including sepsis and inborn errors of metabolism (IEM), should be ruled out.

The attempt to raise the GIR in these infants is achieved by raising the fluid volume or dextrose concentration (up to 30% dextrose concentration) as required. A central line should be secured for a dextrose concentration of more than 12.5%. Target to maintain plasma glucose levels $>3.5$ mmol/L in order to prevent hypoglycaemic brain injury. Discuss the case with HH Specialist from Neonatology/Endocrinology. Retrieve IEM result. Serum ammonia levels are elevated in infants with Hyperammonaemia/Hyperinsulinism (HI/HA) syndrome, the second-most common cause of congenital hyperinsulinism.

HH can be transient or persistent (genetic or congenital). Transient forms are often seen in infants of diabetic and obese mothers, small for gestational age (SGA) infants, following perinatal asphyxia and in Beckwith–Wiedemann syndrome. Most of them resolve spontaneously with increasing feeds or with short periods of high GIR. A prolonged form of HH is seen in some SGA infants, lasting for 6 weeks to 6 months, who respond well to oral diazoxide (DZX). Recessive mutations in the K$_{ATP}$ channel of the $\beta$-cells of pancreas, encoded by the genes ABCC8 and KCNJ11, are the most common cause of congenital hyperinsulinism.

Oral DZX is the drug choice (3–20 mg/kg/day in 2 divided doses) in the treatment of HH. It is a K$_{ATP}$ channel agonist and is effective in most forms of HH, except in diffuse disease due to AR-inherited ABCC8/KCNJ11 mutations. Oral hydrochlorothiazide (1 mg/kg/day in 2 divided doses) should be combined with DZX to combat the fluid retention side effect of DZX. DZX, being 90% protein bound, toxicity could be severe in infants with hypoalbuminaemia. In DZX-responsive infants, on successful weaning of IV dextrose, a safety fast study (SFS) (6 hours fast) can be done to confirm the infant’s

Once weaned off DZX, the infant will be subjected to a resolution fast study (RFS). In DZX-unresponsive infants (inadequate glycaemic response in 3–5 days on max DZX doses), genetic studies should be requested.

While awaiting the results of the genetic studies, use glucagon infusion to transition the infants to octreotide (subcutaneous /IV 5–35 mcg/kg/day). Octreotide in increasing doses has the risk of necrotising enterocolitis. If genetic studies suggest focal disease, DOPA-PET imaging can localise the lesion and the infant can be cured by lesionectomy. If diffuse disease is confirmed, subtotal pancreatectomy (95% pancreas) is the treatment of choice.

# CHAPTER 81

# Neonatal Seizures

Nirmal Kavalloor Visruthan

## Seizure Pattern

Subtle seizures constitute 50% of the seizures in the newborns. They may be in the form of tonic horizontal deviation and jerking of the eyes, repetitive blinking or fluttering of the eyelids, oral and buccal movements (drooling, sucking, yawning), tonic limb posturing, complex purposeless movement ('swimming', 'bicycling'), apnoeas and rhythmic fluctuations of vital signs. Seizures may also present as focal or multifocal clonic seizures, tonic seizures and myoclonic seizures.

## Jitteriness Versus Seizure

A number of 'seizure-like' behavioural states may be confused with seizures. These include jitteriness, movement during rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, decorticate and decerebrate posturing, and autonomic dysfunction.

|  Clinical Features | Jitteriness | Seizures  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Abnormality of gaze or eye movements | Absent | Present  |
|  Autonomic changes | Absent | Present  |
|  Response to stimulation | Yes | No  |
|  Movement ease with passive flexion | Yes | No  |
|  Predominant movement | Tremor | Jerking  |

## Common Causes

- Hypoxic ischaemic encephalopathy: Seizures usually occur at 12–24 hours of life
- Metabolic (hypoglycaemia, hypocalcaemia, hypo/hypernatraemia)
- Neonatal stroke
- Infections: Meningitis, viral encephalitis, TORCH (Toxoplasmosis, Rubella, Cytomegalovirus and Herpes Simplex) infections
- Intracranial bleeds
- Inborn errors of metabolism
- Neonatal abstinence syndrome
Congenital malformations: Schizencephaly, lissencephaly, holoprosencephaly

- Epilepsy syndromes

» Benign idiopathic neonatal seizures: 'Fifth day fits', benign examination and clinical course
» Benign familial neonatal convulsions: Family history of neonatal seizures and spontaneously resolves in a few days to weeks
» Early myoclonic encephalopathy: Inherited metabolic disorder including non-ketotic hyperglycinemia
» Early infantile epileptic encephalopathy: Ohtahara syndrome
Genetic epilepsy syndromes, e.g. KCNQ2 mutations

- Genetic testing for epilepsy syndromes

## Acute Management

See Figure 17.9 for the algorithm for acute management of neonatal seizures.

## Hypoxic Ischaemic Encephalopathy

Hypoxic ischaemic encephalopathy (HIE) is a neurological dysfunction affecting newborn infants. Its prevalence ranges from 1–3/1000, and it affects mainly term infants. The major pathological mechanism is decreased cerebral blood flow and oxygen delivery. This results in primary (immediate hypoxic neuronal death) and secondary (cells 'commit' to die over 6 to 72 hours) energy failure. During this 'window of opportunity', hypothermia may have a beneficial effect. The most common causes for HIE are due to intrauterine asphyxia such as placental abruption, cord prolapse and uterine rupture. However, it is

important to rule out reversible causes of neonatal encephalopathy such as metabolic disorders, infections, neonatal stroke and central nervous system malformations.

## Stages of Encephalopathy

Sarnat recognised 3 stages of neonatal encephalopathy. **Stage 1** lasts less than 24 hours and is characterised by hyperalertness, uninhibited Moro and stretch reflexes, sympathetic effects and a normal electroencephalogram. **Stage 2** is marked by obtundation, hypotonia,

b4854_Sec-17.indd 567

12-Dec-22 3:21:32 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

strong distal flexion and multifocal seizures. Infants in **Stage 3** are stuporous and flaccid, and their brainstem and autonomic functions are suppressed.

## Investigations

It is essential to obtain the umbilical cord gases as well as the baby's blood gas within 1 hour of life. Poor cord gases (pH <7, base excess ≥16), low Apgar scores (≤5 at 10 minutes) and respiratory depression requiring ventilation are suggestive of moderate to severe encephalopathy. Other investigations include:

Full blood count, C-reactive protein
Blood culture
- Renal panel, liver function test (LFT), blood glucose
- Prothrombin time/activated partial thromboplastin time
- Lactate, ammonia, Inborn Error of Metabolism screen (to be repeated once on full feeds)
- Cranial ultrasound
2D echocardiogram
- aEEG (if available)
- Cranial MRI between Day 5 and 10

## Management

- Adequate resuscitation should be initiated at birth.
- Avoid hyperthermia.
- Monitor blood gases and glucose every 4-6 hours.
- Anticipate multi-organ dysfunction. Inotropic support (Dopamine) may need to be instituted for poor cardiac contractility. Daily monitoring of LFT (to monitor hepatic dysfunction) and renal panel (to monitor renal dysfunction) is necessary.
- Fluid restrict to \(50\mathrm{ml / kg / day}\) on Day 1. Subsequently increase fluids based on strict input/output balance. Urinary catheterisation may be necessary.
- A cerebral function monitoring (aEEG) will assist with an assessment of degree of neonatal encephalopathy.
- Correct persistent metabolic acidosis with sodium bicarbonate as a slow infusion (avoid alkalosis since this can compromise cerebral circulation).
- Seizures need to be treated promptly with intravenous phenobarbitone.
- For infants who are 36 weeks' gestation and above, hypothermia (rectal temperature slowly brought down to \(33.5^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\)) initiated at the earliest and continued for 72 hours has become the standard of care. See Figure 17.10 for the algorithm to decide which babies may qualify for cooling.

## Follow-up

- All infants need a full neurological assessment (Dubowitz-Hammersmith) before discharge.
- Neurology follow-up.
High-risk hearing screen at 3 months of age.
- Developmental examination during neonatal follow-up.

# CHAPTER 82

# Haematuria

Leow Hui Min, Esther; Ng Yong Hong

## Definition

Microscopic haematuria is defined by the presence of ≥5 red blood cells (RBC)/μL in an uncentrifuged urine sample. The diagnosis of gross haematuria is made when blood is present in the urine that is visible to the naked eye.

A positive urine dipstick is not good enough for diagnosis. A false-positive result may be caused by haemoglobinuria, myoglobinuria or improper use of strips. Urine may be discoloured by drugs, such as rifampicin, or food, such as beetroot. Urate crystals in the urine of infants may cause a pink discolouration to the nappy. Microscopic examination is essential to confirm haematuria.

## Localising the Origin of Haematuria (Glomerular versus Non-Glomerular)

- Glomerular bleeding is suggested by the presence of RBC casts or granular casts.
- Urine phase contrast microscopy is performed on a fresh urine specimen. Glomerular haematuria yields RBCs with distortion and variation in shape and size (dysmorphic). Lower tract bleeding gives RBCs, which are uniform in shape and size (isomorphic). Sensitivity and specificity is over \(90\%\) in detecting glomerular haematuria. The microscopic haematuria is more likely glomerular in origin if \(>20 - 30\%\) red blood cells are dysmorphic.
- Presence of white blood cells (WBC) casts suggests renal inflammation.

Table 18.1 Causes of Discolouration of Urine

|  Colour of Urine | Causes  |
| --- | --- |
|  Pink/red | Fresh blood (haematuria) Haemoglobinuria, myoglobinuria, porphyrins, urate Foods (e.g. beetroot, rhubarb, blackberries, red dyes, red dragon fruit) Drugs (e.g. chloroquine, chlorpromazine, desferrioxamine)  |
|  Brown | Oxidised haem pigment (blood from upper urinary tract or urine left standing for a long time) Bile pigments, methaemoglobinaemia, alkaptonuria Drugs (e.g. metronidazole, chloroquine, primaquine, nitrofurantoin)  |
|  Dark yellow or orange | Normal concentrated urine Foods containing carotene (e.g. papaya, carrots, pumpkin, etc.) Drugs (e.g. rifampicin, warfarin)  |

# The Baby Bear Book

## History

- Associated symptoms

» Infection: Fever, dysuria, frequency, loin pain
» Stone disease: Pain, passage of 'sand'/stones
» Acute glomerulonephritis (AGN): Preceding upper respiratory tract infection or skin infection, oedema
» Secondary cause: Fever, rashes, arthralgia/arthritis

- Nature of haematuria: Terminal haematuria often suggests local causes such as urethritis/vulvitis
- Previous episodes of gross haematuria
- History of trauma
- Family history: Haematuria, proteinuria, hypertension, renal stones, renal failure, deafness, coagulopathy, polycystic kidney disease
- Drug history, e.g. medications, health supplements or herbal compounds

## Clinical Examination

- Local examination
- Evidence of GN: Hypertension, oedema

## Gross Haematuria

### Investigations (Symptom-directed)

- Urine culture
- Urine calcium/creatinine ratio
- Full blood count
- Renal panel
- Clotting study where appropriate
- Complement level
- KUB (Kidney-Ureter-Bladder) X-Ray for radio-opaque calculi
- Renal ultrasound
- Other imaging studies/biochemical tests where appropriate

### Treatment

- Treat underlying cause
- Follow-up till resolution

### When to Refer to Paediatric Nephrologists (Inpatient/Outpatient)

- Persistent haematuria following treatment for urinary tract infection, e.g. cystitis
- Recurrent and/or painless gross haematuria
- Hypertension, abnormal renal function, presence of proteinuria or structural anomaly on renal ultrasound

# Haematuria

# Microscopic Haematuria

Asymptomatic microscopic haematuria is common. It is found in up to 4% of school-going children on urine screening. With repeated testing on three consecutive samples, <0.5% is positive.

## What Do I Do in a Child with Microscopic Haematuria?

In the absence of proteinuria and symptoms, repeat urine microscopy. If persistent in at least three samples over one month, then proceed to investigate.

## Investigations (Symptom-directed)

- Urinalysis (UFEME)
- Urine culture
- Urine protein/creatinine ratio (uPCR)
- Urine calcium/creatinine ratio
- Urine phase contrast microscopy
- Renal ultrasound
- Others as indicated

## How Often Do I Follow Up if the Above Investigations are Normal?

3–6 monthly for a year and at each review, blood pressure, UFEME and uPCR should be performed together with an assessment for the development of new renal or systemic symptoms or family history of renal disease.

## What if Protein is Detected?

Proteinuria is defined as uPCR >0.2 mg/mg or 20 mg/mmol creatinine. If proteinuria is detected with a random specimen, proceed to quantitate protein by doing timed urine collection. If >4 mg/m²/hour, refer to a nephrologist. If <4 mg/m²/hour, follow-up closely for development of proteinuria at subsequent reviews.

## Indications for Renal Biopsy

- Associated significant proteinuria (40 mg/m²/hour on timed urine collection)
- Azotaemia
Hypertension
- Recurrent gross haematuria
Family history of GN
Systemic diseases (e.g. Systemic lupus erythematosus, ANCA-positive vasculitis)

## Bibliography

1. Avner ED, Harmon WE, Niaudet P, et al. (2016) Pediatric Nephrology, 7th ed. Springer.

2. Milford DV, Robson AM. (2003) The child with abnormal urinalysis, haematuria and/or proteinuria. Clinical Paediatric Nephrology, 3rd ed. Oxford: Oxford University Press.

3. Diven SC, Travis LB. (2000) A practical primary care approach to haematuria in children. Pediatr Nephrol 14(1):65-72.

## Definition

Sudden onset of symptoms of glomerular injury (haematuria, hypertension, oedema) and varying degrees of renal insufficiency.

## Differential Diagnoses

Post-infectious:

Bacterial:

Group A \(\beta\)-haemolytic Streptococcus (most common)
Pneumococcus sp.
Staphylococcus sp.

- Primarily renal disease

IgA nephropathy
C3 glomerulopathy
Hereditary nephritis (Alport's Syndrome)

Systemic involvement

Henoch-Schonlein purpura with nephritis
Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE)
ANCA-associated vasculitis
Anti-glomerular basement membrane disease
Infective Endocarditis related
Shunt nephritis

## History

- Symptoms in relation to underlying cause

History of upper respiratory tract infection 1 to 2 weeks prior to onset of acute glomerulonephritis (AGN)
History of skin infection prior to onset of AGN. Latent period between pyoderma and onset of AGN is variable.
Systemic symptoms: Fever, malaise, anorexia, arthritis, rash (systemic and extra renal symptoms are often seen in SLE nephritis)

Urinary tract symptoms

Oliguria
Painless gross haematuria (typically described as 'coke', 'tea' or 'smoky')

- Symptoms in relation to fluid overload

» Facial swelling and/or generalised oedema
Dyspnoea, exercise intolerance (pulmonary oedema)

- Cerebral complications of hypertension, including headaches, seizures, mental status changes and visual changes
- Family history of autoimmune disorders, renal failure (dialysis/kidney transplantation), haematuria, early onset hearing loss

## Physical Examination

Hypertension
- Oedema, e.g. ascites, pedal and/or periorbital oedema
- Circulatory congestion: Tachypnoea, tachycardia, cardiomegaly, hepatomegaly, raised jugular venous pressure, crepitations in the lungs

# Acute Nephritic Syndrome

## Management

- Monitoring: Strict intake and output charting, daily weight, blood pressure monitoring at least 4 hourly or closer interval as determined by clinical severity
Supportive/symptomatic

Bed rest

- Supplemental oxygen and loop diuretics (frusemide) if hypoxia from pulmonary oedema

- Salt and water restriction to insensible water loss \((400\mathrm{ml} / \mathrm{m}^2 /\) day) plus half urine output
Frequent re-evaluation of fluid status and fluid balance to determine fluid requirement/restriction
Treatment of hypertension
- Drugs that can be used include loop diuretics (frusemide), calcium channel blocker or hydralazine (hydralazine is contraindicated in patients with SLE)
If severe, refer to chapter on Hypertension
- Antibiotics indicated in patients with positive cultures. Penicillin is the drug of choice (erythromycin in allergic individuals) for streptococcal infections

## Natural History

# Resolution of:

Gross haematuria 2 weeks

Oliguria 2 weeks

Azotaemia 2 weeks

Hypertension 4 weeks

Decreased C3 6 weeks

Proteinuria 6 months

Microscopic haematuria 12 months

## Indications for Renal Biopsy

- Severe renal failure of unknown/uncertain aetiology
- Atypical features such as co-existing Nephrotic Syndrome
- Delayed resolution

## Prognosis

- Excellent in post-streptococcal AGN, with restoration of renal function in more than \(90\%\)
Rapidly progressing glomerulonephritis (RPGN) in \(1\%\)

# CHAPTER 84

# Nephrotic Syndrome

Chao Sing Ming; Yap Jia Ying, Celeste

## Introduction

Nephrotic Syndrome (NS) is a syndrome defined clinically by Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO) 2021 guideline as:

- Heavy proteinuria (nephrotic range) with urine protein/creatinine ratio (uPCR) \(\geq 2\) (mg/mg) or urine total protein (UTP) \(\geq 3\) g/L (random sample) or \(\geq 40\) mg/ m²/h (timed sample) and the presence of either
- Low serum albumin \(< 30\mathrm{g / L}\) or
- Oedema when serum albumin level is not available

Hyperlipidaemia is not required for the diagnosis of NS.

Over 80% of childhood NS are Idiopathic NS (INS) with no clear cause. It is presumably an immune mediated disorder that leads to glomerular podocyte dysfunction, resulting in increased protein permeability and massive urinary protein loss. The vast majority of INS will show minimal glomerular changes on histology except for podocyte effacement. Other histological diagnoses include Diffuse Mesangial Proliferation, Focal Segmental Glomerulosclerosis (FSGS), rarely membranous and membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis.

INS by itself is not common (incidence of 1.15–16.9 per 100000 children). However, it is an important and prevalent cause of generalised oedema in children and a major presenting symptom of childhood glomerulonephritis.

Non-INS with known causes include genetic causes, secondary to systemic disease like systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), Haemolytic Uraemic Syndrome (HUS) and IgA nephropathy, and rarer causes include infections, drugs or neoplasia.

## Clinical Classifications and Definitions

- Steroid responsiveness is of greater clinical and prognostic value than histology in INS. Renal biopsy is therefore not needed in the management of most children with INS.
- Clinical response to steroid therapy and the clinical course guide the management and prognosis of INS. Thus, the broad clinical classification into Steroid Sensitive Nephrotic Syndrome (SSNS), found in over \(80\%\) of patients, and Steroid Resistant Nephrotic Syndrome (SRNS), found in \(10 - 20\%\) of patients.

# The Baby Bear Book

Table 18.5 Definitions (KDIGO 2021)

|  Complete Remission | Urine total protein (UTP) <0.2 g/L or urine protein/creatinine ratio (uPCR) <0.2 (mg/mg) or dipstick protein nil/trace for 3 or more consecutive days.  |
| --- | --- |
|  Partial Remission | Decrease in proteinuria of ≥50% over presenting value or uPCR between 0.2 and 2 (mg/mg) or dipstick protein 1+ to 2+ or serum albumin 30 g/L or more.  |
|  Steroid response/ sensitive | Remission achieved within 4 wk of steroid therapy.  |
|  Relapse | UTP ≥ 3 g/L or uPCR ≥2 (mg/mg) or dipstick protein ≥3+ for 3 consecutive days with or without oedema. Oedema is a clear sign of relapse.  |
|  Frequent relapses | 2 or more relapses within 6 mth of initial response, or 4 or more relapses within any 12-mth period.  |
|  Steroid dependence | 2 consecutive relapses during steroid treatment or 1 relapse within 2 wk of its cessation.  |
|  Steroid resistance | Failure to achieve remission after 4 wk of daily steroid therapy at 60 mg/m²/d.  |
|  Late steroid resistance | Steroid resistance develops after being steroid sensitive previously.  |

Management of SRNS, being a heterogeneous syndrome, is complex and will not be covered in this chapter.

- The clinical course further divides SSNS into no relapse or infrequent relapsing NS, which is seen in about half of SSNS patients and frequent relapsing NS (FRNS) or steroid-dependent NS (SDNS) in the remaining half. While infrequent relapsing NS responds well to steroid treatment with prolonged remissions, FRNS and especially SDNS require prolonged steroid and steroid sparing immunosuppression therapy to maintain remission, resulting in more side effects of treatment that require regular monitoring.
- The clinical response and clinical course should be clearly defined and firmly established to form the basis of treatment (Table 18.5).

There are several distinct histological types of INS:

1. Minimal Change Disease (MCNS) seen in \(80\%\) cases typically presents as SSNS. Variants, including IgM, mesangial proliferation and C1q glomerulonephritis, often present with FRNS and SDNS, which are more difficult to treat.
2. FSGS often presents as SRNS or late steroid resistance.
3. Membranous and membranoproliferative nephropathy are rarer and often present as SRNS.

## Investigations

- Confirm diagnosis: Urinalysis, UTP or uPCR, serum albumin. Diagnosis of heavy proteinuria is usually straightforward and does not require timed measurement.

b4854_Sec-18.indd 582
