# Nephrotic Syndrome

583

- Full blood count: Haemoconcentration, if present, is due to intravascular contraction and may increase the risk of thrombosis.
- Renal panel: Dilutional hyponatremia due to over-hydration; azotaemia due to pre-renal dehydration.
- Other investigations, e.g. chest X-ray, urine/blood culture are guided by clinical indications.
- Screening tests for systemic disease (e.g. SLE) to be done only if there are clinical suspicion (e.g. systemic symptoms, haematological abnormalities, nephritic symptoms and signs).

## Principles of Treatment and Management

### 1. To induce remission and treat complications

- Prednisolone is the mainstay of treatment (see Tables 18.6 and 18.7).
- Important to monitor steroid side effects. Explain to parents the side effects and action plans.
- Complications of NS include:

» Infections: Cellulitis, spontaneous peritonitis, pneumonia (prone to Streptococcus pneumoniae infections and thus empirical use of high dose ampicillin or ceftriaxone), varicella infection, pulmonary tuberculosis.
» Shock/pre-shock: NS patients are more prone to intravascular contraction from fluid loss. Triggers include diarrhoea, vomiting and sepsis. Look for symptoms of hypovolaemia, oliguria, pre-renal acute kidney injury with low urinary sodium \((\mathrm{FeNa} < 1\%)\) and raised haematocrit.
Treat with intravenous (IV) normal saline \(20\mathrm{ml / kg}\) over 1 to 2 hours if urgent or IV \(20\%\) albumin \(1\mathrm{g / kg}\) over 2 to 4 hours with continuous blood pressure (BP) and vital signs monitoring.
Venous thromboembolism (renal vein, cerebral sinus venous, pulmonary embolism): rare but can be serious and may require anticoagulation.
Hyponatraemia with seizures (often due to rapid IV over-hydration). Needs IV albumin and frusemide.

### 2. Symptomatic treatment

- Most patients do not require albumin and frusemide for the relief of oedema while awaiting response to steroid.
- Indications for quick relief of oedema include severe ascites, scrotal oedema, labial oedema and hyponatraemia with central nervous system symptoms. As this therapy may not be sustained and effective, repeated infusions may be needed.
- Note that indiscriminate use of albumin in some patients who do not have signs and symptoms of hypovolaemia can result in rapid intravascular expansion and precipitate pulmonary oedema.
- IV albumin is given as \(20\%\) albumin at \(1 - 1.5\mathrm{g / kg}\) (preparation as \(10\mathrm{g}\) per \(50~\mathrm{ml}\) bottle, avoid wastage) over 4 to 6 hours. IV frusemide \(1 - 3\mathrm{mg / kg / dose}\) is given at midway and at the end of infusion.
- Fluid restriction is needed only in severe oedema in an otherwise stable patient (restrict up to two-third normal).

- No additional salt intake. Strict salt restriction is unnecessary, unlike treatment for acute glomerulonephritis, which can cause significant fluid overload.

# 3. Early detection and treatment of relapse

- Home monitoring and caretaker education is essential. Refer to patient information pamphlet and nephrotic diary.

# 4. Monitor untoward steroid side effects

- Monitor growth, BP and refer ophthalmologist to screen for posterior subcapsular cataract or glaucoma if steroid treatment is prolonged beyond 6 months.
- Refer patients with FRNS and SDNS to nephrology for further management and steroid sparing therapy.

# Points to Remember

1. Indications of hospitalisation include:

- Severe oedema that may require IV albumin/frusemide.
Presence or suspect complications (see above).
- NS with systemic symptoms that require further workup and specific treatment.

# Nephrotic Syndrome

585

2. First clinical response to steroid treatment will be an increase in urine output and weight loss (often before resolution of proteinuria), occurring after 7-10 days of prednisolone treatment.
3. Patient can be safely discharged if clinically stable and caregiver understands clearly home monitoring and action plans (Refer to your institution's clinical pathway for uncomplicated INS). Complete remission occurs after 2-4 weeks of treatment (80% by end of 2 weeks, 90% by end of 3 weeks).
4. Relapses are expected in most patients and parents need to be forewarned and to monitor for possible relapse.
5. Home dipstick protein monitoring to detect early relapse can avoid unnecessary admissions. Intercurrent infection, especially upper respiratory tract infection (URTI), may induce unsustained proteinuria. If there is no oedema and improving trend in urine protein to \(< 3+\) after 5 days, treatment for relapse is unnecessary. However, it is important to continue outpatient monitoring.
6. Parent or caregiver education is important for long-term management. Exclude noncompliance in treatment failure (as in most chronic illnesses) and address this issue before considering steroid resistance.
7. Complete resolution of NS can be considered if patient remains well with no proteinuria after 4 consecutive years. A small proportion of patients may continue to relapse into adulthood with a less favourable outcome.

**Clinical features for non-MCNS** that require referral to nephrologist for renal biopsy and management include:

Steroid resistance
Gross haematuria
Family history of NS
Hypertension
Non pre-renal azotaemia
Evidence of systemic disease
- Relative indication for age of onset \(< 1\) year or \(>12\) years (onset \(< 3\) months: congenital NS, 3-12 months: possible genetic NS, congenital infections, \(>12\) years: higher risk of non-MCNS).

## Treatment Regimes

### General Considerations

- Recommended treatment for initial presentation is quite well established with randomised controlled trials and good quality evidence (grade B), while suggested treatment for relapses is less well established (grade C-D).
- Documenting hypoalbuminaemia is not necessary for the diagnosis of relapse.
Eighty per cent of childhood INS are MCNS with \(93\%\) being SSNS. MCNS has an excellent prognosis with no long-term renal morbidity.
- Morbidity is mainly seen in FRNS, especially SDNS due to complications of the nephrotic state and steroid side effects (especially retarded growth, infections,

b4854_Sec-18.indd 585

12-Dec-22 11:11:56 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

eye complications, hypertension), which necessitates the use of steroid-sparing drugs (cyclophosphamide, cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, rituximab).

- All immunosuppressive drugs have significant side effects and their use requires documenting clear indications, parental consent, explanation of side effects and regular monitoring.

## Treatment of Relapse NS as Outpatient

# 1. Non-oedematous patient

- Treat after ascertaining sustained proteinuria, i.e. UTP ≥3 g/L or dipstick protein ≥3+ for >3 consecutive days.
- Unsustained proteinuria may occur with intercurrent infection or URTI, but proteinuria should improve after 3 to 5 days. Up to \(25\%\) of the relapses remit spontaneously and therapy is unnecessary.

2. **Oedematous patient** (swelling, sudden weight gain in the presence of significant proteinuria of >2+) should be treated as relapse irrespective of the duration of proteinuria.

## Immunisations

- Immunisations with inactivated vaccines (Diphtheria, pertussis and tetanus, pneumococcal, Haemophilus influenzae type B, hepatitis B, influenza, Salk polio, meningococcal), in general, will not be harmful but more likely to be effective when given to a patient who is in remission or on low dose alternate day prednisolone.
- Live attenuated vaccination (Mumps, measles and rubella, oral polio, varicella, Bacillus Calmette-Guerin) may be harmful to patients with ongoing steroid or other immunosuppressive treatment and should be deferred until patient is off treatment for 3 to 6 months.
- Timely pneumococcal vaccination is advisable as patients with NS are particularly prone to pneumococcal infection.
- Consult paediatric infectious disease specialist if in doubt.

## Prognosis

Prognosis is excellent in SSNS (<5% progress to chronic renal failure in 10 years) but significant side effects of therapy may incur in FRNS, especially SDNS, and it is crucial to balance efficacy with the side effects of treatment. SRNS, being heterogeneous, has varying prognosis but its prognosis is generally much worse, with 50% patients with FSGS progressing to end-stage renal failure in 5 years.

# CHAPTER 85

# Hypertension

Leow Hui Min, Esther; Ng Yong Hong

## Definition

Defined as average systolic blood pressure (SBP) and/or diastolic blood pressure (DBP) that is ≥95th percentile for gender, age and height on ≥3 occasions.* Refer to blood pressure (BP) level charts below for both genders based on age and height centiles.

*except children with stage 2 hypertension or a child with symptomatic hypertension

## When should Blood Pressure be Measured?

- Children above the age of 3 who are seen in medical care settings should have their BP measured at least once during every clinic visit.
- Children less than 3 years old should have their BP measured in special circumstances:

History of prematurity, very low birth weight or other neonatal complication requiring intensive care
Congenital heart disease (repaired or un-repaired)
Recurrent urinary tract infections, haematuria or proteinuria
Known renal disease or urologic malformations
Family history of congenital renal disease
Solid-organ transplant

Table 18.8 Definitions of Blood Pressure Categories and Stages

|   | 1–13 Years Old | ≥13 Years Old  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Normal blood pressure | <90th percentile | <120/<80 mmHg  |
|  Elevated blood pressure | ≥90th percentile to <95th percentile or 120/80 mmHg to <95th percentile (whichever is lower) | 120/<80 to 129/<80 mmHg  |
|  Stage 1 hypertension | ≥95th percentile to <95th percentile + 12 mmHg, or 130/80 to 139/89 mmHg (whichever is lower) | 130/80 to 139/89 mmHg  |
|  Stage 2 hypertension | ≥95th percentile + 12 mmHg, or ≥140/90 mmHg (whichever is lower) | ≥140/90 mmHg  |

## Initial Diagnostic Evaluation

|  Blood | Full blood count, renal panel, calcium, magnesium, phosphate, uric acid, fasting lipid panel, fasting glucose with HbA1c and insulin level, liver function test, thyroid function test  |
| --- | --- |
|  Urine | Urinalysis (UFEME), urine albumin/creatinine ratio  |
|  Imaging | Renal ultrasound with Doppler Study of renal arteries  |
|  Others (As Required) | Referral to Sleep Clinic for sleep study if history is suggestive of obstructive sleep apnoea Referral to Cardiology for echocardiogram to look for left ventricular hypertrophy or coarctation of aorta Referral to Ophthalmology to look for hypertensive retinopathy  |

Further investigations may be required, as indicated by history and physical examination.

## Severe Hypertension

- Not rigorously defined, thus leading to confusion at times.
- Useful definition:

BP elevation that fulfils (and usually exceeds) the definition of Stage 2 hypertension and that is accompanied by severe symptoms; physical exam and/or laboratory findings of accelerated hypertension are frequently also present.
20 mmHg above the 95th percentile.

- Adult definition:

- Blood pressures that are 50% above normal BP or >180/120 mmHg.

- Children presenting with severe symptoms need to be treated more rapidly:

It is risky to lower the BP too rapidly, especially in those whose hypertension has been longstanding.
In such patients, a shift in cerebral autoregulation occurs, which in the hypertensive state, protects the brain from excessive perfusion.
Too rapid lowering of the BP in patients with such alterations of cerebral flow might lead to an ischemic stroke from under perfusion of the brain.

- Can be subdivided into hypertensive emergency or hypertensive urgency.

b4854_Sec-18.indd 599

12-Dec-22 11:11:58 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

## Hypertensive Emergency

Presence of severe hypertension (as defined above), and
- The presence of end-organ injury, such as cardiac failure (left ventricular hypertrophy, pulmonary oedema), hypertensive retinopathy (papilloedema, exudates and fundal haemorrhages), hypertensive encephalopathy (altered mental state, convulsions), cerebral haemorrhage, cerebral infarction.

## Treatment

- All cases of hypertensive emergency should be admitted to the Children's Intensive Care Unit for continuous BP monitoring.
- Aim of treatment is to lower the BP to the desired range gradually so as to minimise hypertensive sequelae, but yet not compromise perfusion to vital organs due to a sudden decrease in BP.
- The ultimate short-term BP goal in such patients should generally be around in the 95th percentile.
- BP should be reduced by no more than \(25\%\) of the planned reduction over the first 8 hours, with the remainder of the planned reduction over the next 12 to 24 hours.
- Pay attention to pupillary reactions, consciousness level and neurological examination.
- Saline for volume expansion should be available on standby in case of sudden hypotension.
- Choice of antihypertensive agent:

Depends on aetiology of hypertension.
> Antihypertensive with rapid onset of action, short half-life, safe and efficacious is preferred.
Intravenous (IV) route of administration preferred in hypertensive emergency.
See Table 18.12 for the drugs that can be used in hypertensive emergency.
Long-acting oral medications can be introduced after reasonable BP control within 24 to 48 hours of initiation of IV antihypertensive infusion.

## Hypertensive Urgency

Presence of severe hypertension with no evidence of acute end organ damage/ compromise.
Patients may have symptoms of nausea, headache or blurred vision.

## Treatment

- Goal of therapy is reduction of BP over a 24-hour period.
- Choice of either IV or oral antihypertensive. Oral agents can be used to gradually lower BP in patients who are asymptomatic and do not have evidence of immediate end-organ injury.
- The oral drugs that can be used in hypertensive urgency are listed in Table 18.13.

# CHAPTER 86

# Urinary Tract Infections and Vesicoureteric Reflux

Chong Siew Le; Chao Sing Ming

## Introduction

Urinary tract infection (UTI) is one of the most common bacterial infections seen in children and a leading cause of acute sepsis in infants requiring hospitalisation. Incidence of renal involvement or acute pyelonephritis is high, especially during infancy (up to 80%).

Although the majority of children with UTI have an excellent prognosis, there is a definite risk of renal damage or scarring following pyelonephritis, especially in those with vesicoureteric reflux (VUR) or obstructive uropathy.

## Clinical Classification

The epidemiology, pathophysiology and prognosis of UTI are interrelated and differ according to age, sex and, in particular, the site of infection. A useful classification is according to the site of infection (Table 18.14). While more than 80% of patients with

Table 18.14 Clinical Classification of Childhood UTI

|  Clinical Features | Upper Tract Infection | Lower Tract Infection  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Age | Generally <2 yr | Generally >2 yr  |
|  Renal involvement | Neonates and young infants are at high risk | No risk  |
|  Sex | Female = Male in infancy Female > Male past infancy | Female > Male  |
|  Fever | + (All febrile UTI have presumed pyelonephritis) | -  |
|  Voiding problem | - | +  |
|  Dysuria | - | +  |
|  Frequency | - | +  |
|  Suprapubic pain | - | +  |
|  Loin pain (older children) | + | -  |

|  • Past history of UTI/renal or renal tract abnormality/bladder dysfunction  |
| --- |

upper UTI have typical clinical features of unexplained fever with or without urinary symptoms, it is important to look at clinical features of atypical or complicated UTI (Table 18.15), which may suggest underlying renal/renal tract abnormalities that warrant attention and further investigations, including micturating cystourethrogram (MCU).

## Diagnosis and Management

### Upper Urinary Tract Infection

- The cardinal symptom of pyelonephritis is unexplained high fever, often with chills and rigors. UTI should be suspected in such instances, especially in infants. Cloudy urine is a more specific symptom than smelly urine.
- A pre-treatment, properly collected mid-steam urine culture, is mandatory to establish a firm diagnosis of UTI.
- Up to \(9\%\) patients with pyelonephritis may have equivocal or negative urine culture. If urine culture is equivocal and suspicion of pyelonephritis is high, a DMSA scan can help to establish diagnosis.
- When early initiation of antibiotic treatment is necessary, an invasive method of urine collection (suprapubic tap, catheterisation) is justified. Beware and be ready to collect midstream urine as baby may spontaneously void while being cleaned for the procedure.
- \(E.\) coli is the commonest uropathogen \((>80\%)\). Gentamicin is the recommended first-line antibiotic (93% sensitivity) and ceftriaxone (almost 100%) as an alternative.
- Although ceftriaxone has almost \(100\%\) sensitivity, it is used as second-line treatment for fear of the emergence of resistant bacterial strains. However, in patients with compromised renal function, ceftriaxone is the antibiotic of choice.

### Urine culture — points to remember:

- Bagged urine specimen is often contaminated and should not be used for the urine culture.
- Label clearly the method used for urine collection as diagnostic criteria vary (see below).

Antibiotic Treatment for Uncomplicated Febrile UTI

|  Choice of Antibiotics | Duration of Treatment  |
| --- | --- |
|  **Intravenous (IV) Antibiotics:** **First line** • Gentamicin 5–6 mg/kg/d Q24H • Infants <1 mth, add Ampicillin 100 mg/kg/d Q6H (to cover possible enterococci infection). *Precautions for gentamicin therapy:* If serum creatinine elevated for age, ensure adequate hydration and consider second-line antibiotics for fear of gentamicin nephrotoxicity. If continued >48 hr, check gentamicin trough levels. **Second line** • Ceftriaxone 50 mg/kg/DOSE Q24H • Cefotaxime 200–300 mg/kg/d Q6–8H Cefotaxime is preferred in neonates. **Oral antibiotics:** • First-generation cephalosporins, e.g. Cephalexin (50 mg/kg/d Q8H) • Co-amoxiclav (Amoxicillin 80–90 mg/kg/d Q12H) | Total duration: 14 d IV therapy until no fever for 24 hr, then switch to oral therapy according to antibiogram For those with bacteraemia, IV therapy should be continued for 7–10 d  |

Refer to your institution’s antimicrobial guidelines for latest antimicrobial selection and dosing recommendations.

# The Baby Bear Book

## Lower Urinary Tract Infection

Common in young girls.
- Presents with voiding symptoms — dysuria, frequency, urgency and suprapubic pain. Some may present with urge incontinence or secondary nocturnal enuresis.
Commonly afebrile.
- Though troublesome and often recurrent, avoid over investigations unless there is suspicion of febrile or upper UTI.
Essential to perform perineum examination to rule out local inflammation/ infection.
Presence of phimosis in boys could increase risk of infection.

## Antibiotic Treatment of Lower Urinary Tract Infection

|  Choice of Antibiotics  |
| --- |
|  1. Co-trimoxazole (TMP/SMZ)* TMP 8 mg/kg/d Q12H  |
|  2. Nitrofurantoin*: 5–7 mg/kg/d Q6H  |
|  3. Cephalexin: 50 mg/kg/d Q8H  |
|  4. Trimethoprim: 8 mg/kg/d Q12H (Useful for G6PD deficient patients) (*Screen for G6PD deficiency)  |

|  Duration of Treatment  |
| --- |
|  7 d of antibiotics >12 yr old: 3 d TMP/SMZ can be considered instead of the standard 7 d treatment  |

## Approach and Indications for Investigations Following UTI

### Principle

- Most of the experimental and clinical evidence strongly supports that both VUR and UTI are responsible for acquired renal scarring and that this damage takes up to 4-6 months to establish, becoming evident on the DMSA scan.
- Once formed, scarring is irreversible and when extensive, it can significantly reduce the function of the affected kidney (\(<45\%\) differential function on DMSA scan).
- Acute pyelonephritis may heal completely but some may subsequently form scars.
- Dilating VUR (Grade III-V) remains an important risk factor for acute pyelonephritis (APN) and renal scarring and requires further management.

Risk of scarring correlates to:

- Severity of VUR: Dilating VUR (Grade III-V) being at higher risk
Recurrence of UTI

Figure 18.2 Algorithm for the management of uncomplicated febrile UTI in children.

While somewhat controversial, investigations following uncomplicated febrile UTI are basically aimed at discovering any underlying renal and renal tract abnormality that may predispose patient to repeat infections. Parents need to be informed that recurrent UTI is likely to occur in the initial 6–12 months and that they should be advised to be vigilant for possible recurrent UTI when their child has fever of unknown source and the necessary action plan.

## Imaging Studies

Investigations include renal ultrasound (US KUB), DMSA scans and MCU, each with its own purpose and merits.

US KUB

155mmx235mm

b4854 The Baby Bear Book

b4854_Sec-18.indd 607

12-Dec-22 11:11:59 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

- To be done as soon as possible. A good screen for major structural abnormalities, including:

Hydronephrosis, hydroureter
Abnormal renal size and position
Cysts/calculi

- It is not sensitive in detecting VUR, pyelonephritis and renal scarring.

DMSA scans

Gold standard for the diagnosis of APN but may be difficult to differentiate acute inflammatory changes from scarring.
Gold standard for the diagnosis of renal scars when done 4-6 months post infection and is able to assess differential function of the kidneys. May be difficult to differentiate scarring due to dysplasia or acquired scars.
It greatly helps in clinical decision making and is suggested as workup for patients with confirmed or past febrile UTI to see whether renal scarring is present and MCU is required.

MCU

Radiographic MCU is the gold standard for the diagnosis of VUR.
It also detects abnormalities of lower urinary tract: Bladder, urethra.
Most centres do not recommend routine MCU after the 1st febrile UTI, as it is invasive with inherent radiation risk, and should be done in selected patients.

# Urinary Tract Infections and Vesicoureteric Reflux

» Indications generally include:

## Guidelines for Micturating Cystourethrography

### Why MCU

Gold standard for the diagnosis of VUR.
- Definitive diagnosis of posterior urethral valves (PUV) in boys, especially if bilateral dilated upper tract is present on ultrasound.
- Diagnosis of neuropathic bladder where bladder hypertrophy and trabeculation can be seen on MCU.

### Points to Remember

- MCU should only be done when the UTI is adequately treated, usually 2 to 4 weeks after an infection.
- Antibiotic prophylaxis (usually co-trimoxazole \(2 - 3\mathrm{mg / kg}\) /dose ON) should be started if MCU is indicated.
- Antibiotic cover for the procedure (co-trimoxazole or trimethoprim \(8\mathrm{mg / kg}/\) day in BD dose) for 3 days starting the day before MCU.
- In borderline cases of renal pelvic calyceal dilatation, follow-up US KUB is reasonable before considering MCU.
- In patients with significant hydronephrosis (peripheral calyceal dilatation with or without cortical thinning), especially without hydroureter, MAG-3 scan can be considered to rule out pelvic-ureteric junction obstruction.

### Precautions

- Indication must be present. If doubtful, consult senior doctor or renal physician.
- Adequate explanations must be given to parents regarding indications, procedure and possible complications. Risks of procedure are acceptable if indications are present.
- Complications of MCU include:

» Infection

Minimise risk by:
- Proper aseptic technique
Co-trimoxazole cover (Trimethoprim if G6PD deficiency)

» Trauma

Minimise risk by:

- Adequate lubrication/local anaesthesia with sterile \(1\%\) lignocaine gel
- Correct-sized catheter depending on patient size

# The Baby Bear Book

- Advance catheter until urine flows out freely, then advance by about \(2\mathrm{cm}\) before securing with tapes. Catheter length introduced should not exceed 4 to \(5\mathrm{cm}\) in neonates and 7 to \(10\mathrm{cm}\) in older children
- Avoid using force when resistance is met and consult senior doctor

- Aftercare

» Ensure antibiotic cover and prophylaxis.
To seek medical attention if unable to pee or if symptoms suggestive of UTI develop.

Indications for doing MCU must be clear and the disadvantages include:

- Invasive procedure requiring bladder catheterisation.
- Radiation risk to gonads, especially in females.

## Vesicoureteric Reflux

VUR is a heterogeneous group of disorders. It is either a primary developmental disorder with or without renal dysplasia or is secondary to obstruction at the bladder or urethra. In its uncomplicated primary form, only dilating VUR (Grade III–V) needs treatment. The first line of treatment is medical. Indications for surgical treatment are:

- Failed medical treatment
- Associated urological abnormalities, e.g. urethrocele, duplex systems, obstructive uropathy

## Medical Treatment

- Prophylactic antibiotics at night to prevent urosepsis. Check G6PD status.

Choice of antibiotic:

Co-trimoxazole [if G6PD deficiency or \(< 6\) weeks old, to use trimethoprim alone] \((2 - 3\mathrm{mg / kg / dose}\) trimethoprim ON)
Cephalexin (15-20 mg/kg/dose ON)
Nitrofurantoin (2 mg/kg/dose ON) for infants \(>1\) month (Avoid in G6PD deficiency)

- All toilet-trained children diagnosed with VUR or persistent VUR should be screened for bowel and bladder dysfunction.
- Regular follow-up

» Consider to repeat MCU if recurrent UTI and refer surgeon.
Beyond 5-6 years old and if there is no recurrent UTI, focus should be on renal involvement with long-term follow-up for hypertension and proteinuria if there is significant renal scarring.
If VUR still causes UTI past 5-6 years old, treatment is surgical as there is little chance of spontaneous resolution beyond this age.

## When to Refer to the Paediatric Nephrologist

- If VUR is high grade/dilating.

- If significant renal scarring is present, especially if differential function \(< 45\%\) (unilateral kidney scarring) or if bilateral kidney scarring with or without impaired kidney function.
- If there are complicated pathologies, e.g. duplex kidney, urethrocele, trabeculated bladder.

# CHAPTER 87

# Acute Kidney Injury

Indra Ganesan

## Definition

Acute kidney injury (AKI) is an abrupt decrease in glomerular filtration rate (GFR), resulting in the inability of the kidneys to maintain water and electrolyte homeostasis and acid–base balance, resulting in a rise in the serum creatinine.

Classified into:

Oliguric AKI — urine output is <400 ml/m²/day or <1 ml/kg/h

Non-oliguric AKI — urine output is maintained

## Approach

Table 18.17 Causes of Acute Kidney Injury

|  Pre-renal | Volume depletion | Gastrointestinal losses: Vomiting, diarrhoea Haemorrhage Renal losses: Tubulopathies  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|   |  Third space loss | Hypoalbuminaemic states: Nephrotic syndrome, liver disease Burns, crush injury Capillary leak syndrome, septicaemia  |
|   |  Circulatory failure | Cardiac tamponade, congestive cardiac failure, pericarditis  |
|  Renal | Glomerular Glomerulonephritis | Acute nephritic syndrome Post-infectious glomerulonephritis Ig A vasculitis nephritis (previously known as HSP Nephritis)/Ig A nephropathy Lupus nephritis Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis  |
|   |  Vascular | Haemolytic-uraemic syndrome Renal artery and vein thrombosis  |
|   |  Tubulointerstitium Interstitial nephritis | Allergic Post-infectious  |
|   |  Tubular necrosis | Progress from pre-renal conditions Nephrotoxins — drugs (aminoglycosides, amphotericin, ifosfamide, cisplatin) Radiocontrast dye  |
|   | Intratubular obstruction | Pigment injury — haemoglobinuria, myoglobinuria Uric acid nephropathy (tumour lysis syndrome) Crystalluria — acyclovir  |
|  Post-renal | Obstructive uropathy | Posterior urethral valve Obstruction of single kidney or bladder Bilateral ureteric obstruction  |

## Distinguishing between Acute Kidney Injury and Chronic Renal Failure

- Features of chronicity include:

Growth failure
Chronic hypertensive retinopathy
Renal osteodystrophy or rickets

- Features such as anaemia, hyperphosphataemia and hypocalcaemia are not distinguishing features as they may occur in AKI.

$$
\text{Estimated GFR} = \frac{\text{Height (cm)} \times 36.5}{\text{serum Creatinine (μmol/L)}}
$$

If previous baseline serum creatinine unavailable, then estimate eGFR as 100 ml/min/1.73 m².

## Management of Acute Kidney Injury

Reverse reversible factors:

- Correct dehydration
Hypotension
- Infection
- Nephrotoxicity
- Obstruction

In most cases, management is conservative while awaiting recovery of renal function. The aim of management is to:

- Correct fluid/electrolyte abnormalities
- Provide adequate nutrition

## Investigations

Full blood count, blood film, reticulocyte count
- Renal panel, serum calcium, serum phosphate, serum magnesium, acid-base assessment
- Serum and urine osmolality, urine sodium, urine creatinine (to differentiate between pre-renal and renal cause)
Urinalysis, urine protein/creatinine ratio, urine culture
- If clinical features suggestive of glomerulonephritis: Serum C3, C4, ANA, dsDNA, ANCA, ASOT
- Chest X-ray (if cardiac or respiratory signs present)
- Renal ultrasound with Doppler studies of renal vessels
- Renal biopsy if indicated

## Fluid Management

Principle:

- If euvolemic, replace Insensible Water Loss (IWL = 400 ml/m²/day) plus urine output.
- If fluid overloaded, aim to decrease body weight by \(0.5 - 1\%\) per day.
- Weight gain or hyponatraemia during this phase indicative of fluid overload.
- Intravenous (IV) frusemide \((1\mathrm{mg / kg / DOSE})\) promotes renal blood flow during the early stage.
- Larger doses of IV frusemide 2 to \(4\mathrm{mg / kg / DOSE}\) over 15 minutes may be required.
- If there is persistent oliguria despite optimisation of medical therapy, renal replacement therapy should be considered.

b4854_Sec-18.indd 616

# Acute Kidney Injury

## Electrolyte Management

### Hyperkalaemia

- Prompt treatment if T wave changes on ECG or serum potassium \(>6.0\) mmol/L
Monitor ECG
- Drugs: Refer to chapter on 'Potassium Disorders'.

### Hyponatraemia

- Results from fluid overload, need to fluid restrict and IV frusemide.
- If renal salt wasting, then for sodium supplementation.
- Na+ deficit = 0.6 × Body Weight (kg) × [(Desired Na+)-(Actual Na+)]
- Correct slowly, raising serum \(\mathrm{Na^{+}}\) by not more than a maximum rate of \(12\mathrm{mmol / L}\) per day.

### Metabolic Acidosis

- Judicious use of sodium bicarbonate.
- Aim for half correction. Formula for calculating bicarbonate replacement \(= 1/3\) \(\times\) body weight \((\mathrm{kg})\times\) base excess.
- Be mindful that bicarbonate replacement will increase \(\mathrm{CO}_{2}\) tension, and an intact respiratory system is needed to eliminate the \(\mathrm{CO}_{2}\) produced.
- Be aware of \(\mathrm{Ca^{2+}}\) levels as the correction of acidosis will cause shift from ionised to non-ionised form.

### Hyperphosphataemia and Hypocalcaemia

- Hyperphosphataemia is managed by dietary phosphate restriction and phosphate binders (calcium carbonate or calcium acetate).
Calcium-based phosphate binder doses:

|  Age group | Frequency | Calcium acetate 667 mg/Tab equivalent | Calcium carbonate 625 mg/Tab equivalent  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  <1 yr | 3 to 4 times a day with meals | ¼ Tab  |   |
|  1 to 6 yr |   | ½ Tab  |   |
|  6 to 12 yr |   | 1 Tab  |   |
|  12 to 18 yr |   | 2 Tab  |   |

- Doses are titrated based on meal frequency and serum phosphate levels.
- Calcium acetate is preferred where there is hypochlorhydria due to either gut immaturity or the use of medications such as proton-pump inhibitors and \(\mathrm{H}_{2}\) receptor antagonists.

### Nutritional Management

- Minimum of \(25\%\) of the daily caloric requirement is necessary to reduce ongoing catabolism.
- May require up to \(25\%\) dextrose, infused via a central line.
Protein is restricted to \(1\mathrm{g / kg / day}\)

b4854_Sec-18.indd 617

12-Dec-22 11:12:02 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

## Hypertension

Refer to chapter on 'Hypertension'.

## Drugs

- Minimise or discontinue nephrotoxic drugs. If necessary, monitor drug levels and side-effects.
- Doses may have to be adjusted in renal impairment.

## Dialysis

### Aim

Maintain fluid, electrolyte and acid–base balance; remove endogenous and exogenous toxins until renal function recovers.

### Indications

The indications are not specific and should be individualised. In children, the dialysis is usually initiated for the indication of fluid overload.

Indications for initiating dialysis include:

- Oliguria/anuria
- Serum urea \(>35 \mathrm{mmol} / \mathrm{L}\)
Hyperkalaemia \(>6.5\mathrm{mmol / L}\) unresponsive to conservative treatment
- Intractable metabolic acidosis (serum bicarbonate \(< 10 \mathrm{mmol} / \mathrm{L}\))
Pulmonary oedema unresponsive to diuretic therapy
- Symptomatic uraemia — encephalopathy, pericarditis (generally when urea \(>50\) mmol/L)
- Toxins

### Modes of Dialysis in AKI

Peritoneal dialysis
Haemodialysis
Continuous venovenous haemodialysis

## Outcome

Three phases:

- Oliguric phase: Lasts few days to 6 weeks
Diuretic phase
- Recovery phase: May last for months

## Mortality

Dependent on cause. Overall, about 30%, and usually if coincident with multi-organ failure.

# CHAPTER 88

# Nocturnal Enuresis

Chao Sing Ming

Nocturnal Enuresis (NE) is a common, distressing and socially disruptive health problem affecting 5 to 10% of children aged 7 to 10 years old. Prevalence of NE reduces with increasing age by about 15% per annum. However, those ≥5 years who still wet their beds on an average of ≥2 times/week should be given the benefit of treatment without unnecessarily prolonging the distress and consequences of enuresis.

While the cause of NE is largely unknown, it has a strong genetic predisposition and is characterised by two important features that form the basis of its treatment: (1) Failure to awake from sleep in response to signalling of a full bladder, and (2) Urine production in excess of the bladder capacity (a mismatch due to either nocturnal polyuria (NP) or a reduced bladder capacity, or both).

There are two major clinical categories of NE according to the International Children's Continence Society (ICCS): monosymptomatic NE (MNE) and non-monosymptomatic NE (NMNE). It is important to differentiate these two categories as they differ in pathogenesis, clinical features and treatment strategy.

Treatment of MNE is more straightforward when compared to NMNE, by using desmopressin or enuresis alarm as monotherapy with a satisfactory treatment outcome. Treatment outcome may be further improved by better patient selection and expanded therapeutic considerations.

## Definitions (ICCS)

### Incontinence

Involuntary leakage of urine. It can be subdivided into continuous, intermittent, daytime incontinence and enuresis. Total loss of control and awareness of voiding is seen in neuropathic bladder. Continuous or constant wetting is seen in rare anatomical abnormalities (e.g. ectopic ureter in girls, epispadias). Daytime incontinence is intermittent incontinence with a small amount of urine while awake, and is due to underlying bladder dysfunction.

620

b4854_Sec-18.indd 620

12-Dec-22 11:12:02 AM

# Nocturnal Enuresis

# Nocturnal Enuresis/bedwetting

Intermittent incontinence while asleep, beyond 5 years of age.

# Primary Nocturnal Enuresis:

Never been dry for more than 6 months.

# Secondary Nocturnal Enuresis:

Been consistently dry for at least 6 months and then bedwetting recurs. Secondary causes (Urinary tract infection [UTI], polyuria) should be excluded.

# Monosymptomatic Nocturnal Enuresis:

NE as an isolated problem without symptoms of bladder or bowel dysfunction or other co-morbidities.

# Non-monosymptomatic Nocturnal Enuresis:

Usually refers to NE with additional daytime symptoms of an overactive bladder (OAB).

# Overactive Bladder:

Frequent micturition, urgency and sometimes urge incontinence. Holding manoeuvres like squatting, crossing legs to stop the urge. Twenty to thirty per cent of patients with NE have daytime symptoms related to an OAB.

# Clinical Assessment

A good focused history and careful examination, followed by a simple urinalysis to screen for urinary abnormalities are all that is required. Imaging studies and urodynamic studies should be reserved for those with a complicated history, e.g. febrile UTI or abnormal clinical findings such as cutaneous stigmata of occult spinal dysraphism.

History taking involves assessing the severity of bedwetting, onset, screening for features of NMNE and suggestion of NP, which will help towards determining the choice and success of treatment (See Figure 18.5 for the algorithm on clinical assessment and a checklist).

Evidence of NP in enuresis includes the wetting (> 2 nights) covering large areas or using a diaper weight that exceeds 100% of the expected bladder capacity for age, as documented by a 4- to 7-day bedwetting diary. Co-morbidities in NMNE include bladder dysfunction, bowel dysfunction, behavioural and developmental disorders, sleep apnoea, which may need specialist referral and be separately addressed. It is also important to ask for the history of febrile UTI, which may signify underlying urinary tract abnormalities.

History is usually straightforward and a physical examination is unremarkable. However, it is important not to miss an unusual case of neuropathic bladder, anatomical abnormalities and polyuria from diabetes mellitus or insipidus.

# Treatment Modalities (See Figure 18.6 for algorithm on treatment guideline for PMNE)

- Desmopressin (DDAVP) and the use of enuresis alarm are two established treatment options that had been found to be effective in clinical trials. Treatment options should be adequately explained (using NE information pamphlets) to parent and patient, guided by individual wetting pattern and choice.
- Daytime problem in NMNE needs to be treated first before enuresis can be successfully treated.
- Anticholinergic like oxybutynin can be useful when NE is associated with OAB.
- Tricyclic antidepressants (imipramine) is not superior to alarm or DDAVP and is not recommended because of its potential fatal side effects when overdosed.

# Nocturnal Enuresis

623

## Desmopressin (DDAVP)

- It is widely accepted that NP, as seen in one-third to two-third MNE, is mainly due to a lack of the normal circadian rhythm of nocturnal increase in anti-diuretic hormone or vasopressin (AVP) production, which helps to concentrate and reduce urine volume during sleep.
- DDAVP, a potent synthetic anti-diuretic analogue of AVP, is used to boost the effect of endogenous AVP and invariably improves NP. However, it does not improve the sleep arousal in response to a full bladder and its reported success rate in the treatment of NE is a moderate \(50 - 70\%\). Relapse rate varies from 30 to \(50\%\).

## Considerations to Improve Outcome when using DDAVP

- Best result in patients with NP.
- To be taken 1 hour before sleep with minimal amount of water (alternatively, use lyophilisate DDAVP, if available), in order for DDAVP to reach maximum urine concentrating capacity.
- Restrict fluid 1-2 hours before and 8 hours (average duration of action) after administration.
- Use of bedwetting diary to monitor treatment and to reinforce compliance.
- Tapered withdrawal may help to avoid relapse.
- Combine DDAVP with alarm training when monotherapy fails. Combination with anticholinergic, which has a separate side effect profile for treating daytime symptoms, should only be considered in specialist clinic.

The most serious reported complication of DDAVP treatment is water intoxication due to excessive water intake, as DDAVP reduces water excretion and urine production. This may cause sudden dilutional hyponatraemia and seizures. Patients and caretakers should be forewarned of this serious adverse effect and omit its use whenever water intake becomes necessary, e.g. in acute gastroenteritis. Minor side effects reported include headache, abdominal pain and aggressiveness. Information pamphlets on the safety and precautions of DDAVP treatment is available and should be given to patients for their reference.

## Enuresis Alarm

- Enuresis alarm training is effective with a good reported success rate of around \(60 - 80\%\) as it addresses the main problem in NE, which is a lack of arousal from sleep in response to signalling from a full bladder.
- It has a reported relapse rate of \(15\%\) but retreatment is often effective. However, its use can be cumbersome and can be trying in the initial period of training.
- Enuresis alarm training has been found to be particularly effective and showed early results in cases of severe bedwetting due to the intensive training involved from frequent bedwetting.

# The Baby Bear Book

- Proper method of using the alarm also helps to ensure good results. Referral to an occupational therapist has been found to be useful for adherence to the proper method by the patient and caretaker.
- When compared with DDAVP, enuresis alarm training has a more sustained effect when off treatment.

# CHAPTER 89

# Renal Reference Limits

Chong Siew Le

# Urine Excretion Indices for Calcium, Urate and Oxalate

## Notes

1. Measurement of urine calcium, urate and oxalate is part of the metabolic work up of patients with renal calculi and nephrocalcinosis.
2. High urinary excretion of urine calcium and uric acid have also been found in children with microscopic or gross haematuria.
3. Urinary calcium, uric acid and oxalate excretion are affected by various factors, including age, dietary intake and fluid intake.
4. 24 hours' urine sample has been widely regarded as gold standard for the diagnosis of metabolic abnormalities. However, it is challenging to obtain a proper 24-hour urine specimen in young children, particularly amongst those who are not toilet trained. Hence, spot (random) urine sample for calcium, urate and oxalate to creatinine ratio can be used as a screening test.
5. Spot urine sample for calcium, urate and oxalate to creatinine ratio (consider urine calcium/osmolality for patient with decreased muscle mass), together with other metabolic work up (serum creatinine, sodium, potassium, chloride, bicarbonate, calcium, albumin, uric acid, phosphate, magnesium and urine amino acids), should be routinely performed for patients with renal calculi and nephrocalcinosis. Abnormal spot urine screening tests or metabolic tests should be further evaluated by a timed urine collection where feasible.
6. The urinary reference limits (95th percentile [upper limit]) for calcium/ creatinine, calcium/osmolality, urate/creatinine, urate/creatinine, and 24-hour urine calcium, urate and oxalate is detailed in the table below.

# CHAPTER 90

# Headaches and Migraines

Lim Yi Xiu, Jocelyn; Yeo Tong Hong

## Introduction

Headaches are common in children and adolescents, with a worldwide prevalence of 60% under 20 years. Females are 1.5 times more likely to get headaches than males. Migraines occur in approximately 8% of these children and adolescents, with a higher incidence in those above 14 years. They are a significant cause of recurrent hospital visits and school absences.

While prevalence is high, most are due to benign causes, most commonly caused by upper respiratory tract infections and primary headaches. Acute headaches with progressive symptoms raise the suspicion of intracranial pathology.

## Primary Headache

Primary headaches disorders in children can be broadly categorised into:

- Migraines (with or without aura)
- Complicated migraines (hemiplegic, brainstem, retinal)
- Episodic migraine variants (cyclical vomiting, paroxysmal vertigo, paroxysmal torticollis, abdominal)
Tension type headaches (episodic or chronic)

Most are typically tension-type headaches and migraines without aura; the two can frequently co-occur.

## Secondary Headache

Secondary headaches are most commonly caused by head and neck or systemic conditions (see Table 19.3).

## Approach to Headache Evaluation

A comprehensive history and directed clinical neurologic examination are the most sensitive in determining underlying aetiology.

Document onset, frequency, time of day, nature of pain, site, duration and severity (impairment of activities more sensitive than pain score). Ask about triggers, relieving factors, prodrome and associated symptoms.

- Note: Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS), vital signs, cognition, head circumference
- Complete neurological evaluation, in particular:

» Signs of meningism

» Focal neurological deficits
» Gait abnormalities, ataxia, incoordination
» Speech abnormalities (quality and quantity)
» Abnormal movements — dyskinesia, catatonia
- Directed systemic examination depending on presenting symptoms

## Warning Signs

Neuroimaging may be indicated if there is/are:

- signs and symptoms of raised intracranial pressure, including papilloedema
- change in cognition/behaviour or deteriorating school performance
acute onset, severe or progressive headaches
early morning vomiting
- headache waking patient up from sleep
- abnormal neurological findings

## Management Goals for Primary Headaches

- Eliminate secondary contributory causes — treat allergic rhinitis, sinusitis (imaging only indicated if symptomatic), myopia, dental disorders.
- Manage expectations: Response takes time, triggers are often multi-factorial, including psychological stressors.
- Focus on coping mechanisms and optimising function and daily activities rather than a direct reduction in pain score.

# Headaches and Migraines

## Non-Pharmacological Interventions

- Lifestyle measures: Good sleep hygiene (keeping bedtime constant even during weekends and holidays), attention to routine, adequate hydration, regular meals and physical activity.
- Empower coping: A headache diary helps to identify triggers and aggravating factors for interventions (e.g. short nap, quick shower, dark/quiet room), helps assess need for prophylactic therapy and its efficacy.
- Education: Understanding natural course, specific headache characteristics, aggravating and relieving factors, medication use/effect.
- Psychological/psychiatric/medical social worker referral if concomitant anxiety, depression, challenging family or school circumstances.
- Cognitive behavioural therapy, relaxation and/or distraction techniques.

## Pharmacological Treatments

### Acute Symptom Relief

- Use when severe or persistent with restriction of daily activities, despite non-pharmacological measures.
- Consider paracetamol, ibuprofen or naproxen, sumatriptan (age >12 years)
  i. Treatment is more likely to be effective when used earlier within the first hour.
  ii. Treatment is more effective in pain relief than associated symptoms like nausea/vomiting; antiemetics can be prescribed in addition.
  iii. Treatment can be repeated once within a 24-hour period if it is effective but pain recurs.
- Avoid medication overuse: limit pain relievers to ≤2–3 times a week (<15 days/month of paracetamol/NSAIDS, <10 days/month of triptans)

### Prophylactic Medication

Migraine prophylaxis aims to reduce the frequency and intensity of attacks and should be considered when there are more than four attacks per month, when there is poor response to symptomatic treatment and daily activities are significantly affected.

Many different classes of drugs have been used including anti-epileptics, antihistamines, anti-depressants, calcium channel blockers, beta blockers and alpha-agonists. However, there is a lack of definitive evidence for efficacy. The placebo effect is also significant in children (~60% of patients).

The decision to start prophylaxis should be made together with the neurologist, parent and child, taking into consideration psychosocial factors, medication side effects and non-pharmacological interventions. It is inappropriate to continue prophylaxis long term once attacks subside.

# CHAPTER 91

# Seizures and Epilepsy

Ngoh Seow Fen, Adeline; Lim Yi Xiu, Jocelyn; Chan Wei Shih, Derrick

## Seizures

### Definition

A seizure is a transient occurrence of signs and/or symptoms due to abnormal excessive or synchronous neuronal activity in the brain. A seizure is a symptom, not a diagnosis.

### Acute Symptomatic Seizures

Seizures that have an obvious and immediate preceding cause. Common causes include:

- Head trauma
- Electrolyte abnormalities (hypoglycaemia, hyponatraemia, hypocalcaemia)
Central nervous system (CNS) infections (meningoencephalitis, abscesses)
Structural lesions (tumours, bleeds)
- Ischaemic stroke

These are often isolated events and do not recur when the cause is removed.

### Febrile Seizures

Seizures occurring in:

Children aged 6 months to 6 years old with
- Temperature \(>38^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\) (child may only be febrile after the seizure) and
- Absence of CNS infection

Febrile seizures:

- Can be simple: Lasting \(< 15\) minutes long, single episode in 24 hours and generalised
- OR complicated (complex): Lasting \(>15\) minutes long, multiple episodes in 24 hours and focal

Management is mostly symptomatic and supportive:

- Identify source of infection and treat accordingly.
- Antipyretics for comfort care. This does not prevent/reduce febrile seizure recurrence.
- Provide Febrile Seizure patient information leaflet.
- Discharge when well with advice for management of acute seizure.
- Consider standby rectal diazepam if febrile seizures are recurrent and/or prolonged in children \(>1\) year old and \(>10\mathrm{kg}\).

637

b4854_Sec-19.indd 637

12-Dec-22 11:12:41 AM

### Provoked Seizures

Seizures in the context of intercurrent illness (usually gastroenteritis or upper respiratory tract infections) and absence of fever in the preceding 24 hours. Majority do not require treatment. Acute treatment with benzodiazepines and/or phenytoin/phenobarbitone is occasionally required if seizures are recurrent within 24 hours or are prolonged.

**Status epilepticus (SE):** >30 minutes of continuous seizure activity or multiple seizures without intervening recovery of consciousness. A functional definition of status epilepticus is a seizure persisting beyond 5 to 10 minutes. See Figure 19.1 for the clinical pathway of status epilepticus management.

### Epilepsy

In children with no acute cause or triggering factor, a first unprovoked seizure is said to have occurred. Two or more unprovoked seizures at least 24 hours apart constitutes a working diagnosis of epilepsy. In children with significant risk factors such as an abnormal electroencephalogram (EEG) or neuroimaging, epilepsy can also be diagnosed after one unprovoked seizure.

Epilepsy is a disorder of the brain characterised by an enduring predisposition to generate epileptic seizures and by the neurobiological, cognitive, psychological and social consequences of this condition.

### Classification of Seizures

The International League Against Epilepsy (ILAE) Seizure Classification was first presented in 1981, dividing seizure types into generalised and partial, with consciousness as a sub-divider. Since then, it has undergone several revisions, with the latest modification in 2017. This revised framework seeks to be more operational and has three non-hierarchical levels of diagnosis:

- Seizure type
- Epilepsy type (focal, generalised, combined generalised and focal, unknown)
- Epilepsy syndrome

**Focal seizures** — originate within networks in one hemisphere of the brain

- Awareness refers to perception or knowledge of self and environment during a seizure, may be left unspecified if unclear from history.
- Motor onset is subdivided into:
  - Automatisms — repetitive motor activity, e.g. lip smacking or chewing, finger tapping or fumbling
  - Atonic — loss of tone
  - Clonic — rhythmic regular jerking of the limbs, body
  - Epileptic spasms — sudden flexion/extension/mixed movements of predominantly proximal and truncal muscles. More sustained than myoclonus but shorter than tonic movements

Hyperkinetic - e.g. pedalling, thrashing
Myoclonic - irregular jerking typically of arms or legs but can occur in eyelids, body
Tonic - stiffening of limbs/body

- Non-motor onset is subdivided into:

Autonomic - cardiovascular, vasomotor, thermoregulatory
Behavioural arrest
Cognitive - impaired language, déjà vu, hallucinations, illusions
Emotional - fear, joy, anxiety or other affective appearances without subjective emotions
Sensory — somatosensory (tingling, numbness), visual (flickering lights, scotoma), auditory (buzzing), olfactory (unpleasant odour), gustatory (bitter, acidic taste), epigastric (discomfort, churning, nausea)

**Generalised seizures** — seizures that rapidly spread throughout bilateral networks.

- Motor onset is subdivided into the above plus:

Myoclonic-tonic-clonic
Myoclonic-atonic

- Absence seizures can be divided into:

Typical - staring with brief loss of consciousness, accompanied by eyelid fluttering and automatisms like lip-smacking or chewing. Lasts \(< 1\) min, many times daily; triggered by hyperventilation.
Atypical - slow onset, marked changes in tone
Myoclonic
Eyelid myoclonia forced upward jerking of eyelids during an absence seizure

A 'drop attack' is a descriptive term but not a specific seizure type. Patients typically have sudden stiffening or jerking of the body, resulting in a forceful fall to the ground. This can be a spasm or a tonic or myoclonic seizure.

- Structural

Hippocampal sclerosis
Brain tumours
Malformations of cortical development

## Epilepsy Syndromes

A complex of signs and symptoms, investigation findings (EEG, magnetic resonance imaging [MRI]), treatment response and prognosis that define a unique epileptic condition.

### 1. Idiopathic Generalised Epilepsies (IGE)

- Consists of either one or a combination of: Absence, myoclonic, tonic-clonic and myotonic-tonic-clonic; these may have asymmetric features.
- Normal development but may have associated attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), learning and mood disorders.
- EEG shows classical generalised spike-wave discharges accentuated in sleep.
Usually anticonvulsant-responsive with \(80\%\) responding to appropriate anti-epileptic drugs (AED).

#### A. Childhood absence epilepsy (CAE)

- School-age children aged 4 to 10 years (peak 6–7 years), \(2 - 8\%\) of all cases of epilepsy (if older age onset, consider juvenile absence epilepsy).
- Multiple daily absence seizures: Brief (<1 minute) staring and unresponsiveness, behavioural arrest, sometimes with eyelid fluttering, oral or manual automatisms with immediate return to normal activity afterwards.
- Induced by hyperventilation (3 to 5 minutes).
- Treatment with ethosuximide or sodium valproate. Made worse by carbamazepine.
- \(60\%\) remits by early adolescence, others may evolve to other IGE syndromes.

#### B. Juvenile myoclonic epilepsy (JME)

- Commonest adolescent and adult onset IGE, typical age range 10 to 24 years (peak 12–18 years).
- Early morning sudden myoclonic jerks ('clumsiness'), generalised tonic-clonic seizures. \(33\%\) have absence seizure.
- Sleep deprivation worsens myoclonus.
- Interictal EEG — Generalised polyspike and spike-and-wave discharges at 4–6 Hz; photosensitivity is common.
- Valproate usually controls seizures but treatment may be lifelong.
- Carbamazepine and phenytoin aggravate myoclonic and absence seizures.

b4854_Sec-19.indd 641

12-Dec-22 11:12:43 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

C. Juvenile absence epilepsy (JAE)

- Characterised by absence seizures with onset from 8 to 20 years of age (peak 9 to 13 years)
- May also have generalised tonic-clonic seizures (80%). Less commonly, adolescents may present with generalised tonic-clonic seizures before the onset of absences
- Interictal EEG — Generalised polyspike and spike-and-wave discharges at 4–6 Hz (clinical absences can be provoked by hyperventilation)
- Valproate usually controls seizures but treatment may be lifelong. Ethosuximide can help with absence seizures but does not protect against generalised tonic clonic seizures
- Carbamazepine and phenytoin can exacerbate seizures

D. Generalised tonic-clonic seizures alone (GTCA)

- Typical age onset: 10 to 25 years, with \(80\%\) having their first GTC in the second decade.
- Generalised tonic-clonic seizures, other seizure types are exclusionary.
- Seizures are infrequent, usually yearly or less.
- EEG: Generalised spike or polyspike waves \(>3 - 3.5\mathrm{Hz}\), \(50\%\) only showing in sleep.
- Responsive to sodium valproate but treatment may be lifelong.

# 2. Self-limited Focal Epilepsies

- Accounts for up to \(25\%\) of paediatric epilepsies.
Normal birth and development
- Behavioural and neuropsychological deficits may occur during the course of active epilepsy but usually improve after seizures remit
- No significant structural lesion on neuroimaging.
Usually drug-responsive, remission occurs by adolescence.
- Seizures are usually focal motor or sensory, with or without impaired awareness; may sometimes evolve to bilateral tonic-clonic seizures.

A. Rolandic epilepsy/Benign epilepsy with centrotemporal spikes (BECTS)

- Estimated \(15\%\) of all childhood epilepsies; age of onset 5 to 10 years.
- Typically, infrequent seizures in sleep, sometimes awake/asleep, rarely only when awake.
- Simple partial seizures of the face, oropharynx and upper limb, at times with secondary generalisation.
EEG: Centrotemporal spikes
- Treatment: e.g. sodium valproate indicated if frequent seizures or cognitive impairments.
Excellent prognosis with remission by mid-teenage years.

b4854_Sec-19.indd 642

12-Dec-22 11:12:43 AM

# Seizures and Epilepsy

643

B. Panayiotopoulos Syndrome/Early onset occipital epilepsy

- Accounts for \(5\%\) of all childhood epilepsies; age range 1 to 14 years with \(70\%\) between 3 and 6 years old.
- Infrequent focal autonomic seizures with or without impaired awareness. Vomiting is the commonest feature (often mistaken for gastroenteritis), often associated with pallor, flushing, malaise, nausea and abdominal pain. Can evolve to eye/head version, hemiclonic or bilateral tonic-clonic seizures.
- \(70\%\) occur in sleep, seizures are often prolonged, lasting \(>30\) minutes.
- EEG: Multifocal spike or sharp-slow discharges over posterior regions
- Self-limited, with most seizures resolving within 3 years of onset

C. Childhood occipital epilepsy (Gastaut type)

- Onset of seizures between 15 months and 19 months of age, with peak onset between 8 and 9 years of age (slightly later than Panayiotopoulos Syndrome)
- Focal sensory visual seizures occur. These have rapid onset and are usually brief (typically lasting only seconds or \(< 3\) minutes, rarely do they last up to 20 minutes).
Typically, visual phenomena are described as small multi-coloured circles in the peripheral vision
- Other occipital lobe seizure features include ictal blindness, visual hallucinations or orbital pain
- Seizures can spread outside the occipital lobe, resulting in hemiparaesthesia, or evolution into hemiclonic or generalised tonic clonic seizures
- Seizures are usually easily controlled, especially with carbamazepine
- EEG: Occipital spike or spike and wave is seen in the interictal EEG
- Remission occurs within 2-4 years in \(50 - 60\%\) of patients

### 3. West Syndrome

- Epileptic encephalopathy of infancy (seizures are so severe that the brain function and development are affected)
- Incidence: 1 in 4000-6000 live births
- Peak frequency: 3 to 7 months of age, \(93\%\) before 2 years
- Epileptic spasms (flexor, extensor, mixed) can be asymmetric or have focal features, e.g. tonic eye or head deviation. Cognition and developmental deterioration if untreated.
- EEG: Hypsarrhythmia (disorganised generalised high amplitude sharp and slow waves) or modified hypsarrhythmia
Diagnostic work up: Awake and sleep EEG, brain magnetic resonance imaging/ spectroscopy, neurometabolic work up \(\pm\) genetic testing
- Prognosis: Guarded apart from idiopathic infantile spasm. \(75 - 90\%\) have developmental delay. Risk of developing Lennox-Gastaut syndrome.
Treatment:

Oral prednisolone/Adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)
Vigabatrin
Ketogenic diet
Other medication, e.g. Topiramate, Pyridoxine

# The Baby Bear Book

» Surgical treatment for underlying focal cortical dysplasias

# 4. Lennox-Gastaut syndrome

Age of onset: 1 to 14 years
- Severe epileptic disorder with triad of:
Multiple seizure types, typically tonic, atypical absence myoclonic and tonic-clonic seizures
EEG: Diffuse, slow spike-and-wave 1-2.5 Hz complexes, slow background
Diffuse and severe cognitive impairments, learning disability
- Status epilepticus common, especially non-convulsive forms
- Aetiology: Many causes, about \(25\%\) are cryptogenic
- Poor prognosis for seizure control and mental development
Treatment:
\(\gg\) Medication: Sodium valproate, benzodiazepines, e.g. clobazam/nitrazepam, topiramate, but seizures are commonly refractory to medication
\(\gg\) Surgery: Corpus callosotomy for drop attacks, Vagus Nerve Stimulator

# Approach

- Determine whether patient does or does not have seizures (seizures vs. non-seizures)
- Determine the seizure type or syndrome
- Determine aetiology where possible
Assess co-morbidity
- Formulate a management plan

# History

- Careful and detailed history is essential
- Clear description of semiology, pre- and post-ictal symptoms and duration, frequency of episodes
- Past medical history, including recent trauma and illness, associated symptoms, long-term medications
Developmental history
Social history
Family history

# Neurological and Physical Examination

# Investigations

- Electroencephalography (EEG)
Value: May help to localise seizure origin and assist in syndromic classification.
\(\gg\) Limitations: Cannot be used to make or exclude a diagnosis of epilepsy (epilepsy is a clinical diagnosis); easily affected by drugs, toxic or metabolic changes; does not elucidate cause.

☐ First EEG can be normal in 50% of persons with epilepsy.

b4854_Sec-19.indd 644

12-Dec-22 11:12:43 AM

# Seizures and Epilepsy

☐ A small percentage of normal children have epileptiform activity on EEG but never a seizure.

- Neuroimaging — computed tomography (CT) if emergency, MRI preferred

Indicated in focal epilepsies, neurological deficits, young age, difficult-to-control seizures
Not required in Rolandic epilepsy, childhood absence epilepsy

- Laboratory tests, lumbar puncture as indicated to elucidate genetic/metabolic/immune causes

## Management

- Making a diagnosis of epilepsy is not necessarily the same as a decision to treat. Some seizures are minor and infrequent, and parents may choose to avoid the side effects of medication. Each treatment decision should be individualised between clinician and caregiver/patient.
Aims: Stop or reduce seizures, maintain quality of life.

Establish firm diagnosis of epilepsy before starting treatment.
Consider seizure type, severity, recurrence risk, epilepsy syndrome, aetiology, co-morbidity and precipitants.
> Mainstay of treatment — anti-epileptic drugs (AEDs).

- Indications to treat:

Recurrent frequent seizures (every few months or more), severity (tendency for status epilepticus, risk of injury), specific epilepsy syndromes.
Presence of risk factors (e.g. epileptiform discharges on EEG).
Accompanying neurological, neuropsychological or psychosocial problems.

- Initiate monotherapy using the appropriate medication, titrating up to a maintenance dose.

Balance between drug efficacy and tolerability.
Holistic approach — psychosocial management and epilepsy support are important.
Refer for epilepsy counselling by pharmacy and specialty nurse; acute seizure advice \(\pm\) standby rectal diazepam.

Duration of therapy

- Syndrome diagnosis and treatment response are important considerations, with a minimum of 2 years in self-limiting epilepsy syndromes.

- Fifty per cent will respond to first anticonvulsant; ~20% to the second, the rest tend to be refractory and non-pharmacological treatments, such as epilepsy surgery, ketogenic diet or vagal nerve stimulation, etc., should be considered

# CHAPTER 92

# Encephalopathy and Stroke

Lim Yi Xiu, Jocelyn; Terrence Thomas

## Introduction

- Children with acute central nervous system (CNS) syndromes commonly present with acute encephalopathy. This may include a depression in consciousness (lethargy and drowsiness) or agitation and confusion.
In infants and younger children, ask caregivers about a deviation from baseline activity.
- Look for focal manifestations: Seizures, motor weakness, sensory and autonomic deficits, speech abnormalities and visual disturbances.
Systemic symptoms like fever, vomiting and decreased appetite may be present.
- Although stroke typically presents as a new onset neurological deficit (usually hemiplegia with/without aphasia) without encephalopathy, the general principles in assessment, stabilisation, time to neuroimaging and initial treatments are similar to a child with new onset encephalopathy.

## Aetiologies

Table 19.6 Aetiologies of Acute Central Nervous Syndromes in Children

(Continued)

- Assess Airway, Breathing, Circulation with a set of vital signs: Heart rate, respiratory rate, blood pressure, oxygen saturation; do point-of-care test for electrolytes and blood glucose

» Consider Children's Intensive Care Unit (CICU) admission if GCS <9 or rapidly deteriorating
In those with GCS 14-15, perform a mini-mental state exam (see Tables 19.7 and 19.8)

# The Baby Bear Book

- Signs of meningism: Bulging fontanelle in an infant, nuchal rigidity or positive Kernig's sign in older children.
- Signs of raised intracranial pressure: Papilloedema, falling coma score, altered breathing patterns, abnormal pupil responses, Cushing's reflex with hypertension and bradycardia, decerebrate and decorticate posturing.
- Seizures may be subtle, e.g. tonic eye deviation, tonic posturing or minor clonic movements of the face or limbs.
- Observe for dyskinesia: Dystonia (oromotor or limb; ophisthotonus), chorea or motor stereotypies. Hyperkinetic (agitation) or negative (posturing) catatonic features may be present.

## Investigations

Discuss investigations with neurologists. They should be tailored to each individual child based on history and examination findings.

## Recommended Tests

- Full blood count, renal panel, liver function tests, electrolytes (calcium, magnesium, phosphate), erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR), C-reactive protein (CRP), plasma glucose, ammonia, thyroid function tests (TFT)
- Microbiological studies: Blood cultures, respiratory pathogen multiplex PCR, and mycoplasma throat swab/serology, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis (opening pressure, microscopy, culture, meningitis/encephalitis pathogen multiplex PCR)
- Immunological studies: Serum complements C3/C4, antinuclear antibody (ANA), double-stranded DNA (dsDNA), anti-thyroglobulin, anti-thyroperoxidase (TPO) antibodies (TFT can be normal) and anti-myelin oligodendrocyte glycoprotein (MOG) antibodies; paired CSF-plasma oligoclonal bands and anti-N-methyl-d-aspartate receptor (NMDAR) (\(\pm\) autoimmune encephalitis panel) antibodies; CSF blast cells.
- Genetic/metabolic investigations: Acylcarnitine profile, serum copper and caeruloplasmin; paired CSF and plasma lactate, amino acids; urine organic acids
- Electroencephalogram (EEG) may be considered if subclinical seizures are suspected

## Neuroimaging

- Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) brain with contrast is the preferred imaging modality. Add on MR Angiography or Venography if clinical suspicion of stroke as a cause of encephalopathy.
- Computed tomography (CT) brain helpful to exclude neurosurgical emergencies such as a space-occupying lesion or an intracranial bleed.

If MRI Brain demonstrates Arterial Ischaemic Stroke, add on the following investigations:

- Thrombophilia screen: Prothrombin Time (PT), Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time (APTT), International Normalised Ratio (INR), Protein C, Protein S, anti-

b4854_Sec-19.indd 650

12-Dec-22 11:12:43 AM

# Encephalopathy and Stroke

651

thrombin III, Factor V Leiden, lupus anti-coagulant, anti-cardiolipin, serum homocysteine level. (Optional further tests: MTHFR [methylenetetrahydrofolate reductase], lipoprotein A, Prothrombin gene mutations)

- Evaluate for systemic vasculitides (consult Rheumatologist), e.g. Takayasu disease, anti-neutrophil cytoplasmic autoantibody (ANCA) vasculitides
- Cardiac assessment: Electrocardiogram (ECG) and 2D echocardiogram (with bubble study); carotid Doppler study.

## Management

If child is ill, escalate care to High Dependency Unit or CICU

### Supportive Care

1. Keep nil by mouth (NBM), minimise handling.
2. Maintain general homeostasis — normothermia, normal oxygen saturation and partial pressure of carbon dioxide (pCO2), glucose and electrolytes, heart rate and respiratory rate.
3. Maintain cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP) — adequate hydration with full maintenance intravenous (IV) fluids and normal blood pressure.
4. Elective intubation for cerebral protection if GCS \(< 8\) or rapidly dropping.
5. If agitated, consider IV lorazepam 0.05 to \(0.1\mathrm{mg / kg}\) dose Q8-12H (IV midazolam and/or morphine infusion if intubated).

### Seizure Management

- As per Institution's Status Epilepticus protocol.
- Aim to stop all subclinical seizures.

### Antimicrobials (see Hospital Antimicrobial guidelines)

IV ceftriaxone \(100\mathrm{mg / kg}\) Q24H
IV acyclovir \(10\mathrm{mg / kg}\) dose Q8H
IV ciprofloxacin \(10\mathrm{mg / kg}\) Q8H (if suspected mycoplasma or rickettsial infection)

### Immunotherapy (in immune encephalitis, demyelination or CNS vasculitis;

consult Neurologist)

IV methylprednisolone \(30\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) (max. 1 g) for 5 days \(\pm\) oral (PO) prednisolone taper
IV Immunoglobulins (IVIG) \(2\mathrm{g / kg}\) over 2-5 days
IV omeprazole \(1\mathrm{mg / kg}\) Q12H or IV ranitidine \(1\mathrm{mg / kg}\) Q8H or similar as gastric protection, if IV steroids are given.
- If active gastrointestinal bleed, may reduce IV methylprednisolone to \(20\mathrm{mg / kg}/\) day (max. 1 g) or split into \(10\mathrm{mg / kg}\) Q12H or Q8H doses

### Tone Management (consult Neurologist)

- Either IV or PO clonidine, PO baclofen or benzhexol

# The Baby Bear Book

## Neurorehabilitation

- Physiotherapy, occupational therapy, speech and language therapy $\pm$ music therapy should be instituted as early as appropriate

## Anticoagulation (If Arterial Ischaemic Stroke or Cerebral Sinovenous Thrombosis seen on MR imaging)

- Consult Haematologist, Neurologist $\pm$ Cardiologist (if cardiac disease present) for decision to anticoagulate.

## General Principles (acute stroke period):

- Perform lumbar puncture for CSF studies prior to starting anticoagulation, if possible.
- Ensure no contraindications to anticoagulation.
- If carotid or proximal cerebral artery occlusion, cardiac aetiology or cerebral sinovenous thrombosis:

- Start with IV Standard (unfractionated heparin) or subcutaneous (SC) Enoxaparin [Follow current Institution Guidelines for anticoagulation]

- Aim for APTT 60–80 ms (standard heparin) or factor Xa 0.3–0.7 $\mu$/ml (for both agents).

- If cerebral arterial malformations, e.g. Moya-moya syndrome, or small vessel focal cerebral arteriopathy: Start PO Aspirin 3–5 mg/kg/day.

## Bibliography

1. Lim YXJ, Kwek SY, How CH, et al. (2020) A clinical approach to encephalopathy in children. *Sing Med J* 61(12): 626-632.

4. Kossorotoff M, Chabrier S, Dinomais M. (2019) Arterial ischemic stroke in non-neonate children: Diagnostic and therapeutic specificities. *Neuropediatrics* 176(1-2):20-29.

# CHAPTER 93

# Acute Flaccid Paralysis

Lim Yi Xiu, Jocelyn; Simon Ling

## Introduction

Acute flaccid paralysis (AFP) is a clinical syndrome characterised by acute rapid onset of weakness and the lack of corticospinal tract signs. Flaccid suggests a picture of hypotonia and hyporeflexia, but the term indicates absence of spasticity, hyperreflexia and extensor plantar responses (i.e. the corticospinal tract signs) on examination. When referring to degree of weakness, 'paresis' generally indicates milder loss of motor strength and 'plegia' or 'paralysis' indicates severe loss of strength.

## Aetiologies

AFP can be caused by a wide variety of conditions affecting the neuro-axis. Rather than detailing each of these, this chapter will give an overview of the clinical approach and reasoning. AFP in children can be a challenging neurologic emergency and some can progress rapidly to involve life-threatening bulbar or respiratory muscle weakness. Thus, an accurate diagnosis of the cause and timely appropriate investigation have implications for treatment and outcome. A logical approach focusing on the history, clinical examination and investigations helps to narrow the differential.

## Approach

- First establish the presence of neurologic muscular weakness.

Gait abnormality or inability to walk may be due to various causes, including joint pain, myalgia, incoordination or dyskinesia.
Young pre-verbal children may present with non-specific symptoms such as irritability, poor feeding or refusal to walk.
History, observation (bedside, in the ward) and neurological examination will determine weakness.

**Detailed History**

- Onset and progression of weakness

Hyperacute (within hours) — vascular (haemorrhagic or ischaemic)/traumatic/compressive
Acute (hours to days) — infective/inflammatory

653

# The Baby Bear Book

☐ Subacute to chronic (days to weeks) — infective/inflammatory/infiltrative

» Pattern/distribution of weakness

□ Symmetrical versus patchy
Proximal versus distal
Cervical versus thoracolumbar or lumbosacral spinal pattern
Cranial nerve involvement — ocular, facial, bulbar

» Associated symptoms

□ Sensory deficits — sensory spinal level, paraesthesia/numbness (dermatomal versus distal)
Pain — muscle, joint, neuropathic
□ Autonomic features — urinary retention, constipation, temperature dysregulation, cardiac rhythm abnormalities, labile blood pressure
Central nervous system (CNS) symptoms — headache, encephalopathy, vomiting
Systemic features — fever, rash, oral ulcers

» Recent exposures

Illnesses, trauma, drug/toxins

» Existing systemic diseases and/or long-term medications

# Clinical Examination

» Assess patient stability and vital signs — temperature, heart rate and rhythm, blood pressure, respiratory status.
Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) status, cognition
Full neurological examination — motor, sensory, cranial nerves with the aim of localising the lesion.

☐ Corticospinal tract signs are typically absent in the initial spinal shock stage, so acute spinal cord lesions can present as flaccid paralysis before upper motor neuron signs evolve.

» Examine for palpable bladder, anal tone, abdominal reflexes.
» Examine the back for step deformity, muscle tenderness.
Systemic features as guided by history.

# - Investigations as Determined by Clinical Features

» Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) spine with contrast
» Lumbar puncture
» Cerebral spinal fluid analysis: Microscopy, culture, infective panel (e.g. Biofires)
» Infective and autoimmune screen
» Antibodies: Anti-AQP4 antibody (Ab) (Neuromyelitis optica), anti-myelin oligodendrocyte glycoprotein (MOG) Ab, Antiganglioside Ab (Guillain–Barré Syndrome [GBS] and its variants)
» Consider nerve conduction studies if suspicious of neuromuscular junction disorders (e.g. botulism or myasthenia gravis) or radiculoneuropathies (e.g. GBS)

# Acute Flaccid Paralysis

» AFP surveillance and reporting is required by the Ministry of Health (MOH) in Singapore to ensure continual eradication of polio

Stool samples for enterovirus

2

## Management

Treat a child with AFP as a neurological emergency.

## Supportive

- Monitor vital signs, cardiac monitoring, if necessary.
- Respiratory and nutritional support if there is respiratory or bulbar weakness.
- Continued serial examination for disease progression.
Urinary catheterisation and urology referral as indicated.
- Manage constipation if present.

Table 19.9 Differential Diagnoses in a Child with AFP

|  Site of Lesion | Disorder/Diagnosis  |
| --- | --- |
|  Spinal cord | - Compressive myelopathy (e.g. trauma, epidural abscess, haematoma, tumour) - Transverse myelitis or other inflammatory demyelinating disorder - Acute flaccid myelitis - Anterior spinal artery infarct (or other vascular ischaemia) - Fibrocartilaginous embolism  |
|  Anterior horn cell | - Viral myelitis affecting grey matter - Polio, enterovirus 71, enterovirus D68, West Nile, Japanese encephalitis  |
|  Nerve roots and peripheral nerves | - Guillain–Barré syndrome - Infections (Lyme, diphtheria) - Metabolic (porphyria) - Traumatic nerve injury  |
|  Neuromuscular junction | - Myasthenia gravis - Infant botulism - Toxicity/poisoning  |
|  Muscle | - Viral myositis - Inflammatory myopathy - Hypokalaemic periodic paralysis  |

## Specific (depending on the cause):

- Timely neurosurgical referral and intervention if traumatic, vascular or compressive cause.
- Immunotherapy:

- Guillain–Barré syndrome: Intravenous Immunoglobulin (IVIG) 2 g/kg over 2 days or plasma exchange (IVIG is first line because of the ease of administration; both are equally efficacious)
