# CHAPTER 94

# Cerebral Palsy

Ng Zhi Min; Yeo Tong Hong; Arjandas Mahadev; Choong Chew Thye

## Introduction

- Cerebral Palsy (CP) describes a group of permanent, though not unchanging, disorders of movement, posture and motor function causing activity limitation that are attributed to non-progressive disturbances occurring in the developing foetal or infant brain. These are often accompanied by dysfunction in sensation, perception, cognition, communication and behaviour, epilepsy and secondary musculoskeletal problems.
- Cerebral palsy remains a clinical diagnosis. Evidence of motor dysfunction must be present; clinical findings and symptoms may evolve over time. Most patients exhibit symptoms as infants or toddlers.
- Incidence: Estimated 2–2.5 per 1000 live births, highest for babies before \(1500\mathrm{g}\) and before the 28th week of gestation.

## Causes and Risk Factors

Most cases are believed to be due to prenatal factors, with prematurity and low birth weight as significant risk factors. More than one aetiological factor is often identified.

Table 19.10 Causes and Risk Factors of Cerebral Palsy

|  Prenatal | Perinatal | Postnatal  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  • Infections • Vascular pathology • Placental insufficiency • Genetic factors • Maternal metabolic disturbances • Intrauterine exposure to toxins • Developmental brain lesions | • Prematurity • Infections • Foetal/neonatal stroke • Hypoxia-ischaemia | • Brain infections • Hypoxia-ischaemia • Toxic or metabolic encephalopathy, e.g. kernicterus, heavy metals • Traumatic brain injury- accidental or non-accidental • Seizures • Stroke  |

657

b4854_Sec-19.indd 657

12-Dec-22 11:12:43 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

## Classification

Different classification systems for CP serve different functions. Classification based on clinical findings is most widely used. It may be classified according to the extremities involved and/or characteristics of neurologic dysfunction.

# 1. Spastic CP (70%).

At least two of:

Abnormal pattern of posture and/or movement
» Increased tone (not necessarily constantly)
» Pathological reflexes (hyperreflexia or abnormal Babinski response)

- Quadriplegia: All four extremities equally involved
- Diplegia: Legs more affected than arms
- Hemiplegia: One-sided, arm more affected than leg
- Monoplegia

# 2. Dyskinetic CP (10%)

» Involvement of basal ganglia and extra-pyramidal tracts, characterised by an abnormal pattern of posture and/or movement.
» Involuntary, uncontrolled, recurring, occasionally stereotyped movements of affected body parts.

- Choreo-athetoid: Dominated by both chorea and athetosis
- Dystonic: Dominated by involuntary movements and sustained abnormal posture

# 3. Ataxic CP (10%)

# 4. Mixed CP (10%)

In the evaluation of the severity of motor disorders in CP, the Gross Motor Function Classification System (GMFCS) is mostly widely used. This scale assesses a child's independence when performing basic motor function like walking or moving, with the aid of ancillary equipment (crutches, walking frames and wheelchairs).

Table 19.11 Gross Motor Function Classification System (GMFCS)

|  Level of Performance | Characteristics  |
| --- | --- |
|  I | Walks independently  |
|  II | Walks independently with mild limitations  |
|  III | Walks using ancillary equipment  |
|  IV | Moves on their own but with certain limitations; may use an electric wheelchair  |
|  V | Not able to move on their own; transported in a wheelchair by a carer  |

b4854_Sec-19.indd 658

12-Dec-22 11:12:43 AM

## Associated Problems

- Intellectual disability
- Language and speech impairments
- Psychosocial behavioural problems
- Epilepsy
- Feeding and swallowing difficulties
- Hearing impairment
- Visual disorders, strabismus, refractive errors
- Scoliosis
- Pulmonary disease
- Poor growth and nutrition
- Orthopaedic problems, e.g. joint subluxations and dislocations and hip dysplasia
- Urinary disorders, e.g. voiding dysfunction

## Approach

### History

- Antenatal, perinatal, postnatal history
- Developmental history
- Exclude familial or neurodegenerative disorder masquerading as CP. Ask about regression of milestones, family history of similar affected siblings/relatives, consanguinity, episodic exacerbation of symptoms
- Social history, impact on family
- Others: Education, special aids
- Medications

### Clinical and Neurological Examination

- Define the type of CP
- Assess mental and physical functional status
- Assess associated problems
- Growth, development and head circumference
- Dysmorphism
- Neurologic deficits, abnormalities of muscle tone, power and gait, brisk tendon reflexes, clonus, presence of involuntary movements, persistence of primitive reflexes, contractures

### Laboratory Studies

- No specific tests to confirm the diagnosis of CP.

![img-31.jpeg](img-31.jpeg)

Figure 19.2 Evaluation and management of the child with Cerebral Palsy (CP).

- Neuroimaging should be performed to establish an aetiology and for prognostic purposes. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is preferred to Computed Tomography (CT) scan.
- Children with atypical features in the history or neurologic examination should be thoroughly evaluated to exclude other conditions, including metabolic and

b4854_Sec-19.indd 660

12-Dec-22 11:12:45 AM

# Cerebral Palsy

genetic disorders. Useful investigations include karyotype, metabolic testing, TORCH (Toxoplasmosis, Rubella, Cytomegalovirus, Herpes Simplex) screen in neonates.

- Children with hemiplegia or imaging evidence of cerebral infarction may have a prothrombotic coagulation disorder and may benefit from screening.

## Management

Treatment goals are directed at reducing disability and maximising potential in all areas of development, including activities of daily living. Goals should be functional and realistic with periodic re-evaluation. Optimal management of the child with cerebral palsy requires a collaborative goal-oriented approach, in which a multidisciplinary team works with the family to address the child's medical, social, psychological, educational and therapeutic needs.

## Treatment Modalities

- Rehabilitation therapy
  » Physiotherapy
  » Occupational therapy
  » Speech therapy
  » Behavioural therapy
- Mechanical devices to assist patients in sitting, walking or communicating, to prevent and correct deformities and control involuntary movements.
  » Orthoses, serial casting, braces, special chairs
- Pharmacotherapy
  » Anti-spasticity medications
    i. Oral agents (baclofen, diazepam)
    ii. Botulinum toxin A injections for focal spasticity
  » Medications for seizures, involuntary movements
- Surgery
  » Orthopaedic tendon lengthening, tendon transfer, bony fusion
  » Neurosurgery, e.g. selective dorsal rhizotomy
  » Gastrointestinal
- Special education may assist children with special needs
- Psychosocial therapy

## Common Presentations to the Emergency Department/Reasons for Admission

- Respiratory problems, especially pneumonia
- Status epilepticus/uncontrolled seizures
- Unexplained irritability — consider acute infections, pain from fractures/subluxation, intestinal obstruction, brain trauma and possibility of non-accidental injury

# CHAPTER 95

# Movement Disorders

Yeo Tong Hong

## Introduction

Movement disorders in childhood are frequently seen in a wide spectrum of neurological conditions, from benign, self-limiting transient phenomena to progressive disorders associated with significant disability. Clinical presentations may be acute, subacute or chronic.

They are often classified into two phenomenological groups:

(1) Hyperkinesia/Dyskinesia (including tics, myoclonus, dystonia, chorea, athetosis, tremors, and stereotypies).
(2) Hypokinetic-rigid syndromes (Parkinsonism)

The evaluation of movement disorders is usually clinical, either through history, examination and videos or patient's/caregivers' reports based on self-assessment.

## Definitions

**Tics** are brief involuntary purposeless movements and/or sounds.

**Myoclonus** is a sudden, brief involuntary jerk of a muscle or a group of muscles.

**Dystonia** is characterised by sustained or intermittent muscle contractions causing abnormal, often repetitive, movements, postures or both. Typically patterned, twisting and may be tremulous, dystonia is often initiated or worsened by voluntary action and associated with overflow muscle activation.

**Chorea** is a rapid, abrupt, irregular, unpredictable, non-stereotyped, semi or non-purposeful movement.

**Athetosis** is a slow continuous stream of writhing movement, typically of the hands and feet.

**Tremor** is an involuntary, rhythmic, oscillatory movement of a body part.

**Stereotypies** are repetitive and purposeless movements.

## History and Clinical Examination

Normal or abnormal pattern of movements?
- Excessive or paucity of movements?

664

b4854_Sec-19.indd 664

12-Dec-22 11:12:49 AM

# Movement Disorders

Voluntary or involuntary?
- Paroxysmal? i.e. sudden onset and offset?
- Continual? i.e. repeated again and again?
- Continuous? i.e. without stopping?
- Changed over time? Wax and wean? Diurnal variation?
- Environmental stimuli? Emotional triggers?
Abates with sleep?
- Suppressible?
- Premonition? Premonitory sensation or urge?
Family history of similar or related conditions?
- Examination findings? Focal neurological deficit? Systemic involvement?
- Any parental/caregiver's video recordings?

## Benign Movement Disorders of Childhood

A number of benign movement disorders of childhood are recognised, and they are transient in nature, and usually have a good long-term prognosis.

### Neonatal Jitteriness

High frequency and low amplitude tremor
- Location: Jaw and extremities
- Multiple factors, including metabolic disturbance, hypoxic-ischaemic encephalopathy (HIE), drug withdrawal
Often mistaken for neonatal seizures
- Suppressed easily by gentle restraint
- No specific treatment; treat the underlying factors
Usually resolves by 7 months

### Benign Neonatal Sleep Myoclonus

- Self-limiting movement disorder
- Myoclonic jerks during non-rapid eye movement (NREM)/quiet sleep
- Stopped by arousal
Usually resolves by 3 to 4 months

Benign Polymorphous Movement Disorders of Infancy (BPMDI) are part of a heterogeneous group of movement disorders, including shuddering attacks, benign myoclonus of early infancy, head nodding, benign infantile shaking body attacks, brief tonic contraction, benign non-epileptic infantile spasms and sleep-related infantile tremor.

Characteristic features:

- Onset: 4 to 7 months
- Paroxysmal, sometimes in clusters
- Brief
Without impaired consciousness

## Transient Idiopathic Dystonia of Infancy

- Onset is usually before 5 months
- Hemidystonia — usually affecting single upper limb and trunk
Persistent
- Abolished when infant moves purposefully
- Resolves without sequelae at 12 months

## Motor Stereotypies/Stereotypic Movement Disorder

- DSM-5 Category: Neurodevelopmental Disorders
  - Repetitive, seemingly driven, purposeless motor behaviour
  - Early onset; \(>4\) weeks' duration
  - Can interfere with normal activities or results in self-injury
  - Not explained by another neurodevelopmental or psychiatric disorder
Primary Motor Stereotypies
Common amongst normal children
Transient in nature; good prognosis
Common behavioural stereotypies: Rocking, head banging, hair twisting, pencil tapping
Complex stereotypies: Head nodding, hand shaking, opening/closing of hands, arm flapping, flexion/extension of wrists, mouth opening/facial grimaces
- Secondary Motor Stereotypies can be seen in children with autism, Rett syndrome, neurodegenerative disorders and metabolic disorders.

Table 19.12 Indications for Neurology Referral for Movement Disorders

|  Abnormal neurology/focal neurological deficit  |
| --- |
|  History of epilepsy/epileptic seizures  |
|  Evidence of psychomotor regression  |
|  Diagnostic doubt as to the nature of the event  |
|  Clinical course no longer associated with existing diagnoses  |

## Tic Disorders and Tourette Syndrome

- Tics are the most common movement disorder in childhood and can affect up to \(20\%\) of children.
- Tics are brief involuntary purposeless movements and/or sounds. Tics can either be 'simple' involving one muscle or 'complex' involving a group of muscles.
Examples include:

a. Simple motor tics — eye blinking, facial grimaces, head and neck jerks, and shoulder shrugs

b4854_Sec-19.indd 666

12-Dec-22 11:12:49 AM

# Movement Disorders

b. Complex motor tics — jumping, touching objects/clothes
c. Simple vocal tics — grunting, coughing, sniffing and throat-clearing sounds
d. Complex vocal tics — animal-like noises, swearing, etc.
• Some children experience premonitory feelings or sensations that are relieved by the execution of the tics.
• Tics are often transient and can be self-resolving within a few weeks or months. This is known as the Transient Tic Disorder.

# Tourette Syndrome and Chronic Tic Disorder

• Chronic Tic Disorder is a condition in which a child experiences single or multiple occurrences of either motor or vocal tic for more than 1 year.
• Tourette Syndrome is a Chronic Tic Disorder, in which a child experiences both motor and vocal tics.
• Children with Tourette Syndrome can also have other conditions such as Anxiety, Obsessive Compulsive Disorder (OCD) and/or Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD).

# Diagnosis

• Clinical history and careful observation.
• No role for laboratory tests or neuroimaging.
• Electroencephalography (EEG) is usually not indicated.
• Medications may induce tics, especially stimulants such as methylphenidate.
• DSM-5 criteria for Tourette Syndrome are as follows:
  » A child or young person must have multiple motor and one or more vocal tics that happen regularly although not necessarily at the same time.
  » Tics occurring many times a day or intermittently for a period of more than 1 year.
  » Age of onset: <18 years old.
  » No other conditions that could cause tics.
• Some useful tools to measure outcomes include the Yale Global Tic Severity Score and ICF for sleep, pain and quality of life (QoL).

# Management

• Majority of children with tics do not require medication.
• Psychological interventions, including anxiety/stress management, cognitive behaviour therapy, are helpful. Treatment for associated conditions, such as OCD and ADHD. These conditions may be more disruptive than the tics and require different treatments based on the symptoms.
• Medical options: Clonidine, Guanfacine, Aripiprazole, Risperidone

# Dystonia and Status Dystonicus

Dystonia is characterised by sustained or intermittent muscle contractions causing abnormal, often repetitive, movements, postures or both. Typically patterned, twisting

b4854_Sec-19.indd 667

12-Dec-22 11:12:49 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

and may be tremulous. Dystonia is often initiated or worsened by voluntary action and associated with overflow muscle activation.

**Status Dystonicus (SD)** is defined as a movement disorder emergency characterised by severe episodes of generalised or focal dystonic with or without other hyperkinetic movements (e.g. chorea). Up to 60% of reported cases are children. SD is probably under recognised and can be fatal in up to 10% of cases.

It is important to recognise SD as the severe end of a continuum of deterioration through the application of **Dystonia Severity Action Plan (DSAP)** (Figure 19.5).

|   | Description |  | Dystonia Severity Action Plan (DSAP)  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  1 | - Sits comfortably - Normal sleep - Stable on medication | - No assessment - No change in individual *Dystonia Action Plan* |   |
|  2 | - Irritable and cannot settle - Dystonic posturing interferes with sitting - Can only tolerate lying | - Adjust medication as per individual *Dystonia Action Plan* - Clinic review within 1-2 weeks | **Brittle Dystonia Management Plan:** - As per existing individual *Dystonia Action Plan* - Adjust individual *Dystonia Action Plan* to prevent deterioration through dystonia severity grades leading to status dystonicus - Clinic review if required - Suggestions:     - Benzhexol or L-dopa     - Gabapentin: esp. generalised dystonia     - Clonidine: enteral/ IV /transdermal. Useful for sleep disturbance/ agitation     - Other: benzodiazepines (diazepam, clonazepam, nitrazepam), baclofen, tetrabenazine  |
|  3 | - Sleep disturbed - Unable to tolerate lying - No signs of metabolic (e.g. pyrexia, dehydration) or respiratory compromise | - Clinic review within days - Consider metabolic screen: CK, renal, liver and bone profile - Escalate management - +/- hospitalisation  |   |
|  4 | - As per Grade 3 - Early multi-organ failure. - Clinically as above with: Pyrexia, dehydration, abnormal electrolytes, myoglobinuria. CK>1000 IU/L | **Status Dystonicus Management Plan:** 1. **Supportive Care**     - Urgent admission: KKH HD or CICU     - Multisystem support as required     - Supportive care including IV hydration, anti-pyretics and analgesia     - Monitor Urea & Electrolytes, Renal, Bone and Liver profile, CK     - Identify and treat triggers (consider IV antibiotics) 2. **Temporising measures**     - Chloral hydrate to establish sleep     - Consider Enteral / IV clonidine (non-respiratory depressant)     - ± IVI midazolam     - ± General anaesthesia/ Paralytic agents 3. **Dystonia-specific measures**     - Consider oral medication trial: Benzhexol, L-dopa, Gabapentin, Baclofen, Benzodiazepines, Tetrabenazine     - Consider Neuromodulation (ITB, DBS) or Pallidotomy  |   |
|  5 | - Severe generalised dystonia - As per Grade 4 with full metabolic and renal decompensation and/ or cardiorespiratory compromise  |   |   |

CK - creatine kinase, HD - high dependency unit, CICU - Children's Intensive Care Unit, IV - intravenous, IVI - intravenous infusion, ITB - intrathecal baclofen, DBS - deep brain stimulation

Figure 19.5 Dystonia severity action plan (DSAP).

# Movement Disorders

669

Patients at risk of SD are those with:

- Chronic dystonias (primary and secondary)
- Generalised quadriplegic cerebral palsy
- Neurodegenerative disorders (e.g. glutaric aciduria type 1 — GA1)
- Neurotransmitters disorders
Post-acquired brain injury (e.g. traumatic brain injury, autoimmune encephalitis)
- Acute drug withdrawal (e.g. Baclofen or other anti-dystonic drugs)

Common triggers of SD: Pain, infection, surgery, drugs

Main features of SD:

Hypertonia
Abnormal dystonic posturing
Preservation of consciousness
Elevated creatine kinase (CK)
- Other complications — rhabdomyolysis, respiratory compromise

Initial management of SD

- Admission to HD/CICU
- Address precipitants
- Begin supportive care
- Calibrate sedation with a combination of oral chloral hydrate, oral or IV clonidine, and benzodiazepines (e.g. per rectal diazepam or IV midazolam); consider IVI clonidine/midazolam
- Dystonia specific therapy (under Neurologist's guidance)

Clonazepam, Trihexyphenidyl, baclofen, L-dopa, Gabapentin, Tetrabenazine, Dantrolene
Deep brain Stimulation/Intrathecal baclofen

# CHAPTER 96

# Bell's Palsy

Lim Yi Xiu, Jocelyn; Terrence Thomas

## Introduction

- Bell's palsy (BP) is an acute, peripheral facial nerve paralysis of unknown cause, making up about \(50\%\) of all paediatric facial nerve paralysis.
- Some proposed aetiologies include post-infectious inflammatory factors, vascular ischaemia and immunologic causes.
- Incidence in children aged 1 to 15 years is lower (6.1 per 100000 person-years) compared to adult (20 per 100000 person-years).
- The key to diagnosis lies in good clinical history and thorough clinical examination to exclude the other causes of facial nerve paralysis.

Table 19.13 Differential Diagnoses to Bell's Palsy in Children

|  Congenital | Traumatic delivery: Primiparity, birth weight >3.5 kg, forceps or caesarean section, prematurity Syndromic: Mobius syndrome, Goldenhar syndrome, Arnold-Chiari syndrome, syringobulbia Genetic: Hereditary myopathies: Congenital myaesthenic syndromes, myotonic dystrophy  |
| --- | --- |
|  Acquired | Infectious: Ramsay Hunt syndrome Ebstein–Barr virus, cytomegalovirus, adenovirus, rubella, mumps, HIV mycoplasma pneumonia, haemophilus influenza, tuberculosis, acute otitis media, chronic otitis media/cholesteatoma Inflammatory: Guillain–Barré Syndrome, Henoch–Scholein purpura, Kawasaki disease Neoplastic: Vestibular schwannomas, haemangiomas, rhabdomyosarcoma, temporal bone histiocytosis, leukaemia, parotid gland tumours Traumatic: Temporal bone fractures, iatrogenic  |

Note: It is most important to exclude other conditions that mimic presentation similar to BP. This can be achieved with thorough history-taking and competent clinical examination.

# Bell's Palsy

# Clinical Presentation

- Acute onset of decreased forehead movement, incomplete eye closure, loss of nasolabial fold and asymmetric smile.
- Hyperacusis, decreased tearing and altered taste can also occur depending on site of facial nerve injury.
- Sparing of forehead muscles indicates a central upper motor neuron (UMN) lesion.
- In younger patients, observe facial movements and asymmetry while crying.
- Severity of paralysis can be assessed using the House-Brackmann (HB) facial nerve grading scale (according to the worst functioning branch) (see Table 19.14).
- Symptoms and signs suggesting an alternative aetiology:

Gradual onset, progressive nature \(>3\) weeks
» Ear discharge, tympanic membrane erythema, mastoid tenderness (acute otitis media)
» Vesicular rash and vestibulocochlear dysfunction (Ramsay Hunt syndrome)
» Other cranial neuropathies or neurological signs
» Other systemic features, e.g. hypertension, pallor, easy bruisability, lymphadenopathy, organomegaly, weight loss

- No investigations are necessary if there are no atypical features or red flags.
- Otherwise, investigations to consider include:

Neuroimaging — CT or MRI brain
ENT referral \(\pm\) audiogram/tympanogram
» Autoimmune, malignancy or serological screen
Lumbar puncture

Table 19.14 House-Brackmann (HB) Facial Nerve Grading Scale

|  Grade | Description  |
| --- | --- |
|  I | Normal  |
|  II | Mild dysfunction *Slight weakness, normal symmetry at rest*  |
|  III | Moderate dysfunction *Obvious but not disfiguring, synkinesis, symmetry at rest, complete eye closure with maximal effort, good forehead movement*  |
|  IV | Moderately severe dysfunction *Obvious and disfiguring asymmetry, significant synkinesis, incomplete eye closure, moderate forehead movement*  |
|  V | Severe dysfunction *Almost no movement*  |
|  VI | Total paralysis *No movement*  |

b4854_Sec-19.indd 671

12-Dec-22 11:12:50 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

## Management

- Treatment depends on underlying aetiology.
- Eye care to prevent corneal abrasions, exposure keratitis and vision loss: Eye patch and saline eye drops.
- While steroids and acyclovir have been shown to improve recovery in adults with BP, paediatric studies have remained inconclusive, with most studies showing no significant difference in recovery.
- Outpatient follow up in 4 to 6 weeks for resolution.
- If recurrent or persistent, refer to Neurology for further evaluation.

## Prognosis

- Children with BP mostly recover well spontaneously and completely.
- Recovery in children is better (up to \(90\%\)) compared to adults (up to \(70\%\)), with most recovering within 3 months of onset.
- In general, a lower HB grade (II or less) and younger age at presentation (< 8 years old) confer a better prognosis with faster and more complete recovery.
- Persistent or recurrent lesions will need re-evaluation.

## Bibliography

1. Lunan R, Nagarajan L. (2008) Bell's palsy: A guideline proposal following review of practice. J Paediatr Child Health 44:219-220.

# CHAPTER 97

# Acute Leukaemia and Oncological Emergencies

Soh Shui Yen

## Acute Leukaemia

Acute leukaemia is the most common malignancy in childhood. In general, there are two types of acute leukaemia in children — acute lymphoblastic leukaemia (ALL) and acute myeloid leukaemia (AML). Although urgent diagnosis and treatment are important, immediate supportive care is also necessary to monitor, prevent and treat complications such as acute tumour lysis syndrome, hyperleucocytosis or rarely disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC).

## Investigations for Patients Newly Diagnosed with Acute Leukaemia

### Blood Tests

Full blood count (FBC); peripheral blood film (PBF)
- Coagulation profile (PT and aPTT). Serum fibrinogen and D-dimer should be done in patients with suspected or possible DIC, e.g. in sepsis or acute promyelocytic leukaemia (APL).
Biochemistries: Renal panel and electrolytes — including serum creatinine, potassium, calcium and phosphate; uric acid; lactate dehydrogenase (LDH); liver function test (LFT)
- Serum immunoglobulins — IgG, IgA, IgM
Viral immunity status

Mandatory: Hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg), Hepatitis B surface antibody (anti-HBs), Hepatitis B core total antibody (anti-HBc), Varicella-zoster virus (VZV) IgG
Others: Hepatitis C total antibody (CMIA), Cytomegalovirus (CMV) IgG, Epstein-Barr virus (EBV), Viral Capsid Antigen (VCA) IgG, Herpes Simplex Virus (HSV) total antibody, Human Immunodeficiency Virus (HIV) screen, Measles IgG, Mumps IgG, Rubella IgG

G6PD screen (if G6PD status unknown)
Blood cultures (if febrile)
- Group and Cross Match

# Acute Leukaemia and Oncological Emergencies

## Imaging Studies

- Chest X-ray (CXR) — should be performed before the patient receives any sedation (to exclude mediastinal mass)
- Echocardiogram (prior to first dose of anthracyclines)

## Bone Marrow Aspirate

- Bone marrow aspirate (BMA) examination or morphology
BMA immunophenotyping (by flow cytometry)
BMA cytogenetics
- Other investigations as required by the treatment protocol, e.g. flow cytometry analysis and/or molecular characterisation for subsequent monitoring of minimal residual disease (MRD); molecular genetic profiling. Trephine biopsy may be required if there is a dry tap.

## Lumbar Puncture

The timing of the first lumbar puncture (LP) is specified by the treatment protocol and the patient's clinical condition. The first LP should be performed by an experienced practitioner to reduce the risk of traumatic or bloody tap. Ensure adequate platelet counts before LP: $>50 \times 10^9/\text{L}$; preferably $>100 \times 10^9/\text{L}$ for the first LP. Do not perform LP on patients who have or are at risk of DIC. Adequate procedural sedation (or general anaesthesia) and analgesia are also important to help reduce the risk of traumatic LP. Send the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) for the following investigations:

CSF profile
CSF cytospin for blasts

Following collection of CSF samples, intrathecal chemotherapy (e.g. methotrexate) is administered.

## Immediate Supportive Management

A child suspected to have acute leukaemia should receive the following supportive management:

1. Monitoring and management of tumour lysis syndrome and hyperleucocytosis (see 'Oncological Emergencies')
2. Blood product support

a. Platelet transfusion as guided by the clinical condition:

- If the patient is asymptomatic: Keep platelets more than \(10 - 20 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\).
- If the patient is febrile or septic or having hyperleucocytosis: Keep platelets more than \(20 - 30 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\).
- If there is bleeding or DIC: Keep platelets more than \(50 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\), or even higher (\(>50 - 100 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\)) if there is severe bleeding or if the patient is undergoing invasive procedures.

b4854_Sec-20.indd 675

12-Dec-22 11:13:26 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

b. Red cell (RBC) transfusion is generally indicated for patients who are symptomatic from moderate–severe anaemia (Hb <6–7 g/dL). In patients with high white blood cell (WBC) count, RBC transfusion may contribute to hyperviscosity. Therefore, RBC transfusion should be avoided, especially in asymptomatic patients, until the WBC is lower.

c. Coagulopathy (e.g. DIC) in patients with hyperleucocytosis or APL should be aggressively managed: Transfuse fresh frozen plasma (FFP) and cryoprecipitate to keep PT/aPTT within normal range and fibrinogen above 1.0–1.5 g/L. Maintain platelets above 30–50 × 10⁹/L. Have a low threshold for urgent Computed Tomography (CT) Head in patients with neurologic symptoms worrisome for possible intracranial haemorrhage. In patients with suspected APL, all trans retinoic acid (ATRA) should be started as soon as possible.

3. Start broad spectrum intravenous (IV) antibiotics if patient is febrile or septic.

## Oncological Emergencies

### Tumour Lysis Syndrome

Tumour lysis syndrome (TLS) is a metabolic emergency that results from massive tumour cell destruction, leading to biochemical derangements that overwhelm the body’s excretory mechanisms.

Laboratory TLS, as defined by Cairo-Bishop, is 2 or more of the following within 3 days before and 7 days after the commencement of chemotherapy:

- hyperuricaemia \(\geq 476\mu \mathrm{mol} / \mathrm{L}\) or \(25\%\) increase from baseline
hyperkalaemia \(\geq 6.0\mathrm{mmol / L}\) or \(25\%\) increase from baseline
hyperphosphataemia \(\geq 2.1\mathrm{mmol / L}\) or \(25\%\) increase from baseline
- hypocalcaemia \(\leq 1.75\mathrm{mmol / L}\) or \(25\%\) decrease from baseline.

In clinical TLS, deposition of uric acid and phosphate in the kidney results in acute renal impairment. Severe electrolyte derangements such as hyperkalaemia and hypocalcaemia can lead to cardiac arrhythmias, tetany and seizures and can be life-threatening. Clinical TLS is therefore defined by the presence of laboratory TLS, together with one or more clinical complications: renal impairment (serum creatinine ≥1.5 times upper limit of normal), seizures, cardiac arrhythmias or sudden death.

Patients with acute leukaemia and high white cell count (>50 × 10⁹/L) are at risk of developing TLS. Other malignancies with high risk of tumour lysis include high grade bulky lymphomas, e.g. Burkitt lymphoma/leukaemia. Patients with underlying renal impairment are at higher risk of developing TLS.

# Acute Leukaemia and Oncological Emergencies

## Hyperleucocytosis

Hyperleucocytosis is defined as a WBC count higher than $100 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}$. Hyperleucocytosis increases blood viscosity (hyperviscosity), especially in the microcirculation, and leads to leucostasis, which is the aggregation of blasts in the blood vessels. Leucostasis is more common in AML as myeloblasts are larger in size compared to lymphoblasts. Leucostasis symptoms most often involve the lungs and the central nervous system. Symptoms reflect tissue or organ congestion and ischemia, and can, therefore, include respiratory distress, desaturation, headache, confusion, drowsiness, seizure, visual disturbance, stroke, renal impairment, cardiac impairment, bowel ischemia, priapism, and so on. The risk of symptomatic leucostasis is higher in ALL with WBC $\geq 300 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}$ and in AML with WBC $\geq 100 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}$ (especially in M4/M5 subtypes).

## Management of TLS and Hyperleucocytosis

- Hydrate with IV dextrose/saline at \(3\mathrm{L} / \mathrm{m}^2/\) day for at least 72 hours. Patients with very low WBC may be given less fluids.
- Do not add potassium or phosphate to IV fluids.
- Start oral (PO) allopurinol \(100\mathrm{mg / m^2}\) dose TDS and continue for up to 7 days and until peripheral blasts are reduced and serum uric acid level is normal.
- Reduce dose in renal impairment. Consider recombinant urate oxidase (rasburicase) in patients with high risk of tumour lysis or established TLS or renal impairment (see Box on the next page).
- Urine alkalinisation is no longer routinely recommended but may be considered if there is metabolic acidosis. Alkalinisation increases solubility of uric acid in the urine and prevents crystallisation in the renal tubules. However, over-alkalinisation causes crystallisation or precipitation of xanthine, hypoxanthine and phosphate, and also worsens hypocalcaemia. To alkalinise, add \(15\mathrm{ml}8.4\%\) sodium bicarbonate \(\mathrm{(NaHCO_3)}\) to each \(500\mathrm{ml}\) of IV dextrose/saline and subsequently adjust the amount of added \(\mathrm{NaHCO_3}\) to keep the urine pH 6.5-7.5. Urine pH should be measured at each void when the patient is on alkalinisation. Do not alkalinise when patient is on rasburicase.
- Specific electrolyte abnormality (hyperkalaemia, hyperphosphataemia) should be treated accordingly. Hypocalcaemia is not treated unless it is symptomatic.
- If clinical TLS develops or fails to improve with the above measures, urgent renal dialysis will be necessary.
- Hyperleucocytosis should be managed by hydration to reduce hyperviscosity, as well as prompt initiation of chemotherapy for cytoreduction. Leucopheresis may be considered for patients with symptomatic leucostasis not responding to the above supportive care, if prompt chemotherapy cannot be initiated. Leucopheresis is not recommended in asymptomatic patients and is contraindicated in APL. Red cell transfusions may worsen hyperviscosity and should be avoided.
Clinical and laboratory monitoring.

# The Baby Bear Book

# Use of Recombinant Urate Oxidase (Rasburicase) in Tumour Lysis Syndrome

- Indications:

Patients with high risk of TLS:
ALL or AML with WBC \(\geq 100\times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\)
Burkitt lymphoma or lymphoblastic lymphoma
Bulky high grade lymphoma — bulky disease as defined as elevated LDH \(\geq 2\times\) upper limit of normal, or large tumour mass, e.g. \(>10\) cm in adults, or organ infiltration
High grade haematologic malignancy with pre-existing renal dysfunction
Patients with established TLS

- Contraindications: G6PD-deficiency, haemolytic anaemia, hypersensitivity
- Dose: IV 0.15–0.2 mg/kg/dose Q24H for 1–7 days, to be decided each day by a senior physician.
- For 96 hours after the last dose of rasburicase, blood samples for uric acid measurement should be sent to the laboratory immediately without delay, and preferably transported in cool condition (e.g. with cooling packs). Uric acid results may be falsely low post-rasburicase if there is a delay in measurement, and especially if the blood samples are at room temperature.
- Do not administer concurrent allopurinol when rasburicase is being used.

# Monitoring

- Monitoring during hyperhydration: Strict intake/output monitoring with fluid tally every 4–6 hours during hyperhydration. Ensure urine output \(>100 \, \text{ml/m}^2/\text{h}\) (or \(>4 \, \text{ml/kg/h}\) for infants). IV furosemide \(0.5 \, \text{mg/kg/dose}\) may be needed if there is concern about fluid overload (e.g. positive fluid balance exceeding \(600 \, \text{ml/m}^2/\text{day}\)). Monitor weight while on hyperhydration.
- Daily blood tests during first week of induction: FBC, Renal Panel, Calcium, Phosphate, Uric Acid
- Close monitoring (every 4–6 hours, or less frequently if low risk of TLS) of serum potassium, phosphate and calcium until disease load has reduced and no evidence of tumour lysis.
- Patients with high risk of TLS may need more frequent monitoring of FBC, uric acid, renal panel and/or coagulation tests.

# Febrile Neutropenia

Oncology and hematopoietic transplant patients are immunocompromised as a result of cancer treatment and immunosuppressive therapies. Febrile neutropenia is a medical emergency as the progression of infection in neutropenic patients can be very rapid, especially with gram-negative organisms. Prompt attention and administration of appropriate broad spectrum antibiotics (within 60 minutes of presentation) and good supportive treatment are critical for a favourable outcome.

b4854_Sec-20.indd 678

12-Dec-22 11:13:26 AM

# Acute Leukaemia and Oncological Emergencies

- Fever is defined as a single temperature of \(\geq 38.3^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\) or two or more episodes of \(\geq 38^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\) (sustained over a 1-hour period).
- Significant or moderate neutropenia refers to ANC \(\leq 500\) cells/μl (0.5 × 10\(^9\)/L), or one that is expected to fall below 500 cells/μl (0.5 × 10\(^9\)/L) over the next 48 hours. This is the working definition for most febrile neutropenia guidelines. However, our institution guideline will use ANC of \(< 1000\) cells/μl (1 × 10\(^9\)/L) as the cut-off.
- Presence of indwelling vascular access devices and mucosal barrier injuries (e.g. from chemotherapy or radiation) are additional risk factors for infections in immunocompromised patients.

## History and Clinical Examination

- Perform a thorough history-taking and physical examination to look for aetiology or focus of infection, paying special attention to the skin, mucosal surfaces (oral cavity, perineum), central line sites. DO NOT perform rectal examination in patients suspected to have neutropenia.
- Obtain the oncology and transplant history, as well as the information on recent and ongoing medications.
- Measure and monitor vital parameters (pulse rate, respiratory rate, blood pressure, oxygen saturation).
- Look for symptoms and signs that may suggest severe sepsis, such as altered mental state, lethargy, poor peripheral perfusion (capillary refill time \(>3\) seconds), respiratory distress, decreased urine output.

## Investigations

FBC
- Group and cross match
- Routine biochemistries: Renal panel and electrolytes, LFT
C-reactive protein
Blood cultures — Aerobic and anaerobic

One set from central lines — Port-a-cath; each lumen of central venous catheters (e.g. Hickman, PICC).
One set percutaneously from peripheral veins — This is mandatory for febrile patients without central lines, but may be omitted in stable patients with central lines. If peripheral blood cultures are taken, they should be drawn simultaneously with 'Central' blood cultures, to enable evaluation of differential time to positivity (DTP).
Each specimen should be clearly and correctly labelled (e.g. aerobic culture from small lumen).

- Urinalysis, urine culture and urine fungal smear in patients in whom a clean-catch midstream specimen is readily available. Urine collection must not delay initiation of antibiotics.

# The Baby Bear Book

- Imaging (e.g. CXR) and other investigations (e.g. respiratory viruses, stool investigations, coagulation profile) as clinically indicated and must not delay initiation of antibiotics.

## High-Risk Factors in Patients with Febrile Neutropenia

- Haematopoietic Stem Cell Transplant (HSCT) within 6 months and/or still on immunosuppressants
ALL on induction or re-intensification phases
- AML
High grade lymphomas, e.g. Burkitt
High-risk neuroblastoma
- Relapsed/progressive malignancy on intensified treatment
Down syndrome
- Recent overwhelming sepsis
- Clinically unwell — chills/rigours, hemodynamic instability, altered mental state, respiratory distress

## Initial Management

- General supportive and resuscitative measures as required.
- Monitor parameters (heart and respiratory rate, blood pressure) at least 4 hourly. Look out for inappropriate tachycardia (impending shock) or tachypnoea (pneumonitis/chest infections).
- Review the patient's ongoing medications:

Stop any ongoing chemotherapy, e.g. PO 6-mercaptopurine or 6-thioguanine.
Prophylactic antimicrobials (e.g. co-trimoxazole for Pneumocystis prophylaxis, acyclovir for HSV prophylaxis), if present, should be continued.
If the patient is on granulocyte colony-stimulating factor (GCSF), this should be continued till neutrophil recovery.
Steroids should be continued. Abrupt discontinuation during sepsis may trigger an adrenal crisis.

- Prompt initiation of empirical broad-spectrum antibiotic within 60 minutes of presentation:

Piperacillin-tazobactam is a recommended first-line empirical monotherapy agent for febrile neutropenia as it has broad spectrum gram-negative (including antipseudomonal), gram-positive and anaerobic cover. It is also well tolerated and has a good toxicity profile.
If the patient is clinically unstable (e.g. haemodynamic instability — hypotension, excessive tachycardia), or when a resistant infection is suspected, antibiotic cover should be broadened to include a second gram-negative agent (e.g. aminoglycoside amikacin) and/or glycopeptide (vancomycin). Consider escalating piperacillin-tazobactam to meropenem.

- Blood product support may be necessary:

b4854_Sec-20.indd 680

12-Dec-22 11:13:26 AM

# Acute Leukaemia and Oncological Emergencies

Maintain Hb \(>8\mathrm{g / dL}\) in patients with febrile neutropenia; if there is bleeding, maintain \(>9\mathrm{g / dL}\).
Maintain platelets \(\geq 20\times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\) in patients with febrile neutropenia. If there is bleeding, maintain \(\geq 50\times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\), or \(\geq 100\times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\) for life-threatening bleeds, including intracranial haemorrhage, massive gastrointestinal haemorrhage).

- Other supportive care

Fever increases insensible fluid losses. Ensure that the child receives adequate IV/PO fluids.
» Correct electrolyte abnormalities.
» Do not administer rectal enemas or suppositories to neutropenic patients.

## Ongoing Management

- If the patient is responding to initial empirical antibiotic(s): Discontinue the second gram-negative agent (e.g. amikacin) and glycopeptide (vancomycin) after 24–72 hours unless continued therapy is supported by relevant positive microbiology results.
- If the patient has persistent fever but is clinically stable: Consider repeat blood cultures and other evaluations as clinically indicated. It is not recommended to modify initial antibiotics based solely on persistent fever.
- If the patient has persistent fever and becomes clinically unstable: Repeat blood cultures. Repeat other evaluations as clinically indicated. Antibiotic cover should be broadened to include a second gram-negative agent (e.g. amikacin) and/or glycopeptide (vancomycin). Consider escalating piperacillin-tazobactam to meropenem.
- Empirical antifungal therapy:

Patients at high risk of invasive fungal disease (IFD): AML, high-risk ALL, relapsed acute leukaemia, allogeneic HSCT, prolonged profound neutropenia \((\mathrm{ANC} \leq 0.1 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\) for \(>7\) days), high-dose corticosteroids \((\geq 2 \mathrm{mg} / \mathrm{kg} / \mathrm{day}\) or \(\geq 20 \mathrm{mg} / \mathrm{day}\) of prednisolone or equivalent, for \(\geq 2\) weeks). All other patients are considered IFD low-risk.
In IFD high-risk patients with prolonged \((\geq 96\) hours) febrile neutropenia not responding to broad-spectrum antibiotics:

- CT Chest; abdominal ultrasound; other imaging only if there are localising signs or symptoms.

- Initiate empirical antifungal therapy — ambisome or caspofungin.

- Adjust antimicrobial regimen based on clinical, radiological (if any) and/or microbiology results:

If cultures are negative at 48 hours: Discontinue empirical antibiotics (i) if the patient is afebrile for \(\geq 24\) hours, and (ii) when there is evidence of marrow recovery (especially in patients with high-risk factors).
If cultures are positive: Definitive antimicrobial therapy should be given for at least 7-14 days. Removal of central line and longer duration of systemic antimicrobial therapy may be necessary for complicated central line-associated blood stream infection (CLABSI).

# The Baby Bear Book

## Anterior Mediastinal Mass

The mediastinum is the central part of the thoracic cavity and can be the site of involvement of the several tumours such as:

- Anterior mediastinum: Lymphoma (Hodgkin or non-Hodgkin), malignant germ cell tumour, teratoma, thymoma, mediastinal extension of thyroid tumours.
Posterior mediastinum: neuroblastic tumours (i.e. neuroblastoma, ganglioneuroblastoma, ganglioneuroma), neurofibroma.

Anterior mediastinal masses (AMM) can have aggressive behaviour and may cause life-threatening structural compression on the airways (trachea, carina, bronchus, bronchial branches), the heart and the great vessels (pulmonary vessels, superior vena cava).

## History and Clinical Examination

- Onset and duration of symptoms.
- Close monitoring for airway/respiratory/haemodynamic instability.
- Neck/face/upper extremities: Engorged neck vessels, facial and neck swelling and congestion — Pemberton sign, upper limb swelling, lymphadenopathy, neck mass, syncope, cyanosis.
- Chest: Cough, dyspnoea, tachypnoea, orthopnoea, noisy breathing (stridor or wheeze), chest pain/discomfort. There may be signs of pleural and/or pericardial effusion.
Systemic: Fever, night sweat, weight loss.
- Other: Hepatosplenomegaly, Horner syndrome (involvement of sympathetic ganglia), genetic syndrome (e.g. Klinefelter).

## Initial Evaluation and Management

- General supportive and resuscitative measures as required. Maintain spontaneous respiration.
- Allow the child to assume the most comfortable position. Establish the 'position of comfort' or 'position of rescue'. Avoid procedures that will cause agitation or distress to the child.
- Immediate referral to multi-disciplinary team: Oncologist, surgeon, intensivist, anaesthetist.
- The minimum level of care and monitoring in the initial period is the High-Dependency Unit. The child should be in the Children's Intensive Care Unit if there is any clinical concern or radiologic evidence of significant airway obstruction or vascular compression.
- Avoid placement of IV cannula in the upper limbs, especially in patients with superior vena cava (SVC) obstruction.
- Initial investigations (to be with urgent multi-disciplinary discussion):

FBC, PBF, renal panel and electrolytes, uric acid, LDH, alpha-fetoprotein (AFP), human chorionic gonadotropin (HCG)
PT, aPTT, Group and Cross Match
CXR postero-anterior and lateral

» Ultrasound doppler of neck vessels
Echocardiogram
» Urine vanillylmandelic acid (VMA)/creatinine and homovanillic acid (HVA)/ creatinine (for posterior mediastinal mass)

Table 20.1 Anterior Mediastinal Mass — High-Risk Features Which Have Been Shown to Be Associated with Complications

|  Clinical Findings | CT Findings | Echo Findings  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  • Any respiratory distress: Orthopnoea, dyspnoea, noisy breathing, cough • Upper body oedema • Syncope or cyanosis • Abnormal vital parameters: Desaturation, hypotension, inappropriate tachycardia | • Mediastinal mass ratio >45% • Tracheal compression >50% • Severe carinal or bronchial compression • Severe SVC or great vessel compression • Pericardial effusion | • Pericardial effusion • Pulmonary artery (PA) outflow obstruction • Impaired ventricular function  |

- Multi-disciplinary management is necessary to plan the best and safest way to perform the necessary diagnostic evaluations.

» A tissue biopsy is required for histopathological diagnosis, unless the diagnosis can be made from elevated serum tumour markers (AFP and HCG) or from PBF and flow cytometry (lymphoblastic leukaemia/lymphoma).
» General anaesthesia and heavy sedation are avoided, especially in patients with any high-risk features.
» Local anaesthesia with the child in spontaneous respiration and in a position of comfort may suffice to allow less invasive diagnostic procedures on accessible sites to be performed safely — e.g. cervical lymph node biopsy, bone marrow aspiration/biopsy, pleural tap.

- Although sufficient diagnostic work-up is ideal prior to commencing definitive cancer treatment, patients with life-threatening airway and vascular compression may require urgent steroids or chemotherapy or radiotherapy. Tissue biopsy should still be obtained once it is safe enough to do so, otherwise these interventions may result in rapid tumour response and adversely affect accurate and timely diagnostic confirmation.
- Other oncologic evaluations and procedures as appropriate: Tumour lysis monitoring and management, central line placement, other diagnostic work-up (e.g. LP, PET scan).

## Spinal Cord Compression

Spinal cord compression can be caused by tumours such as paravertebral neuroblastic tumours, bone or soft tissue sarcoma, myeloid sarcoma, lymphoma, germ cell tumours, primary or secondary intraspinal tumours. These can occur at initial presentation of the malignancy or at disease progression or recurrence. Spinal cord compression can

b4854_Sec-20.indd 683

12-Dec-22 11:13:26 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

also be caused by haematoma from traumatic lumbar punctures, especially in patients with thrombocytopenia or coagulopathy. The signs and symptoms may be insidious and non-specific initially, especially in young children. A high index of suspicion and prompt appropriate evaluation and management can improve short- and long-term neurological and spinal outcomes.

## History and Clinical Examination

- Onset and duration of symptoms
- Oncologic history; recent spinal procedures
- Pain — back pain; radicular pain radiating to limbs
- Numbness or paraesthesia; sensory level may be present
Weakness
- Bladder/bowel symptoms — retention of urine, constipation, loss of continence

## Initial Evaluation

- Close monitoring of clinical signs/symptoms to look for rapid progression.
- Urgent magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of whole spine.
- Urgent multi-disciplinary referral: Spine surgery, neurosurgery, oncology, neurology.
- Urgent laboratory investigations:

FBC, renal panel and electrolytes, LFT, LDH, uric acid, AFP, HCG
PT, aPTT, group and cross match
» Urine HVA/creatinine; urine VMA/creatinine

- Other imaging and laboratory investigations as necessary, for example, MRI brain, CT chest, BMA/trephine.

## Initial Management

- Nil-by-mouth. Give adequate IV fluids but avoid hyperhydration, which may in some situations increase peri-tumoral swelling and worsen cord oedema.
- Consider dexamethasone if there is evidence of neurological deficit.

Initial \(1 - 2\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (max. \(10\mathrm{mg}\)), followed by \(0.25 - 0.5\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (max. \(4\mathrm{mg}\)) Q6H.
Haematologic malignancies may respond rapidly to steroids, and hence definitive diagnostic procedures should not be delayed once steroids have been administered.

- Discuss diagnostic and surgical procedures — biopsy for histological diagnosis, versus debulking and decompressive laminectomy; central line placement; other diagnostic procedures, e.g. bone marrow evaluation, biopsy of accessible site (if available and feasible).

- Further management of spinal cord compression include surgical debulking and decompressive laminectomy or emergency chemotherapy or emergency radiotherapy. The exact modality of choice will require urgent discussion on clinical and disease factors.

- Patients with severe presentation or rapid progression may require emergency surgical intervention.

» For patients with chemo-sensitive malignancies (such as lymphoma, neuroblastoma, Ewing sarcoma, rhabdomyosarcoma, myeloid sarcoma), chemotherapy is as effective as surgery in relieving cord compression. Biopsy of an accessible tumour, followed by urgent initiation of chemotherapy is generally the preferred strategy.

» For patients with disease progression or recurrence, prognosis and goals of care would have to be considered when planning further treatment intervention.

# CHAPTER 98

# Infection Prophylaxis in Immunocompromised Patients

Mya Soe Nwe

## Introduction

The following are **general measures** to reduce infections in immunocompromised oncology and haematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) patients:

- All staff and visitors should wear a surgical facemask and practice hand hygiene. Sick staff/visitors should not enter the oncology/HSCT ward.
- Patients with active contagious diseases (e.g. respiratory illness, shingles, chickenpox) should be nursed in single isolation rooms away from the other immunocompromised patients.
- Patients are advised to follow food safety measures. In general, food should be well cleaned and eaten fresh. All meat and eggs should be thoroughly cooked. Dairy products, honey and juices should be pasteurised.
- Patients should practise frequent hand washing and regular baths to maintain personal hygiene and cleanliness, with special attention to oral cavity and perianal area.
- Plants, including fresh or dried flowers, are not allowed in the oncology/HSCT rooms because of the possible presence of fungal moulds and spores.
- Patients at risk of invasive mould infections (e.g. allogeneic HSCT recipients, patients with severe combined immunodeficiency) are nursed in single rooms with double doors, HEPA (high efficiency particulate air) filtration, positive air pressure and high rates of room air exchanges (\(>12\) exchanges per hour).

## Post Viral Exposure Prophylaxis

### Chickenpox

- Varicella (chickenpox) is an extremely contagious infection due to the varicella-zoster virus (VZV), found only in humans.
- Transmission is by respiratory contact with airborne droplets, or direct contact or inhalation of aerosols from vesicular fluid of skin lesions of varicella, or by a transplacental route.

# The Baby Bear Book

**Post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP)** includes VZIG (VZ immune globulin) or VZ vaccine, but not acyclovir currently (except in unusual circumstances; consult Infectious Disease specialist)

1) VZ vaccine: It is contraindicated if the patient is on active chemotherapy or high-dose steroids (should have their steroids discontinued for at least 1-3 months before receiving VZ vaccine) and for those with recent administration of blood, plasma or immune globulin, including IVIG (should have IVIG 11 months before receiving the VZ vaccine).
2) Varicella Immune globulin (VZIG): It is indicated if an immunocompromised patient is exposed to active chickenpox within 96 hours (up to 10 days) of exposure.

Dose (specific to Varitec® CP, intravenous (IV) preparation of VZIG):

- 25 IU/kg, or \(1\mathrm{ml / kg}\) (compared with 125 IU for every \(10\mathrm{kg}\) body weight for intramuscular (IM) preparation)
There is no maximum dose for Varitec® CP
- Do NOT overdose, as this may lead to fluid overload and hyperviscosity

# Administration:

IV
- Infuse at \(0.1\mathrm{ml / kg / h}\) for the first 10 minutes and monitor for side effects; if tolerated well, may increase to \(0.2\mathrm{ml / kg / h}\) for the next 10 minutes, then \(0.5\mathrm{ml}/\) \(\mathrm{kg / h}\) for another 10 minutes and subsequently to \(1\mathrm{ml / kg / h}\) till completion

# Possible Side Effects:

- Local reactions — pain, redness, swelling (uncommon)
Systemic reactions — fever, headache, malaise, chills, nausea, vomiting, arthralgia (uncommon)
- In cases of adverse reactions, reduce the rate or stop the infusion till symptoms disappear
- Severe reactions — angioneurotic oedema, anaphylaxis (rare); aseptic meningitis, reversible haemolysis (very rare)
- For acute anaphylaxis, give intramuscular adrenaline \(0.01\mathrm{ml / kg}\) (1:1000), repeating every 15 minutes if necessary

The incubation period is 21 days (if VZIG is not given), or 28 days (if VZIG is given). During this period, it may be necessary to consider withholding intensive chemotherapy.

# Treatment

If chickenpox should develop, IV acyclovir should be given for at least 7 days, until all lesions have crusted and with no appearance of new lesions for 48 hours. The dose of IV acyclovir is 500 mg/m²/dose Q8H (or 10 mg/kg/dose Q8H for children younger than 1 year), given with adequate hydration.

# Infection Prophylaxis in Immunocompromised Patients

# Measles

- Measles is a highly contagious, acute infection caused by the measles virus (MV) and usually seen in children
- Transmission is by direct contact with respiratory secretions of infected persons or airborne droplets

**Post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP)** includes Human Immune Globulin (IG) or measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine

MMR vaccine is contraindicated in immunocompromised patients.

Immune globulin (IG): IV/IM

Contraindications/Precautions:

- Hypersensitivity to immune globulin or any component of the formulation
- GAMASTAN® must not be given intravenously, and must not be administered intramuscularly in cases of severe thrombocytopenia and in other disorders of haemostasis, and in IgA deficient patients with antibodies against IgA.

Dose:

IV Immunoglobulin:
» Immunocompromised: \(400\mathrm{mg / kg}\) as a slow infusion

# OR

- IM Immunoglobulin (GAMASTAN® 2 ml vial)

Usual dose: \(0.25\mathrm{ml / kg}\)
» Immunocompromised: \(0.5\mathrm{ml / kg}\)
Maximum dose: 15 ml

Administration:

IV (current preparation)
» Infuse at \(0.5\mathrm{ml / kg / hr}\) for the first 15 minutes and monitor for side effects; if tolerated well, may increase to \(1\mathrm{ml / kg / hr}\) for the next 15 minutes, then \(2\mathrm{ml}/\) \(\mathrm{kg / hr}\) for another 15 minutes and subsequently to \(4\mathrm{ml / kg / hr}\) till completion
IM (GAMASTAN® 2 ml)
» If anatomically feasible, the full dose should be administered at a single site
» Otherwise, the dose can be re-distributed to multiple sites to reduce local pain and discomfort

Possible Side Effects:

- Local reactions — pain, redness, swelling, itching, rash (IM: common)
Systemic reactions — chills, headache, fever, vomiting, nausea, dizziness, low blood pressure, tachycardia, arthralgia, hyperhidrosis, pallor, paraesthesia, back pain
- In cases of adverse reactions, reduce the rate or stop the infusion till symptoms disappear
- Severe: Anaphylactic/anaphylactoid reactions, thromboembolic events, aseptic meningitis, acute renal failure, transfusion-related acute lung injury (rarely)

# The Baby Bear Book

- For acute anaphylaxis, give IM adrenaline 0.01 ml/kg (1:1000), repeating every 15 minutes if necessary

The incubation period is 14 days, during which chemotherapy should be withheld if possible.

## Splenectomy/Asplenia

Splenectomised patients or those with other causes of asplenia or impaired splenic function are at risk of developing severe sepsis from encapsulated bacteria such as *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae* and *Neisseria meningitidis*.

## Prevention of Post-Splenectomy Sepsis

The patient who has undergone splenectomy or who is functionally asplenic needs to be educated about the need for prompt medical attention after the onset of fever.

## Immunisations

- Immunisation with pneumococcal, meningococcal and conjugate \(H.\) influenzae type b vaccines.
- If a patient will be undergoing elective splenectomy, the pneumococcal, meningococcal and \(H.\) influenzae vaccines should be administered at least 14 days prior to surgery. If it is not possible to administer these vaccines prior to splenectomy, they can be given after the 14th post-operative day.

## Antibiotic Prophylaxis

Post-splenectomy, antibiotic prophylaxis is advised. Antibiotic prophylaxis also applies to patients with asplenia or functional asplenia (e.g. sickle cell disease, post-splenic irradiation or graft vs. host disease). Duration of antibiotic prophylaxis is still controversial, but should be until a minimum age of 5 years and at least 1–2 years post-splenectomy, or longer (until age 18 years, or even lifelong) for higher-risk patients.

## Antibiotics Prophylaxis for Invasive Dental Procedure

Immunocompromised patients should receive standard antibiotic prophylaxis against infective endocarditis during invasive procedures such as gastrointestinal or genitourinary operations and invasive dental procedures (refer to Table 7.13 in Chapter 28: ‘Infective Endocarditis’).

## Vaccination

### Vaccination for Close Contacts of Immunocompromised Patients

- Household contacts should *not* receive oral polio vaccine (OPV). Individuals with recent OPV (within 4–6 weeks) should not come into close contact with immunocompromised patients.

# Lymphadenopathy

Prasad Ramanakrishnan Iyer; Chan Mei Yoke

## Differential Diagnoses

- Infections

Bacterial: Staphylococcus aureus, Streptococcus pyogenes, Bartonella (Cat Scratch Disease), Brucellosis, Tularaemia
Viral: Epstein-Barr virus, Cytomegalovirus, Human immunodeficiency virus, Measles, Rubella
Mycobacterial: Mycobacterium tuberculosis, Mycobacterium avium-intracellulare, Mycobacterium scrotulareum
Protozoan: Toxoplasmosis, Malaria
Fungal: Histoplasmosis, Coccidiomycosis, Cryptococcus, Aspergillosis

Autoimmune conditions

Juvenile chronic arthritis
Systemic Lupus Erythematosus

- Immunodeficiency syndromes

Chronic granulomatous disease
Hyper-IgE syndrome (Job Syndrome)
Leucocyte adhesion deficiency

Malignant conditions

Leukaemia/lymphoma
Metastatic solid tumours, e.g. neuroblastoma

- Miscellaneous

Kawasaki Disease
Kikuchi Disease
Sarcoidosis
Histiocytosis
Storage disease, e.g. Gaucher disease, Niemann-Pick Disease
Medications, e.g. phenytoin

## History

Age of patient, duration of symptoms
- Travel history exposure to insects, animals, sick persons
- Recurrent infections, recurrent skin sepsis, immunisation status
- Constitutional symptoms, e.g. fever, loss of appetite, loss of weight

- Rash, arthralgia
- Drug history
- Travel history; exposure to insects, animals, sick persons

## Clinical Examination

- Examine enlarged lymph node for: Size, warmth, tenderness, overlying skin erythema, fluctuancy, mobility
- Other lymph node involvement: Cervical, occipital, axillary, epitrochlear, inguinal, popliteal
Presence of pallor, jaundice
- Joints for swelling, tenderness, limited range of movement
- Skin for vasculitis, petechiae, purpura
- Abdomen for hepatosplenomegaly, intra-abdominal masses
- Localised infection

## Investigations and Management

See Table 20.6.

Features that may prompt lymph node biopsy in a child with lymphadenopathy:

- Lymph node size \(>2\) cm or increasing in size
- Supraclavicular lymphadenopathy
Abnormal chest X-ray
- Presence of systemic signs and symptoms: Fever, night sweats, weight loss, hepatosplenomegaly

# If lymph node biopsy is to be done, consult oncologist and ID physician first.

The biopsy specimen is usually sent for the following:

- Histology (inform pathologist beforehand if lymphoma is suspected as a fresh specimen is often required)
- Gram stain, culture and sensitivity
- Acid Fast Bacillus (AFB) smear/AFB culture and sensitivity, AFB Polymerase Chain Reaction (PCR)
- Fungal smear/fungal culture

# CHAPTER 100

# Paediatric Palliative Care

Chan Mei Yoke; Wynn Yi Yi

The World Health Organization (WHO) defines palliative care (PC) as 'active total care of patients whose disease is not responsive to curative treatment. Control of pain, of other symptoms and of psychological, social and spiritual problems is paramount. The goal of PC is the achievement of the best quality of life for patients and their families'. WHO also recommends that paediatric palliative care (PPC) for children begins when life-limiting illness is diagnosed and continues regardless of whether the child receives treatment directed at the disease. It involves giving support to the family and including them in decision-making regarding PC needs of the child.

Clinical skills in history-taking and examination are essential. Investigations are carried out only if the result will contribute to the patient's management. Care of palliative patients always involves assessment and re-assessment to ensure maximal relief of symptoms. Patients and their families are intimately involved in decision-making regarding treatment options and care.

## Paediatric Patients Suitable for PPC

Children with these four categories of diseases may benefit from a referral to PPC:

- Diseases where cure is possible but can fail, e.g. cancer, organ failure — heart, liver or kidney or infections.
- Incurable diseases but child can live for an extended time, e.g. cystic fibrosis, Duchenne muscular dystrophy, human immunodeficiency virus/acquire immunodeficiency syndrome, cardiovascular anomalies, extreme prematurity.
- Progressive conditions from the diagnosis without curative options, for example, neuro-degenerative, progressive metabolic disorders, chromosomal abnormalities, advanced metastatic cancer on first presentation.
- Irreversible, non-progressive with severe disability, e.g. cerebral palsy, genetic disorders, congenital malformations, brain/spinal cord injury.

Many are non-malignant and some of the conditions, e.g. genetic disorders, which are extremely rare with diagnosis specific to childhood, may have affected more than one child in the family. Some have uncertain prognosis and the course may stretch for years, requiring lifelong palliative needs.

Earlier integration of PC has improved clinical outcomes and provided an extra layer of support to the child and family. Referral can be initiated in the following conditions:

- Upon diagnosis of life-threatening fetal condition (peri-natal PC).

698

b4854_Sec-20.indd 698

12-Dec-22 11:13:27 AM

# Paediatric Palliative Care

699

- At the time of new life-threatening diagnosis with poor prognosis, e.g. diffuse intrinsic pontine glioma, Stage IV Neuroblastoma.
- New diagnosis with complex pain and/or symptom management issues.
- When disease becomes refractory to treatment.
- Disease progression with development of life-threatening complications (organ failure or prolonged intubation).
Complex clinical course with complications requiring multiple admissions.

## Adult versus PPC

The paediatric palliative model of care is integrated and seamless throughout initial diagnosis, active curative treatment and symptom relief, sharing care with community, home and inpatient PC and bereavement services. It is important to gradually move from curative treatment to PC without rigid boundaries.

The adult model of 6 months prognosis as referral criteria cannot apply in PPC. The focus of care may be more 'aggressive' as well, in that there may be more hospitalisations, and 'active' treatment may be ongoing. A parent should never have to choose between PC and hope for a cure.

PPC focuses on symptoms control, maintaining a good quality of life and supporting families when a child has a condition that is highly likely to end in premature death at any time prior to adulthood. PPC is not confined to end-of-life care but is incorporated throughout the disease trajectory as part of the continuum of care.

Providing PPC for children with life-limiting illness requires attention to the physical, developmental stage, psychosocial, ethical, spiritual and relational phenomena that are unique to children. Children undergo continual developmental changes and their age-dependent cognitive abilities will impact their medical, social, emotional needs and understanding of illness and death. They have changing information needs, recreational and educational needs and modes of coping with stress. Child life specialists, play therapists and behavioural specialists can facilitate through activities that assist with emotional distress and can provide important understanding into the fears and wishes of the child.

A child with a life-threatening disease may strain or challenge existing relationships within the family. Role reversal, overly enmeshed relationships, family dynamics and conflicts between family members are common and cause significantly impact on the child's quality of life. Assessment of family function and efforts to resolve conflict or dysfunction is thus essential.

Emphasis is placed on excellent communication skills with both patients and their families. This requires sensitivity to the child's developmental stage and to the language, culture and illness understanding of both the patient and family. PPC entails taking time to assess parents' awareness of their child's condition and explore their concerns, practical challenges, care preferences and facilitate discussion for future wishes and care. It is also important to clarify with regard to the preferred place of care as families may prefer the child to be at home rather than in a hospice.

'Parallel care planning' refers to the development of plans that allow for unpredictability in the course of the condition. There will be concurrent plans for potential

b4854_Sec-20.indd 699

12-Dec-22 11:13:27 AM

survival and plans for deterioration and death. This allows clinical teams and parents an opportunity to review care plans according to the child's disease progression and needs, but also spare parents and family from the sense of abandonment and anxiety when the child's condition deteriorates and death becomes imminent.

### Barriers to PPC

Children may be referred late or not at all to PPC due to barriers such as:

- Physician's prognostic paralysis regarding the child's illness.
- Stigma surrounding death and PC by both the physician and parents.
- Misunderstanding of purpose of PPC, e.g. abandonment or giving up and stopping all treatment.

### PPC can help with the following categories of needs:

- Symptom management, e.g. pain, vomiting, breathlessness, excessive secretions.
- Psychosocial support, e.g. identify the child and family's fears, coping and communication styles.
- Spiritual support, e.g. review child's hopes, dreams, values, life meaning, role of prayer and ritual and beliefs regarding death.
- Discussion of Advance Care Plan with parents and child (if child is old enough).
- Preparation and support of the child and family for end-of-life care, including management of end-of-life symptoms and selection of preferred location of death.
- Facilitates and coordinates continuity of care across settings to ensure a smooth transition from hospital to home or hospice.
- Bereavement support for the family following the death of the child.

### Conclusion

PPC aims to improve the quality of life for children of all ages with life-threatening illnesses and their families regardless of age (i.e. new born to young adults), stage of illness (i.e. curative treatment, chronic stage, recovery, terminal stage) and outcome (i.e. death or survival).

PPC adopts a holistic approach to provide the child and his/her family needs in all dimensions (physical, psychosocial, spiritual and practical) of care, which is consistent with the goals of the child and family. On-going regular assessment of their needs is essential and requires collaboration and coordination with all healthcare teams across different settings (hospital, home and hospice), with the aim to provide 'seamless' transitions for children with life-threatening conditions.

### Bibliography

1. World Health Organization. (2018) Integrating palliative care and symptom relief into paediatrics: A WHO guide for health care planners, implementers and managers, World Health Organization, Geneva.
2. American Academy of Pediatrics. (2000) Committee on bioethics and committee on hospital care. Palliative care for children. Pediatrics 106(2 Pt 1):351–357.

# CHAPTER 101

# Agitated and Violent Patients

Radha Srikanth; Ong Say How

## Introduction

Violent and agitated behaviour can be the manifestation of many psychiatric as well as medical conditions. It is often not essential and, in fact, not possible to establish and manage the cause of the agitation and aggression before managing the behaviour.

## Risk Factors

- Patients with psychosis or bipolar affective disorder.
- Children with non-accidental injury (NAI) or have reason to fear for their own safety due to their own trauma.
- Patients with disruptive behaviour disorders like conduct disorder, attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), impulsivity, etc.
Patients who have misused substances/acute intoxication.
- Children with high levels of anxiety can also present with agitation and violent behaviour.

## Approach to Managing Behavioural Disturbance in Children and Adolescents

- All aggressive and violent children and adolescent should have a comprehensive diagnostic evaluation.
- Physical restraint can be emotionally traumatic for children; hence, behavioural strategies or seclusion should be preferred.
- First-line management — De-escalation
Second-line management — Seclusion and restraint
- Third-line management — Use of oral medications such as benzodiazepines and antihistamines. If there is a history of psychotic illness, intramuscular injection of haloperidol can be used.
- The lowest possible dose of medications should be used.
- A senior medical staff should be involved in decision-making for using medications.
- If the child or adolescent continues to be unmanageable, the child psychiatrist on-call must be consulted and if safety is a concern, admission to psychiatric inpatient unit should be considered.

702

b4854_Sec-21.indd 702

12-Dec-22 11:14:00 AM

# Agitated and Violent Patients

# Verbal De-Escalation When a Child is Restrained

- Talk to the child
- Use a calm space
- Listen to the child
- Acknowledge his feelings of anger
- Set time limits
- Motivate him — 'I want you to calm down', 'I want you to help me to calm you down'
- Use relaxation techniques — ask the child to take deep breaths and relax his muscles. Demonstrate this to the child.
- Distract the child by changing the focus to positive things.

# Rapid Tranquillisation

- Defined as 'the use of psychotropic medication to control agitation, threatening or destructive psychotic behaviour'
Aims

» Reduce the risk of physical harm to other patients, staff and the patient
» Reduce the distress to the patient caused by acute agitation
» Calm the patient and not sedate. (However, sometimes sedation is unavoidable.)

- Side effects

» Paradoxical reactions, including hyperactive/aggressive behaviour have been occasionally reported with benzodiazepine use. This may be more likely to occur in children/adolescents and patients with developmental delay or organic brain syndromes.
» The most serious acute complication of benzodiazepines remains respiratory depression.
» Children and adolescents are more likely to be antipsychotic-naïve and there is a greater risk of dystonic reaction and other acute side effects.
» The most commonly encountered problems (can happen even after a single dose) after administration of haloperidol are:

Acute dystonia,
Oculogyric crisis (type of acute dystonia, which includes blepharospasm, periorbital twitches and protracted staring episodes), and
Akathisia.

- The parameters to be monitored when medications are given to manage agitated and violent behaviour are:

» Blood pressure
» Heart rate
» Respiratory rate
» Temperature
» Oxygen saturation

- Concerns raised must be followed up and the child may need referral to specialists who can deal with the issues in detail.

# CHAPTER 102

# Self-Harm and Suicidal Behaviours

Ong Say How

It is not uncommon for troubled adolescents to be referred for self-harm and suicidal behaviour. Deliberate self-harm (DSH) is also known as non-suicidal self-injury (NSSI). The motivations for carrying out self-harm are often multiple and certainly multi-factorial, with interpersonal conflicts and disputes being frequent precipitating factors. Individuals who have impulsive traits, poor frustration tolerance, anxiety, depression, conduct disorders and borderline personality traits are more predisposed to engaging in self-harm behaviour.

Self-harm is a set of maladaptive behaviour used to cope with intense and negative feelings, and could range from cutting, overdosing, peeling/scratching of skin, hitting and biting of self, etc. Individuals with self-harm behaviour are not suicidal in intent per se but the risk of suicide is elevated in this group of patients: 60 to 100 times higher than in the general population. As the frequency of self-harm increases, so do the risk of suicidal thoughts and plans. The recurrence of self-harm behaviour also increases with a past history of self-harm.

Self-harm typically happens when other means to self-soothe fail or the individual does not know how to do so. A common misconception is that self-harming adolescents are seeking attention. They are not.

Observe the following when attending to individuals who self-harm:

- Attend to the injury and assess whether surgical intervention or superficial first-aid is required. Be mindful to check for cuts in obscure locations on the body such as thighs. Self-cutting in the inner thighs could be a sign of prior or existing sexual abuse.
- Even if surgical intervention is not needed, decide whether admission is required, especially for individuals coming from complex family and social backgrounds, when there is concomitant high suicide risk or when the adolescent is visibly distressed over an ongoing stressor that will not go away any time soon. Admit the adolescent if you are uncertain, as it is better to err on the side of caution.
- If admission is not required, consider referring the patient to the Psychology Service outpatient clinics. Many individuals with self-harm behaviour benefit from cognitive behavioural therapy (CBT) and antidepressants (e.g. selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors).
- Stay calm as the attending doctor. Do not judge or criticise the behaviour of the adolescent. Let them know that even though you cannot help solve their problems, you or someone in the hospital/clinic could lend a listening ear.

705

b4854_Sec-21.indd 705

12-Dec-22 11:14:00 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

- If there is a chance you are able to engage the adolescent in greater depth, use the HEADSS assessment to assess in greater details the presence of adverse psychosocial factors. This screen can be used to guide further management like referrals to other services.

Adolescents who self-harm do not stop the behaviour overnight. Many continue to do so for years before they either reduce the frequency of self-harm or stop completely. Improvements in self-harm is likely attributed to their developing maturity and self-esteem with time.

Fortunately, the suicide rate for youth in Singapore remains low at about 1.3 per 100000 (2011) compared to other countries. Reasons behind the low rate include availability of youth outreach, effective healthcare and emergency response systems, low poverty rate and high education attainment, firearms ban and strict anti-drug policies. However, there are still individuals who choose suicide to resolve their problems. Up to \(70\%\) of suicide completers had a mental disorder prior to suicide. About \(30\%\) of completed suicides have a past history of suicide attempts. Repeat suicide attempt rates are high: \(16\%\) at 1 year and \(23\%\) at over 4 years.

![img-34.jpeg](img-34.jpeg)

Source:IMH,2012.

Figure 21.1 Suicide rate from 2006 to 2011.

## Suicide Risk Assessment

The major risk factors for suicide amongst adolescents (adapted from Cash and Bridge [2009]) are as follows:

Previous suicide attempt
Psychopathology

» Especially major depressive disorder, bipolar disorder, conduct disorder and substance use disorders
Psychiatric co-morbidity, especially the combination of mood, disruptive and substance use disorders

» Dysfunctional personality traits (especially antisocial, borderline, histrionic and narcissistic traits)

• Family factors
  » A family history of depression or suicide
