• Suicidal intention (in terms of motivation and planning such as writing suicide note, last acts, attempts to conceal suicide)
• Availability of lethal means and lethality of method used
• Lack of relief after being saved
• Having been exposed to suicide (e.g., suicide or suicide attempt in family members or friends; media reporting)
• Gender (males age 15–19 years) and ethnicity (Indians and Chinese)

It has been shown that psychiatrists are no better than general physicians in predicting suicide. Hence, possessing the necessary knowledge and skills in performing an adequate suicide risk assessment is extremely helpful in guiding your management of the suicidal patient.

## Management of Inpatient Suicidal Patients

• Commence on a Monitoring/Observation Record Chart
• Hourly observation on:
  » Whereabouts of patient
  » What is patient doing
• Referral to Child Psychiatrist on duty
• Nurse in a bed visible to nurses and nearest to nurses' station
• Curtains open at all times
• Remove hand-rub from the foot of bed
• Accompany to toilet/bathroom by nurses/parents/caregivers
• Not allowed out of the ward unless accompanied by the parents/ caregivers
• Upon consulting the Child Psychiatrist on duty and if the decision is to transfer to tertiary psychiatry hospital (adolescent still at high risk for suicide but otherwise medically stable), ward team to initiate referral
• Remove suicide precaution if the adolescent is no longer at high suicide risk and upon instruction by the reviewing child psychiatrist

Vicknesan Jeyan Marimuttu; Roselyne Shirley Pat Fong; Poon Ngar Yee;
Hong Ling Feng; Paulette Reyes Po; Loh Hong Yan, Abigail

# Anxiety Disorder

# Introduction

Anxiety disorders are one of the most common psychiatric disorders in children and adolescents, but they often go undetected or untreated.

It is normal for children and adolescents to feel worried from time to time. However, it is considered a disorder when the individual's feelings of nervousness and anxiousness become excessive and persistent to the extent that they begin to interfere with their school, home or social life.

The prevalence of any anxiety disorder in youth ranges from 2 to 4%, with 6–12 months' estimates between 10% and 20%, and lifetime estimates being only slightly higher.

# Presentation

The impact of anxiety symptoms in young children may be clinically significant, even if the full criteria are not met. These symptoms of anxiety can be in the form of thoughts, physical sensations or behaviours. Some examples include:

- Physical sensations
  - Headache or giddiness
  - Nausea or complaining of tummy aches
  - Racing heart beat
  - Chest tightness and feeling breathless
  - Sweating more than usual
  - Urgency and frequency of using toilet
- Behaviour associated with anxiety
  - Being clingy
  - Repeatedly asking parents and friends for reassurance
  - Frequent crying
  - Increase irritability or being out of control during outburst
  - Going to great lengths to avoid what they perceive to be anxiety-provoking situations, e.g. school refusal due to bullying issues
  - Insomnia or waking up with nightmares
  - Poor appetite

710

b4854_Sec-21.indd 710

12-Dec-22 11:14:01 AM

# Mental Health

- Anxious thoughts

Finding it hard to concentrate on tasks
Repeatedly worrying about the same problem

# Treatment

The type of treatment offered will be dependent on the child's age and the cause of their anxiety.

Non-pharmacological treatment

Evidence-based psychological interventions can be effective treatments for anxiety disorder. They are recommended as the first-line treatment in preference to pharmacological treatment.

» Counselling: This can help the child understand what the cause of their anxiety is and allow them to work though the situation.
Therapy: There are various type of therapy depending on the child's age and presentation. These include Behavioural Therapy, Cognitive Behavioural Therapy (CBT), Art Therapy, Sand Tray and Symbol Play Therapy. CBT is a talking therapy that is often used for older groups of children. This form of therapy can help the child to manage their anxiety by changing the way they think and behave.

- Pharmacological treatment

Pharmacological interventions are usually not offered as first-line treatment for children and youth who suffer from anxiety disorders. Medications are reserved for situations where evidence-based therapy (e.g. CBT) alone is not adequate to reach the desired outcome or in situations where therapy is not a feasible option.

The following factors must be taken into account:

The age of the patient
Risk: The likeliness of accidental overdose or deliberate self-harm using medications
Tolerability or side effects reported
Interaction with other medication used by the patient
Preference of the patient
Costs

- Antidepressants should be the only pharmacological intervention used for longer term management of anxiety disorders.

Selective Serotonin Reuptake Inhibitors (SSRIs) are the medication of choice for the treatment of anxiety disorders in children and adolescents. Studies support that SSRI such as fluoxetine and sertraline have proven efficacy in the management of anxiety disorders.
Serotonin-Norepinephrine Reuptake Inhibitors (SNRIs), e.g. venlafaxine and duloxetine have also been approved by the FDA for treatment of paediatric generalised anxiety disorder. This class of medication is generally less well tolerated compared to SSRIs. Nevertheless, this class of medication can be considered as a third-line

b4854_Seo-21.indd 711

12-Dec-22 11:14:01 AM

# *The Baby Bear Book*

treatment for anxiety disorder when two consecutive trials with different SSRIs prove to be ineffective.

- Important note: Treatment with SSRIs and SNRIs appear to be associated with an increase in suicidal ideation and self-harm in a minority of people under the age of 30. It is therefore vital to monitor the risk of suicidal ideation and self-harm closely, especially in the first month after starting medication or after any increase in dosage.
- People starting on antidepressants should also be informed about the delayed onset of effect, the time course of the treatment and possible discontinuation/withdrawal symptoms.
- Other Medications
  - Hydroxyzine is an antihistamine that has been used for patients presenting with milder anxiety symptoms.
  - Propranolol is a beta-blocker drug used to slow the heart rate and reduce blood pressure. It can help with physical sensations of anxiety such as palpitation and tremors. However, it may not be suitable for patients who suffer from asthma or low blood pressure.
  - Benzodiazepines (BDZ): This category of medication is not supported by controlled trials in children and may lead to paradoxical disinhibition in some. Nevertheless, it may be considered in certain clinical situations. Important note: BDZ are only recommended for short-term usage and to be used only when necessary, as prolonged use may result in dependency.

# **Practical Tips to Help Parents and their Child in Dealing with Anxiety**

- Teach the child or parents to recognise signs of anxiety
- Encourage the child to manage their anxiety and to ask for help when needed
- Many children find routines reassuring; when possible, try to stick to a regular daily routine, which makes the child feel more in control and that life is being more predictable.
- Create a 'worry box' using an empty tissue box. Get the child to write or draw about their worries and put them into the box.
- Relaxation breathing technique — improper breathing technique may lead to an imbalance of oxygen and carbon dioxide level in the body, which can contribute to anxiety attacks. Simple relaxation techniques such as taking in a slow deep breath for a count of 3, holding it for another count of 3 and breathing out for the count of 3, and repeating the whole thing 3 times.
- Apps: Headspace, Clear Fear, Calm
- Progressive muscle relaxation exercise
  - Step 1: Lie down or sit in a comfortable position
  - Step 2: Gently close your eyes (can keep them open if the child prefers)
  - Step 3: Take in a slow deep breath

# Bipolar Disorder

## Introduction, Definition and Subtypes

Bipolar Disorder is a mood disorder that is characterised by cyclic changes in mood. In its classic form, the mood changes between Mania and Major Depressive episodes (Bipolar I Disorder). In order to diagnose Bipolar I Disorder, at least one lifetime episode of mania is required. The other subtypes include episodes of Major Depression alternating with hypomania (Bipolar II Disorder), multiple episodes of hypomania with periods of depressive symptoms, not reaching the threshold for Major Depression (Cyclothymia) and sub-threshold (insufficient symptom count or duration) episodes of mania or hypomania, with or without depression (Other Specified Bipolar and Related Disorders BD-OD).

Mania or a manic episode, as per DSM V, is defined as;

A. A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood and abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy, lasting at least 1 week and present for most of the day, nearly every day (can be any duration if hospitalisation is necessary).

AND 3 or more of the following (4 if mood is only irritable)

B. During the period of mood disturbance and increased energy or activity, the following are present to a significant degree and represent a change from usual behaviour

1. Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity
2. Decreased need for sleep (e.g. feel rested after only 3 hours of sleep)
3. More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking
4. Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing
5. Distractibility (i.e. attention easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli) as reported or observed
6. Increased goal-directed activity (either socially, at work or school, or sexually) or psychomotor agitation (i.e. purposeless non-goal directed activity)
7. Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences (e.g. engaging in unrestrained buying sprees, sexual indiscretions or foolish business investments)

Additionally, the mood disturbance should be sufficiently severe to cause marked impairment in social and occupational functioning or to necessitate hospitalisation to prevent harm of self or others from their psychotic features. Also the episode should not be attributable to the physiological effects of a substance (e.g. a drug of abuse, a medication, other treatment) or to another medical condition.

Hypomania is a milder form of a manic episode and is diagnosed when there is a distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive or irritable mood and abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy, lasting at least 4 days and present for most of the day, nearly every day. The additional symptoms (see criterion B above) are also present with the same number of criteria to be met as mania. However, importantly, hypomania differs from mania, in that the symptoms are not severe enough to cause marked impairment to social and occupational functioning. There also should not be a necessity for hospitalisation.

# Mental Health

## Epidemiology and Aetiology

A meta-analysis about the epidemiology of Bipolar Disorder in youth around the world reported that the overall rate was 1.8% (Van Meter *et al.*, 2011). If sub-threshold symptoms are included, prevalence rates are between 5.7 and 13.3%. Bipolar Disorder can have its onset in childhood, but its prevalence is much higher during adolescence. Similar to adults, studies in clinical populations suggest that the rates of bipolar spectrum disorders in youth are equally common in males and females.

Bipolar Disorder is highly heritable, with twin and family studies showing a concordance rate of 70% amongst identical twins. Multiple susceptibility genes are likely to be involved but have not have been fully elucidated. Stress and traumatic experiences can trigger an episode of Bipolar Disorder but episodes can also arise without any trigger.

## Presentation

Children and adolescents with Bipolar Disorder can show a continuum of symptoms from sub-syndromal to full syndromal, with more frequent mood episodes as well mixed mood symptoms (when compared to adults). A sub-syndromal presentation can manifest with an insufficient symptom count (as per above criteria) or insufficient duration of symptoms. Hence, initial presentation in youth may not fulfil diagnosis for a Bipolar I or II disorder but may be classified as Other Specified Bipolar and Related Disorders (BD-OD) or a Bipolar Spectrum disorder. The most common presentation in youth is a sub-syndromal presentation. Retrospective studies in adults with Bipolar Disorder have reported that 10–20% had the onset before 10 years of age and up to 60% before the age of 20. As such, Bipolar Disorder often initially presents before adulthood with possible sub-threshold symptoms that can make diagnosis challenging. Untreated Bipolar Disorder in youth is associated with poor academic performance, impaired family and peer relationships, chronic mood symptoms, disruptive behaviour disorders, anxiety disorders, substance misuse disorders, hospitalisation, suicide attempts and completed suicides. Approximately 50% of youth with sub-syndromal presentation convert into Bipolar I Disorder or Bipolar II disorder later on in life (Axelson *et al.*, 2011).

## Diagnosis and Treatment

Refer patients with suspected Bipolar Disorder to see a Child and Adolescent Psychiatrist.

Psychoeducation is important to help the young person and family understand the cause, varying symptoms, course of the illness, treatments and the consequences on different areas of life if untreated.

In terms of pharmacotherapy, the main aim is to stabilise the mood and prevent relapses. Second-generation atypical antipsychotics, sodium valproate, lamotrigine and lithium have been found to be efficacious. Antidepressants, like the SSRIs, should be used with caution in depressive episodes within a Bipolar Disorder as they may precipitate a manic episode.

In terms of psychosocial treatments, individual psychotherapy can be helpful to enhance insight into the disorder, sleep hygiene (poor sleep can trigger a relapse), problem-solving skills and self-care. Working with the family to enhance their understanding of the illness as well as any communication difficulties can also be beneficial. Liaison with

b4854_Sec-21.indd 715

12-Dec-22 11:14:02 AM

schools can help adjust academic expectations, provide additional academic and pastoral support.

### Conclusion

In conclusion, Bipolar Disorder in children and adolescents can present with sub-threshold symptoms with more frequent mood episodes compared to adults. Such presentations can be highly impactful, both in terms of the experience of the mood symptoms and how they affect daily function, academic performance, family and peer relationships.

## Depression

### Introduction

Depression is a mood disorder with core symptoms consisting of pervasive and persistent sadness, loss of interest and enjoyment. In children and adolescents, irritability may also be a core symptom. Other associated symptoms include decreased concentration, sleep disturbance, change of appetite or weight, excessive fatigue, excessive guilt, increased tiredness, psychomotor agitation or retardation, thoughts or acts of self-harm, suicidal thoughts or behaviours. Both ICD-10 and DSM-V require that symptoms be present for more than 2 weeks and have significant impact on functioning.

Depression can be categorised into severity depending on the symptoms and level of impairment in functioning. Subtypes include Major Depressive Disorder being the commonest, Dysthymia, which is a chronic milder depression lasting over a year, and psychotic depression, which is rare before mid-adolescence and is a more severe form of depression with psychotic symptoms. However, even milder forms of depression in adolescence are a risk factor for depression in adulthood.

In childhood, prevalence in various studies reported between 0.5 and 2.5% with an equal male to female ratio, whereas in adolescence, prevalence increases to between 2 and 8% with a female to male ratio of 2 to 1.

### Aetiology

- Predisposing factors
  - Genetic: Increased risk when there is a family history of depression or bipolar mood disorder
  - Gender: Equal risk for males or females before puberty, subsequently, increased risk in females
  - Temperament: Child's cognitive or behavioural style, e.g. tendency to blame self
- Precipitating Factors
  - Environmental: Stressful life events, including parental ill health and/or family conflict, bullying, physical, emotional and/or sexual abuse
- Perpetuating Factors
  - Ongoing stressors
  - Delay in seeking help due to lack of understanding, stigma, etc.
  - Comorbid disorders, e.g. anxiety or conduct disorder, substance abuse

# **Assessment**

- • Parental Interview
  - » Enquiry about presenting concerns, presence of symptoms of depression, premorbid functioning, assess any impairment of functioning (deterioration in academic results, social withdrawal, etc.), risk assessment (enquiry about deliberate self-harm, suicidal ideation or behaviour)
  - » Presence of physical symptoms to suggest underlying organic illness
  - » Any medication prescribed that may be impacting on mood
  - » Ask about behavioural changes or reported symptoms to suggest presence of mood swings, psychotic symptoms or other comorbid psychiatric disorder
  - » Family history, including psychiatric illness such as depression or bipolar disorder
  - » Family functioning, stressors
  - » History of abuse or neglect
  - » Somatic symptoms: Loss of appetite, sleep disturbance, decreased energy, headaches, chest or abdominal pain
- • Separate interview with the child or adolescent
  - » Confirm information obtained from parental interview
  - » Elicit any relevant information that the child may not be comfortable discussing in front of parents, any history of abuse or neglect, alcohol or substance misuse
  - » Assess mental state, ask about self-harm and suicidal ideation, presence of psychotic symptoms
- • Information from school
  - » Past and current functioning, academically and socially
- • General physical examination
  - » Assess if reporting symptoms suggestive of a physical illness as well as to exclude a physical cause of depression
- • Differential diagnosis
  - » Organic illness, Bipolar Mood Disorder, Psychosis, Eating Disorder
- • Comorbidity
  - » Anxiety Disorder, Conduct Disorder, Psychosis, Obsessive-compulsive Disorder, Drug and alcohol abuse
- • Investigations
  - » Baseline blood investigations: Full blood count (FBC), renal and liver profile, thyroid function test

# **Treatment**

Biopsychosocial interventions include healthy diet and exercise.

Psychological: Mild depression may improve with psychoeducation and supportive counselling as well as addressing any environmental stressors, including those within the family or in school.

With at least moderate or more severe depression, presence of active suicidal intent or severe self-harm should give rise to consideration of whether inpatient psychiatric care is required for crisis intervention. If inpatient admission is not deemed necessary, referral

b4854\_Sec-21.indd 717

12-Dec-22 11:14:02 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

for individual cognitive behavioural or interpersonal therapy is recommended. Medication may also be prescribed concomitantly specially in moderate to severe depression.

Pharmacological: Antidepressants should ideally be used alongside psychological therapy. SSRIs such as fluoxetine and escitalopram are usually prescribed after psychoeducation about the fact that medication takes two to four weeks to take effect. Side effects to look out for include nausea, headache, abdominal pain, insomnia, increased agitation and rarely worsening of suicidal ideation. The risk of suicidal ideation is far outweighed by the benefit conferred by treatment with antidepressants. Nevertheless, a careful discussion about safety and contingency planning is done with the teenager and carers prior to starting on antidepressant medication.

A review appointment will usually be offered within a month to monitor for side effects. If pharmacological treatment is effective, it is usually continued for at least 6 months to a year after symptoms have remitted and normal functioning is achieved. Subsequently, a joint decision is ideally made by the young person and parent in agreement with the psychiatrist about an appropriate time to gradually reduce and stop the medication.

## Schizophrenia Spectrum and Other Psychotic Disorders

### Introduction

Psychosis is a mental illness characterised by positive and negative symptoms that affects an individual's functioning. Psychosis in the younger population is associated with more severe symptomatology and poorer functioning. Early detection and treatment can help with overall prognosis. Treatment entails biological, psychological and social components.

### Epidemiology

The worldwide prevalence of early-onset psychosis (defined as onset before 18 years old) has been estimated at 0.5% of the population across epidemiologic studies, and that of childhood-onset (defined as onset before 13 years old), psychosis is even lower, at about 1 in 10000.

### Pathogenesis

Neurochemical abnormality hypothesis is characterised by imbalances of various neurotransmitters, most commonly that of dopamine and, hence, the use of antidopaminergic agents to treat psychosis. Other neurotransmitters, which are postulated to be involved include glutamate, serotonin and GABA. Genetic vulnerability, environmental factors, obstetric complications, trauma, social adversities and substance use are other factors that may contribute to the risk of acquiring a psychotic illness.

### Symptoms

Diagnosis of schizophrenia is made based on the DSM-V classification and is characterised by at least two of the following symptoms, with at least one being (1), (2) or (3) present during a one-month period:

b4854_Sec-21.indd 718

12-Dec-22 11:14:02 AM

# *Mental Health*

1. Delusions (which are defined as firmly held false belief that is in contrary to that of the wider community)
2. Hallucinations (which can happen in any of the five sensory modalities but more commonly auditory and visual)
3. Disorganised speech
4. Grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour
5. Negative symptoms (which are defined as diminished emotional expressions)

There also needs to be disruption of functioning in various aspects such as work, education or self-care for at least a significant portion of a 6-month period and it is below what was previously achieved.

# **Differential Diagnoses**

Other diagnoses to consider when a child presents with the above symptoms may include the following:

- Medical diseases that may present with psychotic symptomatology
  - Seizure disorder
  - Encephalitis
  - Central nervous system tumour
  - Chromosomal abnormalities (e.g. 22q11.2 deletion syndromes)
  - Substance or medication induced
  - Autoimmune disorder, e.g. systemic lupus erythematosus
  - Metabolic disorder
- Delirium
  - Characterised by waxing and waning symptoms associated with evidence of substance ingestion, metabolic derangement, etc.
- Non-psychotic children
  - Pseudo-psychotic symptoms such as delusional thinking, hallucinations and looseness of association may occur in healthy non-psychotic children, especially before age 6. History, including decline in cognitive functioning, attention and presence of negative symptoms, would be more indicative of psychosis.
- Post-traumatic stress disorder
  - Symptoms of hypervigilance and flashback may be misinterpreted as paranoia and hallucinations.
- Autism spectrum disorder (ASD)
  - Impairment of social communication, which occurs in ASD may be misinterpreted as negative symptoms. Understanding the developmental history and baseline thought processes would be useful to distinguish between the two.
- Mood disorders
  - Careful history looking at mood episodes would be useful.

# **Assessment**

A thorough medical history and physical examination is important in the assessment of all patients presenting with psychotic symptoms.

b4854_Sec-21.indd 719

12-Dec-22 11:14:02 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

- Systematic features to screen for:

Neurological: Headache, recent head injury, abnormal movements and/or coordination, hearing or visual impairment, fits or fainting, altered consciousness.
Respiratory: Dyspnoea
Cardiovascular: Chest pain, palpitations
Gastrointestinal: Nausea, vomiting
Genitourinary: Urinary symptoms, menstrual history for females

- A good mental state examination and history will help elicit pertinent features.

Psychotic symptoms

Hallucinations: Nature/description, modality, timing and frequency, intensity and impact of the experiences
Beliefs: Odd or bizarre beliefs, paranoid or persecutory content; determine intensity of beliefs (fleeting thoughts vs. overvalued ideas vs. delusional intensity)
Other phenomena: Control or passivity phenomenon (motor, volition, thought insertion/broadcast)

- Mood screening, including anxiety symptoms

Other functional aspects

Sleep disruptions
□ Appetite changes
Daily self-care and activities (e.g. going to school)
Orientation
□ Attention and concentration
Screen for cognitive function and memory problems (this would be more suggestive of organic disease)

Physical examination

A full physical examination is essential in the initial work-up.
This includes a neurological examination, particularly for:

Gait changes
Motor and sensory deficits
Coordination issues (e.g. hand-eye coordination)

- Investigations

Investigations will further aid in diagnosis and management of patients presenting with psychosis.
Blood tests

Routine - FBC, renal panel and electrolytes (including calcium), liver function test, thyroid function test, glucose.
Autoimmune panel, if psychotic symptoms are accompanied by neurological signs, antecedent systemic symptoms and/or other suspicions of autoimmunity

# *Mental Health*

☐ Consider if indicated — parathyroid hormone, tumour markers, 24-hr cortisol/catecholamines
» Urinary tests
☐ Microscopy and culture, if screening for infection
☐ Drug screen (if suspicious for substance use, particularly stimulants and cannabis)
» Radiological/Procedural
☐ CT or MRI Brain
☐ CXR, when there is suspicion of comorbid respiratory/cardiovascular issues
☐ EEG if indicated (Temporal Lobe Epilepsy, in particular, has associations with psychosis)

# **Treatment**

Initial treatment is largely determined by risk assessment. Risks include:

- The risk they currently pose to themselves — self harm, reputational, impact on family and peer relationships, school going, forensic/police involvement
- Risk of violence — the nature of risk and any association with current
- Degree of insight retained
- Whether transfer to a psychiatric ward is warranted to assess and manage the acute symptoms (with or without use of the Mental Health Care and Treatment Act [MHCT])
- Also consider the child's current social circumstances and the level of support available to them, including the need for rehabilitation or re-socialisation arrangements

If starting treatment inpatient

- First line includes use of second-generation antipsychotic (SGA), e.g. olanzapine, risperidone, quetiapine at an effective dose
- Use long-acting BDZ, e.g. diazepam to control anxiety/behavioural disturbance
- Other options: Low-potency first-generation antipsychotic (FGA), depot injections (rare)

Provide information and discuss the benefits vs. possible side effects of each drug, including:

- Metabolic (including weight gain and diabetes)
- Extra-pyramidal (including akathisia, dyskinesia and dystonia)
- Cardiovascular (including prolonged QT interval)
- Hormonal (including increasing prolactin levels)
- Other (including unpleasant subjective experiences)

Consideration to treat other symptoms such as mood or anxiety with antidepressants or anxiolytics.

# CHAPTER 104

# Chronic Cough

Biju Thomas

## Introduction

Cough is one of the commonest symptoms with which children present for medical attention. Up to 10% of preschool and primary school children may have chronic cough without wheeze at some point in time.

## Definition

**Acute cough** due to common infective causes usually resolve by 3–4 weeks. Occasionally, post infective cough may last longer (3–8 weeks) as in the case of pertussis or **post-viral cough**. Young children may get recurrent viral respiratory tract infections (up to 6–8 episodes per year) causing **recurrent cough**, and this may be confused with chronic cough, if the symptom-free intervals between episodes are short. Although **chronic cough** may be defined as a cough lasting longer than 4 weeks, a definition using a duration of more than 8 weeks may be more appropriate in identifying children with chronic cough that may require further investigations. Chronic cough may be classified into **specific cough** (in which there is an identifiable cause) and **non-specific cough** (persistent isolated dry cough in otherwise well children with no signs of respiratory disease and normal investigations).

## Evaluation

The differential diagnoses for chronic cough in children is wide-ranging. The diagnosis depends on meticulous and accurate history-taking and targeted investigations based on specific pointers in the history. A **detailed history** addressing the following questions should be undertaken.

How and when did the cough start?
- What is the nature (e.g. brassy, croupy, barking, paroxysmal, staccato, bizarre and honking) and quality (dry or wet) of the cough?
- Is the cough an isolated symptom? If not, what are the associated symptoms?
- Are there any obvious triggers (e.g. exercise, feeding) or diurnal variation?
- Is there a family history of respiratory symptoms, disorders or atopy (asthma, allergic rhinitis, eczema)?
- Is there any contact history with tuberculosis (TB) or other infectious diseases?

# Chronic Cough

- Is the child on any long-term or regular medication, what treatment has the child had for the cough and what effect have they had on the cough frequency and severity?
- Is there exposure to tobacco smoke?
- Does the cough cease when asleep or when distracted?

**Physical examination:** Children with chronic cough should have a thorough physical examination that includes an assessment of growth (weight, height, body mass index), ENT and respiratory examination. A normal physical examination DOES NOT rule out significant pathology. Certain features in the history or examination raise suspicion of serious underlying respiratory disease, and these are listed in Table 22.1.

Table 22.2 lists the causes of chronic cough in children in relation to pointers in the history suggestive of specific aetiology and suggested investigations. A simplified algorithm for the assessment and management of the common causes of chronic cough is given in Figure 22.1.

**Management of chronic-specific cough** depends on the underlying aetiology. Detailed description of the assessment and management of various causes of chronic-specific cough is beyond the scope of this chapter.

#

# Chronic Cough

# Chronic Non-Specific Isolated Cough

This term denotes a chronic isolated dry cough that may be seen in a significant number of otherwise well children, with no signs/symptoms of underlying significant illness and normal investigations. The cause is usually not identifiable, though a small proportion of such children may have asthma. Some may have heightened cough receptor sensitivity (e.g. post-viral cough). If the cough is not troublesome, parental reassurance with a period of watchful observation with no diagnostic tests or treatment can be considered. These children need careful follow-up to ensure that their cough is resolved and no specific pointers have developed. Empirical anti-asthma, anti-allergic rhinitis or anti-gastroesophageal reflux therapies are unlikely of benefit and are generally not recommended. If a trial of empirical therapy is commenced, careful follow-up and assessment of response to therapy in a time-framed manner are warranted and the treatment should be stopped if the response is questionable.

3. Gupta A, McKean M, Chang AB. (2007) Management of chronic non-specific cough in childhood: An evidence-based review. Arch Dis Child Educ Pract Ed 92(2):33-39.

4. Chang AB, Glomb WB. (2006) Guidelines for evaluating chronic cough in pediatrics: ACCP evidence-based clinical practice guidelines. Chest 129(1 Suppl):260S-283S.

5. Shields MD, Bush A, Everarad ML, et al. (2008) BTS guidelines: Recommendations for the assessment and management of cough in children. Thorax 63(3):iii1-iii15.

# CHAPTER 105

# Asthma

Teoh Oon Hoe, Chay Oh Moh

## Definition

- Heterogeneous disease characterised by chronic airway inflammation
- Characterised by a history of respiratory symptoms of wheeze, shortness of breath, chest tightness and cough that vary over time and in intensity, associated with variable expiratory airflow limitation

## Diagnosis

- Often based on a thorough history and physical examination in young children
- Therapeutic response to bronchodilator and/or controller treatment or the presence of bronchodilator response on spirometry in older children may further help to establish the diagnosis
- Asthma should be considered in individuals with the following typical features, especially if there is positive family history of asthma or personal history of atopy:

More than one symptom of wheeze, shortness of breath, cough or chest tightness
Symptoms often worse at night or in the early morning
Symptoms vary over time and in intensity
Symptoms triggered by viral infections, exercise, allergens, weather changes, laughter, smoke or pollution

- For children below 5 years old, a probability-based approach to diagnosis is suggested by the Global Initiative for Asthma (GINA) guideline as episodic wheezing and cough are also common in young children without asthma, especially those below 2 years old. In children with the following symptom pattern, GINA suggests that most would have asthma:

Personal history of atopy (allergic sensitisation, atopic dermatitis, food allergy) or family history of asthma
More than three episodes of wheeze per year or severe episodes and/or night worsening
Between episodes, child has cough, wheeze or heavy breathing during play or when laughing
Symptoms (cough, wheeze, heavy breathing) for more than 10 days during upper respiratory tract infections

# Asthma

731

- For children who have atypical symptoms/signs or do not respond as expected to therapy, especially those below 1 year old, differential diagnoses need to be considered and excluded

## Differential Diagnoses

Recurrent viral wheeze
Pulmonary tuberculosis
- Recurrent aspiration (e.g. gastroesophageal reflux, swallowing dysfunction)
- Chronic rhinitis/sinusitis
Foreign body inhalation
- Chronic lung disease (e.g. bronchiectasis, interstitial lung disease, bronchopulmonary dysplasia)
Airway structural abnormalities (e.g. tracheobronchomalacia, tracheobronchial stenosis)
Airway compression/obstruction (e.g. mediastinal mass, vascular rings, bronchial tumours)
- Genetic disorders (e.g. cystic fibrosis, primary ciliary dyskinesia)
Congenital or acquired immunodeficiency
- Cardiac conditions (e.g. heart failure, congenital heart disease)
Vocal cord dysfunction, hyperventilation, dysfunctional breathing
Pulmonary embolism, pulmonary arterial hypertension

## History

- Personal history of atopy — allergic rhinitis, eczema, allergic conjunctivitis
- Family history of atopy — asthma, allergic rhinitis, eczema, allergic conjunctivitis
- Triggers (e.g. viral infections, house dust mites, animal dander, tobacco smoke, pollution, physical activity, stress)
- Exposure to environmental tobacco smoke, smoking history in adolescents
- Assessment of asthma control/severity

Frequency and severity of exacerbations — use of written asthma action plan (WAAP), level of medical care attended
Daytime and nocturnal asthma symptoms
Physical activity-related asthma symptoms, activity limitation due to asthma
Reliever medication use
Questionnaire-based asthma control tests (e.g. Asthma Control Test, Asthma Control Questionnaire, Test for Respiratory and Asthma Control in Kids)

Medications

Understanding of asthma action plan, controller and reliever medications prescribed
Adherence to controller medications
Reasons for non-adherence (e.g. steroid phobia, child/parental over-estimation of asthma control, inconvenient medication regime)

# The Baby Bear Book

» Inhaler technique assessment

- Assess co-morbid conditions (e.g. allergic rhinitis, eczema, allergic conjunctivitis, obesity)
- 'Red flag' symptoms (e.g. chronic moist/productive cough, symptoms onset after choking episode, noisy inspiration, persistent tachypnea even at rest, syncopal episodes)

## Clinical Examination

- Growth parameters — obesity, failure to thrive ('red flag' sign)
- Signs of co-morbid atopic conditions (e.g. dry eczematous skin, pale and edematous nasal turbinates, infraorbital shiners)
Signs of chronicity — Harrison's sulci, barrel chest
Air entry, wheeze and bronchodilator response, signs of respiratory distress
Signs of complications of treatment oral thrush, reduction in linear growth, Cushingoid features (long-term systemic or high-dose inhaled corticosteroids [ICS])
- 'Red flag' signs (e.g. clubbing, coarse crepitations, cardiac murmur, loud second heart sound, stridor, biphasic wheeze)

## Investigations

- In children above 5 to 6 years old, peak expiratory flow assessment and spirometry can be helpful for diagnosis at presentation and an assessment of asthma control on follow-up.
- Other investigations are not mandatory for the asthma diagnosis but are important for the exclusion of differential diagnoses, especially in young children or in the presence of 'red flag' symptoms and signs.

Chest X-ray — useful for exclusion of differential diagnoses (e.g. foreign body, pneumothorax, bronchiectasis, persistent lobar collapse, lymphadenopathy).
Peak expiratory flow (PEF) assessment — performed with a peak flow meter. A PEF \(>80\%\) of predicted PEF for height is considered to be within normal limits, but it is important to take into consideration the personal best and the trend of percentage-predicted PEF on serial measurements. Serial measurements may provide a better objective assessment of response to treatment.
Spirometry — bronchodilator response with an increase of forced expiratory volume in 1 second (FEV1) of more than \(12\%\) suggests a diagnosis of asthma or partly controlled/uncontrolled asthma in a child with a clinical presentation consistent with asthma. FEV1 has greater sensitivity that PEF. Advise to avoid the use of bronchodilators before performing spirometry (6 hours before for short-acting beta-agonists [SABA], and 12 to 24 hours before for long-acting beta-agonists [LABA]).

# Asthma

» Bronchial provocation tests — mannitol/exercise challenge tests are not routine investigations. They may be useful in the clinical setting when spirometry is inconclusive for asthma or the evaluation of asthma as the cause of exercise-induced symptoms when spirometry is normal. Refer to a paediatric pulmonologist for bronchial provocation tests.
» Fractional concentration of exhaled nitric oxide (FeNO) — FeNO is elevated in eosinophilic asthma, but also in non-asthma conditions (e.g. allergic rhinitis). FeNO may help guide diagnosis and management of asthma in certain situations, but there is no established guideline available at this point to recommend routine use.
» Skin prick tests — assess for sensitisation to allergens, e.g. house dust mites, cat dander, dog dander, mould. May be useful for advising trigger avoidance/interventions. Advise to stop the use of antihistamines 7 days before a skin prick test.

## Assessment of Asthma Severity, Control and Risk Factors

- The GINA asthma control assessment includes asthma symptom control assessment and an assessment of future risk of adverse outcomes — refer to the current GINA guideline for details and updates.
» Asthma symptom control assessment is based on daytime and nocturnal asthma symptoms, reliever medication use, and activity limitation, and classified as 'well controlled', 'partly controlled' or 'uncontrolled'.
» Important risk factors for poor asthma outcomes in children include low FEV1 (especially if <60% predicted), smoking or exposure to tobacco smoke, poor treatment adherence, history of severe exacerbation, Children's Intensive Care Unit (CICU) admission or intubation. Having one or more risk factors increases the risk of exacerbations even if symptoms are well controlled.
» The GINA guideline advocates that asthma severity be assessed retrospectively from the level of treatment required to control symptoms and exacerbations.

## Asthma Medications and Management

- All children diagnosed with asthma should be prescribed reliever medications, e.g. salbutamol metered dose inhalers (MDI) for use when having asthma symptoms/exacerbations.
- For safety reasons, the GINA and the Agency of Care Effectiveness (ACE) guidelines for asthma do not recommend treatment for asthma for adults, adolescents and children (6 years old and above) with SABA alone without an ICS controller medication.
» ICS controller medication can be administered either as regular daily treatment or in mild asthma with as-needed SABA whenever needed for asthma symptoms.

b4854_Sec-22.indd 733

12-Dec-22 11:14:34 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

- In adults and adolescents with mild asthma, as-needed ICS-formoterol can be administered as reliever therapy when needed for asthma symptoms. In adults, adolescents and children 6 years old and above with moderate to severe asthma, ICS-formoterol is an option for both daily maintenance and as-needed reliever therapy, referred to in short as MART.

- Children whose asthma symptom control is assessed as 'partly controlled' or 'uncontrolled' should be initiated on treatment with regular daily controller medications, e.g. ICS, leukotriene receptor antagonists (LTRA), or ICS/LABA combination therapy.

- In children 5 years old and below, regular controller therapy (e.g. ICS, LTRA) should be considered if the asthma symptoms are not controlled, if wheezing episodes are frequent (e.g. 3 or more episodes within 3 to 6 months) or if wheezing episodes are less frequent but more severe (e.g. 2 episodes requiring hospitalisation within a few months).

- The controller medications commonly used are ICS (fluticasone propionate or budesonide), ICS/LABA combination therapy (salmeterol/fluticasone or formoterol/budesonide combination), and oral LTRA (montelukast). When considering montelukast, weigh the benefits against the risks before prescribing, and counsel patients/parents about the potential neuropsychiatric adverse effects on sleep and behaviour.

- The inhaled controller medications can be in form of MDIs or dry power inhalers (DPI), e.g. accuhaler or turbuhaler.

- The use of a spacer device with the MDI helps to deliver more medication to the lungs, decreases oropharyngeal deposition and addresses the challenge of coordinating MDI actuation and inhalation at the right time for children. Spacer devices are recommended for all children who are prescribed ICS in the form of MDIs.

Children below 6 years old should always use their MDI together with spacers, while children above 6 years old have the additional option of DPIs to choose from if they are able to use the DPIs correctly.
In children less than 3 to 4 years old, the spacer should be used with a face mask.

- Asthma education is an integral part of asthma management.

The child and the caregiver must be educated on the disease, triggers and trigger avoidance and the use of the types of medication prescribed.
The safety and importance of adherence to controller therapy needs to be emphasised, and the correct inhaler technique and maintenance of spacer devices need to be taught to the child and their caregiver.

- Individualised WAAP should be given and explained to each child and caregiver, to empower them to manage mild exacerbations at home and provide instructions on when they should seek medical attention.

- Smoking cessation advice and advice on setting up a smoke-free environment for the child should be given if the child/caregiver smokes or is exposed to environmental tobacco smoke.

b4854_Sec-22.indd 734

12-Dec-22 11:14:34 AM

## Review of Controller Therapy

- • Review response to treatment within 1–3 months after initiating controller therapy.
  - » Subsequent reviews can be every 3–6 months if there is good response.
  - » Younger children, children with uncontrolled asthma or children requiring high step controller therapy should be reviewed at closer intervals.
- • If there is no or poor response, review controller adherence, inhaler device technique, ongoing trigger factor exposure, co-morbidity as contributory factors, before considering stepping up controller therapy.
- • In general, if the asthma is partly controlled, consider stepping up controller therapy, and if the asthma is uncontrolled, controller therapy should be stepped up.
  - » The step of the controller therapy the child is at, the duration on the step of controller therapy, and the general trend of asthma control should be taken into consideration before stepping up controller therapy. For example, a 7-year-old child with a history of monthly hospitalisations for asthma started on and adherent with Step 4 controller therapy for 1 month may not need a further step up in controller therapy, even though his asthma may still be classified as 'uncontrolled' because of an attendance to the Children's Emergency (CE) for mild exacerbation 1 week before the clinic review.
- • Review if the diagnosis of asthma is correct if treatment response is persistently poor or worsening or if there are 'red flag' signs or symptoms.
- • If good asthma control is achieved and maintained for at least 3–6 months, controller therapy may be reduced stepwise to the lowest step required to maintain control.
  - » A child may lose asthma control when controller therapy is stepped down or ceased, so advice needs to be given to the child and caregivers to recognise symptoms of loss of asthma control and a follow-up review scheduled, so that controller therapy can be restarted or stepped up if there is worsening of asthma control.

## High Risk Asthma

A small group of children may have asthma with high disease burden. They have frequent acute care attendances or admissions, and are at increased risk of asthma morbidity and mortality, and should be managed under the close supervision of paediatric pulmonologists. Following the 2008 Ministry of Health (MOH) Singapore's Management of Asthma Clinical Practice Guidelines, children at our institution with asthma who meet the following criteria are referred to a paediatric pulmonologist:

1. 1. Poor asthma control
   1. a. Two or more exacerbations per month needing acute care b.  
      Two or more hospitalisations for asthma within 3 months c.  
      Requires reliever medication three times or more per week

d. Severe exacerbation needing High Dependency Unit/CICU care
e. Other risk factors (e.g. poor socioeconomic background, recalcitrant controller medication non-adherence)

2. Below 3 years and requires medium to high doses of ICS and not responding as expected
3. Requires high dose ICS
4. On prolonged low to moderate ICS for more than 6 months and remains symptomatic

## Management of Acute Exacerbations

Refer to your institution's Asthma Clinical Pathway for management of acute exacerbations at the CE and in the ward. The general principles of management in the outpatient setting are as follows:

- Assess severity of the exacerbation — refer to Table 22.3 for a guide on assessing the severity of asthma exacerbation.
- Consider and exclude alternative diagnoses, associated complications, especially if 'red flag' history, symptoms or signs are present, e.g. foreign body aspiration, pneumonia, pneumothorax.
- Maintain oxygenation — maintain \(\mathrm{SpO}_2 \geq 95\%\) with oxygen supplementation
- Relieve bronchospasm with intensive bronchodilator therapy

Salbutamol MDI 10 puffs (5 puffs if child \(< 10\mathrm{kg}\)) with ipratropium bromide MDI 4 puffs (2 puffs if child \(< 10\mathrm{kg}\)) every 20 minutes up to 3 cycles for mild to moderate exacerbation (As per our institution's CE Asthma Clinical Pathway)
Nebulised continuous \(0.5\%\) salbutamol solution \(1\mathrm{ml}\) \((0.5\mathrm{ml}\) if child \(< 10\mathrm{kg})\) with \(0.025\%\) ipratropium bromide solution \(1\mathrm{ml}\) \((0.5\mathrm{ml}\) if child \(< 10\mathrm{kg})\) with at least \(6\mathrm{L / min}\) oxygen if severe exacerbation with \(\mathrm{SpO}_2 < 90\%\) on oxygen supplementation (As per our institution's CE Asthma Clinical Pathway)
If good response to treatment and meets the discharge criteria \((\mathrm{SpO}_2 \geq 95\%)\) in room air, mild end expiratory or no wheeze, mild or no retractions, not tachypnoeic on 4 hourly inhaled bronchodilator, adequate hydration and nutrition), discharge with salbutamol MDI 4 hourly (0.3 puffs/kg, minimum 2 puffs, maximum 8 puffs), to taper over a few days to as-needed basis. A WAAP should be provided.
» Arrange for admission, or refer/transfer to an Emergency Department if severe exacerbation on presentation, deterioration despite treatment, or does not respond well enough to meet discharge criteria.
For severe exacerbation presenting at a hospital setting, may consider additional treatments — intravenous (IV) magnesium sulphate, IV salbutamol or IV aminophylline — refer to your institution's Asthma Clinical Pathway.

- Relieve underlying inflammation

- Oral prednisolone (1–2 mg/kg/day, maximum 40 mg/day) for moderate to severe exacerbations, for 3 to 5 days. Refer to your institution's Asthma Clinical Pathway for other indications for initiating oral corticosteroids.

# CHAPTER 106

# Common Respiratory Conditions

Arun Pugalenthi, Teoh Oon Hoe

## Croup (Acute Laryngotracheobronchitis)

- Croup is inflammation of the upper airway, larynx and trachea.
- Most commonly caused by the parainfluenza virus. Other viruses include the respiratory syncytial virus and adenovirus.
- Affects children 6 months to 5 years old, peak age between 6 months and 2 years old.
Usually begins with symptoms of upper respiratory tract infection and fever, followed by a gradual onset of hoarseness, harsh 'barking' cough and stridor over 1 to 2 days.
- Croup symptoms usually worsen at night. The stridor usually worsens with crying or agitation. Symptoms resolve usually over a few days to a week.
- 'Red flag' symptoms and signs — sudden onset, toxic looking, drooling or dysphagia — consider alternative diagnosis, e.g. acute epiglottitis, retropharyngeal abscess, bacterial tracheitis, foreign body aspiration, angioedema.

## Assessment

Assessment of severity is based on the Westley croup score:

|  Inspiratory stridor | Not present | 0  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|   |  When agitated | 1  |
|   |  At rest | 2  |
|  Intercostal recession | Mild | 1  |
|   |  Moderate | 2  |
|   |  Severe | 3  |
|  Air entry | Normal | 0  |
|   |  Mildly decreased | 1  |
|   |  Severely decreased | 2  |
|  Cyanosis | None | 0  |
|   |  With agitation | 4  |
|   |  At rest | 5  |
|  Level of consciousness | Normal | 0  |
|   |  Altered | 5  |

# Common Respiratory Conditions

2 or less suggests mild croup
3-7 indicates moderate croup
8 or higher indicates severe croup

- Regardless of the severity, all children with croup should be approached in a calm manner.

- Keep the child comfortable and avoid unnecessary distress.
- Avoid examination of the throat.
- Assess the degree of airway obstruction, not the loudness of stridor.
  - Beware of quietening stridor and increasing respiratory distress.
  - Assess work of breathing and effectiveness of respiration, look for recessions, tiring and falling of oxygen saturations.
- Biphasic stridor, dysphonia, drooling and dysphagia are all concerning.
- Clinical deterioration and altering consciousness is a sign of worsening severe upper airway obstruction.

## Management

- Mild croup

- Oral dexamethasone 0.15 mg/kg stat dose. Observe for 2 hours and re-assess Westley croup score. An additional dose of 0.45 mg/kg can be given, with consideration for discharge if Westley croup score <2 after another 2 hours.

Moderate croup

Oral dexamethasone \(0.6\mathrm{mg / kg}\)
Supplemental oxygen if \(\mathrm{SpO}_2 < 95\%\)
» Nebulised adrenaline (1:1000) 3ml:1ml normal saline if Westley croup score 4 or higher

Severe croup

» Nebulised adrenaline (1:1000) 3ml:1ml normal saline
Supplemental oxygen if \(\mathrm{SpO}_2 < 95\%\)
» Intramuscular/intravenous (IV) dexamethasone \(0.6\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (max. \(12\mathrm{mg}\))
» Consider intubation if altered sensorium or impending respiratory failure
» Admit to Children's Intensive Care Unit (CICU)

- Admitted for mild to moderate croup

» Minimal handling
» Adequate hydration and nutrition — small frequent oral feeding, or IV hydration if required.
Supplemental oxygen if \(\mathrm{SpO}_2 < 95\%\)
» Monitor for worsening or recurrence of symptoms — if frequent repeated doses of nebulised adrenaline are needed, transfer the child to CICU.

## Criteria for Discharge

Consider discharge if:

- Child not toxic, has adequate hydration and good oral intake.
- No stridor at rest, or minimal stridor only when agitated.

- Parents confident of care — advise caregivers to monitor closely and return if worsening of symptoms occurs.

Refer to your institution's Children Emergency (CE) Guidelines for further details for step-wise management.

## Bronchiolitis

- Bronchiolitis is a viral lower respiratory tract infection.
- It is most commonly seen in infants between 3 and 6 months of age but can present in children up to 2 years.
- Most commonly caused by the respiratory syncytial virus. Other viruses include rhinovirus, parainfluenza virus, human metapneumovirus and adenovirus.
- Usually begins with symptoms of upper respiratory tract infection and fever, progressing to tachypnoea, wheezing and respiratory distress over a few days. Crepitations and/or wheezing are present on auscultation.
- Symptoms tend to worsen over the first 3-5 days of illness and peak between the 5th and 7th day of illness for most children. The symptom of cough resolves by 2-3 weeks in most children.
Diagnosis is clinical

## Risk Factors for Severe Bronchiolitis: Low Threshold for Admission and Close Monitoring

- Chronic lung disease
Congenital heart disease
Age \(< 3\) months
Congenital airway abnormality, e.g. tracheal stenosis
- Immunodeficiency
Neuromuscular disorder

## Differential Diagnoses

- Pneumonia — fever late in the course of illness, localised signs
- Pertussis — consider in children presenting with apnoea, paroxysmal coughing spells or prolonged cough
- Viral induced wheeze — in children above 1 year, especially with recurrent episodes of wheeze
Foreign body
Congenital airway anomaly
Cardiac anomaly

## Investigations

NOT routinely indicated in typical or mild disease
Full blood count only if there is concern of bacterial infection or sepsis

- Renal panel — only if there is concern with feeding, dehydration or commencing on IV fluids
- Blood culture — only if there are features of sepsis
- Viral testing — has little impact on outcome but may influence antibiotic prescription or guide isolation for infection control.
- Chest X-ray is not required for diagnosis, but indicated if

Severe illness
Diagnosis is uncertain
» Localised signs on auscultation
» Cardiac signs on examination

## Management

Management of bronchiolitis is supportive and includes:

- Minimal handling
- Supplemental oxygen to keep \(\mathrm{SpO}_2 \geq 95\%\)
- Adequate hydration and nutrition — small frequent oral feeding, nasogastric feeding or IV hydration, if required.
- Nasal suction is not routinely recommended — superficial nasal suction may be considered in those with moderate disease to ease work of breathing and improve feeding. Normal saline nasal drops can be considered.
- Do not routinely prescribe inhaled bronchodilators, nebulised adrenaline or hypertonic saline, antibiotics or oral steroids.

A trial of nebulised adrenaline (1:1000) 3ml:1ml normal saline driven by 8 L/min oxygen may be given at CE for moderate disease, with view for discharge if there is good response.
An inpatient trial of nebulised salbutamol (0.5 ml 0.5% salbutamol with 3.5 ml normal saline driven by 8 L/min oxygen) or salbutamol (100 mcg) Metered dose inhaler 2-4 puffs may be considered in children with history of atopy or in moderate /severe respiratory distress, to continue as needed 4-6 hourly ONLY if there is clinical response to intervention.
» Disease severity and response to intervention can be assessed with the aid of clinical scores for bronchiolitis, e.g. the modified Respiratory Index Score.
Refer to your institution's Clinical Pathway for details.

- Chest physiotherapy is not routinely indicated.

The signs of deterioration that should be looked for are:

- Respiratory rate \(>60 / \mathrm{min}\)
- Apnoea, bradypnoea or cyanotic episode despite supplemental oxygen.
- Persistent tachycardia.
- Drowsiness/lethargy.
- Increasing oxygen requirement to maintain oxygen saturations \(\geq 95\%\).

Consider high flow nasal cannula (HFNC) on continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) support if there is clinical deterioration or severe disease.

# CHAPTER 107

# Pneumonia, Parapneumonic Effusion and Empyema

Biju Thomas, Teoh Oon Hoe

## Pneumonia

- Lower respiratory tract infection involving the lung parenchyma.
- Affects all ages, higher incidence in children \(< 5\) years.
- Common causes of community-acquired pneumonia include viruses, Mycoplasma pneumoniae and Streptococcus pneumoniae (the most common bacterial cause). Table 22.4 summarises common infective aetiology and empiric antibiotic options for different age groups.
- Typically presents with fever, cough and tachypnoea \(\pm\) signs of respiratory distress, but symptoms vary with age and infective aetiology. Young children may present with isolated fever, malaise, altered mental state, abdominal pain or vomiting.
- Adventitious sounds on auscultation may be subtle in young children with shallow breathing.
- 'Red flags' in history, symptoms and signs: Recurrent pneumonia (e.g. immunodeficiency, congenital airway/lung abnormality), pleuritic chest pain (empyema), night sweats and weight loss (pulmonary tuberculosis [TB]), clubbing, failure to thrive, chronic wet/productive cough (bronchiectasis) and significant background medical problems (malignancies, haemodynamically significant cardiac disease, neuromuscular disease, chronic lung disease, primary/secondary immune deficiency).
- For children who need inpatient management, commonly performed investigations include Chest X-ray (CXR), Full blood count, C-reactive protein and nasopharyngeal aspirate for respiratory viruses (multiplex PCR). Additional imaging (Ultrasound [US]/Computed Tomography [CT] of the chest) may be required in complicated pneumonia.
- Full blood count and C-reactive protein may help clinical decision-making regarding the need to commence antibiotics but are not specific in differentiating infective aetiologies. Blood culture is recommended before commencing intravenous (IV) antibiotics, though the yield is very low. Other investigations (Renal panel, Urine S pneumoniae antigen and Mycoplasma serology) may be required in selected cases.

# The Baby Bear Book

- Consider repeat imaging and referral to respiratory medicine for children with persistent high fever or failure to improve/clinical deterioration despite having had 48 hours of treatment with appropriate antibiotics.
Management:

Indications for admission include significant tachypnoea, respiratory distress, \(\mathrm{SpO}_2\leq 95\%\) in room air, tachycardia for age and temperature, poor perfusion and poor feeding.
Maintain \(\mathrm{SpO}_2\geq 95\%\) with supplemental oxygen.
Supportive management: Maintain adequate hydration, control of fever.
Monitoring: Temperature, Heart rate, Respiratory rate, \(\mathrm{SpO}_2\) and Blood pressure.
> Antimicrobial therapy is guided by the age, clinical presentation, presence of drug (penicillin) allergy, co-existing medical problems, prior antibiotic treatment, local antibiotic resistance patterns, as well as travel and contact history. Table 22.4 provides guidelines for the choice of empiric antibiotic therapy for children with normal immune function. Ideally, the first dose of antibiotic should be administered within 4 hours upon hospital arrival.
Monitor serum sodium and watch for the syndrome of inappropriate secretion of anti-diuretic hormone if on IV fluids.
Indications for referral to Respiratory Medicine/Infectious Disease Service: Persistent high fever or failure to improve/clinical deterioration despite having had 48 hours of treatment with appropriate antibiotics, complicated pneumonia (significant effusion OR effusion with US features of echogenic fluid \(\pm\) loculations/empyema/necrotising pneumonia/pneumothorax/lung abscess), pre-existing significant lung disease and suspected TB/foreign body inhalation.
There is no role for chest physiotherapy in previously healthy children presenting with pneumonia.
Refer for chest physiotherapy if there is significant collapse and empyema post video-assisted thoracoscopic surgery (VATS).
Convert to oral antibiotics when the child is afebrile for 24-48 hours.
Follow-up CXR not necessary in previously well children with uncomplicated pneumonia. Indications for follow-up CXR (4-6 weeks later) include extensive atelectasis (e.g. \(\geq\) one lobe), round pneumonia, complicated pneumonia (e.g. pleural effusion, empyema, extensive pneumonia, lung abscess), past history of recurrent CXR confirmed pneumonia and persistent symptoms/auscultatory signs on follow up.

# The Baby Bear Book

# Parapneumonic Effusion and Empyema

## Definition

Parapneumonic effusion is defined as an abnormal collection of fluid in the pleural space in association with underlying pneumonia. Uncomplicated small parapneumonic effusions may be sterile exudates that often resolve with appropriate antibiotic treatment of the pneumonia. Occasionally, this could evolve into complicated parapneumonic effusions with fibrinopurulent changes and may require drainage in addition to appropriate antibiotic treatment. Empyema occurs when frank pus develops from a parapneumonic effusion, with organisation and formation of loculations and pleural peel. Empyema complicates about 1–2% of childhood pneumonia.

## Aetiology

The most common organisms are *Streptococcus pneumonia*, *Mycoplasma pneumonia* and *Staphylococcus aureus*, depending on the age of the child. In neurologically impaired children and aspiration-related pneumonia, anaerobic bacteria may be the causative organism.

## Diagnosis

Parapneumonic effusion is suspected when the CXR shows consolidation with pleural fluid accumulation in a febrile child with signs and symptoms of lower respiratory tract infection. Complicated parapneumonic effusion or empyema development should be suspected in children with persistent high fever or failure to improve/clinical deterioration despite having had 48 hours of treatment with appropriate antibiotics. Pleuritic chest pain and referred abdominal pain may be present. A referral to respiratory medicine is warranted for such children.

The choice of US thorax or CT chest for evaluation of the empyema should be discussed with the Respiratory Consultant and Paediatric Surgeon. In general, US thorax is preferred unless it is a complicated case or when surgery (VATS) is considered.

## Differential Diagnoses of Pleural Effusion

- Transudative
  - Heart failure
  - Hypoalbuminemia, e.g. nephrotic syndrome, liver disease, protein losing enteropathy, malabsorption
- Exudative
  - Pneumonia
  - TB
  - Connective tissue disease, e.g. systemic lupus erythematosus
  - Malignant effusion
  - Abdominal infection/inflammation, e.g. hepatic abscess, pancreatitis
- Chylothorax
- Other diagnoses, e.g. lung abscess, malignancy (pleuropulmonary blastoma)

b4854_Sec-22.indd 746

# Management

Refer to the Respiratory Medicine Service if a significant parapneumonic effusion (moderate/large effusion and/or echogenic fluid on imaging) or empyema is suspected. Management is often individualised, taking into consideration the clinical presentation, prior treatment, complexity of the effusion and patient factors. Figure 22.2 shows our institution's algorithm to guide management.

- Antibiotics: Empiric antimicrobial therapy is guided by the age, clinical presentation, presence of drug (penicillin) allergy, co-existing medical problems, prior antibiotic treatment, local antibiotic resistance patterns, as well as travel and contact history.
- High dose IV ampicillin is the first-line treatment if S. pneumoniae is suspected. Antibiotic choice should be reviewed once microbiological results are available or if there is poor response to the initial empiric treatment. Treatment should be IV until the child is afebrile for at least 24-48 hours and the chest drain (if

![img-37.jpeg](img-37.jpeg)

Figure 22.2 Algorithm for the management of parapneumonic effusion and empyema.

b4854_Sec-22.indd 747

12-Dec-22 11:14:35 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

inserted) removed. In most cases, this would be for at least 7–14 days depending on severity of the disease and response to treatment and drainage. This is followed with oral antibiotics, and the total duration of antibiotic treatment should be at least 3–4 weeks.

- Chest tube drainage: Large parapneumonic effusions causing respiratory distress and complicated effusions require drainage in addition to antibiotics. As an alternative to a chest drain insertion by the medical team, ultrasound guided pigtail catheter placement by the interventional radiologist may be considered if the drainage is not urgent for relief of respiratory distress or if the size and location of the effusion is better accessed safely with ultrasound guidance.
- Intrapleural fibrinolytic therapy: If the pleural fluid is turbid or if there is evidence of early loculation on chest ultrasound, consider fibrinolytic therapy following chest tube insertion. Usually, intrapleural urokinase is given twice daily for 3 days. Each dose is \(40000\mathrm{U}\) in \(40\mathrm{ml}\) normal saline (10000 U in \(10\mathrm{ml}\) normal saline if child \(< 10\mathrm{kg}\)), to leave to dwell in the pleural cavity after instillation for 4 hours before unclamping the chest tube.
- VATS: If there are significant loculations and adhesions, these need to be broken down for effective drainage of the pleural cavity. Thick pleural peels may entrap the lung and prevent re-expansion if not decorticated. VATS is the procedure of choice for these purposes and may be associated with a shorter hospital stay compared to chest tube with intrapleural fibrinolytic therapy.

# CHAPTER 108

# Joint Pain

Teh Kai Liang; Thaschawee Arkachaisri

## History

- Location/Anatomic site of pain
Duration of symptoms
Number and pattern of joint involvement
Functional impairment
- Time of the day with maximal discomfort: Morning, after activities or nocturnal
- Aggravating or relieving factors
- Associated signs or symptoms — for systemic diseases

## Clinical Examination

- Arthritis: Signs of inflammation include swelling (effusion and/or synovial thickening), warmth, tenderness, erythema (usually absent without non-infectious chronic arthritis) and limitation in range of motion

## Clinical Pearls

- Inflammatory causes: Infectious (septic) or non-infectious (reactive or idiopathic arthritis)/part of systemic diseases, malignancy associated arthritis, etc.
- Mechanical causes: Trauma, fracture, ligament or tendon tears, overuse, hypermobility, etc.
- Careful physical examination is important to identify areas of pathology. The site of pain may not correspond to the joints affected. For example, hip arthritis may be referred to as anterior thighs/knees.
- Presence of muscle atrophy usually indicates chronicity or denervation.
- Septic arthritis and trauma less likely to be polyarticular.
- Acute arthritis less than 6 weeks generally reflects aetiologies related to trauma or infection, while chronic arthritis reflects more inflammatory aetiology.
- The presence of systemic features like, fever, rashes, nail changes, oral ulcers, redness of eye and diarrhoea may suggest systemic illness.
- Arthritis with pain resulting in nocturnal night awakening should be evaluated further to rule out bone tumours or malignancy.

# Joint Pain

- Enthesitis-related arthritis is one of the most common forms of juvenile idiopathic arthritis locally. It is therefore important to ask about the presence of buttock pain, and if present, attention must be given to the examination of sacroiliac joints and entheses.
- Diagnosis of arthritis is clinical through careful physical examination. Patients with arthritis can have normal inflammatory markers and X-ray findings.

## Nocturnal Pain

- Benign nocturnal pain of childhood or “growing pains”

» Pain never occurs during the day and only at night
» Affects 4-12 years old — male almost equal to female in distribution
Usually lower limb and bilateral
» Relieved by massage and/or analgesics
» Normal joint examination and all investigations

- Bone tumours

» Benign tumours: osteoid osteoma, bone cyst, etc.
Malignant bone tumours

Primary bone malignancy

- Secondary bone tumours or metastasis — leukaemia, lymphoma, neuroblastoma

# CHAPTER 109

# Common Rheumatological Conditions

Teh Kai Liang; Thaschawee Arkachaisri

## Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis

- Idiopathic chronic arthritis that begins in childhood
- Different from adult rheumatoid arthritis (RA)
- Aetiology unknown. Genetic predisposition with environmental triggers leads to cytokine mediated inflammation (such as interleukin 1 [IL-1], interleukin 6 [IL-6] and TNF alpha). Possibly a complex genetic trait associated with human leukocyte antigen (HLA) genes and cytokine gene polymorphisms.
- In Singapore, the incidence is estimated to be 4-5/100000 children at risk, below the age of 16 years. Asian children lack the female predominance and uveitis described in the West.

## Diagnosis

- Arthritis for \(\geq 6\) weeks (joint swelling, or two out of three of pain, warmth, restriction of movement)
- Onset before the age of 16
- Exclusion of secondary causes

## International League of Associations for Rheumatology (ILAR)

### Classification

Systemic juvenile idiopathic arthritis (JIA)
Oligoarticular

Extended oligoarticular JIA
Persistent oligoarticular JIA

Polyarticular

Rheumatoid factor (RF)-positive JIA
RF-negative JIA

- Psoriatic arthritis
- Enthesitis-related arthritis
Undifferentiated JIA

b4854_Sec-23.indd 753

# The Baby Bear Book

# Systemic JIA (13% of JIA patients locally)

- Arthritis with or preceded by daily spiking fever for 2 weeks (quotidian pattern documented for at least 3 days) and one or more of the following:

Evanescent, erythematous rash
Hepato/splenomegaly
Generalised lymphadenopathy
Serositis

- May be complicated by pericarditis and macrophage activation syndrome.
Systemic features may precede the onset of arthritis.

# Polyarticular Disease (15% of JIA patients locally)

- Arthritis affecting five or more joints during the first 6 months of disease
RF-negative, typically younger age at onset.
RF-positive, usually older girls/adolescents, mimics adult RA with early erosive disease.
- The presence of RF must be documented on at least two occasions at least 3 months apart.

# Oligoarticular Disease (34% of JIA patients locally)

- Oligo-persistent disease: Classically younger girls with knee involvement, commonly antinuclear antibody (ANA) positive.
- Arthritis is usually easily treated with non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) or intra-articular steroid injection, but chronic uveitis is common and can lead to blindness if untreated.
- Oligo-extended disease, i.e. more than four joints after the first 6 months.

# Enthesitis-Related Arthritis (35% of JIA patients locally)

- Arthritis and enthesitis, or arthritis or enthesitis with at least two of the following:
Sacroiliac joint tenderness and/or inflammatory spinal pain
Presence of HLA-B27
Family history in one or more first-degree relative(s) of medically confirmed HLA-B27-associated disease
Anterior uveitis that is usually associated with pain, redness or photophobia
Onset of arthritis in a boy after 6 years of age

# Juvenile Psoriatic Arthritis (35% of JIA patients locally)

- Arthritis and psoriasis or arthritis and at least two of the following:

Dactylitis (sausage digit)
Nail pitting or onycholysis
Family history of psoriasis in a first-degree relative
» Arthritis may predate onset of psoriasis by many years

# Complications

- Limb pain, swelling, deformity
- Loss of function, independence and ambulation

b4854_Sec-23.indd 754

12-Dec-22 11:15:09 AM

# Common Rheumatological Conditions

- Contracture, limb overgrowth, limb length discrepancy
- Osteoporosis, fracture, avascular necrosis
- Growth and pubertal delay (short stature, FTT)
- Uveitis, loss of vision, blindness
- Social and financial cost — loss of schooling, social development, poor self-image, hospitalisation

## Clinical Evaluation

- History and physical examination aim to determine evidence of synovitis/ arthritis/enthesitis, exclude secondary causes for limb symptoms (especially infection, malignancy, reactive arthritis) and assess subtype of arthritis, severity and complications
History: Elicit inflammatory pain (especially morning stiffness)
- Physical Findings: Signs of chronicity, systemic features (including fever, rash, nail changes, organomegaly, lymphadenopathy, weight loss, tendonitis/enthesitis), measure limb length discrepancy
- No investigation is pathognomonic of JIA, but may be useful to rule out other differentials.
- Most patients with JIA do not have significantly raised inflammatory markers (except systemic JIA). If raised, will need to rule out other systemic illness
- Role of X-ray mainly to exclude other diseases (infection and malignancy), as bony changes in JIA are relatively late (including sclerosis, erosions, ankylosis)
- Ultrasound/MRI with contrast useful to assess hip, sacroiliac and temporomandibular joints
- Joint aspirate, if done, should be sent for cell count. Joint aspirate in JIA is neutrophil predominant, with negative gram stain and cultures.

## Management Principles

- The goal is to suppress inflammation and prevent joint deformity and disability
Physical - rest, pain relief, physiotherapy, occupational therapy
Medical therapy

Intra-articular joint injections
NSAIDs
Conventional disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs (DMARDs)
Biological DMARDs (biologics against TNF, IL-1, IL-6)
Targeted synthetic DMARDs (including JAK inhibitors)

- Eye review for uveitis and toxicity of medication
- Patient education, support (social, financial, emotional) — support club, social worker

## Systemic Lupus Erythematosus

- Multi-systemic disease of immune dysregulation characterised by circulating antibodies to nuclear and other tissue antigens (99% positive ANA).

# The Baby Bear Book

- Wide variety of clinical features, can evolve with time, affecting different organ systems; renal, neurological, cardiac disease and infection are major causes of mortality.
- Aetiology unknown, probably an interplay between genetic factors and environmental triggers.
- More common in females overall, possible due to hormonal differences. Equal sex distribution below the age of 5
- Sometimes familial.

## Evaluation and Diagnosis

- Definite diagnosis of systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) is made when four out of eleven of ACR classification criteria are present (at any time) and no alternative explanation exists.
- However, lupus should be considered when characteristic features occur in combination or evolve over time. Treatment should be started if clinical disease is significant, even if less than four criteria are met.
- High erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) and normal C-reactive protein (CRP), low complement levels (especially C3) are suggestive of active SLE.

## Other Features of SLE

- Constitutional — fever, malaise, loss of appetite, loss of weight
- Dermatological — alopecia, periungal erythema, vasculitis rash, livedo reticularis, Raynaud's Phenomenon, gangrene
- Liver, spleen, lymph node enlargement
Musculoskeletal - arthritis, arthralgia, tenosynovitis, myopathy
- Cardiac — myocarditis, endocarditis, valvulitis, Libman-Sacks vegetations, cardiac failure, Ischaemic Heart Disease
- Respiratory — pneumonitis, pulmonary hypertension, pulmonary haemorrhage
Gastrointestinal - colitis, peritonitis
Thrombotic events, APS
Neurological - central or peripheral manifestations
Fundus - exudates, cytoid bodies, papilloedema, retinopathy

## Renal Disease

- Nephritis is a major cause of morbidity and mortality in SLE.
- Can present clinically (as nephritic or nephrotic syndrome, mixed picture, hypertension, acute or chronic renal failure) or asymptomatic biochemical abnormality (haematuria, proteinuria, raised creatinine, abnormal creatinine clearance test)
- Renal biopsy — guide to management when considering immunosuppression such as cyclophosphamide or if there is doubt about the reversibility of renal damage to prognosticate
- Evaluation of lupus nephritis: Urinalysis for sediments and casts, measurement of glomerular function, serum creatinine and urea, measurement of proteinuria (using spot urine protein: creatinine ratio or 24-hour urine total protein)
