Class I: Normal
Class II: Minimal change (A)/mesangial glomerulitis (B)
Class III: Focal and segmental proliferation
Class IV: Diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis (GN)
Class V: Membranous GN
Class VI: Glomerular sclerosis

## Antiphospholipid Syndrome

- Antiphospholipid syndrome (APS) is present if one of the clinical manifestations and one of the laboratory tests on two or more occasions (at least 12 weeks

b4854_Sec-23.indd 757

12-Dec-22 11:15:09 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

apart) are demonstrated — recurrent foetal loss or vascular thrombosis (arterial and venous) with lupus anticoagulant, anticardiolipin antibody or anti-beta 2 glycoprotein 1 antibody.

- Other features not included in the classification criteria include leg ulcer, livedo reticularis, haemolytic anaemia and thrombocytopenia.

## Neurolupus

Clinical features may be due to leucostasis, vasculitis, APS with thrombosis, infarct, haemorrhage
- May be overt (seizures, stroke) or subtle (mood change, behavioural change, deterioration in schoolwork)
- Central nervous system: Cerebrovascular disease (stroke/transient ischaemic attack), seizures, headache (excluding migraine and benign intracranial hypertension), demyelinating syndrome, movement disorder (chorea), acute confusional state, anxiety disorder, cognitive dysfunction, mood disorder, psychosis
- Peripheral nervous system: Acute inflammatory demyelinating polyradiculoneuropathy (Guillain-Barré Syndrome), autonomic disorder, mononeuropathy, single/multiplex, myasthaenia gravis, cranial neuropathy, plexopathy, polyneuropathy
Investigations (Baseline)

- Full blood count, reticulocyte count, ESR, CRP, urea, creatinine, ALT, AST, GGT, C3, C4, ANA, anti dsDNA, ENA profile, lupus anticoagulant, anticardiolipin antibody of immunoglobulin M (IgM)/IgG, TGAb, TRAb, TPOAb, anti-beta 2 glycoprotein 1 antibody of IgM/IgG

- Urinalysis, spun urine for casts, urine protein/creatinine ratio

## Management Principles

- Aim to bring disease under control and prevent progressive tissue damage
- Avoidance of triggering factors (sun or UV exposure, fatigue, intercurrent infection)
- Steroids remain the mainstay for the induction of remission and treatment of acute flares
- Maintenance medication thereafter to minimise steroid toxicity, choice of medication dependent on manifestations and systems involved
- Plasmapheresis for rapid removal of antibodies and immune complexes, together with steroids and cytotoxic drugs in life-threatening SLE

## Differentiating between Lupus Flare and Infection

- Both can present with fever and ill health
- Infection can precipitate a flare out
- Infection important cause of mortality, so have a high index of suspicion
- Often, infection will have high CRP, but active lupus has normal CRP despite high ESR

# Common Rheumatological Conditions

- Look for evidence of active lupus: Rash, arthritis, serositis, low C3, high dsDNA titre
- If uncertain, may need to cover for both infection and active disease flare until investigations return

## Juvenile Dermatomyositis

- Characterised by a diffuse microangiitis resulting in cutaneous manifestations and muscle weakness amongst other complications
- Not uncommon locally, under- and/or delayed diagnosis happens often
- Aetiology unknown. May be triggered by infection

## Clinical Features

- Commonly presents with muscle tenderness and weakness, rash on face and extensor surface, constitutional symptoms
- Calcinosis, arthritis, muscle atrophy, alopecia common
- Gastrointestinal, respiratory and CNS disease potentially fatal
- Range of clinical features:

» Constitutional: Fever, malaise, loss of appetite, loss of weight
» Skin/mucosa: Heliotrope rash and periorbital oedema, malar rash, Gottron papules, erythroderma, erythematous rash, vasculitic rash, livedo, telangiectasia, periungal capillary changes, skin ulcers, oral ulcers, alopecia, panniculitis, calcinosis, subcutaneous fat atrophy (looks muscular), mechanic's hands (hyperkeratosis, fissures), palmar plantar hyperkeratosis
» Muscle: Proximal muscle weakness, truncal weakness, speech (nasal), swallowing, breathing difficulty (shortness of breath, exertional dyspnoea)
» Joints: Arthritis, arthralgia, contractures
» Gastrointestinal tract: Gut vasculitis (potentially life-threatening), pancreatitis, hepatitis
» Central nervous system: Vasculitis with seizures, stroke, chorea
» Lung: Interstitial lung disease, pneumothorax, abnormal lung function
» Cardiac: Pericarditis, myocarditis, arrhythmia
» Infection: Prone to infections of skin (calcinotic lesions, S. aureus abscess), lung (suppurative, TB, Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia)
» Other unusual presentations (fluid collections, e.g. seroma of back)

## Diagnosis

- Bohan and Peter criteria — presence of all five makes diagnosis definite, four probable and three possible

» Dermatologic features: Heliotrope rash, Gottron papules
» Proximal and symmetrical muscle weakness
» Elevated enzymes (CK, aldolase, AST, ALT, LDH)
» Typical electromyogram findings
» Muscle biopsy

# The Baby Bear Book

- Invasive EMG and muscle biopsy are not done in children unless the diagnosis is uncertain. MRI helps to localise an ideal site for biopsy as muscle disease is patchy (vastus medialis of thigh is a good site). This modality has become a replacement for muscle biopsy in recent years.
- 'Dermatomyositis sine myositis' or Amyopathic juvenile dermatomyositis — characteristic skin disease (e.g. heliotrope rash, Gottron papules, vasculitis, calcinosis) without muscle disease could be a variant, or muscle disease may have subsided or may develop later
- Important to exclude other connective tissue disease (e.g. SLE, MCTD or overlap syndrome)

## Evaluation at Diagnosis

- Baseline muscle enzymes, inflammatory markers
- Myositis specific and associated antibodies (myositis panel)
- Document rash, presence of Gottron papules and calcinosis, nailfold capillaritis, muscle power grading
- Objective evaluation of muscle weakness using Manual Muscle Testing (MMT) and Childhood Myositis Assessment Scale (CMAS)
MRI of bilateral thighs
- Screen for major organ involvement — 2D echocardiogram, ECG, CXR, pulmonary function test/HRCT thorax, barium swallow and meal
- Eye examination/referral to ophthalmologist — baseline for steroid and hydroxychloroquine toxicity monitoring

## Treatment

Glucocorticoids
- Hydroxychloroquine for control of skin disease
- Immunosuppressants: Methotrexate, mycophenolate mofetil, azathioprine, cyclosporine, tacrolimus
- Intravenous immunoglobulin
Biological and targeted therapies

## Henoch-Schonlein Purpura

- Acute small vessel leucocytoclastic IgA1-mediated vasculitis
- The commonest small vessel vasculitis affecting children in Singapore
History of preceding infection is implicated in the majority
- Self-limiting but can recur in up to a third of affected children

## Clinical Presentation

- Cutaneous Purpura (Non-thrombocytopenic)
- Classically non-blanchable palpable purpura, predominantly affecting the dependent area, especially lower limbs and buttock
- Rash may vary from petechiae to ecchymosis, purpura and bullous type

# Common Rheumatological Conditions

- Rash may persist for weeks to months
- Bruise-like rash is due to deep-seated vasculitis. These ecchymoses are often associated with underlying subcutaneous oedema
- The presence of subcutaneous oedema may precede or be associated with gastrointestinal involvement.
GI Involvement

Secondary to bowel wall swelling
» May present with bloody stool, abdominal pain (colicky if intussusception presents), acute abdomen (if perforation occurs)
GI symptoms may precede an onset of rash in a small number of children

- Arthritis/arthralgia

Presence of arthritis may be confused with underlying subcutaneous oedema. Differentiation between arthritis and subcutaneous oedema is important in the management of patient
» May be migratory; involvement of knees and ankles are common
Usually transient, does not result in deformity

- Renal Involvement

Microscopic haematuria (common), proteinuria, renal impairment, end-stage renal failure
» May develop months after onset

## Investigations

- Blood tests are often not needed unless clinical suspicion of thrombocytopenia/ coagulation defect presents
Elevated IgA level helps with diagnosis but a normal IgA level does not exclude diagnosis
- Urinalysis (to look for haematuria/proteinuria). Urine protein/creatinine ratio or 24 hours urine protein collection may be required.
- Abdominal ultrasound is sometimes required if history/clinical assessment is suggestive of intussusception (may also show thickened bowel wall, suggestive of enteritis/colitis)
- Skin biopsy is often not required (unless in atypical cases)

## Treatment

- Supportive (adequate hydration, monitoring of GI and renal involvement)
- Use of steroids is still controversial. However, the use of steroid is well-accepted in patients who have abdominal pain. In some studies, steroid use has shown to reduce the duration and severity of abdominal pain, hospitalisation stay and the need for surgery.
- Early use of steroids does not prevent the progression of renal disease or change the course of illness.
- In the presence of subcutaneous oedema, steroids may be helpful in reducing the swelling and GI-associated complications.
- Serial urinalysis at routine clinic visit is required for 2 years from the disease onset, particularly important for the first 6 months.

- Immunosuppression therapy (cyclophosphamide or azathioprine) may be necessary in refractory or relapse cases or those with renal and/or major organ involvement.

## Prognosis

- Self-limiting, good prognosis
- \(< 1\%\) develop end-stage renal failure
Recurrence rate of \(10 - 30\%\)

# CHAPTER 110

# Drugs in Rheumatology

Teh Kai Liang; Thaschawee Arkachaisri

## Introduction

A wide array of drugs is available for symptomatic, anti-inflammatory and immunomodulation of rheumatic disease to control pain, damage and progression:

- Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs)
Steroids
- Disease-modifying anti-rheumatic drugs
Cytotoxics
Biological agents
Targeted therapies

Other modalities of treatment include:

- Plasmapheresis
- Intravenous Immunoglobulins
- Bone marrow/stem cell transplant

## Clinical Pearls

- Patients on long-term steroids have suppression of hypothalamic-pituitary axis. Steroids should not be stopped abruptly, especially when the patient is sick. Stress dose may be depending on the clinical status.
- Other immunosuppressants should be stopped if:

High fever more than \(39^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\)
Evidence of sepsis/bacterial infection
» Course of illness expected to be long for specific viruses (e.g. influenza, varicella)
Unstable clinical status awaiting diagnostic tests to rule out infection

- If in doubt, contact on-call rheumatologist before stopping immunosuppressants

## Nonsteroidal Anti-inflammatory Drugs (NSAIDs)

- First-line of treatment for arthritis (together with intra-synovial steroids)
Inhibit the activity of COX enzymes
Dose-dependent effects:
Low dose: anti-platelet

763

b4854_Sec-23.indd 763

12-Dec-22 11:15:09 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

» Low-medium: anti-pyretic
Medium: analgesic
High: anti-inflammatory

## Aspirin (Acetylsalicylic Acid)

- Dose:

» Anti-inflammatory: 80–100 mg/kg/day (max. 3.6 g/day)
» Anti-platelet: 3–5 mg/kg/day

- Adverse reactions:

» Gastric irritation (50%)
» Tinnitus and/or diminished hearing (15%)
» Hepatotoxicity (50%, 15% cessation of therapy)
» Reye's syndrome (rare)
» Hypersensitivity (in asthma) (rare)
Salicylism

## Cyclo-oxygenase-2 (COX-2) Selective Inhibitors

- Selectively inhibit COX-2 enzyme with minimal effect on COX-1 enzyme at recommended doses, thus safer (gastrointestinal) and comparably effective for acute and chronic pain relief
- Potentially useful in patients with:

» Poor gastrointestinal tolerance of traditional NSAIDs
» Low platelet patients
» Sensitivity to traditional NSAIDs

## Steroids

- Among the most potent anti-inflammatory drugs. Mainstay of treatment for many severe rheumatic diseases such as SLE, JDM, vasculitis and, rarely, arthritis.
- Dramatic effects but prolonged administration at high dose gives unacceptable toxicity, therefore limit the dose and duration of treatment.
- Use as induction therapy
- Irreversible adverse reactions in children include

» Posterior subcapsular cataract
» Osteonecrosis/AVN
» Striae rubrae

- More susceptible to infection, e.g. tuberculosis (TB), herpes virus to avoid crowded places, seek treatment early if febrile, contact with chickenpox, measles. Signs of infection may be masked, so have a high level of suspicion
- Physical appearance changes: Cushingoid habitus, weight gain, rounded face, buffalo hump, acne, stretch marks, hirsutism
- Metabolic changes: Hyperglycaemia, hypercholesterolaemia, sodium and water retention, hypokalaemia, hypertension

# Drugs in Rheumatology

- Neurological: Euphoria, depression, mood lability, psychosis, pseudotumour cerebri
- Musculoskeletal: Osteopenia, proximal myopathy, avascular necrosis

## Disease Modifying Anti-Rheumatic Drugs (DMARDs)

- Onset of action slow over weeks or months

### Methotrexate (MTX)

- Effective and generally well-tolerated in children
- Give with folic acid daily, avoid sulphur drugs and alcohol
- Side effects: Bone marrow suppression (megaloblastic anaemia, lymphopenia), hepatic (enzyme elevation), gastrointestinal, including nausea, oral ulcers

### Sulphasalazine

- Before use: Ensure no allergy and/or G6PD deficiency
- Side-effects include rash, gastritis, liver dysfunction, renal dysfunction and bone marrow suppression

### Hydroxychloroquine

- Anti-malarial drug with moderate anti-inflammatory properties
G6PD deficiency - relative contraindication
- Side-effects: Macular degeneration, corneal deposit (therefore requires annual eye check), gastric irritation, skin rash (reversible, dark skin discoloration)

## Immunosuppressives and Cytotoxics

### Cyclophosphamide

- Alkylating agent with potent anti-inflammatory action, use limited by toxicity
- Side effects: Nausea and vomiting, myelo- and immunosuppression greatest in the second week post IV pulse (fall in total WBC), liver toxicity, haemorrhagic cystitis, long-term risk of secondary malignancies, gonadal damage with hormonal deficiency and sterility, SIADH

### Mycophenolate Mofetil (MMF)

- Inhibits purine synthesis in T and B cells (selective non-competitive inhibitor)
- Side effects: Bone marrow suppression, gastrointestinal, weakness/numbness, tremors

### Azathioprine

Purine analogue
- Side-effects: Oral ulcers, gastrointestinal, bone marrow suppression, liver toxicity increased by enzyme deficiency (thiopurine methyltransferase [TPMT])

b4854_Sec-23.indd 765

12-Dec-22 11:15:09 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

## Cyclosporin A

- T-cell mediated immunosuppression (calcineurin inhibitor)
- Side effects: Alopecia, hirsutism, gingival hyperplasia, tremors, paraesthesia, hypertension, renal damage, nausea and vomiting
- Drug monitoring: Trough levels (150–250 mcg/ml, but do not correlate well clinically)

## Intravenous Immunoglobulins

- First-line for Kawasaki disease, as adjunct therapy in other rheumatic diseases
Dose:

- Replacement therapy: 400–500 mg/kg/dose

Anti-inflammatory: 2 g/kg/dose

- Post IVIg haemolysis may occur, requiring a short course of oral prednisolone. To monitor FBC, retic and DCT.

## Biologic Agents

### Etanercept

- Tumour necrosis factor (TNF) receptor analogue — competitively binds TNF and inhibits natural receptors from binding
- Combination MTX and Etanercept increases efficacy with acceptable toxicity
- Side effects: Injection site reactions are not uncommon
- No increased rate of infection, but try to complete immunisation before starting etanercept; if in contact with chickenpox, give VZIG or PO/IV acyclovir

### Infliximab

- Chimeric monoclonal antibody to TNF alpha
- Risk of anaphylaxis, reactivation of TB
- Contraindicated in TB, heart failure and during intercurrent infection

### Adalimumab

- Humanised monoclonal antibody against TNF alpha
- Side effects similar to Etanercept

# Erythrocyte Sedimentation Rate (ESR)

Non-specific test that indicates the presence and intensity of inflammation
Non-diagnostic but can be used to follow inflammatory activity
Normal values

Male: 1-10 mm/hr
Female: 3-15 mm/hr

Table 23.4 Factors that Interfere with ESR Level

|  Increase | Decrease  |
| --- | --- |
|  Increase in fibrinogen, gamma or beta globulins | Abnormal RBC shape, i.e. sickle cell, spherocyte, microcytosis  |
|  Macrocytosis | Hypofibrinogenaemia  |
|  Hypercholesterolaemia | High WBC, polycythaemia  |
|  Drugs (dextran, methyldopa, penicillamine, throphylline, vitamin A) | Congestive heart failure  |
|  Technical factors | High dose adrenal steroids  |
|  Tilted tube | Cachexia  |
|  High room temperature | Drugs (aspirin, quinine, drugs causing high sugar level)  |
|  Female | Technical factors  |
|  Pregnancy | Low room temperature  |
|  Smoking | Delay test >2 hr  |
|   | Clotted blood sample  |
|   | Excess anticoagulation  |
|   | Bubbles in tube  |
|  Adapted from Wallach J. (2007) *Interpretation of Diagnostic Tests*, 8th ed.  |   |

# C-Reactive Protein

- Appears within 24–48 hours with the peak at 72 hours. Half-life approximately 18 hours
- Increase indicates active inflammation except in SLE and related diseases unless infection is present
Normal values: \(< 10\mathrm{mg / L}\)

# The Baby Bear Book

## Rheumatoid Factor

Antibodies against Fc portion of IgG
- Non-specific tests can be increased in certain infections and many other systemic autoimmune disorders
Normal value: \(< 10.3\) U/ml

## Antinuclear Antibodies

Detected by immunofluorescence (IF) or ELISA
Non-specific even for autoimmune disorders
- Could be positive in non-systemic autoimmune diseases, including infections, malignancy, old age, especially female, chronic active hepatitis
Normal: \(<  1:100\) titre

## Anti-dsDNA

- Present in \(60 - 90\%\) of childhood onset SLE (cSLE), \(\sim 90\%\) of local cSLE
- Aids in SLE diagnosis and often fluctuates with lupus disease activity, especially lupus nephritis in some patients
- Crithidia assay detects antibodies that bind to double-stranded DNA located at Crithidia luciliae kinetoplast (a protozoan organism with a double-stranded circular DNA structure at one pole). This assay detects antibodies to double-stranded DNA almost exclusively.
- Should not be used alone in following cSLE patient's disease activity or predicting flare
Normal: \(< 25\mathrm{U / ml}\) (ELISA)

## Antiphospholipid Antibodies (APA)

- Bind chiefly to negatively charged phospholipids like cardiolipin
- Three tests available that detect slightly different but overlapping populations of antibodies:

Venereal Disease Research Laboratory test false positive - limited use
Lupus anticoagulant - more sensitive functional coagulation assay
> Anticardiolipin antibodies
> Anti-beta 2 glycoprotein 1 antibodies

- Significant value: >40 GPL/MPL (IgG or IgM)

## Antineutrophil Cytoplasmic Antibodies (ANCA)

- Binds to antigens in cytoplasm of neutrophils two patterns on alcohol-fixed IIF:

Cytoplasmic ANCA (cANCA; cytoplasmic diffuse staining)
Perinuclear ANCA (pANCA; peripheral staining around edge of nucleus)

- Anti-myeloperoxidase (MPO) normal value: \(< 20\mathrm{RU / ml}\)

## Complements

- Complement cascade plays a central role in cell lysis, opsonisation of bacteria, clearance of immune complexes
Elevated in inflammation as part of the acute-phase response
- Fall with immune complex consumption (especially active lupus nephritis, cytopenia, haemolysis)

# CHAPTER 112

# Healthy Sleep in Children

Ting Chun Yi; Cheng Zai Ru

## Introduction

Many people often mistake sleep as a period of inactivity that one passively enters at the end of a day’s activities. Rather, there are many important physiological processes that actively take place during sleep. Sleep is essential for many vital functions such as growth hormone secretion, energy conservation, immune response modulation, cognition, physical performance, vigilance and mood regulation. Sleep is as crucial as the pillars of nutrition, exercise and social interactions are towards a healthy childhood.

## Normal Sleep Development

Both sleep quantity and sleep quality are equally important. Good quality sleep in children can be defined by:

- The ability to fall asleep independently within 30 minutes of bedtime
- Maintaining sleep through the night
- Waking up refreshed

Newborns do not understand the concept of day or night and may wake every 1.5 to 3 hours. They may wake due to hunger, the need for a diaper change or to be carried.

A regular diurnal rhythm is usually established after 3 months, with gradual consolidation of sleep into the night and daytime sleep organised into naps. However, only 70% of 3-month-old babies regularly sleep throughout the night. This rises to about 90% by 9 months. Night awakenings in infants still happen often and many do not have the ability to sleep through the night. Daytime naps are usually dropped from around 3 to 5 years old.

Sleep quantity gradually decreases with age and can vary from person to person. Table 24.1 illustrates the general guidelines for total sleep requirements amongst children of varying ages.

|  Age Group | Recommended Sleep Durations (hours of sleep)  |
| --- | --- |
|  Newborn 0–3 mth | 14–17  |
|  Infant 4–11 mth | 12–15  |
|  Toddler 1–2 yr | 11–14  |
|  Pre-schooler 3–5 yr | 10–13  |
|  School age 6–13 yr | 9–11  |
|  Teen 14–17 yr | 8–10  |
|  Young adult 18–25 yr | 7–9  |

## Assessment of Sleep Problems

The BEARS questionnaire provides a practical guide in obtaining a comprehensive sleep history:

|   | Pre-school (2–5 years) | School-aged (6–12 years) | Adolescent (13–18 years)  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  **Bedtime problems** | P: Does your child have any problems going to bed? Falling asleep? | P: Does your child have any problems at bedtime? C: Do you have any problems going to bed? | C: Do you have any problems falling asleep at bedtime?  |
|  **Excessive daytime sleepiness** | P: Does your child seem overtired or sleepy a lot during the day? Does he/she still take naps? | P: Does your child have difficulty waking in the morning, seem sleepy during the day, or take naps? C: Do you feel tired a lot? | C: Do you feel sleepy a lot during the day? In school? While driving?  |
|  **Awakenings during the night** | P: Does your child wake up a lot at night? | P: Does your child seem to wake up a lot at night? Any sleepwalking or nightmares? C: Do you wake up a lot at night? Have trouble getting back to sleep? | C: Do you wake up a lot at night? Have trouble getting back to sleep?  |
|  **Regularity and duration of sleep** | P: Does your child have a regular bedtime and wake time? What are they? | P: What time does your child go to bed and get up on school days? Weekends? Do you think he/she is getting enough sleep? | C: What time do you usually go to bed on school nights? Weekends? How much sleep do you usually get?  |
|  **Sleep-disordered breathing** | P: Does your child snore a lot or have difficulty breathing at night? | P: Does your child have loud or slightly snoring or any breathing difficulties at night? | P: Does your teenager snore loudly or nightly?  |

C: Child; P: Parent

### Other Corroborative History

- Sleep environment
  » Sleep props, screen use, bedroom sharing arrangement
- Factors that disrupt sleep
  » Acute: Recent illnesses, stressor, change in sleep schedule or naps
  » Chronic medical conditions, e.g. eczema, asthma, allergic rhinitis
- Medical history
  » Comorbid medical problems together with a child's distress will understandably present challenges for parents to implement behavioural strategies
- Developmental history
  » Developmental delays should be accounted for, as sleep problems reflect the child's developmental age, not chronological age
- Family history
  » Maternal depression, anxiety
  » Family members with sleep disorders
- Social history
  » Tolerance of family towards the child's behaviour at night, presence of consistence routines and/or caregivers
- Psychological history
  » Child's temperament, e.g. daytime anxiety, insecure parent–child attachments

### Clinical Examination

- Signs of chronic medical conditions affecting the child's sleep, e.g. asthma, eczema, allergic rhinitis
- Signs and presence of risk factors of obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA)

### Investigations

- Diagnostic tests usually not indicated unless concerns of symptoms and signs of underlying OSA or medical disorders, e.g. reflux or restless legs syndrome

## Common Paediatric Sleep Issues

### Limit-Setting Issues

- Typically begin after age 2 years.
- Child able to independently climb out of their crib or bed
- Inadequate enforcement of bedtime limits by caregiver
  » Requests are based on what the child has learned will elicit a parental response
  » Bedtime refusal and stalling, resulting in a delay in sleep and inadequate sleep to meet the child's needs
  » Delaying bedtime, e.g. watching more TV
  » Prolonging bedtime routine, e.g. reading another bedtime story
  » Curtain calls occurring after lights out, e.g. requests for another hug or drink of water

b4854_Sec-24.indd 774

12-Dec-22 11:15:34 AM

# Healthy Sleep in Children

» Bedtime refusal, e.g. refusal to get ready for bed, attempts to leave bedroom
• No limits
  » Falling asleep while watching TV
  » Giving the child the liberty to decide on screen use limits, allowing screen devices within easy reach of the child in the bedroom
• Inconsistent limits
  » Intermittently allowing the child to choose where and when to fall asleep
  » Limits change with the caregiver, e.g. conflicting styles amongst grandparents, domestic helper, parents

# Sleep Onset Associations

• Inappropriate sleep onset associations that require parental intervention and cannot be re-established independently during the child's night awakening (e.g. rocking, nursing, swinging)
• During normal periodic night-time arousals, the child is unable to self-soothe and return to sleep as these same conditions and associations are required for child to fall back to sleep

# Management

• Developmentally appropriate and consistent sleep schedule
  » Keep daytime naps till at least 3 to 5 years of age
• Consistent 20–30 minutes calming bedtime routine ending up in a darkened and cool bedroom free from screen devices
• Transitional objects
• Teaching the child to sleep independently and self-soothe
  » Provides significant sleep benefits yet convey no long-term adverse effects on parent–child attachment or the child's emotions and behaviour
  » Avoid responding to the infant's movements or sounds immediately during the night, allowing opportunity for the infant to self-soothe
  » Methods:
    i. Extinction 'crying it out' — may be emotionally challenging for parents
    ii. Graduated extinction — similar to extinction, but with scheduled 'check-ins'
    iii. Fading of adult intervention
• Weaning off night-time feeds
• Reward charts (for pre-schoolers)
• When to refer:
  » Child has symptoms of another underlying sleep disrupter (e.g. sleep-disordered breathing, restless legs)
  » Clinically distressing and disruptive, not responding to initial first-line behavioural strategies

b4854_Sec-24.indd 775

12-Dec-22 11:15:34 AM

## Parasomnias

Parasomnias are undesirable behaviours or events that disturb sleep and may occur during entry into sleep, within sleep or during arousals from sleep. Most parasomnias such as confusional arousals, sleepwalking and sleep terrors occur during NREM sleep, whereas nightmares occur during REM sleep. NREM-related parasomnias tend to occur in the first half of the sleep period, and children usually do not have recollection of the episode.

### Risk Factors for Parasomnias

- Positive family history of parasomnias
- Associated conditions, e.g. migraine headaches, Tourette syndrome
- Factors that trigger disorders of arousal:
  - Inadequate sleep
  - Irregular sleep schedules
  - Sleep disturbances, e.g. sleep-disordered breathing
  - Caffeine

### Types of Parasomnias

- **Confusional arousals**
  - Onset 2 to 10 years of age, typically before age 5 years
  - Child sits up in bed, cries and may appear agitated, distressed, disoriented or even inconsolable
  - Episodes are brief, lasting for about 2 to 15 minutes
- **Sleepwalking**
  - Onset 4 to 6 years of age, peak occurrence between ages 8 and 12
  - Child may sit in bed, walk around the room or house or engage in other (sometimes bizarre) activities
  - Child does not interact meaningfully with others, and speech is often unintelligible
  - May persist till late adolescence or even adulthood
- **Sleep Terrors**
  - Onset usually 4 to 12 years of age
  - Sudden arousals from sleep that are accompanied by extreme fear, often involving crying, screaming and agitation
  - Associated with autonomic manifestations such as facial flushing, hyperventilation, tachycardia, mydriasis and diaphoresis
  - Child may resist comfort with escalation of symptoms (e.g. kicking, screaming) when caregiver intervenes
    - ☐ This may be distressing to watch but episodes present no harm to the child
  - In most children, the episodes gradually improve spontaneously and decrease in frequency over the years and almost always resolve by late childhood
- **Nightmares**
  - Onset 3 to 6 years of age, peak prevalence between ages 6 and 10

b4854_Sec-24.indd 776

12-Dec-22 11:15:34 AM

# Healthy Sleep in Children

» Vivid dreams that are associated with negative emotional responses such as fear, but these emotions are not as intense as sleep terrors
» Child often fully awakes and has clear memory of the event (unlike NREM-related parasomnias)

## Evaluation

History
Usually benign
» Ask for risk factors of seizures and other sleep problems
Find out about any recent acute sleep disruptors, e.g. illness, stressor, change in sleep schedule or naps
» Development and child's behaviour are usually normal
» Family history may be present
Clinical examination: Generally normal
Diagnostic tests: Polysomnography not routinely indicated unless concerned about an underlying sleep disorder. These arousal events are episodic and may not be captured in a single night study

## Differential Diagnoses

- Nocturnal seizures: May involve stereotypic or tonic-clonic movements, happen multiple times per night, occur after the first third of the night and are usually associated with daytime sleepiness
- Nocturnal panic attacks: Child generally has similar episodes that occur in the daytime and the child has recollection of the episode the next day

## Management

- Parental reassurance that parasomnias are generally benign and sleep-limiting and not indicative of an underlying psychopathology
Pharmacotherapy is rarely indicated

Table 24.2 Main Clinical Characteristics of Parasomnias

|   | Confusional Arousal | Sleepwalking | Sleep Terrors | Nightmares  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Peak time of occurrence | First third of sleep period | First third of sleep period | First third of sleep period | Last third of sleep period  |
|  Autonomic activity | No | Maybe | Increased | No  |
|  Arousal threshold | High | High | High | Low  |
|  Getting out of bed | Rare | Usual | Sometimes | No  |
|  Recall of event | No | No | No | Yes  |
|  Terminated by | Returning to sleep | Returning to bed and sleep | Returning to sleep | Usually awaken the sleeper  |

# The Baby Bear Book

- For NREM-related parasomnias:
  - Environmental safety:
    - ☐ Safety measures — use of gates, locking the outside of doors and windows, removing sharp or dangerous objects
    - ☐ Parent notification measures — attach bell to the bedroom door
  - Healthy sleep habits:
    - ☐ Ensure sufficient sleep (as inadequate sleep can trigger such episodes)
    - ☐ Good sleep hygiene, avoid caffeine
  - Parental response during an event:
    - ☐ Avoid waking the child up as he/she is truly asleep, and attempts to awaken child usually increases agitation
    - ☐ Guide the child back to bed
    - ☐ Avoid trying to terminate the episodes
    - ☐ Avoid next-day discussions — as child cannot recollect event and may cause undue anxiety, resulting in bedtime resistance
  - Scheduled awakening
    - ☐ May help to prevent partial arousal parasomnias, especially if the episodes occur nightly
    - ☐ Parents wake their child 15 to 20 minutes before the expected time of occurrence, just to the point of arousal. This helps to modify the sleep state and thereby abort the episode
    - ☐ Should be continued for 2 to 4 weeks but may be difficult for parents to implement practically
- For nightmares:
  - Parental response: Offer comfort and reassurance
  - Reduce likelihood of nightmares:
    - ☐ Gently question child to identify any possible fearful experiences, and then try to minimise potential triggers
  - Management of frequent nightmares
    - ☐ Imagery rehearsal — develop an alternative ending to a frequently experienced dream and rehearsing that imagery during the day
    - ☐ Systematic desensitisation — develop a hierarchy of fear-inducing activities from least to most frightening, and then pair these activities with a relaxing activity that counteracts the fear response
- When to refer:
  - Night wakings resulting in injury to the child or exposure to potentially dangerous scenarios or nightmares that are extremely disruptive
  - Very frequent episodes disrupting family members' sleep
  - Has symptoms of another underlying sleep disorder, e.g. sleep-disordered breathing

# CHAPTER 113

# Obstructive Sleep Apnoea

Tan Yi Hua; Teoh Oon Hoe

## Introduction

The incidence of obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA) is estimated to be 1.2–5.7% in children of all ages.

Paediatric obstructive sleep disordered breathing exists in a continuum:

- Primary snoring — snoring without associated apnoea, hypoventilation, hypoxaemia, hypercarbia, sleep disruption or daytime symptoms.
- Upper airway resistance syndrome — partial upper airway obstruction sufficient to cause sleep disruption and daytime symptoms with no observable obstructive events and gas exchange abnormalities.
- Obstructive hypoventilation — continuous partial upper airway obstruction during sleep with hypoxaemia and hypercarbia in the absence of cyclic discrete obstructive events.
- OSA — recurrent episodes of prolonged partial upper airway obstruction or intermittent complete or partial upper airway obstruction during sleep, usually associated with hypoxaemia, hypercarbia and sleep fragmentation.

## Risk Factors and High Risk Groups

The most common risk factors in children are adenotonsillar hypertrophy, allergic rhinitis and obesity. Many other risk factors for OSA involve conditions that affect the neural control, size or collapsibility of the upper airway. Other important high risk groups are children with:

- Trisomy 21 (hypotonia, macroglossia, mid-face hypoplasia, obesity, hypothyroidism)
- Genetic disorders, e.g. achondroplasia, Prader-Willi syndrome, mucopolysaccharidoses
- Craniofacial abnormalities, e.g. Apert syndrome, Crouzon syndrome, Pierre-Robin sequence
- Upper airway abnormalities, e.g. laryngomalacia, glottic/subglottic stenosis, choanal stenosis, cleft palate
- Neuromuscular disorders, e.g. Duchenne muscular dystrophy, spinal muscular atrophy, myasthaenia gravis
Spina bifida/Chiari malformation
- Prematurity

780

b4854_Sec-24.indd 780

12-Dec-22 11:15:35 AM

# Obstructive Sleep Apnoea

- Family history of OSA

## Presentation

Habitual snoring (3 or more nights per week) is the key presentation in children with OSA. However, not all children with OSA present with a history of snoring, possibly due to limited awareness and observation by caregivers, or misinterpretation of snoring as other respiratory noises at night, e.g. wheezing, nasal congestion. Symptoms or signs suggestive of OSA in snoring children include:

- Observed cyanosis/apnoea, snorting, gasping during sleep
- Laboured/paradoxical breathing during sleep
- Unusual sleeping position, hyperextended neck
- Mouth breathing
- Restlessness and frequent awakening
- Diaphoresis
- Primary or secondary nocturnal enuresis or nocturia
- Difficulty getting up in the morning
- Unrefreshed after an overnight sleep
- Morning headaches
- Excessive daytime sleepiness
- Behavioural and learning problems, e.g. hyperactivity, inattention, aggressive behaviour, poor school performance
- Failure to thrive or obesity

Consequences of longstanding untreated significant OSA include:

- Neurobehavioural and cognitive impairment
- Growth failure
- Systemic hypertension, pulmonary hypertension, cor pulmonale
- Gastroesophageal reflux
- Insulin resistance and metabolic syndrome
- Obesity hypoventilation and death in severe cases

## History

As children with OSA may have other co-existing sleep disorders, in addition to the risk factors and symptoms above, history should also include:

- Bedtime, wake time, sleep duration and regularity on weekdays and weekends
- Sleep hygiene, e.g. activity before sleep, sleep environment
- Bedtime problems, e.g. bedtime resistance, insomnia
- Parasomnias, e.g. sleepwalking, confusional arousals

If there is moderate to severe excessive daytime sleepiness (e.g. sleep attacks, falling asleep during exams, while talking to someone), consider narcolepsy and screen in the history for cataplexy (sudden loss of muscle tone triggered by strong emotions, e.g. laughter, surprise, anger), sleep paralysis and hypnagogic (sleep onset) hallucinations.

b4854_Sec-24.indd 781

12-Dec-22 11:15:35 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

If there is restless sleep with reported leg movements, consider restless leg syndrome and periodic leg movement disorder and screen in the history for an urge to move the legs, accompanied by uncomfortable or unpleasant sensations in the legs. The urge and sensations often begin or worsen during periods of rest/inactivity (e.g. lying, sitting), are partially or totally relieved by movement (e.g. walking, stretching) and worse in the evening/night than during the day.

## Clinical Examination

- Growth parameters, BMI — obesity, failure to thrive
Blood pressure — hypertension
- Craniofacial features — mid-face hypoplasia, flat nasal bridge, facial asymmetry, retrognathia, micrognathia
- Nasal patency — signs of allergic rhinitis, deviated nasal septum, mouth breathing, hyponasal speech
- Oropharynx examination — tonsillar hypertrophy (using the Brodsky grading scale), oropharyngeal narrowing, large tongue, high-arched palate, Mallampati classification
- Noisy breathing — stridor, hoarse/weak voice
- Chest deformity — pectus excavatum
Pulmonary hypertension — loud second heart sound

## Investigations

If OSA is suspected, refer to the Sleep Clinic for review and scheduling of an overnight polysomnography (PSG), which is the gold standard for diagnosis of OSA.

While a PSG is generally preferred, there are real-world issues, including high costs, limited availability, longer wait time and tolerability of the many sensors placed on the body during PSG in very young children, especially those with special needs. Overnight oximetry is easier and less costly to perform, but has its limitations, e.g. low sensitivity, inability to differentiate between obstructive and central apnoea. The Sleep Clinic will discuss with parents about the investigation best suited to their child's situation.

Home unattended PSGs and other multichannel sleep apnoea tests are well established in the adult setting, but are not as well validated in paediatrics. These are emerging alternative investigations that may play an important role in time to come.

If narcolepsy is suspected, a multiple sleep latency test (MSLT) that is performed the next day after an overnight PSG can be considered.

## Management

Management of each child depends on the cause(s) and severity of the OSA, extent of impairment from the OSA, and co-existing medical conditions (e.g. congenital heart

b4854_Sec-24.indd 782

12-Dec-22 11:15:35 AM

# Obstructive Sleep Apnoea

disease, metabolic complications of obesity). Treatment options may involve one or a combination of the options below:

- Tonsillectomy and adenoidectomy (T&A) is the first-line treatment for children with adenotonsillar hypertrophy, with good response in most children. Outpatient surgery is not recommended in children with increased risk of post-operative respiratory complications, e.g. younger than 3 years, severe OSA, severe obesity, failure to thrive, co-existing cardiac/respiratory conditions, cor pulmonale, craniofacial abnormalities, neurological/neuromuscular disorders, Trisomy 21 and achondroplasia. These children require close post-operative monitoring. As they are also at higher risk of incomplete resolution of the OSA after T&A, a low threshold for repeat evaluation is recommended, especially if there are persistent or recurring symptoms. Nutritional counselling and physical activity advice should be provided to overweight children, as T&A may be associated with increased weight gain in children after surgery.
- Continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) therapy is indicated for long-term use in children with significant OSA, where surgery has no role (e.g. OSA due to obesity without adenotonsillar hypertrophy), or children with significant residual OSA after T&A. Bi-level positive airway pressure may be used for better tolerance in severe OSA requiring high CPAP or children with certain neuromuscular disorders. Although CPAP therapy is effective, acceptance and adherence can be a limiting factor in children. CPAP education before initiation of CPAP, behavioural interventions and supportive interventions for side effects of CPAP use can help to improve adherence with CPAP therapy. Regular titration PSGs are necessary as pressure requirements can change in a growing child.
- Medications such as intranasal steroids and/or oral montelukast may be treatment options in children with mild OSA where parents do not wish to consider surgical options. Children on medications alone for treatment should be reviewed for response, and treatment options reconsidered if there is no improvement or worsening of the OSA. Medications that help with maintaining nasal passage patency, e.g. intranasal steroids and/or nasal saline are also important adjunctive therapy for OSA, particularly for children on CPAP therapy.
- Weight loss is an important part of management for overweight children with OSA. All overweight children should be given nutritional and physical activity counselling. Referral to a weight management clinic should be considered if a child has difficulty with weight management, metabolic complications of obesity or is morbidly obese.
- Other surgical techniques such as uvulopalatopharyngoplasty, epiglottoplasty, tongue base reduction, tracheostomy, mandibular advancement and other craniofacial surgery are treatment options considered only in a select group of children with complex OSA and not routinely performed.
- Orthodontic procedures such as rapid maxillary expansion may be considered in a select group of children with complex OSA related to maxillary constriction.

b4854_Sec-24.indd 783

12-Dec-22 11:15:35 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

- **Myofunctional therapy** are specialised oral and oropharyngeal exercises strengthening dilator muscles of the upper airway that play a pivotal role in the patency of the airway during sleep. There is limited but positive evidence for its role as an adjunct treatment in mild to moderate OSA in children currently.

Most children with mild to moderate OSA due to adenotonsillar hypertrophy alone have good response to T&A with a complete resolution of symptoms. Repeat PSG after surgery to evaluate for residual OSA is indicated in children with partial resolution of symptoms, severe OSA and significant risk factors, e.g. Trisomy 21, morbid obesity. Children with high risk for OSA should have long-term follow up with the Sleep Clinic to monitor for recurrence, while children with low risk and resolution of symptoms after T&A can return to follow up with their primary care physicians.

|  Antibiotics  |   |   |
|  Amikacin | IV/IM 15–22.5 mg/kg/day Q8–24H Higher doses may be utilised for other indications (i.e. drug-resistant tuberculosis, infections caused by non-tuberculous mycobacteria, pulmonary infection in cystic fibrosis/bronchiectasis etc.). | Nephrotoxic and ototoxic. Therapeutic drug monitoring is recommended. Recommended therapeutic levels: - Target peak: 25–40 mcg/ml - Target trough: <10 mcg/ml (*ideal: 4–5 mcg/ml), *especially if existing nephrotoxic drugs or poor renal function  |
|  Amoxicillin | PO: Usual dose: 50 mg/kg/day Q8–12H Higher dose (for penicillin-resistant Streptococcus pneumoniae or severe infections): 80–90 mg/kg/day Q8–12H Max.: 4 g/day | Use higher oral doses for infections, which may be caused by intermediate susceptible/resistant Streptococcus pneumoniae, such as acute otitis media, pneumonia, sinusitis, etc.  |

Dosing is usually based on the Amoxicillin component. Use higher oral doses for infections which may be caused by intermediate susceptible/resistant Streptococcus pneumoniae, such as acute otitis media, pneumonia, sinusitis etc.  |
|  Ampicillin | IV/IM: Mild to moderate infections: 100–200 mg/kg/day Q6H Severe infections (e.g. meningitis, endocarditis): 300–400 mg/kg/day Q4–6H Max.: 12 g/day |   |
|  Ampicillin + Sulbactam (Brand Name: Unasyn) | PO (as per Sultamicillin) <30 kg: 25–50 mg/kg/day Q12H ≥30 kg: 375–750 mg/dose Q12H IV/IM: Mild to moderate infections: 100–200 mg (Ampicillin)/kg/day Q6H | Oral Ampicillin-Sulbactam (Unasyn) is formulated as sultamicillin (the oxymethylpenicillinate sulfone ester of ampicillin). Dosing is based on the total Sultamicillin component.  |

Heart failure (Maintenance dose): Preterm neonates PO: 5 to 7.5 mcg/kg/day Q12H IV: 4 to 6 mcg/kg/day Q12H Full-term neonates: PO: 8 to 10 mcg/kg/day Q12H IV: 5 to 8 mcg/kg/day Q12H 1 to 24 months: PO: 10 to 15 mcg/kg/day Q12H IV: 9 to 15 mcg/kg/day Q12H 2 to 5 years: PO: 8 to 10 mcg/kg/day Q12H IV: 6 to 9 mcg/kg/day Q12H | Therapeutic drug monitoring: Digoxin serum concentrations are monitored because digoxin possesses a narrow therapeutic serum concentration range. Digoxin serum concentrations should be drawn at least 6–8 hours after the previous dose, regardless of route of administration (optimally 12–24 hours after a dose). For initiation of therapy with loading dose given: draw level within 12–24 hours after the initial loading dose administration. For initiation of therapy without loading dose given: draw level after 3–5 days of therapy.  |

For exclusively breastfed infants, 3rd dose of 2 mg at 4 weeks of age. Treatment in neonates: IM/IV: 1 mg/dose Correction may take 12 to 24 hours, other treatments may be necessary (e.g. fresh frozen plasma, platelets) Reversal of vitamin K antagonists (e.g. warfarin): When continued anticoagulation required or no significant bleeding: IV: 0.03 mg/kg/dose (max. 1 mg/dose) for 1 dose, dose may be repeated as necessary When continued anticoagulation NOT required or if significant bleeding: IV: 0.3 mg/kg/dose (max. 10 mg/dose) for 1 dose | IM therapy is preferred (vs. oral) as incomplete oral therapy may leave the infant at risk of Vitamin K deficient bleeding. IV route may be used in preterm neonates with very low birth-weight if IM is not possible; however, it may not provide the prolonged protection of the IM route (subsequent oral doses may be required). Vitamin K is fat soluble. Phytomenadione (Vitamin K) injection can be given orally.  |

# Useful Formulae

## Emergency Intubation

Rapid Sequence Induction (RSI) drugs:

Pre-medication: Atropine (consider)
- Sedation/analgesia: Ketamine/midazolam/morphine
Neuromuscular blockade: Suxamethonium

## Medications Suitable for Endotracheal

## Administration

Naloxone

Atropine

Ventolin (salbutamol) Neb

Epinephrine (adrenaline 1:1000)

Lignocaine

## Formulae for Endotracheal Tube

Uncuffed ETT size (internal diameter) = [(age in years + 4) + 4]

Cuffed ETT size (internal diameter) = [(age in years + 4) + 3.5]

ETT length (oral) = [(age in years + 2) + 12] cm

OR ETT internal diameter ×

ETT length (nasal) = [(age in years + 2) + 15] cm

Estimated Size/Length of ETT

|  Age | Weight (kg) | Size | Oral (cm) | Nasal  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Neo | 3 | 3.5 | 8.5 | 10  |
|  3m | 5 | 3.5 | 10 | 12  |
|  1y | 9 | 4.0 | 11 | 14  |
|  2y | 12 | 4.5 | 12 | 15  |
|  4y | 16 | 5.0 | 14 | 17  |
|  8y | 24 | 6.0 | 16 | 20  |
|  12y | 38 | 7.0 | 18 | 22  |

ETT size for neonates:

<1 kg 2.5 mm

1–2 kg 3 mm

>2 kg 3.5 mm

## Normal Ranges

## Maintenance Fluid Requirements

Fluids for neonates:

For INFANTS and OLDER:

1st 10 kg 4 ml/kg/h

Next 10 kg 2 ml/kg/h

Every kg >20 kg 1 ml/kg/h

## Normal Urine Output

Neonates >0.5 ml/kg/h

Infants and above >1 ml/kg/h

## Fluid Resuscitation

Bolus volume: 10–20 ml/kg

Fluid type: 0.9% saline, 5%

albumin, blood, FFP

Total body blood volume: 80 ml/kg

## Expected Systolic Blood Pressure

<1 month of age: SBP >60 mmHg

1 month to 1 year: SBP >70 mmHg

1 to 10 years:

SBP > 70 + (age in years × 2) mmHg

# Useful Formulae

# Burns

Burn Depth:

Superficial — dry, red, no blisters

Partial thickness — wet or dry, blisters

Full thickness — insensate (black/white eschar)

Beyond full thickness — extend to deep tissues

Estimate % BSA of burn: 1 child's palm = 1% BSA

# Parkland Formula

Volume replacement (2nd/3rd degree burns):

In first 24 hours, total volume = 3–4 ml/kg per % BSA burn

Give half over first 8 hours after burn, half over the next 16 hours.

# Rhythm Disturbances

# Bradycardia

# Stable

Close monitoring

Optimise oxygenation

Consider causes

# Unstable — signs of shock present

CPR (chest compressions + BVM)

Adrenaline or Isoprenaline

Atropine (if vagal aetiology)

Consider pacing

# Tachycardia

# Stable

Narrow complex (SVT)

Adenosine

Amiodarone if refractory

Wide complex (VT)

Amiodarone

Consider lignocaine

# Unstable — signs of shock present

Narrow complex (SVT)

Synchronised cardioversion

First dose 0.5–1 J/KG; second dose 2 J/kg

Wide complex (VT)

As per SHOCKABLE RHYTHMS

# Pulseless Arrest — All Rhythms

Commence chest compressions and BVM with 100% O₂

Secure airway; obtain vascular access

# Non-Shockable Rhythms

# Asystole / Pulseless Electrical Activity

Adrenaline every 3–5 min

Consider Hs and Ts:

Hypoxaemia, Hypovolaemia, Hydrogen ions (acidosis), Hyper/hypokalaemia, Hypoglycaemia, Hypothermia

Thromboembolism, Tension pneumothorax, Trauma, Tamponade (cardiac), Toxins
